## Ch. 25 — The Door

*Written at s51, at design time.*

1. **His father was sitting down.** From just inside the door over the boy's dark near shoulder: his mother at the table with her hand flat on a sheet of paper, and his father *in a chair*. **Passed over at s51** — the subject is a posture that has never happened, and a picture cannot say *never*; it would lean entirely on twenty-four chapters the viewer has to be carrying. Candidate 3 gets the man into the chair anyway, without asking the frame to argue it.
2. **The pot of mint.** Chest-up across the table: the father back from the fire and pouring, the steam coming up between them, the boy's hands closing round the cup, the bound arm on the boards. No dialogue anywhere in it, and **the only picture in the set of Vask giving something.** Kept on the shortlist; candidate 3 covers the same table one beat later with all three faces in it.
3. ★ **Four seconds.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch25-four-seconds.png`, run 2; sheet `ch25.md`.** Run 1 lost the anchor: a laugh with the eyes creased shut took Kael's identity out with his expression and he came back nine years old. Run 2 gave the laugh its eyes and authored the hair, and both faults closed at once. The lamp and the pot of mint between them: the boy laughing *properly, once, out of the middle of him*, his mother turning her cup round with two fingers, and his father's small sound in the nose. **The only happy picture in the folder**, and the matched opposite of `ch24/the-smile` one chapter later — there a mouth moved and nothing else did; here the whole face goes at once.
4. **Everything is by the door.** Two roped canvas bundles at the dark edge of the lamp's reach and the boy turned in his chair to look at them, the count finally arriving somewhere. The one image in the chapter about what happens after it; **an object at its own distance with a face at another** is the shape `ch19/the-third-board` and `ch24/the-ice` were both rejected for, and this one only survives because the face is in the frame with it.
5. ★ **She took his face in both hands.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch25-both-hands.png`, run 2; sheet `ch25.md`.** Run 1 came back with dead faces and one hand: the working part written for her was a movement, which a still picture cannot draw, and two hands in a profile resolved to one. Run 2 rewrote both expressions as states and sited the hands against the viewer. First light on the stone step, the mountain going grey behind the house: both her hands on his face, *which she had done perhaps eleven times in his life. She looked at it. She said nothing. Then she let go.* **Repeats a picture the book already has and dodges it by composition** — `ch02/the-doorframe` is this woman in this doorway with a hand on the frame, so here the hands come off the timber and onto the boy, the door goes behind her instead of around her, and the light is cold pre-dawn instead of flat overcast.
6. ★ **What has she not got?** The father's half of the door, and the block that would pair with candidate 5 instead of 3: the arm just let go of, one turn of cloth freshly tightened, thumb well below the cut, and the boy who has just answered him. **Unspent, and deliberately left so** — the dawn is two plates' worth and only one was taken.

---

## Ch. 26 — The Third Bell

*Written at s51, at design time.*

**THE CHAPTER'S CONSTRAINT IS THE FILTER, AND IT IS PRICED ONCE HERE FOR ALL SIX.** Most of Ch. 26 is two thirteen-year-olds cutting and burning each other. `ch21/dead` was refused with nothing cut, no blood, the edge stopped clear of the skin and the adult out of frame from the elbow up — **so the boundary is a weapon held against a child, whatever the weapon is doing**, and no candidate below puts a blade in a living hand.

1. ★ **Go on, Ghost.** The road up: a man with a coil of rope over his shoulder, hat off in both hands, stepped back into the ruts — and the boy going by along the top of the ditch, a cart behind with a painted bed sheet roped to the rail and illegible. Four years of nobody turning round, reversed in one cart-length. **The safest thing in the chapter and the only one with no injury anywhere in it.**
2. **I am asking anyway.** Elarine alone at her mark before the bell — hair bound, sword in the right hand, the left open and empty and held out from the body because doctrine keeps one hand for the Art. **The empty hand is the subject, and the chapter ends with it empty for good.** Must be close on her: two figures at distance on this sand is `ch23/empty` one day earlier.
3. **The smile.** — **BUILT, RUN ONCE, DROPPED (s51, author: *drop both of those*); sheet `ch26.md`, as `the-good-part`. Do not re-propose.** Run 1 was liked and the exertion pass was composed and never run. **What it taught is kept**: the crop above the hands is what makes a fight chapter drawable, and *a face is not a state; a body under load is*. *It was the face of a boy doing the only thing in the world he had never had to be taught to want*, with red on his teeth he does not know is there. The viewer stands where Elarine stands, which puts the object of his gaze at the camera and solves what `ch18` lost two runs to; the crop goes above the hands so no blade is in the frame.
4. ★ **Both hands on her.** Severin kneeling on the white floor with both hands on her and **nothing showing** — no light, no sound, nothing coming off him — while eleven thousand people go quiet. **The folder has no picture of the Art doing nothing visible and none of Severin working.** Unspent, and the strongest thing left on this chapter.
5. **You were not kind to me.** — **BUILT AND DROPPED (s51, author: *loose the not kind to me*); sheet `ch26.md`. Do not re-propose.** Run 2 fixed both of run 1's faults and the moment was let go anyway. **What it taught is kept**: damage must not be paid for out of the features that separate a character, now in `../prompt-system.md` §Injury. The eastern gate after: her hands in her lap palms up, the sword beside her she has not looked at once, her hair out of its binding down one side, and the boy standing in front of her because there was nowhere else he was going.
6. **Valeria stands up.** The only person in the north curve on her feet, one hand on the rail, **not watching his hands and not watching his feet.** Rhymes against `ch23`'s never-built candidate 4, where the beat was that she stayed sitting.
7. **The princess forward in her chair**, one hand flat on the rail, not answering the woman behind her. **Passed over at s51:** it is `ch22/the-box` again with the hands changed — and that plate's whole argument is that her hands do nothing, so spending the same composition to show them doing something is an argument worth having later, on a chapter that is not already carrying two.
8. ★ **Aftermath.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch26-aftermath.png`; sheet `ch26.md`.** *Author-requested and author-ruled:* `scene-tests.md`'s `aftermath-kael`, promoted from reference to page as it stands. Standing on spent legs with the garment destroyed and still on him, bruised and sooted over old scars, the face emptied out. **Three departures were named and spent** — a stone courtyard, a charcoal tunic and no burn on the left forearm — and it stands as drawn. **Its block is now `../prompt-system.md` §Injury's specimen** for a battered character.
9. ★ **The fan.** *(author-requested, s51.)* — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch26-the-fan.png`, first run; sheet `ch26.md`.** Hip-up, side-on: the sword low in her right hand and a flat sheet of flame going out of her open left, low across the sand. **Her doctrine as a single picture** — one hand for the blade and one for the Art — in the last chapter it will ever be true of her. He is out of frame, which is what makes the moment drawable at all. The fire came back as a built shape on the first run, on `ch21/the-surge`'s lesson.
10. ★ **The fan, downrange.** *(author-requested, s51.)* — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch26-the-fan-downrange.png`, first run; sheet `ch26.md`.** The same beat with the camera where Kael is: the sheet of flame coming off her palm at the viewer and widening past the edges of the frame, the fire between her and the lens and lighting her from in front and below. **A second picture, not a correction** — side-on is a shape and head-on is an event. **Her doctrine as a single picture** — one hand for the blade and one for the Art — in the last chapter it will ever be true of her. He is out of frame, which is what makes the moment drawable at all.
11. **Both bundles still by the door.** He stops on the path with the house in sight and smoke off the roof-hole, and the two roped bundles are where they were. **Ch. 25's candidate 4 finally earning itself** — there the count did not arrive anywhere, and here it does.

---

## Ch. 27 — The Fog

*Written at s51, at design time.*

**THE CHAPTER'S WORST MATERIAL IS DRAWABLE AND THE FIRST PASS SAID OTHERWISE.** Candidates 5 and 6 were proposed at s51 as moments the tool would refuse — **wrongly.** `ch21/dead` was refused and then ran on a rewording and was adopted, and `../prompt-system.md` §Injury says a refusal is usually a fault in the block. The real constraint is narrower: **do not draw the stroke, draw the men.** The framing that carries each is written into its entry.

1. ★ **The sky.** *(author-requested, s51.)* — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch27-the-sky.png`, first run; sheet `ch27.md`.** Three burning stones in the air over a packed bowl, a fourth already down through three rows of timber with smoke coming up through the boards in rows — and one small dark figure standing alone and still on the sand with both hands empty. **It absorbs the paralysis beat**: no face needed to say that nothing in him is producing an instruction.
2. **One hand out.** — **BUILT, NEVER RUN, DELETED (s51, author: *delete one-hand-out*). Do not re-propose.** The block is in `git log -p` on `ch27.md`. A burning stone stopped dead in the air over the north curve with a man's length to spare, the fire on it going nowhere, and an old man on a walkway below it with one arm up. *Nothing had shown. No light. No sound.* **The folder's second Art-with-no-effect**, after `ch26`'s unspent *both hands on her*, and both are Severin, which is the character.
3. **Boats.** Through the seaward arch the grey moves and does not close: a line of hulls as far as the arch allows, another line behind it, and men already going over the side into the surf. **The hinge of the book**, and at that distance nothing violent is in frame.
4. ★ **The correction / I need you.** *(author-requested, s51: this chapter needs Kael and Valeria somehow.)* — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s51): `images/plates/ch27-i-need-you.png`, first run; sheet `ch27.md`.** Her face in front of his, his head just turned by the flat open hand, both blades still in her hands, blood on her forearm that is not hers, a burn on her neck she does not know she took. *"There. I need you."* **Composed the instant after the hand lands**, which is the framing that carries it.
5. **The gate.** From the sand through drifting smoke: the mouth of the eastern gateway with thirty men standing square in it, seen from behind and side-on, no face resolved, **nobody mid-stroke**, the ground in front of them empty and the smoke a few feet from crossing them. The page's horror is that they are good and unhurried and reset and wait — **a picture of workmen in a doorway carries that**, and what they have made stays out of frame by framing, which is what the hard rule prescribes.
6. **Marun.** *A woman said that one name about nine times while the crowd took her along the rail, and each time she said it exactly the same way.* **One resolved face inside a wash of moving small shapes**, mouth open on a word, carried past the rail — the inverse of the crowd instrument the folder already owns. Nobody down and nobody under.
7. ★ **Oss.** Three rows up, hands already going, **turned the wrong way against the flow of the whole world** to get a smaller boy off the bench behind him, with the north curve about to stand up as one thing. Kael finds him in a second and a half and never sees him again. **The last time the reader sees him**, and a picture of one person doing the opposite of everyone else.
8. **Eleven or twelve times.** The loft in the dark, a boy on his back with his hands out on the blanket, losing to somebody who is not there. The strangest and most private image in the chapter.
9. **The arena out of the grey.** It arrives all at once, close enough to touch, full to the top rail, the noise standing straight up into the fog like smoke off a fire in still weather. The dark twin of `ch22/kael-of-the-island`.
10. **Valeria out of the western gate**, hair bound, both blades, not looking at the crowd once — and the thing that goes across her face at her own name, *what a man's face does when a door shuts behind him in a corridor he has walked down a thousand times.* **Passed over at s51:** `ch14/both-hands-full` is already this girl with both blades full, and the face is the only new thing in it.
11. **Tomorrow.** The table, the mint, and the boy who stopped, with his mother standing behind her chair instead of sitting in it. **Passed over at s51:** it is `ch25/four-seconds` two chapters later — same table, same lamp, same three people.

---

## Ch. 28 — Turning Point

*Written at s53, at design time. **The author was offered the eight below and handed the pick back** (s53: *you choose*), so the two ★ were taken.*

**THE FENCE IS Ch. 27's, ON WORSE MATERIAL: you do not draw the stroke, you draw the men.** This is the chapter where a thirteen-year-old kills for the first time and a thirteen-year-old girl goes down a road like a woman going down a row of beans. Every entry below is composed on the second before or the second after, and the framing that carries each is written into it.

1. ★ **The hand back.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch28-the-hand-back.png`, first run, on the author's own optimised block; sheet `ch28.md`.** *Then the weight went off his arm and down, and Kael got his hand back and stood in the lane with it out in front of him, open, not touching anything.* Waist-up, close: the left arm out in front of him, the hand open and touching nothing, red to the elbow over the new pink skin Severin gave him yesterday, the face given three working parts and no verdict because *he reached for his own face and there was nothing there to reach for.* The man is a second out of frame below the bottom edge. **The chapter's centre.**
2. ★ **Rearranging the road.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch28-rearranging-the-road.png`, first run; sheet `ch28.md`.** *She put both hands down at her sides and set her feet and the lane came up.* Side-on, mid-thigh up: perfectly still, both blades hanging point-down, nothing coming off her hands — and three broken masses of roadbed standing up the lane away from her in a line, the near one at the height of the house roofs. **The earth twin of `ch26/the-fan`**, and the folder's first picture of her art at this scale. No body is drawn on any of them.
3. **The white on the blade.** The threading held visible the whole length of the short blade, in fog, where fifteen men can see it: *It showed. It had always showed. White, running the length of the steel.* Chest-up, the blade up across the frame. **The coat coming off the Art** — *Four years of putting it away. It took him no time at all to stop* — and the first picture of Kael's own threading. **Passed over at s53:** it is `ch21/the-surge`'s register a second time, and that plate is his father's; the moment survives the chapter and can be taken when it is not competing with two better ones.
4. **"You didn't ask them anything."** The emptied road between two walls of dry stone, chest-up, the two turned three-quarters to each other — his hands still half up with nothing to put them on, her breathing not different, looking at him for about a second before *No.* The four are below the bottom edge. **Passed over at s53:** it is `ch27/i-need-you`'s composition with the tempers swapped, one chapter later.
5. **Please. Let us pass.** Half a step forward, one hand out and open, mouth open mid-word — *It came out in his mother's accent, with the ends of the words where she had put them* — the nearest man turning round to see what spoke, and Valeria at the frame edge already moving and not looking at him. **The chapter's moral hinge as a picture of a boy asking politely**, composed the instant before she answers it.
6. **The crossroads.** Both of them stopped on open ground at the top of the village: *blood to the elbow on both arms, a cut on her jaw, her hair out of its binding on one side*, standing and breathing and not sitting down; him beside her, hands hanging, looking at the left one. Waist-up, both grounded. **Carries the death-education answer without a word of it** — and it is the only entry here that puts the two of them in one frame after the lane.
7. **The island in front of the gate.** From the top of the rise: cut stone high on the shoulder of the ground with men on it, one gate, and in front of it a crowd with no edges — back down the road, off both sides into the terraces and the ditches, over the shoulder of the hill and out of sight. Two small dark figures at the near edge. *He tried to count it … then the number came apart in his hands.* **Priced at s53 and left standing:** it is `ch27/the-sky`'s shape — a wash of thousands and one small figure — one chapter later, and the wall is not finished with the book.
8. **The man with the coil of rope.** In the ditch with his hat still on. Yesterday he stepped into Kael's path on the road down to the arena and took the hat off. Seen from the road going past, no face resolved, nothing in the frame explained. **The horror is entirely in the reader's memory**, which is the cheapest and quietest thing this chapter has.

---

## Ch. 29 — The Gate

*Written at s53, at design time. **The author handed the pick back** (s53: *you choose*), so the two ★ were taken.*

1. ★ **The eighteen inches.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch29-the-eighteen-inches.png`, first run; sheet `ch29.md`.** Both his hands flat on the edge of the open leaf, his face turned up, *"Sir. Please. My mother is on the mountain"* — and the gap a tall slot of warm light with a broad shape filling it and no features on him. **Halden's face is not drawn and cannot be** until `images/portraits/halden.png` exists (`ch13.md`).
2. ★ **The shawl.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch29-the-shawl.png`, first run; sheet `ch29.md`.** *"You're bleeding, love."* A woman past fifty crouched beside him with the corner of her own shawl against his shoulder and a stoppered bottle in the other hand, talking about somebody called Ilva. She is working and he is looking at her face. **The only warm picture in Part IV, and nothing in it is lit warm.**
3. **She let herself be seen.** Valeria alone in the strip of open ground in front of the spear line, covered to the elbows, hair out of its binding on one side, two blades, saying nothing, with the wall above and thirty thousand people behind. **Passed over at s53:** a solo Valeria one chapter after `ch28/rearranging-the-road`.
4. **Elarine on the wall.** A hundred and forty strides out and below: forty men on a stretch of parapet, two of them children, one of them stopped. *They were in and he was out.* No face resolved at that distance, which is the entry's whole point.
5. **The count.** Thirty-one thousand at the low end, taken off a strip and checked against a second strip. **Priced at s53 and left standing:** it is `ch27/the-sky`'s shape — a wash of thousands and one small figure.
6. **The mountain is not empty.** From the high ground east: eleven fires spread across four miles of ground that has thirty holdings on it, men going along the field walls the way water goes along a gutter, boats on the eastern water, and the mountain standing in the haze with its top in cloud. **The widest thing in Part IV** and the strongest unbuilt candidate on this chapter.
7. **Go back to your place.** The old woman putting the bottle away, working four steps back into the press, and standing in it — after being given the only thing he owned that was worth anything, correct to the minute and checked twice.

---

## Ch. 30 — The Houses

*Written at s53, at design time.*

1. **The fold under the terrace.** A woman and three children in a hole in the bank with dead bramble pulled across it so that it looks like it came down on its own. *"Stay until it's dark. Not dusk. Dark."* **His conscience acting, at a cost of eleven minutes he counted and carried** — and he never learns whether it held.
2. ★ **The dock leaves.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch30-the-dock-leaves.png`, first run; sheet `ch30.md`.** Down in the corner where the wall meets the bank, forearm over the bright part of his face, eyes open on purpose, six backlit men going past nine feet above his head. **His father's whole school in one frame and none of it explained.**
3. **Nobody looks up.** Flat on the tiles eight feet above a man who has stopped at the wall to look along the terrace, close enough to count the back of his neck. The chapter's cleanest piece of fieldcraft.
4. **The lamp on the tread.** A dark stair, a lamp caught before it landed and set upright on the tread still burning, and a boy going up past it. **A hand-and-object crop, and cold as anything in the book.**
5. **I'm sorry, little one.** Fifteen feet apart on a threshing floor, the big man in a plain coat with nothing on it, saying it gently in Kael's mother's accent with the ends of the words where she puts them. **Blocked:** he has no portrait and he is not finished with this book.
6. ★ **The white in all of him.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch30-the-white-in-all-of-him.png`, first run; sheet `ch30.md`. It draws the lightning coming off him, which the page forbids at Ch. 30 and describes at Ch. 33; **ruled to stand as drawn (author, s53)** and named on the sheet.** The father coming in through the gate at the height of a man with lightning held in the whole of his body and not coming off, a longsword carrying the same white and *the sword was the least of it*, in his own working jacket with the sleeves rolled the way they are rolled every morning.
7. **Carried.** One arm under the back and one under the knees, the white running down the chest against the boy's cheek and going round him, the ground going past, the mountain black above with its top in the cloud. **Passed over at s53** only because it is the same figure in the same minute in the same light as candidate 6.

---

## Ch. 31 — The Line

*Written at s53, at design time. **Neither plate is of Valeria, in her own chapter** — she is the eye in both and appears in neither, which is what she does for the whole of it.*

1. ★ **The hand flat.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch31-the-hand-flat.png`, first run; sheet `ch31.md`.** By the cistern head in the evening: a woman who has just put her own name to a wall gives her daughter instructions, and then puts her hand flat against the side of her head and leaves it there. **The mother's face is out of the frame and Elarine's is the picture.**
2. **Drusane's two hands.** *Then he took his son's face in both hands and held it, and Lucan stood and let him.* **Blocked:** no `images/portraits/lucan.png`.
3. **You were always enough.** Oss's father holding him by both arms, not mentioning the name once. **Blocked** for the reason `ch13.md` gave in the first place: there is no portrait of Oss and a plate is not where a face gets decided.
4. **Severin agreed with by the wrong man.** *There was nothing on his face at all, and she thought that a man who has just been agreed with does not usually look like that.* **Passed over at s53:** it asks the block for a face doing nothing, which §Gaze forbids and which renders as vacancy. Available to a session willing to solve it.
5. ★ **The shutter.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch31-the-shutter.png`, first run; sheet `ch31.md`.** A man goes to the window himself rather than sending anybody, closes it, comes back and finishes his sentence — with nineteen people standing round a bare table in lamplight behind him and the last of a cold daylight going out of the gap. **No face is resolved anywhere in it.**
6. **The princess's one contribution.** *"Then I do not see the difficulty,"* and the turn of a hand on the arm of a chair, with nineteen standing people and one seated girl. **Passed over:** it is `ch22/the-box`'s composition indoors.

## Ch. 32 — The Mountain

*Written at s53, at design time.*

1. ★ **The white in her hair.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch32-the-white-in-her-hair.png`, first run, on the author's optimised block; sheet `ch32.md`.** Firelight, two faces, and one loose piece of her hair hanging between them going white from the root the way frost runs across the top of a pail — with her hands on his chest and his father's hands on his shoulders. **The whole family in one crop and the father is two hands.**
2. **The two short blades.** Put down on the boards beside his right hand where he can reach them lying down, unmatched, one with a nick dressed out of it by somebody who knew how. *Kael did not say thank you, because it would have made it a smaller thing.*
3. **The briefing.** A boy of thirteen giving the ground to a captain of the royal guard, pointing with a short blade — the path, the two bends, the scree, the woodpile that will go with three men on it. *He has been telling us for four years that everything is measurable and we have all been extremely funny about it.*
4. **The princess in the yard.** In the shoes she wore on a raised floor, a soldier's cloak over a gown with gold along a hem ruined to the knee, her hair down on one side, standing where they put her and looking at the orange in the cloud and not at the house.
5. **Valeria in front of the house.** *It was a small house. One room, a loft, a door that held.* She looks at it for a long moment and asks which way the water is. **A year, a treeline, a sea court, and she still does not ask.**
6. **Oss's half-step.** **Blocked:** no portrait.
7. ★ **The lights on the mountain.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch32-the-lights-on-the-mountain.png`, first run; sheet `ch32.md`.** From the top of the woodpile he has stood on since he was four: seven lights below the bend, nineteen strung along the terrace under the olive line, one mass of light at the bottom of the path. **The deliberate inverse of `ch01/fallen-stars`.**

---

## Ch. 33 — The Breach

*Written at s53, at design time. **The chapter's worst material is not on this list as a picture** — s51's dropped grim plates are the folder's guidance on which half of a chapter like this to take.*

1. ★ **The lamp.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch33-the-lamp.png`, first run, on the author's optimised block; sheet `ch33.md`.** *"The lamp. Bring it to my left, hold it at the height of my shoulder, and do not put it down."* A princess of the empire holding a light at the height of an old man's shoulder with a dried stain down her sleeve. **The mother, the child and the work are all out of the frame.**
2. ★ **The sword.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch33-the-sword.png`, first run; sheet `ch33.md`.** The blade taken in his left hand halfway down, the grip turned over into his son's right, the boy's fingers closed on it by his father's own, one press, and let go. *Both hands. It is not a knife. You will grow into it in a year.*
3. **Facing the wrong way.** Carried down the slot over an old man's shoulder, awake, arms swinging, the sword still in hands that would not open — watching the yard, the pens, the grey flat and the roof of the only house he has ever lived in take at one corner and go up. **The strongest unbuilt image left in Book One.**
4. **Walking out with nothing in either hand.** The father crossing his own ground toward four hundred men, seen from behind, empty-handed, after the sword has changed owner.
5. **The mountain going.** An old man on one knee at the eastern edge of the yard with both palms flat on the stone, and four hundred feet of terrace and olive line letting go and going down the way water goes down.
6. **Kael holding his brother.** *He weighed about as much as a lamb. Less.* — a fingernail, a strip of linen, and four seconds of being completely and unspeakably happy. **Deferred at s53** to a chapter that can hold it without the mother's body or the newborn's eye in the frame; `ch36/the-finger` is where it went.
7. **Nobody was surprised.** The mother watching her elder son's face and not the child's, from the floor. **Blocked by the seal**, not by the tool: what it draws is a reaction to a thing this sheet has kept out of frame.

---

## Ch. 34 — The Run

*Written at s53, at design time. **Both plates are the mother, which is the chapter.***

1. ★ **The elbow.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch34-the-elbow.png`, first run; sheet `ch34.md`.** *She took the girl's elbow and moved it about an inch, and looked at the result, and let go.* A newborn going into a princess's arms on a shelf of rock, with the instructions given by hand. *"You will be extremely good at this."*
2. ★ **The bowl.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch34-the-bowl.png`, first run, on the author's optimised block; sheet `ch34.md`.** From eight feet behind her: the ground taken bald for eighty strides in a line as clean as a scythe cut, four hundred fires gathered into one thing two hundred strides wide, and three old olive trees coming apart along the grain into long white strips going out flat. **Her face is never on the page during it and it is not in the picture.**
3. **Elarine lighting the bowl.** Nothing coming off her hands, nothing crossing the air, and three hundred strides of hillside catching at once exactly where the fuel was. **Passed over:** the same picture as candidate 2 with a different woman in front of it.
4. **Aurelian talking.** One good hand out and not touching a princess, correct in every particular, for two miles, for nobody. *"Put your foot flat, do not go on the toe of it, flat, all of it, like a horse — that's it."* **The warmest thing in Part IV.**
5. **Both faces wet.** Mother and son running side by side with the water coming down both of them and neither mentioning it, their eyes going over each other for something under a second.
6. **Eleven feet of live wood.** Root and thorn driven up through solid rock across the mouth of a cut in half a minute, with three-inch thorns standing off it, by an old man bleeding out of the nose.
7. **I have got you. I love you.** **Passed over at s53:** a hand on the side of a head for the third time in four chapters.

---

## Ch. 35 — The Range

*Written at s53, at design time.*

1. ★ **The white room.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch35-the-white-room.png`, first run, on the author's restructured block; sheet `ch35.md`.** Eight people on the boards of a boat inside a room made of steam, the walls of it standing on the sea and going up past the mast, lit from underneath through the water, and the sky above it open and black. **The best thing in Book One that nobody had drawn.**
2. ★ **The spotter.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch35-the-spotter.png`, first run, on the author's optimised block; sheet `ch35.md`.** A boy kneeling at the strake with a day-old child up on his shoulder and his hand flat on its back, mouth wide open on a shouted word, calling a man's feet for his mother. **Two open mouths and neither of them is heard.**
3. **The mother in the bow.** Standing up in it facing the island with her hands loose at her sides, and then not choosing any more. **Passed over:** `ch34/the-bowl` with water in front of her instead of stubble.
4. **The sail going over the side.** Valeria up on the thwart with a wet coat and Aurelian taking the bottom of it one-handed, walking a burning sail over the side while it is still going.
5. **Cut it.** Elarine getting up off the boards with nothing left in her and cutting the mast away.
6. **Getting smaller.** Lying along the strake watching the man who killed his father stand on a rock with his hands behind his back. **Blocked:** no portrait of him, and `ch13.md` says the work that unblocks that is a portrait.

## Ch. 36 — The Quiet

*Written at s53, at design time. **The last two plates of Book One.***

1. ★ **The finger.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch36-the-finger.png`, first run; sheet `ch36.md`.** A fist no bigger than two joints of his finger, closed on it and not letting go, and a boy sitting completely still on the boards of a drifting boat counting the breathing. **The first picture in the set where his hands are doing nothing at all.**
2. **The loaf.** A day-old loaf broken by hand and sent down a boat from person to person, because *it will not be worth eating tomorrow.* **Wants eight resolved faces in a hull forty feet long.**
3. **The boat words.** The old man giving him twenty of them in an hour at the tiller and making him say them back in a different order. *"Good. Tomorrow I'll teach you some more."* **The strongest single thing left unbuilt in Book One, and the folder still has no plate of Severin.**
4. **The princess holding him.** Sitting down on the wet boards in what is left of a gown with her ruined hands under a herder's child, getting the head wrong and correcting it before anybody said anything. **Passed over:** `ch34/the-elbow` one day later.
5. ★ **No lights.** — **BUILT AND ADOPTED (s53): `images/plates/ch36-no-lights.png`, first run, on the author's optimised block; sheet `ch36.md`. THE LAST PLATE OF BOOK ONE.** The island black on the horizon at about the width of two hands, the orange gone out of the cloud, a sword he cannot lift lying along the boards — and nothing lit anywhere on it. **The third picture in the `ch01/fallen-stars` — `ch32/the-lights-on-the-mountain` rhyme.**
6. **The inventory.** A knife, a length of cord and a flat grey stone laid out on the boards between them. *That is what I have. You have a knife, a bit of cord and a stone. And I have you, and I have him.*

---

*Book One closed with thirty-six chapters and two plates each. The chapters below run on from Ch. 37 in `manuscript/book-two/`; the register, the anchors and the rules are unchanged.*

# FILE: plates/ch01.md

# Ch. 1 — The Stars That Fell · plate sheet

Three candidates from `candidates.md` built as prompts. **None has been run.** Nothing here is adopted; when one is, the adopted note goes above its block and the image goes in `images/`.

**WHY THESE THREE TOGETHER.** They are three different kinds of picture — a two-figure night exterior, a single-figure gesture, an interior with nobody in it — and **all three are lit from below.** That is an accident of the chapter and it is the reason to run them as one batch: the portrait system's fixed lighting is soft and directional and has never once been asked for a source under the subject. If low warm light works here, Aurelian's candle in Ch. 9 is unblocked.

**ANCHORING COST, and it is the real cost of Part I.** Kael at four and both parents exist as **head-and-shoulders portraits only**; there is no full figure for any of them at any age below fourteen. So in plates 1 and 2 everything below the collar is authored in the prompt and anchored to nothing, and **it will not repeat in the next Part I plate.** Plate 3 has no reference at all. Two of these three pictures share a face with the library and share a body with nothing.

**THE GREY: FRAMED OUT BY DESIGN, RULED ON BY THE AUTHOR IN THE RUN.** The plan was that no plate may show Kael's eyes while the grey is unspent, so this one puts both faces away and says so twice. The run turned his face toward his mother anyway, and **the author ruled the eyes not visible and adopted the image (s40).** The rule is unchanged and still binds the next plate; what moved is one picture, on the author's reading of it.

**THE INHERITED COLOUR PARAGRAPH HAD TO BE OVERRIDDEN IN TWO OF THE THREE.** The house style ends *the warmth belongs to the skin alone, not the paper or the cloth* — written for portraits, where the only warm thing is a face. A plate lit by a fire or by a town has its warmth landing on timber, stone, wool and rock, and one of these three has no skin in it at all. Both blocks now say so in their own words. **This is a general plate problem, not a Ch. 1 problem**, and it is written up in `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Plates.

**DECIDED, so it is not defaulted:** Neris is **not pregnant** in this chapter. Kael is four; the pregnancy belongs to the tournament season nine years later.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: fallen-stars

**ADOPTED — the first plate in the book.** `images/plates/ch01-fallen-stars.png`, first run. Anchored on `images/portraits/kael-4.png` and `images/portraits/neris.png`.

**THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S REVISION AND IS VERBATIM AS RUN.** It is not the block this sheet first generated — that one was rewritten before it was ever run, and what it produced is not recoverable from it. `PLATE-VERBATIM` means the build script emits this block untouched, with no style header prepended, because the text already carries its own. **Do not reassemble it.**

**THE EYES: AUTHOR RULING (s40).** The boy's face came into frame turned toward his mother rather than down at the water, and the ruling is that **his eyes are not visible in the adopted image and the plate is legal.** The picture is adopted as drawn. What the ruling does not do is move `03` or the brief: Kael's grey stays unspent on the page, and the next plate still frames it out.

**WHAT THIS RUN PROVED, and the last one is the one that travels:**

1. **The house look survives into a plate.** Granulation, ink linework, wash blooms, bare paper, muted and earthy — no drift, on the first run, in a night exterior. This was the open question and it is answered.
2. **Costume ownership held between two figures in the same undyed wool.** Pale oatmeal blanket on the boy, mended grey-brown layers on the woman, no bleed either way. The rule holds outside the scene tests it was written in.
3. **The stars did not compete with the lamps.** The worry that two reserved-paper elements would claim the same value slot was wrong; the lamps won on warmth alone.
4. **AN IN-FRAME WARM SOURCE IS NOT LIGHT FROM BELOW.** The lamps render beautifully *as* a source and throw nothing: no warm rim on the shoulder, no lit edge on the blanket, the figures lit by ambient sky. **So Aurelian's candle in Ch. 9 is still unproven** — a face lit from below is a different and harder ask, and nothing here has tested it.
5. **A described absence loses to a described thing.** *Neither face is visible* did not survive contact with a renderer that knows what a carried child does; it turned his face to his mother. A positive instruction — what the viewer sees instead of a face — is the version to write next time.
6. **Word-described child proportion lost to an attached face reference.** The boy reads several years above four. When a picture hides the head, the reference is arguing for an age the prompt cannot outvote.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy — take his hair colour and
colouring from it only. He is much younger here than in the
reference; ignore its age entirely.
Image 2: portrait reference of the woman — take her hair, colouring
and apparent age from it only.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, visible paper tooth and pigment
granulation, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms with hard irregular
edges, bare unpainted paper reserved for the lights.

Setting: a bare shelf of rock high on a mountainside at night, open
sky above, the ground falling away steeply in front of them. Loose
wash suggestion only — the rock implied in a few dry-brush strokes,
no detail anywhere, everything past the two figures going down into
deep cool wash.

Composition: both figures FULL LENGTH, SEEN FROM DIRECTLY BEHIND at
about their own height, the view angled slightly downward past them
so the drop opens beyond the shelf edge. The two of them small in the
frame and set high in it, the drop and the water far beneath them
filling the lower two thirds of the picture. A woman stands at the
edge of the shelf with a small boy carried on her hip, wrapped to the
chin in a large coarse wool blanket. Both are facing away from the
viewer, out and down over the drop.

THE SILHOUETTE: woman and boy read as one dark upright shape against
the drop, his head clearly above her shoulder on the far side, her
carrying arm making the break between them. Do not merge them into a
single bulk and do not separate them into two standing figures.

NEITHER FACE IS VISIBLE, AND THAT IS DELIBERATE. The woman is seen
from directly behind — the back of her head is what the viewer sees.
The boy's face is turned down and away, mostly lost in the wool and
in shadow. NO EYES ARE VISIBLE ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE. Do not turn
either head toward the viewer.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND:
Her left arm is under the boy, carrying his weight against her hip.
Her right hand is up at his jaw, fingers resting along it and turning
his face DOWNWARD — away from the sky, toward the water far below.
The touch is light; she is showing him something, not holding him
still. Both her hands are in the frame and both are occupied. The
boy's hands are inside the blanket and not visible. Nothing else is
in any hand. Neither of them is carrying a light.

THE LIGHT COMES FROM BELOW, AND IT IS THE SUBJECT. Far down at the
foot of the mountain, where the land meets the sea, a heap of small
warm orange lights lies scattered along the shore — a town's lamps at
night, gathered in one corner where the shore meets the water,
doubled and broken in the black water beneath them, the reflections
trembling and slightly displaced. The lamps and their reflections are
reserved as bare unpainted paper with a warm tint only at their
edges: they are the lightest value in the picture, the only warm
colour in it, and the one fully resolved thing in an image that is
loose everywhere else. Their light reaches the two figures only
faintly — a thin warm rim along the woman's shoulder and the near
edge of the blanket, and nothing more. Everything above them is cold:
rock, cloth, hair, sky. A few small cold stars in the upper sky,
picked out as tiny reserved flecks, clearly separate in colour and
character from the warm lights below.

Nothing is happening. Nobody is in danger, nobody is hurrying. It is
a still, mild night and the two of them are standing looking down.

Bare skin is nearly absent from this picture: her two hands are the
only skin anywhere in the frame, small and held in cool shadow, no
warm ochre in them. No other skin is visible.

THE WOMAN: a grown woman of a mountain village. Tall, straight and
completely still. Long dark hair falling loose past the shoulders,
brown rather than black, kept low and desaturated — the warmth is in
the hue only, never in the light. Plain coarse village working cloth,
much mended, in dull grey-brown and undyed oatmeal. No ornament of
any kind.

THE BOY: four years old and built like it — the head large in
proportion to the body, the limbs short and soft with no length in
them at all, the whole child small enough to sit on one hip. Wrapped
to the chin in coarse undyed wool in dull oatmeal, dark hair on the
crown of his head.

Do not mix their cloth: the blanket belongs only to the boy and
covers him chin to feet; the mended working clothes belong only to
the woman. They must read as two separate garments, not one
continuous wrap.

No other figures anywhere in the frame. No buildings, no fences, no
animals, no boats. No fire, lamp or torch anywhere near them — every
light in this picture is far below them.
```

Image 1: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: the yard of a small stone-and-turf mountain house in a
storm, as loose wash suggestion — a low roof-line with slates on it,
a fenced goat pen, the mountain beyond lost in grey wash. Rain across
at a steep angle in dry-brush drag and flicked spatter. Flat cold
storm light from a low sky; the only warm value is skin.

Composition: the man waist-up, turned away from his own raised hand
toward a goat in the pen at the left, mid-sentence, talking to the
animal.

His right arm is straight up above and behind his head and his right
hand has closed on ONE flat grey roof slate, held flat in the palm,
caught and stopped, not falling and not being thrown. His left hand
is down at his side, empty.

HE IS NOT LOOKING AT THE SLATE — his face is turned to the goat,
mouth open mid-word, his expression unchanged for the catch: level,
unhurried, giving nothing, no effort anywhere in the body. Do not
have him look up at it, and do not make him alarmed, braced, heroic
or surprised.

One goat in the pen, unsettled by the weather, looking at him.

THE MAN: broad through the shoulders and no further, heavy across the
top of the back with nothing wasted below it. Iron-dark hair of
medium length, wet, pushed back off the forehead. Weathered hard.
Coarse undyed working cloth soaked dark, plain collar closed to the
throat, THE INK CARRYING THE CLOTH in dense scratchy linework at the
seams and the mended places.

### PLATE: room-of-true-things

*Candidate 3.* No anchor at all — the test of whether the house look survives with nothing to hold it.

```
No reference image.

Subject: the single room of a small, poor, very well-kept mountain
house at night, seen from ABOVE — from the height of a sleeping loft,
looking down over the edge of the loft floor, so a roof beam and the
near edge of rough boards frame the top of the picture. Interior
only, the whole room in one view.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS A SMALL FIRE DOWN IN THE FLOOR: a square stone-
lined hole in the middle of the room with a low contained fire in it,
banked, not a blaze, no flames reaching. It throws warm light up the
walls and up the underside of the table, the beams and the tools, and
leaves the corners and the roof in deep cool shadow.

In the room, readable at a glance: a heavy hand-made table; two low
beds against one wall with folded coarse wool on them; a closed
wooden chest; one heavy plank door, shut, with a wooden bar down
across it in iron brackets. And ONE WHOLE WALL OF TOOLS — a dozen or
more hand tools on pegs in deliberate order, each with its own place,
evenly spaced, every one hanging straight and level and not one
crooked. After the fire it is the most important thing in the frame.

NOBODY IS IN THE ROOM: no figures, no hands, no silhouettes, nobody
in the beds, nobody at the door.

Colour: muted, earthy, desaturated — dull grey-brown timber, dead
olive wool, cold grey stone, and the single warm orange source down
in the floor. There is no skin in this picture; the warmth belongs to
the firelight and falls on timber, stone and wool.

His father split wood in one stroke, always. Crack. "Not this winter." / "Next winter?" / "Perhaps." Crack. / Kael nodded and went to tell the goat.*

**IT TESTS THE TWO THINGS THE FALLEN STARS LEFT OPEN.** (1) The boy's face is away from the viewer for a **staged reason** — he is walking to the goat — rather than by a described absence, which is the failure mode that turned his face to his mother last time. (2) **No face reference is attached for him**, so word-described child proportion has nothing arguing against it. Image 1: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: the yard of a small stone-and-turf mountain house in winter,
as loose wash suggestion — a low roof-line, a woodpile, a fenced pen,
snow on the ground and the roof, the mountain beyond going up into
pale wash. Bare unpainted paper carries the snow. Flat cold overcast
daylight, no warm source anywhere.

Composition: full length, three subjects on the same ground plane,
reading left to right — THE MAN at a chopping block, mid-stroke; THE
BOY small in the middle distance, walking away from the viewer and
away from his father toward the pen; THE GOAT in the pen at the
right.

THE BOY IS FOUR AND MUST READ FOUR: the top of his head no higher
than the man's hip, the head large for the body, shoulders narrow and
soft, arms and legs short, the walk slightly wide the way a small
child walks in snow. Seen from directly behind — the back of his
head, his back and his heels, no feature of his face drawn. Plain
undyed wool, coarse and much mended and a little too big for him.

The man's two hands are together on the haft of one axe, raised above
and behind his head at the top of the stroke, nothing else in either
hand. He is looking down at the block, not at the boy — level, mouth
closed, unhurried, already back at work. Coarse undyed working cloth,
much mended, closed at the throat, THE INK CARRYING THE CLOTH at the
seams and the mended places.

ONE GOAT, side-on in the pen, ITS HEAD TURNED AWAY, wholly
indifferent — not curious, not friendly, not alarmed, not attentive.

### PLATE: talking-the-goat-down

*Author-requested (s40): the warm one.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png` for the man; **no reference for the boy**, as with `telling-the-goat`. Ch. 1 canon: *once, when a slate slid off the roof in a storm, his father's hand went up and the slate was in it, and his father kept talking to the goat the whole time, because the goat was upset about the storm.*

**HOW THE WARMTH IS CARRIED, and this is the whole design.** `03` and the brief both forbid warmth in Vask's face — *nothing in his eyes that is angry and nothing that is anything; not hostile, not cold, not sad, not kind.* A tender expression would be the wrong man, and the sheet does not move for a picture. So the warmth is staged instead: **he is down on the animal's level, his hand is flat on its neck, he is mid-word, and the boy is tucked in against his side** — and the animal has stopped being frightened, which is the only evidence in the frame that anything kind is happening. The face stays exactly as level as it is everywhere else.

**The slate is deliberately not in this picture.** `the-catch` owns that object and it would split the frame's attention; here the subject is the talking, not the hand.

**Third test of the same question:** no reference for the boy, and his face away for a staged reason — he is watching the goat.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: the yard of a small stone-and-turf mountain house in a
storm, as loose wash suggestion — a fenced pen, a low roof-line, the
mountain lost in grey wash. Rain across at a steep angle in dry-brush
drag and flicked spatter, everything soaked and dark. Flat cold storm
light; the only warm value is the skin of the man's face and hand.

Composition: three figures close together and touching, cropped at
the knee, on the same ground plane. THE MAN down on one knee in the
mud at the goat's level, turned in toward it. THE GOAT in front of
him, side-on, its head near his. THE BOY at the man's side, pressed
against his shoulder, very small.

A LARGE MAN FOLDED DOWN SMALL is the subject. His right hand is flat
and still on the goat's neck, fingers spread, not gripping and not
scratching; his left rests open on his own raised knee; the boy's
near hand holds a fold of the man's sleeve. Nothing is held in any
hand and there is no tool, rope or bucket in the picture.

HE IS TALKING TO THE GOAT: mouth open mid-word, face turned to the
animal and not to the boy. HIS EXPRESSION DOES NOT CHANGE FOR ANY OF
IT — level, unhurried, giving nothing, as unreadable as a man
discussing weather. NOT smiling, NOT tender, NOT soft, NOT
gentle-faced. The kindness is in where his body is and what his hand
is doing.

THE GOAT HAS STOPPED BEING FRIGHTENED: soaked, standing still, head
lowered slightly toward the man, ears loose, no white in the eye.

THE BOY IS FOUR AND MUST READ FOUR: his head no higher than the
kneeling man's shoulder, head large for the body, arms and legs
short. His face is turned away from the viewer and down toward the
goat — the back and side of his head only, no feature drawn. Dark
hair plastered flat with rain.

Both wear coarse undyed mended wool soaked dark — the man's heavy and
sized to him, the boy's thin and too large — THE INK CARRYING THE
CLOTH at the seams and the mended places.
```

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch01-vask-kael-goat.png`, first run, author-written. Anchors: `images/portraits/vask.png`, `images/portraits/kael-4.png`.

**A COMPOSITE OF THE CHAPTER'S WORLD, not one of its beats** — the mountain, the pens, the goat, the father and the boy at four are all Ch. 1, and the picture stands them together rather than staging a moment the chapter stages. **Nothing unspent appears in it**, so it costs nothing; recorded here because it is a looser fit to the page than `fallen-stars` and the next session should know that was deliberate.

```
Image 1: reference portrait of Vask.
Image 2: reference portrait of Kael, aged four.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: high mountain pasture as loose wash suggestion — thin grass
and bare rock underfoot in dry-brush, the ground falling away behind
them, a distant ridgeline in pale flat grey wash, the sky left as
bare paper. Low side light, late in the day, long soft shadow.
The man, the child and the goat are all grounded on the same ground
plane.

Composition: full figure, all three in frame, head to feet, feet
included. The man standing, the child beside him and slightly
forward, the goat on the child's far side.

SCALE — the child is FOUR YEARS OLD and must read four:
The top of his head reaches roughly the man's hip.
His head is large for his body, roughly a fifth of his total height.
Short limbs, a round torso, no waist, no length in him anywhere.
The goat's shoulder is at about the child's chest.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND:
Vask's right hand rests flat on the child's upper back, between the
shoulder blades — resting only, not gripping, not pulling him in.
Vask's left hand hangs empty and open at his side.
Kael's right hand is flat against the goat's shoulder, fingers buried
in the coat.
Kael's left hand hangs empty at his side.
Four hands, nothing held in any of them. No crook, no staff, no rope,
no lead, no pail, no bell and no tool of any kind anywhere in the
frame.

ONE GOAT, and only one — a shaggy mountain milch goat, curved horns,
standing square on all four feet, side-on to the viewer. No second
goat, no herd on the slope behind, no dog, no bird, no other animal
anywhere in the picture.

NOTHING IS HAPPENING. This is an ordinary afternoon on the hill. No
threat, no event, no drama, nothing approaching, nobody reacting.

THE ATTENTION RUNS IN ONE DIRECTION AND IT IS THE SUBJECT OF THE
IMAGE. The man is looking down at the child. The child is not looking
back — he is looking at the goat. The goat is looking out of the
frame. Three separate lines of sight and no two of them meet.

VASK: standing still and square, weight even, shoulders level,
nothing braced and nothing on display. Perfectly still — a man who
moves exactly as much as is required and is not moving now. Broad
across the top of the back and the neck, nothing wasted below it, an
economy rather than a bulk. His head is tipped down toward the child,
chin lowered, the face angled down but still turned enough that it is
fully visible in three-quarter view; he is looking at the boy and
only at the boy. Iron-dark hair to the jaw, roughly cut long ago and
grown out, greying early: pale strands scattered evenly right through
the dark mass, thickest at the temples, plainly visible at a glance —
scattered, never gathered, no streak, no seam, no patch. Weathered
hard, the wear on the surface and the face under it young. Coarse
undyed working cloth, heavy weave, much mended, in dull grey-brown
and dead olive. The collar is closed to the base of his throat and
BOTH SLEEVES ARE FULL LENGTH AND DOWN TO THE WRIST — no skin showing
on the forearm, nothing rolled or pushed up.

THE CONTRADICTION, AND IT MUST SURVIVE: he is looking at his son and
his face is giving nothing away. The attention is entirely on the
child; the expression is not. Level, mouth closed, brows unmoved,
completely unreadable — NOT fond, NOT warm, NOT tender, NOT proud,
NOT sad, NOT worried, not smiling, not softening. A man looking at
something he is simply keeping account of. Do not resolve this into
an affectionate father looking down at his son.

KAEL: standing easily, weight on both feet, leaning very slightly
into the goat. His head is turned down and out toward the animal,
chin lowered, the face angled away from the man but still visible in
three-quarter view from the front; he is looking at the goat's head
and nowhere else. Expression OPEN and entirely unguarded, absorbed in
the animal, wholly present, nothing withheld and nothing watchful — a
child who has never once had a reason to conceal anything. Mouth
closed and relaxed. NOT flat, NOT still, NOT level, NOT guarded, NOT
wary, not sad, not solemn. Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and
colourless, large and wide open in a small face. Dark hair, fine and
soft, cut short and uneven at home by somebody who was not being
careful. High colour across the cheeks and nose from the cold; cool
grey dust on the skin and in the creases of the neck — the grime
stays cool grey, never warm brown. A child's skin under it, no
weathering and no hardness anywhere in it. Plain undyed wool, coarse
and much mended and visibly too big for him, sleeves swallowing his
wrists, in dull oatmeal and dead olive.

Do not mix their clothing. Both wear undyed mended village cloth, and
the difference is fit and closure only: the man's is heavy, sized to
him and closed at the throat; the boy's is thin, loose and too large.
Neither wears any ornament, buckle, badge or metal of any kind.

Preserve both faces exactly as shown in the reference images — bone
structure, hair, colouring and apparent age. The references show head
and shoulders only; extend both to full standing figures. Kael's eyes
near-white grey, Vask's iron-dark near black. Do not blend their
features.
```

# FILE: plates/ch02.md

# Ch. 2 — Part of the Mountain · plate sheet

**None of these has been run.**

**THE AGE IS UNPINNED AND STAYS SO.** Kael is four in Ch. 1 and eight in Ch. 3; Ch. 2 names no number, and the sheet does not invent one. **The scale-by-measurement finding solves this** — every block below gives his height as a ratio (against the fence post, against his father's ribs) and never states an age. *An unpinned age is not a problem for measurement; it is what measurement is for.* Attach `kael-4.png` for hair and colouring only, and let the ratios do the age.

**THE GREY IS FRAMED OUT** in all three, and in this chapter the page does the work for us: the long game requires his face down, and the eyes are at distance or turned away throughout.

**NERIS IS NOT PREGNANT HERE.** Decided, not defaulted — the tournament season is years off.

### PLATE: long-game

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch02-long-game.png`, run 2 — the first run after the geography fix, and the block below is what produced it.

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-4.png` (hair and colouring only). Ch. 2 canon: the boy at the foot of the fence post, and the six-oared boat with its oars folded, far below.

**WHAT THE ADOPTED RUN CONFIRMS:**

1. **ALTITUDE AS A CAMERA POSITION WORKS, AND IT WORKS FIRST TRY.** Naming where the viewer stands, forbidding the shoreline by name, and stacking the frame in thirds put the world back on a mountain after run 1 had built a beach. The three instructions were given together, so which one carries the weight is unknown — **keep them together until something forces them apart.**
2. **SCALE-BY-MEASUREMENT HOLDS A SECOND TIME, and the self-check clause is worth reusing.** *The entire boat is smaller than the boy's head; if the boat is bigger than that, the viewpoint is wrong and the picture has failed* — the boat came back at exactly that size. Giving the renderer a condition it can test itself against appears to be stronger than giving it a description.
3. **BARE PAPER NEEDS THE ABSENCE SPELLED OUT.** Run 1 painted clouds into a sky asked for as bare paper. *No clouds are drawn at all, no cloud shapes, no cloud edges, nothing rendered in it* got flat paper. Same shape as every other finding here: name the absence.
4. **REMOVING A FIGURE BEAT HIDING ONE.** The woodpile is in frame and empty, and it is a better sentence about the father than any drawn shape would have been.

**THE FATHER IS NOT IN THIS PICTURE (author ruling, s40), AND THAT IS THE FAITHFUL VERSION.** Run 1 drew him standing and obvious; the revision tried to specify a figure that resolves badly on purpose — same value as the woodpile, outline broken by the logs, head below the top of the stack. The author's call is simpler and better: **leave him out.** The page says *his father was four steps away and then there was no father, only a shape by the woodpile that the eye slid off*, and a man who is genuinely hard to see is, at the limit, a man you do not draw. **The woodpile stays in the frame with nobody at it**, which is exactly what Kael is looking at when he tells himself the shape is his father.

**So the hard-to-see-figure test is retired unrun.** If a picture ever truly needs one, the technique drafted for it is in this file's history.

**RUN 1 ALSO FAILED ON GEOGRAPHY, AND THE CAUSE IS THE FINDING (s40).** The first version put the altitude in its Setting paragraph and then staged the frame as *read it left to right: the boy, the man, the boat.* **That is a flat instruction, and staging beats intent** — the run built a shoreline at sea level with the boat close inshore, which is the wrong world: this book is a house high on a mountain with the sea a long way below it. **Altitude is a camera position and a vertical stack, not a setting.** Written up in `../scene-prompt-system.md`. Run 1 also gave the boat two oars in the water instead of six folded up, and painted clouds into a sky asked for as bare paper; both are stated harder below.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy — take his hair and colouring
from it only. His face is barely visible in this picture.

THE VIEWER IS HIGH ON A MOUNTAIN, LOOKING OUT AND STEEPLY DOWN. This
is the first thing to establish and everything else depends on it.
The yard is a narrow shelf of ground near the top of a mountain; the
sea is very far below it and a long way off. We are looking down at
the water from a great height, the way you look down from a clifftop,
not across it from a shore. THERE IS NO BEACH, NO SHORELINE AND NO
WATER'S EDGE ANYWHERE NEAR THE BOY.

THE PICTURE IS STACKED VERTICALLY, not arranged left to right:
— the bottom third is the yard itself: thin grass, bare rock, a
  post-and-rail pen, a woodpile against a low stone house wall;
— the middle third is the mountainside falling away below the yard,
  steep and empty, dropping and dropping in loose dry-brush;
— the top third is the sea, seen from far above as a flat pale
  sheet, with the horizon high in the frame.

Setting: a high mountain smallholding on a WHITE-SKY DAY at the end
of summer — flat overcast, no sun, no cast shadows anywhere.
Everything held at close middle values, no strong dark and no strong
light. THE SKY IS FLAT BARE UNPAINTED PAPER — no clouds are drawn at
all, no cloud shapes, no cloud edges, nothing rendered in it.

SCALE, and it carries the whole picture:
The boy is folded at the foot of a fence post and the top of his head
reaches roughly a third of that post's height. His head is large for
his body. He is a child — do not draw a teenager and do not draw a
toddler.
THE BOAT IS TINY. On the water far below, the entire boat is SMALLER
THAN THE BOY'S HEAD in this frame. If the boat is bigger than that,
the viewpoint is wrong and the picture has failed.

THE BOY IS THE ONLY PERSON IN THIS PICTURE. He is being part of the
mountain: folded down at the foot of the post, knees drawn up to his
chest, both hands quiet and still on his shins, his back against the
post, his head down. He is not hiding behind anything and not
crouched ready to move — he has arranged himself into a shape and
stopped. HIS FACE IS DOWN AND HIS EYES ARE NOT VISIBLE; the top of
his head and one cheekbone in shadow is all that is drawn of it.

THE WOODPILE IS IN THE FRAME AND THERE IS NOBODY AT IT. Split logs
stacked against the house wall, ordinary and empty. No figure beside
it, behind it, in front of it or among the logs. No shape that could
be read as a person anywhere near it.

THE BOAT: far below on the flat water, seen from high above, tiny. A
wide open boat with SIX OARS, AND EVERY OAR IS UP OUT OF THE WATER,
lifted and folded back along the hull all together like a beetle
folding its legs. NO OAR IS IN THE WATER AND NO OAR STICKS OUT
SIDEWAYS. The boat is not moving and leaves no wake. One small figure
stands at its bow holding a short dark stick up to his face, pointed
up at the mountain. No sail, no second boat, no bird anywhere on the
water.

NOTHING IS HAPPENING, AND THE STILLNESS IS THE SUBJECT. Nobody is
running, nobody is hiding in fear, nobody is calling out, nothing is
approaching. A boy and a boat are both holding absolutely still, a
long way apart.

The boy wears coarse undyed mended village cloth, thin and a little
too large for him, in dull oatmeal and dead olive. No ornament, no
buckle, no badge, no metal.

NO OTHER FIGURES ANYWHERE IN THE FRAME except the one distant figure
at the boat's bow. No man, no woman, no animals, no dog, no goat, no
birds, no houses other than the one wall behind the woodpile.
```

---

### PLATE: the-doorframe

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch02-the-doorframe.png`, first run. **Run with the two boilerplate paragraphs already stripped by the author**, and the block below matches what ran.

**THE CONTRADICTION TECHNIQUE WORKS, AND THIS WAS ITS FIRST REAL TRIAL.** *Her face and her hand disagree, and both must survive · the only thing in this picture that is frightened is the hand · do not resolve the contradiction by putting the fear in her face* — and the fear stayed in the hand. She came back level, looking out past the viewer, giving nothing. **The predicted failure was a worried mother and it did not happen.**

**WHAT FAILED IS MINE, AND IT RESTATES A MECHANISM THIS FOLDER ALREADY OWNS.** The block said the knuckles were *the palest and most precisely finished thing in the whole image* — and the face won anyway, being more finished, more saturated and more detailed. **Naming the winner is half an instruction.** `../portrait-prompt-system.md` §Neris learned exactly this for eyes against hair: the eyes only won once the hair was demoted in the same breath. **It is not an eye rule. It is a picture rule**, and it is now proven in a second place — see `../scene-prompt-system.md`.

If this is ever re-cut, the missing clause is:

*THE FACE IS DEMOTED SO THE HAND CAN WIN. Her face and hair are held at quiet middle values, softly drawn, the features understated with no hard edges and no strong dark anywhere in them. THE SHARPEST STEP FROM LIGHT TO DARK IN THE WHOLE PICTURE HAPPENS AT THE KNUCKLES — they are the most precisely drawn thing in the image and nothing else competes with them for finish.*

**TWO SMALLER NOTES.** The crop came back longer than the `waist-up` asked for, which shrinks the hand further. And the finish is glossier than the Ch. 1 and Ch. 2 plates — more rendered, less bare paper — **so the plate set is not yet consistent in finish, and that will matter when they sit in one book.**

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/neris.png`. Ch. 2 canon: *One of her hands was up on the doorframe, the knuckles pale, and Kael thought she was holding the door up, and thought no more about it.*

**THE SUBJECT IS A HAND, SO THE CROP IS CLOSE.** Her sheet's standing complaint is that a head-and-shoulders portrait cuts off the only part of her the book ever spends. Here the hand *is* the picture, and it has to be near enough to read the knuckles — so this is waist-up, not a full figure, and the hand is at head height where nothing overlaps it.

She has been standing there some part of the afternoon. Nothing in her face says so.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the woman.

Setting: the doorway of a small stone-and-turf mountain house on a
white-sky day, as loose wash suggestion. She stands INSIDE the
doorway looking out; the room behind her falls away into deep cool
shadow, so she reads against a dark rectangle. Flat overcast daylight
on her from outside, no sun and no cast shadows.

Composition: waist-up, close, the figure filling most of the frame,
the dark doorway around her and a little of the stone wall at the
edges.

HER RIGHT HAND IS UP ON THE DOORFRAME AT ABOUT THE HEIGHT OF HER OWN
HEAD, and it is the subject of the picture. The hand is gripping the
frame — not resting on it, not leaning against it — the fingers
curled over the edge of the timber and THE KNUCKLES DRAWN PALE AND
HARD, the palest and most precisely finished thing in the whole
image. The arm is raised clear of her body with nothing overlapping
it, the whole line of it visible from shoulder to fingertips against
the dark of the room. Her left hand hangs at her side, empty and
open. Nothing is held in either hand.

HER FACE AND HER HAND DISAGREE, AND BOTH MUST SURVIVE. She is looking
out and away past the viewer, down the slope, watching something a
long way off. Expression entirely still and giving nothing back —
level, mouth closed and relaxed, brows unmoved, composed. NOT
worried, NOT afraid, NOT anguished, NOT tender, NOT sad, not tearful,
not straining. The only thing in this picture that is frightened is
the hand. Do not resolve the contradiction by putting the fear in her
face.

THE WOMAN: a grown woman of a mountain village. Fine bones under
skin with weather and sun on it but not coarsened. Long dark brown
hair falling loose past the shoulders, warm rather than cool, plainly
brown and not black, unstyled and naturally settled. Plain coarse
village working cloth — an overdress over a plain undershirt, the
neck closed, in dull grey-brown and undyed oatmeal, worn soft and
much mended. No ornament of any kind. She is not pregnant.

```

### PLATE: done

*Candidate 3.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-4.png` (hair and colouring only), `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 2 canon: *He got up the way the newborn goats got up, in pieces, and went down, and his father stood near him — close enough to catch him, and did not catch him — while the legs remembered.*

**THE CONTRADICTION IS THE WHOLE PICTURE**, and the system's finding is that a contradiction has to be stated as one or the run resolves it into an ordinary kindness: a father would be reaching. This one's hands stay down.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: a mountain smallholding at DUSK — the sky going grey, the
light nearly gone, pens and woodpile flat dark shapes, the sea far
below a cold pale sheet. Loose wash, no detail. ONE BAT high over the
pens, a single small dark mark no bigger than a thumbprint.

Composition: both figures full length, mid-distance, close together
near the corner of the pen, on the same ground plane.

THE BOY IS A CHILD AND HIS HEIGHT IS THE MAN'S LOWER RIBS — head
large for the body, limbs short. He is getting up out of the ground
and his legs do not work: caught partway, unbalanced, weight on one
bent leg with the other knee still down, one hand flat on the ground
taking his weight, back rounded, head hanging. Assembled wrong, like
a newborn animal standing for the first time. His face is down toward
the ground and no feature of it is drawn. Not crying, not grimacing,
not appealing to anyone — working.

THE MAN IS WITHIN ARM'S REACH AND IS NOT HELPING. Square, still,
weight even, turned toward the boy and watching him. BOTH HIS HANDS
ARE DOWN AT HIS SIDES, OPEN AND EMPTY AND UNMOVING — not reaching,
not crouching, not bending, not offering a hand, not steadying him.
He is close enough to catch the boy and he is not catching him. Do
not resolve this into a father helping his son up. His face is level,
mouth closed, completely unreadable: not concerned, not proud, not
stern, not sad, not kind.

Both wear coarse undyed mended village cloth — the man's heavy and
sized to him, the boy's thin and too large — THE INK CARRYING THE
CLOTH at the seams and the mended places.
```

### PLATE: the-wrist

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch03-the-wrist.png`, first run, block below unchanged.

**WHAT IT IS, STATED HONESTLY: A STOP ALREADY MADE, not the catch mid-event.** The knife came back lying on the stone rather than held, so the picture says *a man holds a boy's wrist beside a knife* where the page says *the blade was going for him and the hand got there first.* **Adopted as the moment it actually is** — the aftermath of the stop, a second later — which the chapter also contains. The causal version is available from the fix below if it is ever wanted.

**THE CAUSE IS A REAL FINDING: A LIMB DESCRIBED AS TWO INDEPENDENT FACTS RENDERS AS TWO INDEPENDENT OBJECTS.** The inventory said *the boy's right hand holds one short knife* and, four lines later, *the man's hand is closed around the boy's right wrist.* Those are the same arm — knife, hand, wrist, grip — and splitting them across two entries let the renderer resolve them separately and put the knife down. **Name the chain as one limb, in order.** Written up in `../scene-prompt-system.md`.

The clause that would fix it:

*ONE ARM CARRIES THE WHOLE EVENT — the boy's right arm. Follow it in order: his fingers are wrapped around the knife's handle and the knife IS IN HIS HAND, gripped, not resting on the stone; just behind that hand, the man's hand is closed all the way around that same wrist. Knife, hand, wrist, grip — one continuous limb, in that order. THE KNIFE IS NEVER LYING ON THE STONE.*

**TWO MORE NOTES.** The blade points away rather than back toward the other wrist. And **the scale contrast did not propagate**: the man's hand against the boy's wrist reads perfectly, while the boy's other hand came back adult-sized — so **a size contrast has to be stated per hand, not once for the picture.**

**AND THE THING THAT WORKED, TWICE NOW: `THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH`.** The sleeves in this image are the best cloth in the library — dense scratchy linework in the seams and the mended places, ink plainly dominant over wash. Second confirmation after `portraits/kael-9`. **Put it on anything wearing worked cloth.**

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Ch. 3 canon: *the hide gave all at once where he expected it to hold, and the blade skated off the breastbone toward the soft of his own wrist — and stopped. His wrist was inside his father's hand. He had not seen the hand leave the woodpile.*

**NO FACES, AND NO REFERENCE ATTACHED.** The moment is four hands and a knife; a face in the frame would divide it, and the father's whole character here is that he arrives without being seen. **So the age has to be carried entirely by hand size** — which is exactly what the s40 scale finding is for, and this is its cleanest test: no figure, no landmark, one hand measured against another.

**THE SUBJECT IS THE JUNCTION** where the man's hand closes on the boy's wrist, and the block demotes everything else so it can win — the two-clause mechanism from `../scene-prompt-system.md`.

**RESTRAINT ON THE HARE, deliberately.** The scene system's injury note is that wound-detail vocabulary is what causes trouble, not the subject. The hare is named plainly, kept to the edge, and nothing about it is described beyond its shape.

```
Setting: a flat grey stone slab outdoors at dawn on a mountain,
seen close. Behind and below, the sea as a pale flat wash with the
light coming up grey out of it. Everything beyond the stone falls
away into loose cool wash — no detail, no horizon line drawn.

Composition: a CLOSE crop on hands and forearms only. NO FACES AND
NO HEADS ARE IN THIS PICTURE, no shoulders, no bodies — the frame
holds the flat stone, four hands, two pairs of forearms in mended
sleeves, and one knife. Everything above the elbow is out of frame,
deliberately.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND:
The BOY'S RIGHT hand holds one short knife and nothing else — a
plain working knife, the blade roughly as long as his own palm.
The BOY'S LEFT hand is flat on the stone, holding the work steady.
The MAN'S RIGHT hand is closed around the boy's right wrist, the
fingers all the way round it, holding it still.
The MAN'S LEFT hand is not in the picture.
Three hands in frame, one knife, nothing else held by anyone. No
second blade, no tool, no rope, no cloth in any hand.

SCALE IS THE AGE, AND IT IS THE ONLY THING CARRYING IT: the man's
hand is large enough to close completely around the boy's wrist
with the fingers overlapping, and the boy's whole hand is about the
size of the man's palm. The boy's hands are a CHILD'S — short
fingers, soft knuckles, no tendon standing out anywhere, small
nails. The man's are a grown labourer's — broad, weathered, the
knuckles heavy, permanently half-curled.

THE MOMENT: the knife has skated off the work and its edge has
stopped a finger's width from the inside of the boy's own left
wrist, the blade in front of him and clearly aimed at it. IT IS NOT
TOUCHING HIM AND HAS NOT CUT HIM. There is no wound anywhere in
this picture and no blood on any hand.

THE HAND ARRIVED WITHOUT WARNING AND NOTHING ELSE IN THE PICTURE
HAS MOVED. The man's grip is exact and unhurried — not snatching,
not wrenching, not dramatic. The boy's hand has not dropped the
knife and has not flinched. Both forearms are steady. This is a
stop, not a struggle.

THE JUNCTION IS THE SUBJECT AND EVERYTHING ELSE IS DEMOTED. The
stone, the sleeves and the hare are held at quiet middle values,
loosely washed, with no hard edges and no strong dark anywhere in
them. THE SHARPEST STEP FROM LIGHT TO DARK IN THE WHOLE PICTURE
HAPPENS WHERE THE MAN'S FINGERS CLOSE ON THE BOY'S WRIST, and that
join is the most precisely drawn thing in the image. Nothing else
competes with it for finish.

A hare lies on the stone at the edge of the frame, on its side,
partly out of the picture, its pale underside turned up. It is
described no further than that.

THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH: both forearms are in coarse undyed
mended wool pushed back at the wrist, dense scratchy linework in
the seams, the patches and the coarse weave, ink dominant over wash
throughout. The man's sleeve is heavy and sized to him; the boy's
is thin and too large. No ornament, no buckle, no metal but the
knife.

Light: low cold dawn light from one side, the sun not up. No warm
source anywhere. The only warm value in the picture is the skin.
```
