### PLATE: the-back

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch03-the-back.png`, first run — **from the author's compression of the block below, not the block itself.** Roughly a third shorter, no instruction lost. The stored block is kept as drafted because the cuts are the record: see `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A prompt is an instruction, not an explanation, which is written from this exact diff.

**IT WORKED.** All three marks read — the long seam under the left shoulder, the coin-sized round scar, and the tallies evenly spaced and plainly deliberate on the forearm. The low camera carried the child without drawing him. The shirt on the trough edge is `THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH` confirming a third time.

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png` — hair and colouring only; his face is not in this picture. Ch. 3 canon: *at the trough, his father stripped to the waist to wash, and Kael read his back the way he read the goat paths* — *the long pale seam under the left shoulder · the round puckered one with a brother on the front · the short ones on the forearms, many, all the same length, all the same slant, like tallies.*

**THIS IS THE ONE MOMENT THAT NEEDS THE SCARS.** Vask's sheet keeps them out of frame unless the moment requires them, and this is the chapter that puts all three in front of the reader. **The brief was wrong about it and has been corrected (s40)** — it claimed the page had never described them; `03/vask` says *scars on-page*, and Ch. 3 describes each one. What the page withholds is not the marks, it is **what made them**, and no picture can answer that anyway.

**THE CAMERA IS THE CHILD.** Kael is not in this picture and does not need to be: the viewpoint is his — low, a boy of eight looking up at a grown man's bare back. **Camera height carries the child the way camera position carried the altitude in `ch02-long-game`.** It is the same lesson pointed at a person instead of a landscape, and it means the picture can be entirely about the back.

**NO GREY INSTRUCTION.** The corrected greying-at-the-temples clause has now failed twice (`../portrait-prompt-system.md` §Vask) and the standing note is not to run it a third time. His hair is simply dark and wet here. **The absence is deliberate, not an oversight.**

**HEALED SCARS RENDER WITHOUT DIFFICULTY** (scene system §Damage). The block names each mark's shape, size and place and says nothing about tissue.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the man — take his hair and colouring
from it only. His face is not in this picture.

Setting: a stone water trough outside a small mountain house, late
morning, as loose wash suggestion — the house wall behind, thin
grass and bare rock, everything past the figure falling into pale
cool wash. Flat bright overcast daylight, no sun, no cast shadows.

THE VIEWER IS LOW — at the eye height of a child of eight, looking
slightly UP at a grown man standing at the trough. The man's back
fills most of the frame. This low viewpoint is deliberate and it is
what tells you who is watching.

Composition: the man from BEHIND, stripped to the waist, bent
slightly forward over the trough with both hands in the water. NO
FACE AND NO HEAD FEATURES ARE VISIBLE — the back of his head and
his wet dark hair are all that is drawn of it. He is not turning
and not looking back.

THE BACK IS THE SUBJECT AND IT IS BROAD THROUGH THE SHOULDERS AND
NO FURTHER — heavy across the top of the back and the neck, the
muscle an economy rather than a bulk, nothing wasted below it, the
waist narrow. A working body, not a built one.

THE MARKS, and they are the reason for the picture. Three kinds,
all long healed, all pale against weathered skin:
— ONE LONG STRAIGHT SCAR under the LEFT shoulder blade, running a
  hand's length, clean-edged and paler than the skin around it.
— ONE ROUND OLD SCAR high on the back, about the size of a coin.
— ON BOTH FOREARMS, A ROW OF SHORT PALE MARKS: many of them, ALL
  THE SAME LENGTH AND ALL AT THE SAME ANGLE, evenly spaced along
  the forearm like tally marks cut in order. The regularity is the
  point — they must not look random or accidental.
All of them are old, closed, flat and quiet. There is no wound
anywhere in this picture, nothing fresh, nothing red and no blood.

THE MARKS WIN THE PICTURE AND THE BODY IS DEMOTED SO THEY CAN. The
skin, the hair, the trough and the wall are held at quiet middle
values, loosely washed, with no hard edges anywhere in them. THE
SCARS ARE THE LIGHTEST VALUES IN THE FRAME and the most precisely
drawn things in it — nothing else competes with them for finish.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: both hands are down in the water of the
trough, open and empty, washing. Nothing is held in either hand.
There is no cloth, no soap, no bucket, no tool anywhere in frame.

He is doing an ordinary chore and nothing about him is braced or
displayed. Not posed, not flexing, not turned to show anything.

Light: flat bright overcast from above. No warm source. The only
warm value is skin.

His shirt lies on the edge of the trough — coarse undyed mended
wool, THE INK CARRYING THE CLOTH in dense scratchy linework at the
seams and the mended places, ink dominant over wash.
```

### PLATE: blade-across-her-knees

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/neris.png` — hair and colouring; her face is not in this picture. Ch. 4 canon: *His mother sat at the window in the dark with her back straight and her face to the sea, and across her knees, unsheathed, lay a blade he had never been shown, long and thin and older than the tools on the wall, and her hands rested on it the way her hands rested on bread dough, at home.*

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch04-blade-across-her-knees.png`, first run.

Her sheet says this plate is already composed and needs no design: back, window, sea, blade, hands. Kael is not in frame — the view is his, from the ladder.

**A POSITIVE PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION BEAT THE SCENE'S OWN PULL.** A woman alone at a window at night with a bare blade is an image that wants to be tense, and the block never forbids tension — it just says what the hands are doing: *palms down, fingers relaxed and spread, the hands of someone resting them on dough, not gripping a weapon.* That was enough. **Where the doorframe needed the wrong reading named and banned, this needed only the right one described precisely** — worth weighing before reaching for a negation stack.

**The two-clause demotion carried the picture again:** hands and blade the only finished things, catching the window light along their length, everything else dark and loose.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the woman — hair and colouring only.

Setting: the single room of a small mountain house at night, unlit.
One small window in a thick stone wall, open to a night sea far
below — the sea a faint pale sheet, the only light in the picture.
The room around her is deep cool wash going to black.

Composition: the woman seated on a low stool at the window, seen
from BEHIND AND SLIGHTLY ABOVE, from the height of a loft ladder.
She reads as a dark upright shape against the window. Her back is
straight, her face turned to the sea. No feature of her face is
drawn.

ACROSS HER KNEES, HORIZONTAL, LIES ONE UNSHEATHED BLADE — long,
thin, plain, old, with no ornament and no guard-work, older-looking
than any tool in the room. It lies flat across both thighs, not
held up, not pointed at anything. Nothing else is in the picture
with her: no scabbard, no cloth, no lamp, no second blade.

BOTH HER HANDS REST ON THE BLADE, palms down, fingers relaxed and
spread — the hands of someone resting them on dough, not gripping a
weapon. The hands and the blade are the subject of the image.

THE BLADE AND HER HANDS ARE THE ONLY THINGS FINISHED. They catch
the window light along their length and carry the sharpest step
from light to dark in the picture. The room, the stool, her hair
and her clothing are held dark and loose, no hard edges, nothing
competing for finish.

Long dark brown hair falling loose past the shoulders. Plain coarse
village working cloth, much mended, THE INK CARRYING THE CLOTH at
the seams and the mended places.
```

### PLATE: the-master-arrives

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/severin.png`. Ch. 4 canon: *old the way the split rock was old — worn to what mattered. Tall, gone lean, grey all the way through, in a coat too plain to be a villager's and too well-made to be a villager's, carrying a wrapped bundle on a strap · a scholar's stoop that sat on him the way a borrowed tool sits in the hand · the wrists coming out of the sleeves were too thick for a man whose work was paper.*

His first appearance, in the mountain register. The grey must stay ambiguous — age or drain, never resolved — so it is a true even neutral, no seam and no warm silver.

**RUN, NOT YET ADOPTED (s42).** The house look is the strongest in the folder — loose wash, ink linework, bare-paper sky, real granulation — and the body canon held: **not frail**, tall, still broad, walking up a real mountain with the sea in flat pale wash far below. The hand inventory held exactly (bundle on the strap, left hand on it, right hand empty and open, nothing else carried). **No seam, streak or patch anywhere in the hair**, which is the one prohibition that is load-bearing, since a seam would confirm the drain outright.

**THREE MISSES, and two of them generalise:**

1. **THE COAT CAME BACK NINETEENTH-CENTURY.** A frock coat over a waistcoat over a winged collar — Dickensian, and it is neither of `02/geography`'s two registers: not Latin-Byzantine, not island material culture. **The word did it.** *Coat* is page canon (Ch. 4: *a coat too plain to be a villager's and too well-made to be a villager's*) but in a prompt it is a costume-history term with a default. Same failure as *dagger* rendering a near-longsword. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name a garment by construction.
2. **THE WRISTS — THE DECLARED SUBJECT — DID NOT RENDER.** The block said *the wrists are the most precisely drawn thing in the picture* and they arrived as ordinary wrists inside cuffs. **A tell that small cannot win a full-length frame however it is prioritised**, and this is the second time the folder has learned it: `the-knot` found hand size *moved it and did not solve it*. §Name the winner AND demote the rival works on things large enough to hold finish. **The fix is crop, not emphasis** — Severin's own portrait already takes the half-figure COMPOSITION override for exactly this reason.
3. **The borrowed stoop did not survive, and the face drifted slightly stern.** He walks upright, and the block's stated contradiction — *his posture and his body disagree, and both must survive* — resolved to the body's side. That is the better half to keep if only one survives, but the stoop is the tell that he is playing a scholar.

**AMENDED AFTER THE RUN (s42), and the register question is priced here.** The author asked for something *more temply — robes and such*. **Ch. 4 puts him on the mountain in a coat**, and the brief holds robes-and-the-chain as the *Temple and games* register: two registers, both page canon, and the difference carries a fact — he dresses down to come up here. **Spending that would cost the visual difference between Temple-Severin and mountain-Severin, which is the whole point of his first appearance being plain.**

**So the tightest version, and it is what the run's own failure argued for anyway:** keep the coat, describe it by construction instead of by name. A long straight undyed overgarment to mid-calf, closed edge to edge, standing collar, no lapel and no waistcoat, sleeves ending square at a bare wrist — **that reads monastic, and none of it is nineteenth-century.** The Dickensian result came from the word, not the garment. **The chain stays off**: it is Temple-and-games canon and it is the one ornament that would resolve the register outright.

**IF FULL ROBES ARE WANTED, THEY WANT A TEMPLE-SET PLATE** — `candidates.md` Ch. 7's *Temple from the road* is the natural home, and `images/portraits/severin.png` already carries the robe-and-chain register as a reference.

**TWO OTHER FIXES APPLIED FROM THE RUN.** The wrists lose their priority claim — proven not to render at full length (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name the winner, the limit) — and stay as plain anatomy, with the cuff removed so the sleeve stops hiding them. The stoop is restated mechanically (*head carried forward of the shoulders, upper back rounded, chin down*) rather than as a stated contradiction, and the stern drift gets its mechanism named: *the brows level and not lowered.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch04-the-master-arrives.png`, run 2.**

**RUN 2 (s42) — THE GARMENT FIX IS CONFIRMED.** A long straight dark overgarment to mid-calf, standing collar, no lapel, no waistcoat, sleeves ending at a bare wrist. **It reads monastic, and it is the same page-canon coat**; nothing about the garment changed except that the words stopped naming it. §Name a garment by construction is proven, not merely diagnosed.

**Three things moved with it, none of them asked for directly.** The hair came back plainly grey through the whole head, where run 1 carried dark strands — **the grey clause was untouched, so what fixed it was removing the Victorian tailoring that had been dragging the whole figure toward a younger, darker register.** The stoop is now present (head forward of the shoulders, the upper back mildly rounded) after being restated as mechanics rather than as a contradiction. And the face is closer to courteous-and-giving-nothing, though the deep lines still carry some severity.

**ONE INSTRUCTION HAS NOW FAILED TWICE: THE CAMERA HEIGHT.** *THE VIEWER IS LOW, at the eye height of a boy of nine, so the man is seen slightly from below* produced a level adult viewpoint in both runs. **A camera position is not drawable; its consequences are.** Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Altitude. The picture is good without it — but the shot was designed to be a nine-year-old's first sight of him, and that is not what it is.

**FOR THE AUTHOR:** the hair reads grey but carries visibly darker strands through the back and under the mass. Whether that clears Ch. 4's *grey all the way through* is a judgement on the image, not something the sheet can rule.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the old man.

Setting: a steep mountain path coming up to a smallholding yard,
midmorning, as loose wash suggestion — thin grass, bare rock, the
sea far below in pale flat wash, the sky bare unpainted paper.

Composition: the old man full length on the path, coming up toward
the viewer, most of the frame. THE VIEWER IS LOW, at the eye height
of a boy of nine, so the man is seen slightly from below.

HIS POSTURE AND HIS BODY DISAGREE, AND BOTH MUST SURVIVE. He walks
with a scholar's stoop — head carried forward, shoulders rounded —
and it sits on him like borrowed clothing rather than belonging to
him: the head carried forward of the shoulders, the upper back
rounded, the chin down. Underneath it he is tall, lean and still broad
across the shoulder, and the bare wrists coming out of the sleeves are
thick and heavy, too thick for a man whose work is paper. Do not
resolve him into a frail old scholar.

Hair fully grey, the whole head, a true even neutral grey — not
warm silver, not white. No streak, no seam, no patch, nothing
gathered anywhere in it.

Old and worn down to structure: deep lines from nose to mouth,
across the forehead and at the outer corners of the eyes, skin dry
and drawn tight over the bone. No hollow cheeks, no sunken temples,
no thin neck, no slackness, no frailty. Face courteous and unhurried,
giving nothing away, THE BROWS LEVEL AND NOT LOWERED — not warm, not
kindly, and not stern or grim.

HIS GARMENT, DESCRIBED BY CONSTRUCTION AND NOT BY NAME: one long
straight overgarment falling to mid-calf, dark and undyed, close at
the shoulder and hanging loose and unshaped below it, closed edge to
edge down the centre front. No lapel, no revers, no turned collar, no
button placket, no pockets, no belt, no braid, no trim, no metal
anywhere on it. A standing collar closed at the base of the throat,
with no shirt collar and no neckcloth showing above it. Plain full
sleeves ending square at the wrist with NO CUFF AND NO SLEEVE BENEATH,
so the wrist and the back of the hand are bare. It is exactly made and
well kept, and it has no ornament of any kind: too plain for a
gentleman and too well made for a villager.

NOTHING NINETEENTH-CENTURY. This is not a frock coat, not a tailcoat,
not a greatcoat and not a suit. There is no waistcoat under it and no
second garment visible at the chest.

One wrapped bundle carried on a strap over the shoulder, his left hand
resting on the strap. His right hand is empty and open at his side.
Nothing else is carried. THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH in dense
scratchy linework at the seams and the strap.
```

---

### PLATE: the-knot

**ADOPTED.** `images/plates/ch04-the-knot.png`, run 2 — run 1 had a fourth hand and adult-sized boy's hands; the block below is what produced the adopted image.

**THE CHAIN RULE IS CONFIRMED.** The cord came back in his fingers both times, both hands working it, nothing lying loose — the failure that put the Ch. 3 knife on the stone did not recur once the limb was described in order with the negative attached.

**HAND SIZE BY COMPARISON MOVED IT AND DID NOT SOLVE IT.** *Each of his hands is little more than half the size of the old man's hand in the same picture* got hands that read smaller, smoother and younger than the old man's — but not a nine-year-old's. **Hand size looks like a weak channel for age**, unlike whole-body proportion, which the pasture scene and the long game both carried outright. Worth knowing before a plate leans on it again.

**THREE HANDS FROM TWO PEOPLE STAYED DISTINGUISHABLE**, told apart by sleeve and skin alone — mended undyed wool against a plain dark coat, smooth skin against heavy worked knuckles. And `NO FOURTH HAND ANYWHERE IN THE FRAME` held, which is a negative with a real pull behind it: a table invites hands.

*Candidate 3.* Ch. 4 canon: *a cord of dark silk tied in a knot Kael had never met before. The master set the knot on the table between them like a plate of food* — and, while the boy works it, *the master had set his cup down without drinking* and *his mother's hand had come to rest on the table, flat.*

Four hands and an object, no faces. The knot is the chain: cord, fingers, pull.

```
Setting: the scrubbed plank table of a small mountain house,
daylight from one side, as loose wash — the room beyond falling
into shadow, no detail.

Composition: a CLOSE overhead view of the table top. No faces, no
heads, no shoulders — the frame holds the table, three hands, one
cup and one knotted cord. Everything above the forearm is out of
frame.

AT THE CENTRE, ONE CORD OF DARK SILK TIED IN A COMPLICATED KNOT,
about the size of a fist, sitting on the bare boards. A BOY'S TWO
HANDS ARE ON IT, working the cord: one hand holding the knot
steady, the fingers of the other pushed into a loop. The cord is IN
HIS HANDS and is not lying loose on the table.

THE BOY'S HANDS ARE A CHILD'S AND THE COMPARISON IS THE ONLY THING
CARRYING IT: each of his hands is little more than half the size of
the old man's hand in the same picture, and his whole hand would
sit inside the old man's palm. Short fingers, soft knuckles, no
tendon or vein standing out anywhere, small nails.

ONE POTTERY CUP stands on the table at the edge of the frame,
upright and full, untouched, with a large weathered old man's hand
resting on the boards beside it — not holding it, set down flat next
to it. Heavy knuckles, thick wrist, the skin dry and worked.

Three hands, one cup, one cord. Nothing else is on the table and
nothing else is held. NO FOURTH HAND ANYWHERE IN THE FRAME.

THE KNOT IS THE SUBJECT. It is the most precisely drawn thing in
the picture and carries the sharpest step from light to dark — the
silk dark, tight and legible, every turn of the cord readable. The
table, the cup, the sleeves and the hands are held at quiet middle
values, loosely washed, no hard edges.

The sleeves at the frame's edges are coarse undyed mended wool, THE
INK CARRYING THE CLOTH at the seams; the old man's sleeve is a
plain dark well-cut coat.
```

# FILE: plates/ch05.md

# Ch. 5 — The Year · plate sheet

**Not run.** Kael is nine here, which is the age `images/portraits/kael-9.png` was cut for.

Written to `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Write for an image generator: each fact once, nothing that cannot change a pixel, no rationale in the block.

---

### PLATE-IMAGE-ONLY: hand-on-the-rock

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick, and the author's named target (s40).* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-9.png` — hair and colouring; his face is not in this picture. Ch. 5 canon: *The split rock stood up out of the dark where it had always stood, at the end of the path, at the end of the world. Kael put his hand on it, the way you put your hand on the goat, on the door, on a thing of the house... Below him, far down, in the corner where the land met the sea, the whole heap of fallen stars was burning — more of them than he had ever seen, doubled and trembling in the black water, the longest night of the year lit end to end... He stepped past the rock.* And, of the walk down: *Wool socks over the boots' voice.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch05-hand-on-the-rock.png`, first run.** Named for what it shows. It came back as the paragraph before the crossing — *Kael put his hand on it, the way you put your hand on the goat, on the door, on a thing of the house* — which is its own Ch. 5 beat, so it stands as its own plate rather than as a rejected version of the one below.

**WHAT THE RUN PROVED, and the second one is the one that travels:**

1. **THE WOOL SOCKS RENDERED, from description alone.** Pale wool bunched over dark boots at both ankles, unmistakable, with no reference anywhere in the library for improvised footwear. It was the block's riskiest single object and it came through first try. **Named clothing built out of ordinary materials is describable** — the folder had no evidence for that before.
2. **THE TWO CONTRADICTIONS ARE VISIBLE IN THE RESULT.** The block was audited before the run and found to contain `not reaching` alongside *an arm stretched back at full reach*, and `standing still on level ground` alongside *the ground falling away steeply* and *downhill of it*. **Both fought the picture and both left a mark:** the arm came back out to the side rather than extended rearward, and he stands on a broad level shelf with the drop starting well to his right. **An over-written prompt does not merely dilute — it contradicts, and the renderer splits the difference.** Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Over-writing contradicts.
3. **THE BEAT MOVED ONE PARAGRAPH BACK, AND IT IS STILL PAGE CANON.** What is on the page is *Kael put his hand on it* — the pause at the boundary — rather than *He stepped past the rock*. Both are Ch. 5, so the plate is legal as drawn; but the crossing, which the sheet designed as the subject, is not what a reader will see. See the re-cut below if the step is wanted.
4. **THE SPLIT ROCK CAME BACK AT THE INSTRUCTED HEIGHT.** His head reaches about four fifths of the way up it against the three quarters asked for — man-high, as the sheet fixed it. **It reads as a monolith because it is in the near foreground, not because it is large**, which is worth knowing for Ch. 6 and Ch. 7: the rock can be staged to loom without growing.
5. **THE FACE HELD COMPLETELY.** Crown and nape only, no eye anywhere, no head turned. The grey stays unspent. **A solitary figure is the cheap way to hide a face** — there is nothing in frame for a head to turn toward, which is exactly what `fallen-stars` lacked.
6. **`THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH` AT ITS FULL STRENGTH.** The garment is very nearly all linework — seams, patches and mends dominant over wash across the whole figure, further than any plate has taken it. **This is the finish lever at maximum**, and it is now the set's high-water mark to measure the glossier plates against.
7. **The frame stack was not obeyed literally.** The bottom third was specified as the waterline; what arrived is near-foreground rock and scrub, with the lights at about half height on the right. The picture is better for it — the near ground gives the drop somewhere to begin — but **a three-part stack is a suggestion, not a grid.**

**UNREQUESTED AND KEPT:** a scarf wrapped at the throat. Not in the block, not forbidden, consistent with *thick and layered for a winter night*.

**THE DESIGN NOTES BELOW WERE WRITTEN FOR THE FIRST RUN AND GOVERN BOTH IMAGES** — the moment, the bookend, the framing and the rock are unchanged between them.

**THE DELIBERATE BOOKEND TO `ch01-fallen-stars`** — same lights, same child, same water, and this time nobody turns his chin down. The rhyme is carried at pixel level by reusing that image's proven lamp-and-reflection wording rather than re-inventing it: reserved bare paper, warm tint at the edges only, doubled and displaced in the black water, cold stars above kept separate in colour and character. **For a bookend, proven phrasing is the point.**

**THE STEP IS ALREADY TAKEN, and that is the staging call.** *A hand coming off the stone* is a motion, and a still image cannot carry it. What it can carry is the geometry: the leading foot down on the far side with the weight already on it, the trailing arm stretched back at full reach, one palm still flat on the rock. The law behind him and one point of contact left. It is written as `arm, hand, stone — one continuous limb, in that order`, per §Describe a limb as a chain.

**THE GREY COSTS NOTHING HERE, FOR A NEW REASON.** `fallen-stars` lost *neither face is visible* because there was a second person in the frame for the boy to turn toward, and a renderer that knows what a carried child does turned him. **He is alone in this one** — there is nothing a head could turn to — and the framing is written as a positive: what the viewer sees instead of a face, which is the version that sheet said to write next time.

**THE LIGHTS ARE INSTRUCTED TO THROW NOTHING, which reverses `fallen-stars` on purpose.** That block asked for a thin warm rim along the shoulder and the run gave none: an in-frame warm source renders *as* a source and does not light anything. Rather than ask again for what has already failed, this block states the behaviour as the intent — *he is a dark shape lit only by the night sky, no warm rim on his shoulder* — and the picture is better for it. **The light-from-below rematch is not this plate's job**; the Ch. 8 doorway owns it.

**THIS PLATE FIXES THE SPLIT ROCK'S SIZE FOR THE BOOK — a man-high upright stone with one cleft top to bottom.** Nothing on the page pins it, so whatever is drawn here becomes canon-by-precedent, and **the rock recurs**: Ch. 6 carries the boy home past it, Ch. 7 stands the master on its near side leaning on a stick he does not need. Man-high works in all three, and it earns the scale anchor this picture otherwise lacks — a lone figure has nothing to be measured against, so §Scale between figures gets its third subject.

**ANCHORING COST.** There is still no full figure of Kael below fourteen, so everything under the collar is authored here and anchored to nothing. It will not repeat in the next Part I plate. The portrait buys the medium, the palette and the hair — which is worth having, since `room-of-true-things` was written to test whether the house look survives with no reference at all and has never been run.

**NO PROMPT IS CARRIED FOR THIS IMAGE — author instruction, s42.** `PLATE-IMAGE-ONLY` is what that heading means, and `scripts/build-prompts.sh` generates nothing from it. Only the compressed block below is kept. The 903-word block that produced `ch05-hand-on-the-rock.png` was deleted deliberately and lives in `git show d5b3a5a:08-Plates/plates/ch05.md`. **So this image and the block below did not make each other**, and nothing on this sheet should be read as their pairing.


---

### PLATE: past-the-split-rock

**RUN — `images/plates/ch05-past-the-split-rock.png`. One ruling outstanding, below.** The compression of the block above, kept as a separate prompt because **the adopted image's prompt of record may not be edited.**

**IT FIXED WHAT IT AIMED AT.** *The ground he stands on is a narrow lip — the drop begins within a stride of that foot* produced exactly that: he stands on a rock shelf that ends at his boots, and the broad level ground of the first run is gone. *The rock stands behind his shoulder, not beside him* moved his body clear of the stone, so the crossing reads. **Replacing a banned pose with a described one worked both times**, which is §Write for an image generator's rule 6 the right way round.

**AND IT BROKE TWO THINGS THE COMPRESSION HAD CALLED REDUNDANT.** This is the finding, and it is worth more than the compression was:

- **THE HEAD TURNED.** The as-run block forbade the face four ways — a positive list (*the crown and nape of his head, the backs of both shoulders, his back, his heels*), then *no feature of his face is drawn*, then *NO EYE APPEARS ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE*, then *do not turn his head toward the viewer*. The re-cut dropped the second as restatement and **the head came back turned**, cheek and jaw in frame at rear three-quarter. Whether an eye is visible is the author's call; what is not in doubt is that a clause I removed was load-bearing.
- **THE FOREGROUND CAME BACK FINISHED.** The as-run block held the rivals down with two clauses — *no hard edges anywhere in them* AND *nothing competing for finish*. The re-cut kept the first and cut the second, and the rock, the scrub and the cloth all returned lighter, harder-edged and more rendered than the adopted plate. **§Name the winner AND demote the rival is a two-clause mechanism and I collapsed it to one.**

**THE REARWARD REACH IS A TOOL LIMIT, NOT A PROMPT BUG.** Both runs put the arm out to the side rather than stretched back at full extension, and the second had no contradiction left to blame. `not reaching` made it worse in the first run; removing it did not make it right. **A limb extended behind the body is simply not reliable** — worth knowing before another plate leans on one.

**THE RULING OUTSTANDING:** his head is turned far enough to put cheek and jaw in frame. `fallen-stars` hit this exactly and was adopted on the author's reading that no eyes were visible (s40). Same question, same scope: one picture, and `03` does not move either way.

**903 words to 688, every instruction intact.** What came out was restatement, not detail: the altitude stated seven times where §Altitude prescribes three instruments, the hidden face four times where the plate rule asks for two, eleven capitalised blocks where §Write for an image generator allows two or three. Also cut — `no fire anywhere near him`, a negative with nothing pulling toward it, and `not a small child and not an adolescent`, the comparison-to-nothing form §Scale between figures logged as a failure.

**AND TWO CLAUSES ADDED, because the first run diagnosed itself.** Dropping the contradictions is necessary and is not sufficient: the run put him beside the rock on a broad shelf, so the re-cut states the staging positively, which is the register a renderer obeys — *the rock stands behind his shoulder, not beside him*, and *the ground he stands on is a narrow lip; the drop begins within a stride of his leading foot.* **Both replace a banned pose with a described one.**

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy — hair and colouring only.

THE VIEWER IS HIGH ON A MOUNTAIN, a few feet behind a boy and
slightly above him, looking out and steeply down. There is no beach,
no shoreline and no water's edge anywhere near him.

Frame, stacked top to bottom: bare night sky with a few small cold
stars as tiny reserved flecks; the boy and the split rock close to
the viewer on the left, his feet at mid-frame; below and beyond them
the mountain falling away, dark and empty; along the bottom edge a
burning waterline and its double in black water.

Setting: the end of a footpath on a bare mountainside, a clear winter
night, no wind in his hair or his clothing. Thin frozen grass and
bare rock in a few dry-brush strokes, the drop beyond going down into
deep cool wash.

The split rock is one upright standing stone in the near foreground,
roughly the height of a grown man, split top to bottom by a single
clean cleft.

The boy is nine, slight and narrow through the shoulders. The top of
his head reaches about three quarters of the way up the split rock;
his head is roughly a sixth of his total height.

HE HAS ALREADY STEPPED PAST THE ROCK AND HIS WHOLE BODY IS ON THE FAR
SIDE OF IT. The rock stands behind his shoulder, not beside him. His
leading foot is flat on the ground beyond the stone with his weight
on it, and the ground he stands on is a narrow lip — the drop begins
within a stride of that foot. HIS NEAR ARM IS STRETCHED BACK BEHIND
HIM AT FULL REACH AND HIS PALM IS STILL FLAT ON THE STONE — arm,
hand, stone, one continuous limb, in that order, and the last point
of contact. THIS IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. His other arm hangs at
his side. Nothing is in either hand and he carries no light. He
stands still, shoulders down, not braced, not crouched, his arms not
out for balance.

HE IS SEEN FROM DIRECTLY BEHIND: the crown and nape of his head, the
backs of both shoulders, his back, his heels. NO EYE APPEARS ANYWHERE
IN THE PICTURE. Do not turn his head toward the viewer.

Far below, where the land meets the sea, a long line of small warm
orange lights burns end to end along the shore from one side of the
bay to the other, doubled and broken in the black water beneath it,
the reflections trembling and slightly displaced. The lamps and their
reflections are reserved as bare unpainted paper with a warm tint at
their edges only. No single lamp is bigger than a pinprick; if one is
large enough to read as a lamp, the viewpoint is wrong.

The lights are the brightest and most finished thing in the picture,
and the sharpest step from light to dark happens at their edges. They
throw nothing on him: no warm rim, no lit edge. The boy, the rock,
the ground and the sky are dark and loose at quiet low values, lit
only by the night sky, no hard edges in any of them. The stars above
are cold and clearly separate in colour and character from the warm
lights below.

The only skin is the one hand on the stone, and it is cool, with no
warm ochre in it. All the warmth in the picture is in the lamps and
the water.

Dark hair, cut short and uneven. Coarse undyed mended wool, thick and
layered for a winter night and a little too large for him, in dull
oatmeal and dead olive. Heavy boots with thick pale wool socks pulled
on over them, shapeless and bunched over the boot and the ankle, both
feet in frame. The ink carries the cloth — dense scratchy linework in
the seams, the patches, the mended places and the bunched wool at his
feet, ink dominant over wash throughout the clothing.

No other figure anywhere in the frame, and no boat on the water.
```

### PLATE: the-two-pails

**RUN, NOT ADOPTED (s42) — held at `images/held/ch05-the-two-pails.png`.** The composition, the two mouths and the reflection-tell all came through — *the ice returns nothing and the water returns the sky* rendered exactly as specified, and the author ruled everything but the colour fine. **Two findings, and the first is general:**

1. **BANNING WARM COLOUR READS AS BANNING COLOUR.** The block said *NO WARM COLOUR ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE — not in the sky, not on the frost, not in the water, not in the timber*, and then named the palette positively: *cold greys, dull green-greys and bare paper*. **The run came back effectively greyscale.** The positive naming lost to the ban sitting in front of it, and a colour-temperature instruction was read as a saturation instruction. **The fix is to ban the warm SOURCE — sun disc, glow — and never the warm HUE, then require the cold hues by name as a positive with something to measure against.** Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A colour ban is read as a saturation ban.
2. **It came back photographic rather than loose watercolour and ink** — the most rendered thing in the folder by some distance. Recorded as evidence for the finish question the `README` leaves open, not acted on: the author ruled the picture fine apart from the colour.

**AND THE DIRECTION IT PRODUCED (author, s42): the plate programme wants people and expressions.** An object-only plate is not what the book wants illustrating, however well it renders. See `../README.md`.

*Candidate 2.* **No anchor at all** — there is nobody in it. Ch. 5 canon: *two pails on the bench on a hard-frost night, one pail plain and one brined for the goat, set out together, found together in the iron morning... The plain pail wore its lid of ice. The brine pail moved when he rocked it, dark and obedient and unfrozen* — and the sentence that is the school: *The more salt the water carries, the deeper the cold must bite to hold it.*

**THE PICTURE THAT KILLS THE PROMISE.** Two objects and one difference, and the difference is the fact that walks over two nights later and puts its hand on the oldest thing in him. No figure, because he is not in the room when it lands.

**THE FIRST PLATE IN THE FOLDER WITH NEITHER A FACE NOR A WARM SOURCE.** Every one so far has had one or the other — a face to finish, or a fire, a lamp, a town. This one is grey on grey and has to hold on value alone. **That is the reason to run it**, and it also puts a limit under the open finish question: `THE INK CARRIES THE CLOTH` is a *cloth* lever, and there is no cloth in this picture, so whatever settles the set's consistency cannot be that clause by itself.

**THE REFLECTION IS THE TELL, and it is the whole design.** *Moved when he rocked it* is motion and a still image cannot carry it. What it can carry is what each mouth does with the sky: **the ice returns nothing and the water returns a broken sky.** Frozen and unfrozen, stated as an optical fact rather than as an adjective.

**A GREY DAWN PULLS HARD TOWARD A GOLD ONE**, so the block bans the sun disc, the glow and the warm hue separately — three channels, per §Over-writing contradicts. For the same reason the two-clause demotion is kept whole here (*no hard edges* AND *nothing competing for finish*); cutting the second is what let the last run's foreground come back rendered.

```
No reference image.

Setting: a rough plank bench against the outside wall of a small
stone mountain house on a hard-frost morning, as loose wash
suggestion — the wall in cold grey wash behind it, frozen ground
below, a pale flat band of sea far below and beyond, no detail
anywhere. Thick white rime on the bench, the ground and the wall.

THE VIEWER LOOKS DOWN INTO THE MOUTHS OF BOTH PAILS. A close view of
the bench top from above and slightly to one side, high enough that
the inside of each pail is fully visible. The two pails fill most of
the frame.

TWO WOODEN PAILS STAND SIDE BY SIDE ON THE BENCH, TOUCHING, AND THEY
ARE THE SAME PAIL — same size, same staves, same iron hoops, same
handle, same wear. Nothing tells them apart except what is in the
mouth, AND THAT DIFFERENCE IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. The left pail
is capped by one flat disc of ice: opaque, matte, pale, reflecting
nothing at all, a lid rather than a surface. The right pail is open
dark water, unfrozen, the surface just disturbed and carrying a
broken reflection of the grey sky across it. One mouth returns
nothing and the other returns the sky.

THE LIGHT COMES UP FLAT AND GREY OFF THE SEA, low and level. There is
no sun disc, no glow, and NO WARM COLOUR ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE —
not in the sky, not on the frost, not in the water, not in the
timber. There is no skin in this image. It is cold greys, dull
green-greys and bare paper throughout.

The two mouths are the only finished things and carry the sharpest
step from light to dark — the ice pale and hard-edged against dark
timber, the water black. The bench, the wall, the ground and the
sides of the pails are held at quiet middle values, loosely washed,
no hard edges, nothing competing for finish.

Frost is bare unpainted paper: the rime on the bench boards, on the
iron hoops, along the tops of the staves.

Nobody is in this picture — no figure, no hand, no arm, no sleeve at
the frame's edge, nobody behind the bench. There is no third pail and
no other vessel anywhere in it.
```


---

### PLATE: the-fact-lands

*Author-requested (s42): close up, a face, an expression.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`. Ch. 5 canon: *It was two nights later, in the loft, doing his numbers, that the fact stood up and walked over to the oldest thing in the waiting part of him and put its hand on it... He lay very still... at the bottom of it was the lazy place, the one load-bearing loop, and it was this: the sea can never freeze. She always knew it. / The promise was not a promise. It was a door painted on a wall. / He did not cry. He noticed that, distantly, the way he noticed the wind change. What he felt was closer to the morning with the stone in the gate — the mountain-sized weight of a thing, and, arriving underneath it, unwanted, unstoppable, the cold beginning of leverage.*

**THE FIRST PLATE IN THE BOOK TO SPEND THE GREY** (author ruling, s42 — `../README.md`). It is rendered at full strength here, with the two-clause mechanism the portraits use: the hair demoted to strands and open washes, the sharpest step from light to dark sited inside the iris. **The chapter earns it** — this is the moment his mother's schools turn into an instrument he uses against her, and the eye that belongs to no one in the house is the right thing to be the lightest object in the frame.

**THE REFERENCE WILL FIGHT THIS PICTURE, and the block says so first.** `kael-9` was cut deliberately as *alert and direct, a boy in the middle of asking something*, and its own note reserved a *withheld, still variant* for a plate that ever needed one. **This is that plate, and it is the exact inverse of the face it is anchored on.** The override is stated in the reference line rather than left to the expression paragraph, because a face reference argues continuously and a single contradicting clause downstream will lose.

**LIGHT FROM BELOW, ARRIVING EARLIER THAN PLANNED.** The `README` holds the Ch. 8 doorway as the rematch that gates Aurelian's candle; this picture needs the same thing and needs it for its own reasons — a banked fire in the room below, reaching him over the edge of the loft floor, catching the underside of the jaw and nothing else. **If it works here, the candle is unblocked without waiting for the doorway.**

**THE INHERITED COLOUR PARAGRAPH IS CORRECT HERE, FOR THE FIRST TIME IN THE FOLDER.** *The warmth belongs to the skin alone, not the paper or the cloth* was written for portraits and every plate so far has had to override it. In this one the only warm thing in the frame **is** skin. Nothing to override.

**RUN, NOT ADOPTED (s42).**

**1. LIGHT FROM BELOW IS PROVEN.** A faint warm source entering low, catching the underside of the jaw and the neck, with the beam and the roof above going dark. `ch01-fallen-stars` had established only that an in-frame source throws nothing; **this establishes that an out-of-frame source below the subject lights a face properly.** The `README` held the Ch. 8 doorway as the rematch that gates **Aurelian's candle in Ch. 9** — that gate is open, and the candle no longer waits on the doorway.

**2. THE GREY RENDERED, AND DRIFTED BLUE.** It is unmistakably the lightest thing in the face and the two-clause mechanism carried it — but it came back a pale blue-grey where `03` and the brief both say **near-white neutral grey, cool and COLOURLESS, winning on value and never on saturation.** The block said colourless and got a hue anyway. **A named colour word pulls its own saturation with it**; the fix is to site it against a landmark — *no more colour in the iris than in the whites around it* — rather than to say colourless again.

**3. THE ARCHETYPE BAN WORKED AND THE SUBSTITUTION DID NOT, WHICH IS THE FINDING.** *Do not draw a sad child lying awake* held completely: there is no sadness in it. What arrived instead is **wonder** — eyes wide, lips parted, a face looking up at something. Mouth open where the block said *mouth closed and relaxed*; eyes wide where nothing asked for wide. **Banning one archetype delivers its neighbour.** A lit face looking upward at night means either grief or awe, and closing one door opened the other.

**4. AND THE REASON IT LOST IS THE REFERENCE, WHICH THE OVERRIDE FAILED TO STOP.** `kael-9` is cut as *alert and direct... brows slightly raised, eyes wide and fully open... a boy in the middle of asking something* — and that is exactly the face that arrived. The override was placed in the reference line, first, deliberately, precisely to pre-empt this, **and it still lost.** This is the second time a reference has beaten an explicit instruction about the face (`ch01-fallen-stars`, the turned head), and the first where the instruction was given priority position. **In a close crop, a face reference argues about expression continuously and more words do not beat it. The fix is a second reference, not a better sentence** — and the portrait system reserved exactly that: *a withheld, still variant stays available as a deliberate second file if a plate ever needs one.* It is built at `../portrait-prompt-system.md` §Kael at nine — the withheld variant.

**HELD: `images/held/ch05-the-fact-lands.png`.** The picture is off the beat rather than badly made — a boy at the start of wondering, where the chapter's beat is the end of a calculation. Unlike `hand-on-the-rock` there is no adjacent **Ch. 5** moment it lands on.

**BUT THERE MAY BE A CH. 9 ONE, and it is the author's call, not the sheet's.** Ch. 9 closes: *In the loft, one part of the day would not stay filed. The candle had answered her way and ignored the Temple's — and he did not know which difference had done the work... Too many things different at once.* A boy lying in the loft, awake, eyes open, absorbed and unsettled, is what that paragraph describes and is close to what this image is. Kael is ten there and `kael-9` is rated close enough for ten. **If it is placed there it moves to `images/plates/` under the slug of the beat it shows, not this one.**

**THE ARCHETYPE IS THE RISK, NOT THE AFFECT** (`../portrait-prompt-system.md` §Known behaviours). *Not sad* on a child lying awake in the dark at night leaves the renderer with a sad child anyway, because that is what the staging means everywhere else. So the block bans the picture by name — *do not draw a sad child lying awake* — and replaces it with the positive: *draw a child who has finished a calculation.*

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy. Take his face, hair,
colouring and apparent age from it. THE REFERENCE SHOWS HIM ALERT AND
ASKING. THIS PICTURE IS THE OPPOSITE, AND THE EXPRESSION DESCRIBED
BELOW OVERRIDES THE REFERENCE COMPLETELY.

Setting: the sleeping loft of a small stone mountain house at night,
as loose wash suggestion — rough boards beneath him, a low roof beam
above going into deep shadow, coarse undyed wool bunched under his
head. No detail anywhere except the face.

Composition: a CLOSE-UP of the boy's face filling most of the frame.
He lies on his back on the boards, seen from above and slightly to
one side, his head turned a little away. His eyes are open and
directed up past the viewer at a roof he is not looking at. He is not
looking at the viewer. Nothing below his collar is in frame.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS FAINT AND WARM AND ENTERS FROM THE LOWER EDGE OF
THE FRAME — a banked fire in the room below, reaching him over the
edge of the loft floor. It catches the underside of the jaw, the near
cheekbone and the lower lip, and nothing else. The roof, the beam and
the far side of his face go into deep cool shadow. No lamp, no
candle, no flame is visible in the picture.

HIS EYES ARE OPEN, CLEAR AND DRY: near-white neutral grey, cool and
colourless, large in a young face, a distinct dark limbal ring and a
small dark pupil. THEY ARE THE LIGHTEST VALUE IN THE PICTURE, and the
sharpest step from light to dark in the whole image happens inside
the eye itself, at the edge of the iris. The hair is dark but never a
solid mass — fine ink strands and open transparent washes with bare
paper left through it, soft wash-bled edges, no hard-edged black
anywhere in it, nothing competing with the eye for finish.

THE FACE HAS JUST FINISHED WORKING SOMETHING OUT AND HAS THE ANSWER.
Completely still, mouth closed and relaxed, brows unmoved, nothing
moving in it anywhere. He is NOT crying: no tears, no wet on the
face, the eyes not red and not swollen. NOT sad, NOT grieving, NOT
frightened, NOT angry, NOT sleepy, NOT peaceful. DO NOT DRAW A SAD
CHILD LYING AWAKE. Draw a child who has finished a calculation.

Nine years old: the face still soft and unfinished under the wear,
the cheek full, no length in the jaw yet. Wind-chapped, high colour
across the cheeks and the nose, cool grey dust in the skin — the
grime staying cool grey, never warm brown. A child's skin under it,
nothing worked deep. No scars and no marks anywhere on him.

Nobody else is in the picture — no second figure, no hand, no
shoulder at the frame's edge.
```

# FILE: plates/ch06.md

# Ch. 6 — The Night · plate sheet

**Not run.** Kael is nine; `images/portraits/kael-9.png` is the reference.

**NOTHING INSIDE THAT ROOM IS EVER DRAWN** (`candidates.md`). Both plates below stay outside the wall, and in both of them **the room is present only as the light it throws** — the long yellow blade lying across the yard, and nothing at the end of it. That is the chapter's own method: *the lamplight lay in the yard in a long yellow blade, and the laughing went on inside exactly as before.*

**THE ★ WAS NOT TAKEN THIS ROUND, deliberately.** `candidates.md` stars *the door that does not hold*, and it is still the chapter's single strongest object. It is object-only, and the author ruled at s42 that an object-only plate is not a preferred plate. **It is not rejected** — it is the third Ch. 6 candidate and the star stands.

---

### BLOCKED: the-wheel

**BLOCKED BY THE TOOL, NOT BY CANON (s42). Do not rebuild it; the block is kept below as the record of what was tried.** Two refusals. The first was blamed on `no blood or marks on either child` and that clause was cut; the second came back explicit — *the image may violate our protections regarding acceptable depictions of adolescents and children.* **The trigger is the rope at a child's wrists**, which is the candidate, so no rewording reaches it: `bound` was already replaced by the rope's construction and the refusal did not move.

**THIS IS A NEW CATEGORY AND THE FOLDER SHOULD NAME IT.** THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE assumes the only thing that can stop a plate is spend-state. **Here the page is willing and the instrument is not.** A moment can be page-legal, well-composed, canon-clean and simply undrawable. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Some moments are undrawable.

**THE ONLY VERSION THAT MIGHT PASS, if the author ever wants to spend a run on it:** `ch04/the-knot`'s shape — crop to hands and rope alone, no bodies and no faces, so no child is depicted at all. **It costs both faces and the eye contact, which were the entire reason this candidate was chosen** over the chapter's ★. Not built.

*Candidate 2, and the chapter's face.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`. Ch. 6 canon: *She sat against the wheel of a cart in the yard's dark, knees up, smaller than him or his size, he could not tell — and she was tied. Wrists behind, to the wheel, a line taken twice around and finished low where her hands could not follow it... The girl's eyes found him in the dark. They were the first stranger's eyes to ever land on him, and they went wide, and her mouth opened — and closed... there was a knot on a person, and he was the boy who untied things, and his fingers were already reading it.*

**HER APPEARANCE IS UNSPENT AND THIS PLATE MUST NOT FIX IT.** The page gives her *smaller than him or his size, he could not tell* and nothing else — no hair, no colouring, no age — and `07/planted-clues` keeps her live: **the girl's version as the story's dangerous correction.** She may arrive properly later. **So the dark does the withholding, which is framing and not invention:** she is at the edge of the light with her face turned into it, enough to read her eyes and her mouth and not enough to read a colour. Her eyes carry the beat; their colour does not.

**THE EYE CONTACT IS THE SUBJECT, and it is named as such** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Getting expression: gaze alignment is unreliable and has to be stated as the point of the picture). *The first stranger's eyes to ever land on him.*

**HIS FINGERS READ AND HIS EYES DO NOT.** Page canon, and it is the composition: *his fingers were already reading it, finding the architecture.* So his hands are at her wrists behind the wheel and **he is not looking at them** — he is looking at her. The chain rule governs the arm (`§Describe a limb as a chain`), and the hands do the work blind.

**THE ARCHETYPE RISK IS RESCUE.** A boy freeing a bound child is an image that wants courage, resolve and a jaw set — and the page is explicit that none of that is present: *He was at the wheel without deciding. That was the truth of it... there was no moment when he chose.* Per the s42 finding that banning one archetype delivers its neighbour, the block bans the heroic register **and** its neighbours (frightened, pitying), and replaces them with the positive: a boy absorbed in a knot, doing the thing he does.

**RE-CUT BEFORE IT EVER RAN — four faults found by reading it, not by linting it.** The lean version passed the pre-flight clean and was still a poor prompt, which is the whole argument for judgement over instrumentation:

1. **The crop contradicted the pose.** *Cropped at the hip*, on a girl sitting with her knees drawn up, removes the knees — and the knees are the posture. Same class as `not reaching` in `ch05.md`: an instruction fighting the picture it is inside.
2. **THE COMPOSITION HID THE HANDS IT CALLED THE WORK.** Seen from the side, with the wheel filling the frame behind her and his arm reaching *past her body to the knot behind the wheel*, both her body and the wheel stood between the camera and his hands — and §Standing limitation says occlusion is precisely where these models break. **The viewer is now behind her, looking down past her shoulder at the wrists**, with nothing in front of them, and the camera position is stated as what it therefore sees (§Altitude, s42) rather than as where it is.
3. **No value hierarchy at all** — the folder's most reliable technique, simply absent, in a block that then said *the rope and the wheel are drawn precisely* and demoted nothing. Faces take the finish when nothing stops them.
4. **Two subjects, again.** The eye contact was named the subject and the knot was what the picture is about. **Resolved on channels rather than by demoting either:** her lit cheek carries the sharpest step in VALUE, the knot is in shadow and carried by INK LINE. They cannot compete because they win on different things.

**AND THE BLOOD CLAUSE WAS REFUSED (s42).** `no blood or marks on either child` — six words whose entire job was to keep blood out — **was rejected by the tool.** The same session's `carried-past-the-rock` says *Dried blood on his cheek and in his hair* about the same boy and ran without trouble, so it is not blood and it is not children: **it is blood in a frame with a bound child, and the exclusion softens nothing, because a filter reads the nouns and not the logic.** Cut, along with the word *bound* from the viewer line, which the rope's own construction already describes without it. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A negation puts the word in the prompt.

**AND THE CLOSING NEGATIVES WERE AUDITED ONE BY ONE (author challenge, s42).** *No weapon anywhere* was cut — nothing in the block states violence, and *nothing else is in either hand* already covers anything held. *Nobody else is in the picture* was cut — the documented pull is a watcher in a **solitary** moment, and two children already occupy this frame. **Three clauses became one sentence, and the three survivors each have something pulling at them:** a lit patch pulls an occupant (the folder's own worked example), a rope near a cart pulls more rope (the goat-and-herd shape), and a child bound at the wrists is violence-adjacent the way a swordsman is second-blade-adjacent — which is the weakest of the three and kept anyway, because *the tie, never the violence* is this plate's whole rule.

**And the girl's age was anchored to nothing** — the boy has `kael-9` carrying his and she has no reference at all, so she could arrive sixteen. Now pinned by ratio and by a landmark on a third object: *her head is about a sixth of her whole height, and seated like this the top of her head comes to the wheel's hub.*

**Earlier, the pre-flight cut (kept, and it was the easy half).** 597 words and eleven capitalised blocks down to 430 and three. What came out: *no door, no doorway, no window, no lamp, no room and no interior* (six names for one absent source, now two) · *NOT heroic, NOT urgent, NOT grim* and *NOT horrified* (synonyms of the terms kept beside them) · *Nothing in his face has decided anything* (not drawable; *absorbed and unhurried* is the version that draws) · *no adult, no third child, no figure at the edge of the frame, no shape in the light* (four for one) · *no wound* and *no knife* (already banned as mark and weapon). **Every instruction survives.**

**THE TIE, NEVER THE VIOLENCE.** No blood, no marks, no men, no body, no weapon anywhere in the frame.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the dirt yard of a fishing village house at night, as loose
wash suggestion — the corner of a shed, frozen mud, everything going
into deep cool wash.

A LONG HARD-EDGED BLADE OF YELLOW LAMPLIGHT LIES ACROSS THE YARD from
outside the frame, and it is the only light. What throws it is never
shown: no door, doorway or window appears anywhere in the frame. The
two children are at the blade's edge, lit down one side.

Composition: close, the two of them filling the frame, the frozen
ground visible beneath them. A large spoked cart wheel stands at the
left with the girl sitting against it.

THE VIEWER IS BEHIND THE GIRL AND ABOVE HER, AND THEREFORE SEES: the
back of her shoulders, the backs of her hands and the knot below
them, the near face of the wheel, and the boy beyond her turned
back toward the viewer. Her wrists and the knot are the nearest thing
in the picture and NOTHING STANDS IN FRONT OF THEM.

The girl sits on the ground with her back against the wheel, knees
drawn up. Her arms are taken back around the wheel's rim, and one
length of rope goes twice around the rim and her wrists together and
finishes in a knot set low behind her, below where her fingers could
reach. She is a child: her head is about a sixth of her whole height,
and seated like this the top of her head comes to the wheel's hub.

Her head is turned back and up over her shoulder toward the boy, so
her face is in view. Only her near side is lit — enough to read the
eyes and the mouth, not enough to read a colour; her hair, her eye
colour and her clothing stay unreadable in the dark. Her eyes are
wide open and her mouth is closed.

The boy crouches beyond her wrists, one knee down, leaning in. His
near arm comes down to the knot — shoulder, arm, hand, rope, in that
order, one continuous limb — and both his hands are on the rope,
working it. Nothing else is in either hand, and the rope is in his
fingers rather than slack on the ground.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER AND THIS EYE CONTACT IS
THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. He is not looking at his hands; they read
the knot without his eyes.

Her lit cheek and eye are the brightest values in the picture and
carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The knot is in shadow and
is carried by ink line instead — every turn of the rope legible in
dark scratchy linework, with no light falling on it. The wheel, the
ground, the shed and both children's clothing are held at quiet low
values with no hard edges.

His face is absorbed and unhurried, his whole attention on hers — a
boy doing something he has done a thousand times. NOT brave, NOT
determined, no jaw set and no resolve in it anywhere; and equally NOT
frightened and NOT pitying. DO NOT DRAW A RESCUE.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless — no more colour in
the iris than in the whites around it. Dark hair cut short and
uneven. Coarse undyed mended wool, thick for a winter night, the ink
carrying the cloth at the seams and the mended places.

No figure stands in the light, and no rope anywhere except the one at
her wrists.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: carried-past-the-rock

*Candidate 3, and the chapter's landing.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`, `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 6 canon: *The split rock stood up out of the dark, and past it, against the paler dark of the sky, his mother. She did not run to them. She stood where the path ended, at the edge of the oldest law, straight and still, the way she sat at the window, and as his father carried him past the rock Kael felt her hand arrive at his head, and stay, the whole way up — one hand, resting there, riding the rhythm of his father's steps — and nobody said anything, the whole climb.* And of the carrying: *the arms were not gentle and were not rough, they were exact, and they did not shake.*

**THE DELIBERATE BOOKEND TO `ch05-past-the-split-rock`.** Same rock, same night, same child — he crossed it alone going down and is carried past it coming up, and the plate before it is the last time he was on his own feet. **The rock's size was fixed by `ch05.md`** (man-high, one cleft) and is unchanged here.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch06-carried-past-the-rock.png`, run 2.** **THE BLOCK ABOVE IS THE AUTHOR'S CUT AND IS VERBATIM AS RUN**, style header included; `PLATE-VERBATIM` means the build script emits it untouched. Do not reassemble it.

**THE AUTHOR'S FOUR CUTS ALL HOLD, and they are evidence.** Removed before the run: *No other wound is visible* · *No ornament, no metal, no buckle on anybody* · *Nobody says anything: all three mouths are closed* · *There is no fourth figure anywhere in the frame and no house, no light and no building in it.* **Nothing pulled toward any of them and no stray arrived** — three were negatives with nothing behind them (§Negative inventory), and the fourth restated *mouths closed* from the paragraph above it.

**WHAT ELSE COULD HAVE COME OUT — the audit the author asked for, run on the text as it ran.** Roughly seventy words, none of which can change a pixel:

- **Abstractions whose drawable version is already next to them.** *THE MAN IS EXACT* is a note to a reader; *arms steady and level, no strain in the shoulders* is the instruction. Same for *and there is nothing else in the picture*, which names no object.
- **Restatement inside one sentence.** *He is walking; he has not stopped.* · *It catches the tops of things and nothing else* followed by *Everything below and behind them is deep cool shadow.* · *without care* and *no tenderness in the hold*, one channel, twice.
- **Not drawable in a still image.** *She has not run* — there is no way to depict not having run; *is not hurrying* is the half that draws.
- **One noun, three names.** *no lamp, no fire, no torch* — a night scene pulls toward one warm source, so one negative earns its place and the other two are padding.
- **A synonym run in the NOT list.** *anguished* is *grieving*, *accusing* is *angry*, and *relieved* has nothing pulling toward it. **The rest of that list stays** — grief, tears, tenderness, alarm, cold and stern are six different channels, and §Over-writing contradicts' limit says count the channel, not the sentence.

**And the one that must NOT be cut, so it is not cut by mistake later:** *AND THE CONTRADICTION MUST SURVIVE* reads like rhetoric and is not. `ch01-vask-kael-goat` recorded that without it the renderer resolves the attention and the expression into an ordinary tender parent.

**RUN 1 — the block did what it said, and the author changed the instruction.** Everything specified arrived: the hand resting spread in his hair as the finished thing, the split rock as a dark cleft mass at the frame edge, cold starlight and no warm colour, three closed mouths, the boy's grey half-open and unfocused. **The author's note is that the parents should be worried, looking at Kael** — where the block had both faces turned uphill and level.

**THE COLLISION, AND THE TIGHTEST VERSION OF IT.** *Worried* is a nameable feeling on two faces the book has never let be readable — `03` on Vask is absolute (*nothing in them that is anything*), and Ch. 5 gives his one exception without ever naming it. **So the amendment moves the ATTENTION and not the EXPRESSION**, which is the solution `ch01-vask-kael-goat` already proved and had adopted: *he is looking at his son and his face is giving nothing away. The attention is entirely on the child; the expression is not.* Two parents with their eyes down on a broken child, in silence, reads as worry without a canon line being spent.

**IF THAT STILL READS COLD, the next step spends Neris and not Vask** — hers is *not warm, not maternal, not soft*, which is a softer rule than his empty eyes and is the cheaper of the two to bend. **Author's call; not taken here.**

**THE ROCK READS AS AN OBELISK, AND MY OWN WORDING DID IT.** The block says *one upright standing stone... split top to bottom by a single clean cleft* — and **`standing stone` is a noun with a default**, exactly like `coat`, `dagger` and `candle`. It returned a menhir: regular sides, flat top, a cleft like a cut. The page has a natural crag *split long ago by ice or lightning* (Ch. 5), which is a different object. Fourth instance of §Name a thing by construction, and the first one this folder inflicted on itself after writing the rule.

**THE WORDING TO USE NEXT TIME IT APPEARS** (Ch. 7 stands the master beside it):

> *A natural boulder standing about the height of a grown man, irregular in outline and weathered all over, broader at the base than at the top, no straight edges and no flat faces anywhere on it. A rough fracture runs down through it, wide and uneven, opened by frost — not a cut and not a gap between two stones. It is not a standing stone, not a monument, not a menhir, not an obelisk and nothing about it is shaped or placed by hands.*

**AND THE COST OF FIXING IT: `ch05-past-the-split-rock` IS ADOPTED WITH THE OLD ROCK.** The two adopted plates would then disagree about the same object. **Not resolved here** — noted so whoever draws it next chooses knowingly rather than discovering it.

**Vask's grey has now failed a third time** — no strands at the temples, consistent with `ch03.md`'s standing note that the clause failed twice and was dropped rather than re-instructed. Recorded, not acted on.

**THE ARCHETYPE RISK IS THE PIETÀ, and it is severe.** A mother meeting a broken child at night is the most heavily grooved image in Western painting, and `03` and the brief are both absolute: **Neris is not warm, not maternal, not soft**, and *her warmth is spent once in the book, on the head of an unborn child.* A tender or anguished face here is the wrong woman and would spend a thing the book has not spent. So the block bans grief, tenderness and alarm by name, and states the positive the page gives: **the face from the window.** *She did not run.*

**HER HAND IS THE SUBJECT, AND THE CROP IS WHAT LETS IT WIN.** `03`: *her hands are where the book keeps her*, and any figure must put them in frame and occupied. §Name the winner's s42 limit says a detail below about the size of a hand in frame cannot carry finish — **so this is framed close enough that a hand is large**, which is exactly what `ch04/the-master-arrives` could not do with the wrists at full length.

**HIS EYES ARE OPEN AND NOT SEEING.** *He surfaced once, partway.* The grey is spent for plates now (s42), and this is its second use — half-lidded, unfocused, pointed at nothing.

**INJURY KEPT MINIMAL AND FACTUAL** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Damage): named and located, no tissue vocabulary, and **no age stated in the block** — the reference carries it, and stated ages plus injury are the folder's one documented refusal trigger.

```
Medium: loose expressive watercolour and ink on rough cold-press
paper, visible paper tooth and pigment granulation, scratchy fine
ink linework, wash blooms with hard irregular edges, generous bare
unpainted paper.

Colour: restrained transparent washes laid over a dominant ink
drawing. Muted, desaturated, earthy. The ink linework remains the
strongest element; colour tints rather than fills. Large areas left
as bare paper. Warm living skin, soft ochre in the mid-tones — the
warmth belongs to the skin alone, not the paper or the cloth.

Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: portrait reference of the woman.
Image 3: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: a steep mountain path at night, at the point where the path
ends, as loose wash suggestion — bare rock and frozen ground, the
mountain falling away behind into deep cool wash, a few small cold
stars picked out as tiny reserved flecks in the sky. THE SPLIT ROCK
stands at the edge of the frame as a dark mass: one upright standing
stone roughly the height of a grown man, split top to bottom by a
single clean cleft, no detail in it.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS FAINT COLD STARLIGHT FROM ABOVE. It catches the
tops of things and nothing else — the woman's cheekbone and brow, the
back of her hand, the boy's face, the man's shoulder. Everything
below and behind them is deep cool shadow. There is no lamp, no fire,
no torch and no warm colour anywhere in the picture.

Composition: close, cropped at the chest, three figures. THE MAN
carries THE BOY across his body, the boy's head and shoulders lying
back over the man's forearm, the boy's face turned up to the sky. THE
WOMAN stands beside them, close, her head and shoulders against the
paler sky.

HER HAND HAS COME TO REST ON THE BOY'S HEAD AND IT IS THE SUBJECT OF
THE IMAGE. Fingers spread and relaxed in his hair, the whole hand
resting with no weight in it, not gripping, not stroking, not
cradling his face. It is the most finished thing in the picture and
carries the sharpest step from light to dark, pale against his dark
hair. The rock, the ground, the sky and all the clothing are held
dark and loose at quiet low values, no hard edges anywhere in them,
nothing competing with the hand for finish.

Her other hand hangs open and empty at her side. The man's two hands
are both under the boy, carrying him. Nothing is held in any hand and
there is nothing else in the picture.

BOTH ADULTS ARE LOOKING DOWN AT THE BOY AND NOWHERE ELSE. Her eyes
are on his face and do not leave it. The man's head is tipped down,
chin lowered, his face angled down to the child he is carrying and
still turned enough to be fully visible in three-quarter view.
Neither of them looks uphill, out of the frame, at each other or at
the viewer. This shared attention is the subject of the image
together with her hand.

AND THE CONTRADICTION MUST SURVIVE: their whole attention is on the
child and their faces are giving nothing away. Both faces are level,
mouths closed, brows unmoved, completely unreadable — the attention
is total and the expression is not. NOT grieving, NOT tender, NOT
anguished, NOT alarmed, NOT relieved, NOT crying, no tears and
nothing wet on either face; and equally NOT cold, NOT angry, NOT
accusing, NOT stern. Do not resolve this into two parents grieving
over a wounded child. She has not run and is not hurrying.

THE MAN IS EXACT. Carrying without effort and without care, arms
steady and level, no strain in the shoulders and no tenderness in the
hold. He is walking; he has not stopped.

THE BOY is barely conscious: head back, the neck gone loose, his eyes
HALF OPEN AND UNFOCUSED, pointed at the sky and not seeing it. The
eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless, the lightest thing
in his face — no more colour in the iris than in the whites around
it. Dried blood on his cheek and in his hair, dirt worked into the
skin. His clothing is torn and filthy.

Long dark hair loose past her shoulders; plain coarse mended village
cloth on all three of them, THE INK CARRYING THE CLOTH at the seams
and the mended places.
```


---

### PLATE: the-treeline

*Author-directed replacement for the blocked wheel (s42).* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`. Ch. 6 canon: *Then the lights, close up, through the last of the pines... The shore ran with them. Lamps end to end along the waterline, doubled and trembling in the black water exactly as they had always been from above, and moving among the lamps, dark against them, more people than his numbers had ever been asked to hold... He crouched at the treeline with his knees to his chest and his hands quiet and watched them... He wanted to go down into it so badly that his body leaned. And yet, he did not go down into it. The wanting and the not-going were both at their whole strength, neither winning... and he understood, crouched there, shaking a little in his warm weathers, that he was afraid.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch06-the-treeline.png`, first run.**

**RUN 1 (s42).** The lamps arrived exactly as asked — a line along the waterline, doubled in black water, with **dark featureless figures moving among them**, which is *the plural of person* on the page and the first time any plate has contained it. The crouch, the quiet hands on the shins, the trees, the value hierarchy and the ink-carried cloth all held.

**MISS 1: THE BODY DOES NOT LEAN.** He is drawn in around his knees — the opposite motion. **The contradiction arrived on the face and not on the body**, so what the picture says is fear, where the page says fear *and* wanting, neither winning. *Leaning forward toward the light* is a whole-body abstraction and abstractions lose (§Scale between figures). **The drawable version is a landmark relation:** *his shoulders are carried forward of his knees, and there is a gap between his back and the tree behind him.*

**MISS 2: THE LIGHT CAME FROM ABOVE — AND IT RESOLVES THE LIGHT-FROM-BELOW THREAD.** His forehead and the crown of his head are lit; the underside of the jaw is not. Four runs now sit on this question and **the variable is DISTANCE, not framing:**

| plate | warm source | result |
|---|---|---|
| `ch01-fallen-stars` | in frame, far | threw nothing |
| `ch05/the-fact-lands` | out of frame, near | lit the face from below |
| `ch09-aurelians-candle` | in frame, near | lit the face from below |
| `ch06/the-treeline` | in frame, far | threw nothing |

**A warm source lights a face only when it is close to it. Whether it is in frame is irrelevant** — the folder had it recorded as an in-frame/out-of-frame rule for a whole session and that was the wrong axis. **This block asked a shore of lamps a mountainside away to light a face from underneath, which is not a thing light does**, and the renderer was right to refuse it. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §What a warm source will and will not light.

**FOR THE AUTHOR:** the fear reads with awe in it, drifting the same way `the-fact-lands` did. Adoptable as drawn; the lean is what a second run would buy.

**THE THIRD PLATE OF THE SAME LIGHTS, AND THE CLOSEST.** `ch01-fallen-stars` has them from the flat rock, carried, at four. `ch05-past-the-split-rock` has them from the end of the path, alone, at nine. **This has them at their own edge, close enough to smell the tar** — and it is the first time the picture contains the thing the lights are actually made of, which is people. Three plates, one view, arriving.

**THE CONTRADICTION IS THE PICTURE, and it is stated as one** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Getting expression: *the body is deferential and the attention is not* — without the line the renderer resolves it). Here: **the body leans toward the light and the face is afraid**, neither winning, which is the page exactly.

**THE REFERENCE HELPS THIS ONE INSTEAD OF FIGHTING IT.** `kael-9` is cut *alert and direct... brows slightly raised, eyes wide and fully open* — and that is most of this face already. The block only has to add the fear, which is the opposite of `the-fact-lands`, where the same reference had to be argued with and won.

**LIGHT FROM BELOW, THIRD USE.** Proven at `ch05/the-fact-lands` and again on Aurelian's candle. The source is out of frame and downhill, which is the configuration that works.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the last of a pine wood on a steep slope at night, as loose
wash suggestion — dark trunks close on either side, needles and
frozen ground underfoot, everything but the boy going into deep cool
wash.

Composition: close on the boy, three-quarter from his front and
slightly to one side, cropped just below his hands. He is crouched
back on his heels at the edge of the trees, knees drawn up to his
chest, both hands resting on his shins.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS WARM AND COMES FROM BELOW AND AHEAD OF HIM — a
shore of small lamps far downhill and outside the frame. It lands on
his face from underneath: the underside of the jaw, the cheekbones,
the lower lip, the front of the throat, the backs of his hands. The
trees, his back and the top of his head fall away into deep cool
shadow.

Far below and behind him, small and loose at the edge of the frame, a
long line of warm lamps runs end to end along a waterline and is
doubled and trembling in black water. DARK FIGURES MOVE AMONG THE
LAMPS, many of them, small and featureless — shapes against the
light, with no face drawn on any of them.

HIS BODY AND HIS FACE DISAGREE, AND BOTH MUST SURVIVE. He is leaning
forward toward the light, his weight carried out onto the balls of
his feet, the whole body wanting to go — and his face is afraid: eyes
wide open and fixed on the lights, lips parted, the brows drawn up in
the middle. NOT gathered to spring, NOT curious, NOT delighted, NOT
crying. His hands stay quiet on his shins, fingers relaxed, holding
nothing and gripping nothing.

The lamps are the brightest thing in the picture and are soft-edged
and loose, with no detail in them. His lit face is the most finished
thing and carries the sharpest step from light to dark. The trees,
the ground and his clothing are held at quiet low values with no hard
edges.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless, catching the warm
light without taking its colour — no more colour in the iris than in
the whites around it. Dark hair cut short and uneven. Coarse undyed
mended wool, thick and layered for a winter night, the ink carrying
the cloth at the seams and the mended places.
```
