# Ch. 7 — The Opening · plate sheet

**Both plates adopted (s42).** Kael is ten; `images/portraits/kael-9.png` is the reference — its own note rates it close enough for ten.

**BOTH PLATES ARE LIT BY A SOURCE CLOSE TO THE FACE**, which is the only configuration that works (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §What a warm source will and will not light, s42): a fire in the floor between two people, and flat dawn on an open path.

---

### PLATE: kael-of-what

*Candidate 2, and `candidates.md` says to plant it deliberately.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`, `images/portraits/severin.png`. Ch. 7 canon: *The master was waiting at the split rock. He stood on the near side of it, on the path, leaning on a stick he did not need for leaning, and he watched Kael come down through the pens the way he watched everything, unhurried, and he did not say good morning. He said, "Who are you?" / "Kael," Kael said. / "Kael of what?" / "Of—" and there was nothing to put there. He had never needed anything to put there.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch07-kael-of-what.png`, first run.**

**RUN 1 (s42).** **The beat landed exactly** — his mouth open on a word that is not coming, eyes still up and steady on the man's, chin not dropped. A stop and not a feeling, which was the hard part. Eye contact, the scale (his head at the man's chest), the stick with its tip down and no weight on it, the hand on the bag strap, the flat cold dawn with the sea in pale wash and a bare-paper sky, the grey through the whole head with no seam — all held.

**THE ROCK CAME BACK NATURAL, AND THE SPLIT DID NOT COME BACK AT ALL.** `ch06.md`'s corrected wording killed the menhir outright: irregular, weathered, broader at the base, no straight edges, nothing shaped by hands. **But the fracture that gives the rock its name is not legible in the image.** Fifth confirmation of §Name a thing by construction, and a new failure beside it:

**A CORRECTION CAN BURY THE THING IT WAS CORRECTING FOR.** The old wording said *split top to bottom by a single clean cleft* and got a menhir with a cleft. The new wording spends five clauses on not-being-a-monument and gives the fracture one, sixth in the list — so the renderer built a boulder and never got to the split. **The defining feature has to be said first and given a measurement**, with the shape corrections after it: *a boulder the height of a man, split from top to bottom by a single rough fracture a hand's width across that runs the whole way down; the fracture is the first thing to read about it. It is natural and frost-opened, not a cut — irregular, weathered, broader at the base, nothing shaped by hands.* Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name a thing by construction.

**Minor: the stoop is faint again**, as on `ch04-the-master-arrives` run 1. Twice now it has needed to be stated mechanically to arrive at all, and here it was and still came in weak. **It may simply not survive at full length** — the same size limit that beat the wrists.

**THE SUBJECT IS A HOLE.** The picture has to show a boy discovering an absence he did not know he had — not shame, not confusion, not embarrassment, and above all not distress. `03` on Kael at nine is *alert and direct, in the middle of asking*; this is that face arriving at a question with no answer behind it, which is a stop rather than a feeling. The block states it positively — **his mouth is open on a word that is not coming** — because the negatives alone would leave the renderer to pick something.

**THE ROCK USES `ch06.md`'S CORRECTED WORDING**, which exists because *one upright standing stone* rendered a menhir. Natural crag, irregular, frost-fractured, nothing shaped by hands. **`ch05-past-the-split-rock` is adopted with the old rock and will disagree with this one** — flagged on that sheet, unresolved, and this plate is where it starts costing something.

**SEVERIN IS IN THE MOUNTAIN REGISTER, and it is the garment `ch04-the-master-arrives` was adopted with** — described by construction, not named, because the name produced a frock coat. The stick is in the inventory and explicitly not bearing weight.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: portrait reference of the old man.

Setting: the end of a footpath high on a bare mountainside at first
light, as loose wash suggestion — thin frozen grass, bare rock, the
sea far below in flat pale wash, the sky bare unpainted paper. Flat
cold dawn light coming up off the sea, no sun disc and no warm colour
anywhere.

A natural boulder stands beside them, about the height of the man:
irregular in outline, weathered all over, broader at the base than at
the top, with no straight edges and no flat faces. A rough fracture
runs down through it, wide and uneven, opened by frost. It is not a
monument, not a menhir and nothing about it is shaped or placed by
hands.

Composition: the two of them facing each other on the path, cropped
at the knee, the boulder behind and between them. The top of the
boy's head reaches the man's chest.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER AND THIS EYE CONTACT IS
THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE.

THE BOY HAS JUST BEEN ASKED SOMETHING HE CANNOT ANSWER. His mouth is
open on a word that is not coming, his eyes still steady on the man's
— caught mid-sentence with nothing behind it. NOT ashamed, NOT
confused, NOT distressed, NOT frightened, NOT sullen; his chin stays
up and his eyes do not drop. A stop, not a feeling.

The man waits. Unhurried, courteous, giving nothing away, mouth
closed, brows level — a face that has priced this and is letting it
sit. NOT kind, NOT stern, NOT amused, NOT expectant.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the man's right hand rests on the head of a
plain walking stick whose tip is on the ground beside him, carrying
none of his weight — he stands square and even on both feet. His left
hand is empty at his side. The boy's right hand is on the strap of a
cloth bag over his shoulder; his left hangs empty. Nothing else is
carried and there is no other object in the picture.

The boy's face is the most finished thing in the picture and carries
the sharpest step from light to dark. The man, the boulder, the path
and all the clothing are held at quiet middle values with no hard
edges.

THE BOY: ten years old, eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and
colourless — no more colour in the iris than in the whites around it.
Dark hair cut short and uneven. Wind-chapped, high colour across the
cheeks and nose, cool grey dust on the skin. Coarse undyed mended
wool, a little too large for him.

THE MAN: tall, lean and still broad across the shoulder, standing
with a scholar's stoop — head carried forward of the shoulders, the
upper back rounded. Hair fully grey, the whole head, an even neutral
grey, with no streak, seam or patch anywhere in it. Old and worn down
to structure, deep lines from nose to mouth and at the outer corners
of the eyes, skin dry and drawn tight over the bone, and no
frailty anywhere in him. HIS GARMENT, BY CONSTRUCTION: one long
straight dark overgarment falling to mid-calf, close at the shoulder
and loose below, closed edge to edge down the centre front, with no
lapel, no turned collar, no placket and no pockets. A standing collar
at the base of the throat with no shirt collar showing. Plain sleeves
ending square at a bare wrist, and the wrists are thick and heavy.
Nothing nineteenth-century: not a frock coat, not a greatcoat, not a
suit, and no waistcoat under it.

The ink carries the cloth on both of them, in dense scratchy linework
at the seams and the mended places.
```

### PLATE: the-wasters

*Candidate 3.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-9.png`, `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 7 canon: *Those weeks his father sat by the fire in the evenings with the drawknife and a stack of seasoned wood, and made swords... shavings taken off in whispers, the thing hefted, turned, hefted again, held out level on two fingers to see where it wanted to tip. Two of them, one to Kael's arm and one longer... The boy healed by the fire, and the father built the things they were going to fight each other with by the same fire, and the wind came to the door and pushed, and the door held.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch07-the-wasters.png`, first run.**

**RUN 1 (s42) — THE SHARED-ATTENTION FIX WORKED.** Both of them are looking at the waster, the faces stay lit and readable, and the object is the subject without competing with either. **That is the two-subjects problem solved**, after `ch05/past-the-split-rock` split it across two channels and `ch06/the-wheel` split it across value and line. Three answers now; this is the cheapest.

Everything else held: fire in a stone-lined hole lighting both faces from underneath, the waster resting across two fingers at its balance point rather than gripped, the wall of tools, the barred door, the boy's empty hands in his lap, the drawknife and shavings on the floor, both mouths closed, neither looking at the other.

**MISS: THE WASTER IS A PLANK.** It came back a flat batten with a taper — no grip, no section, nothing a hand would hold. **The negations did it.** The block said *one wooden sword, plain and unfinished, no guard and no ornament*, and stripping the guard and the ornament removed the only features that would have made it read as a sword at all. **Naming a noun gives you its default; negating the default's features gives you nothing.** Both roads end at the same place: describe the construction.

**The corrected object, for a re-run:** *a practice sword cut from one piece of pale seasoned wood, about the length of the boy's arm. A round grip at one end long enough for a hand and a half, then a blade section that is lens-shaped in cross-section, thicker down its centre than at its edges, tapering slightly toward a blunt squared tip. No guard, no crosspiece and no ornament — the shape alone says sword.*

**Minor: the drawknife came back with decorative scrollwork on it.** Nothing in this house has ornament on it, and the block only banned ornament on the waster. Cheap to fix; not worth a run by itself.

**THE CHAPTER'S THESIS IN ONE ROOM**, and it is carried entirely by staging: nothing in either face says what the objects are for. **Do not let the picture explain itself** — no grimness, no foreboding, no significant look between them.

**THE SHARED ATTENTION IS WHAT MAKES THE OBJECT THE SUBJECT WITHOUT COMPETING WITH THE FACES** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Four figures: bind a group by naming one thing they all attend to). Both of them are looking at the waster; the faces stay lit and readable and point at it. That is the fix for the two-subjects problem this session kept hitting.

**FIRELIGHT FROM A HOLE IN THE FLOOR IS THE PROVEN CONFIGURATION** — near, warm, below both faces. Third use.

**The Ch. 6 scar is spent and appears**, healed and long closed, on the LEFT forearm (`03`). Named and located, no tissue vocabulary.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: portrait reference of the man.

Setting: the single room of a small stone-and-turf mountain house at
night, as loose wash suggestion — a heavy plank table, a wall of hand
tools on pegs behind, a barred plank door, everything beyond the two
figures falling into deep cool shadow.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS A SMALL CONTAINED FIRE IN A STONE-LINED HOLE IN THE
FLOOR BETWEEN THEM, banked low, no flames reaching. It lights both
faces from underneath — the undersides of the jaws, the cheekbones,
the brows, the hands — and leaves the roof and the corners dark.

Composition: two figures sitting on the floor on either side of the
fire hole, cropped at the waist, close, turned in toward each other
and toward the fire.

BOTH OF THEM ARE LOOKING AT THE SAME THING AND IT IS THE SUBJECT OF
THE IMAGE: one wooden sword, plain and unfinished, no guard and no
ornament, about the length of the boy's arm, held out level on two of the
man's fingers at its balance point — resting across the
fingers, not gripped, not raised, not pointed at anything.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the man's right hand holds the wooden sword
level on two fingers. His left rests on his knee, holding nothing. A
drawknife and a few pale curls of shaving lie on the floor beside
him. The boy's hands are open and empty in his lap. There is no
metal blade anywhere in the picture and no second wooden sword.

The man watches the sword and nothing else, judging where it wants to
tip. Level, mouth closed, brows unmoved, entirely unreadable — NOT
grim, NOT sad, NOT proud, NOT tender, NOT troubled. Broad across the
top of the back and nothing wasted below it. Iron-dark hair to the
jaw. A face weathered far past its years.

The boy watches the sword too, absorbed, sitting easily, still
healing — a healed line closed and pale across his left forearm, no
other mark on him. Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and
colourless, catching the firelight without taking its colour. Dark
hair cut short and uneven.

Nothing is happening and neither of them is about to speak. Both
mouths are closed. They are not looking at each other.

The fire is the brightest thing in the picture and is loose and
soft-edged with no detail in it. The two lit faces and the wooden
sword are the most finished things and carry the sharpest step from
light to dark. The room, the tools, the table and both sets of
clothing are held dark and loose with no hard edges.

Coarse undyed mended wool on both, the man's heavy and sized to him
and closed at the throat, the boy's thin and too large. The ink
carries the cloth at the seams and the mended places.
```

### PLATE: the-hand-over-the-face

*Candidate 3.* Anchors: `images/portraits/vask.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`. Ch. 8 canon: *his father made a sound... It had been short, low, one note — and his father's hand came up and covered the lower half of his own face, thumb and knuckle, a man rubbing his jaw at the end of a day, and above the hand his eyes had gone bright and creased at the corners, and his mother was not looking at either of them, she was looking at the bread, cutting it, and her mouth was pressed the way she pressed it when the bird got the song wrong on purpose, and the lamplight was moving on both their faces.*

**THE ONE PLATE IN THE BOOK WHERE VASK'S FACE IS ALLOWED TO DO SOMETHING.** `03` and the brief are absolute — *nothing in them that is angry, nothing that is anything* — and every plate so far has enforced it. **Here the page itself spends the exception**, in words, and this is the only time it does. So the eyes go bright and creased, and the rule is not touched: it is the page's one withdrawal from an account the book otherwise never draws on.

**AND BOTH OF THEM ARE HIDING IT FROM HIM, WHICH IS THE PICTURE.** He covers his face with his own hand; she will not look up from the bread. **The viewer is Kael**, so he is not in frame — the plate is the thing he was not supposed to see, seen.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch08-the-hand-over-the-face.png`, run 2.**

**RUN 2 CONFIRMS §NAME BOTH TEMPERATURES.** One clause naming the dark as cool grey-blue, two objects reserved as bare paper, and the ink told to stay the strongest element — **and the same composition stopped being brown.** The lamp is now a warm island in a cold room, the flame and the cut face of the loaf are the two lightest things in the picture, and the tool wall reads as cool iron instead of dissolving. **Nothing about the staging changed between the two runs.**

**RUN 1 (s42) — THE BEAT LANDED AND THE COLOUR DID NOT.** His own hand across his own mouth with the eyes bright and creased above it, her head down over the loaf and both hands working, neither looking at the other or at the viewer: all of it, first run, including the exception the page spends on his face.

**THE AUTHOR'S NOTE WAS MUDDY AND WATERY, AND THE CAUSE IS A CLAUSE THAT WAS NOT WRITTEN.** The block named a warm source and then held everything else *dark and loose with no hard edges* — **it never said what colour the dark was.** With only a lamp's hue in play the whole frame went one warm brown, no bare paper anywhere, and the ink softened into the wash.

**AND IT IS THE EXACT MIRROR OF `ch05/the-two-pails`**, which banned warm colour and came back greyscale. **Whichever temperature a plate does not name is the one that disappears.** Both plates in this folder that failed on colour failed the same way, from opposite ends. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name both temperatures.

**Amended below**: the warm light is told to stop, everything beyond it is cool grey-blue with no brown in it, the flame and the cut face of the loaf are reserved as bare paper, and the ink is told to stay the strongest element instead of being softened along with everything else.

**HER HANDS ARE IN FRAME AND OCCUPIED**, cutting, which `03` requires of any figure of her: *the hands that undid knots... her hands are where the book keeps her.*

**A lamp on the table is a near warm source** — the configuration that lights a face (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §What a warm source will and will not light).

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the man.
Image 2: portrait reference of the woman.

Setting: the single room of a small stone-and-turf mountain house at
night, as loose wash suggestion — a scrubbed plank table across the
foreground, a wall of hand tools on pegs behind, everything past the
two figures falling into deep cool shadow.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS ONE SMALL OIL LAMP STANDING ON THE TABLE BETWEEN
THEM, close to both faces. It lights them from below and to one side,
catching the undersides of the jaws, the cheekbones and the hands.

THE WARM LIGHT STOPS WHERE IT STOPS, AND EVERYTHING BEYOND IT IS
COOL. The room, the roof, the tool wall and every shadow are cool
grey and grey-blue, with NO BROWN AND NO
WARM COLOUR ANYWHERE OUTSIDE THE LAMP'S REACH. The picture is warm in
one small place and cold everywhere else.

The lamp flame and the cut face of the loaf are reserved as bare
unpainted paper — the two lightest things in the picture and the only
places the paper is left bare.

Composition: the two of them seated at the far side of the table,
close, cropped at the chest, seen straight on from across the table
at a seated person's height. There is nobody else in the frame and
no third place set.

THE MAN'S OWN HAND COVERS THE LOWER HALF OF HIS OWN FACE — the heel
of the hand under the chin, the thumb along one side of the jaw and
the bent knuckle of the forefinger across the mouth, so that the
mouth is completely hidden. The posture is that of a man rubbing his
jaw at the end of a day. ABOVE THE HAND HIS EYES HAVE GONE BRIGHT AND THE SKIN
AT THEIR OUTER CORNERS IS CREASED INTO DEEP FOLDS. He is looking down
and slightly away, not at the viewer and not at the woman. He is
laughing, silently, and hiding it. Not grinning, not grimacing, not
weeping — the whole of it is in the eyes and the hand.

THE WOMAN IS NOT LOOKING AT ANYTHING BUT THE BREAD. Her head is
lowered over a round loaf on a board, both hands working — one flat
on the loaf, the other drawing a knife through it — and her eyes stay
on the cut. Her mouth is pressed flat and held, the lips pushed
together, the corners tight. She is holding the same thing in and it
is coming out at the mouth instead. Not smiling, not frowning.

Neither of them is looking at the other, and neither is looking at
the viewer.

Their two faces are the most finished things in the picture and carry
the sharpest step from light to dark. The table, the tools, the room
and the clothing are held at quiet dark values with soft wash edges,
but the ink linework stays the strongest element in all of them and
does not blur away.

THE MAN: broad across the top of the back and nothing wasted below
it. Iron-dark hair to the jaw. A face weathered far past its years,
deep-lined. Coarse undyed working cloth, closed at the throat.
THE WOMAN: long dark hair loose past the shoulders, fine bones under
worked hands. Plain coarse mended village cloth.
The ink carries the cloth on both, at the seams and the mended
places. Neither wears any ornament or metal.
```

### PLATE: the-one-who-did-not-laugh

*From `candidates.md`'s Ch. 8 alternate, corrected to the page.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` — her face and colouring; she is younger here. Ch. 8 canon: *it made the benches laugh — a short crop of laughter, many throats at once*... *the way the girl at the end of the second bench — dark-haired, straight-backed, with a face that gave out nothing it hadn't priced first — had not laughed. Her stillness had changed when he spoke; that was all. Everyone else in the room he had learned nothing about. About her he had learned one thing, and it was the only thing all day he knew how to read.* And, on the slate: *"The minima," Priscian said, and wrote it on the great slate in letters a hand high.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch08-the-one-who-did-not-laugh.png`, first run.**

**AND A SECOND TREATMENT OF THE SAME MOMENT IS ALSO ADOPTED (author, s42):** `images/plates/ch08-the-one-who-did-not-laugh-cold.png`, from `### PLATE: the-one-who-did-not-laugh-cold` below. **Same staging, same four children, same beat — different colour and different edge.** The author kept both: *this one is best... less watery, but the one has its own appeal too.*

**RUN 1 (s42) — THE TWO-CLAUSE MECHANISM DID CHARACTERISATION, and that is the finding.** She is the smallest and quietest figure in the frame and unmistakably the most finished thing in it: the three laughing children came back broadly washed with summary features and soft edges, and her face carries every hard edge in the picture. **`03`'s *forgettable at a glance and unforgettable the third time you notice she hasn't moved* is not a note about her personality — it is a value instruction, and it renders.** Also held: four figures and no crowd, the bound hair against a laughing girl's loose hair (silhouette separation arriving for free), the high collar, no ornament, no warm colour, no master and no lectern.

**THE LETTERING RENDERED CLEAN, WHICH WAS THE GAMBLE.** `MINIMA` came back correctly spelled, in even chalk capitals, at the stated size. **The recipe worth keeping: one short word, all capitals, named exactly, stated as the only thing written on the surface.** The folder had no evidence text could be trusted at all; it now has one clean instance and the conditions that produced it.

**MISS: SHE READS OLDER THAN THE OTHERS.** The three laughing children come in at about eleven and she reads adolescent — a gap inside one frame, on a bench where every child is the same age. The block put her age in the reference line (*she is younger here, about eleven*) and nowhere else, which is exactly the placement `../portrait-prompt-system.md` §AGE block says gets ignored. **This is the second run to point at §A close-up plate and the age line, which is logged there as raised-and-unconfirmed** — the first datum was overturned by the author, so this one is recorded and the section is left open rather than promoted on two soft readings.

**ELARINE'S FIRST APPEARANCE, AND THE COMPOSITION IS THE CHARACTER.** `03`: *forgettable at a glance and unforgettable the third time you notice she hasn't moved.* **So she is not the biggest thing in the frame and she is the most finished thing in it** — the two-clause mechanism doing characterisation rather than decoration. The laughing faces are animated and loose; hers is quiet and exact.

**THE VIEWER IS KAEL**, at his own bench, so the room turns toward the camera. He is not in frame and the laughter is aimed at where he is.

**FOUR FIGURES, WHICH IS THE TESTED CEILING** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Four figures). *A short crop of laughter, many throats at once* is written as three, not as a crowd.

**HER ONE TELL IS DELIBERATELY ABSENT.** `03` keeps the spots of colour high on her cheeks for Ch. 13 and Ch. 17 — *when something is about to be said properly or when she is being spent in public* — and this is neither.

**THE LETTERING IS A GAMBLE AND IS WORTH ONE RUN.** Text renders unreliably; if it comes back as chalk marks rather than a word, the picture still works and the slate is still a slate.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the girl — her face and colouring; she
is younger here, about eleven.

Setting: a stone lecture room in a temple, as loose wash suggestion —
long plank benches, a great dark slate on the wall behind, the far
corners falling into shadow. Flat cool daylight from a high window on
one side. No warm colour anywhere.

On the slate, chalked in white letters a hand high: MINIMA. Nothing
else is written on it.

Composition: looking along a bench from one end, at seated height,
cropped at the chest. FOUR CHILDREN OF ABOUT ELEVEN, and no more than
four. THREE OF THEM ARE TURNED TOWARD THE VIEWER AND LAUGHING —
heads back or tipped together, mouths open, eyes creased, one with a
hand half up. THE FOURTH, AT THE FAR END OF THE BENCH, IS NOT
LAUGHING.

THE GIRL AT THE END: sitting straight-backed and completely still,
her head turned the same way as the others, her attention on the same
point out of frame — but her face gives nothing back. Mouth closed
and unmoved, brows level, no reaction anywhere in it. She is not
frowning, not disapproving, not bored, not cold and not tired. She is
looking at something and pricing it, and the pricing is still
running.

She is small and finely built, narrow through the shoulders, with
nothing on her that does not work. Ink-dark hair, a flat neutral
black with no blue in it, drawn back severely and bound tight at the
nape — nothing loose anywhere. Pale indoor skin, no weathering and no
colour in the cheeks. Eyes very dark, near black. Plain dark
close-fitting clothing of very good quality, exactly made and
buttoned high at the throat, in ink-black and dark olive. No
ornament, no metal and no white on her anywhere.

HER FACE IS THE MOST FINISHED THING IN THE PICTURE and carries the
sharpest step from light to dark, and she is the smallest and
quietest figure in it. The three laughing children are loose and
broadly washed with soft edges, their features summary — no hard
edges and no detail in any of their faces. The benches, the slate and
the room are held at quiet middle values.

The three laughing children wear plainer, looser clothes than hers,
and none of them is dressed like her. Nobody else is in the frame:
no master, no lectern and no adult.
```

### PLATE: seeing-about-the-goat

*Author-requested (s40); built then, never run, unchanged.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png` — though his face is not in this picture. Ch. 8 canon: *his father stood up and went to see about the goat, who did not need seeing about, and was some time in coming back.*

The other half of `the-hand-over-the-face`, and it is the same beat: the man leaves a lit room in order to have a feeling about his son where nobody can see it.

```
No reference image.

Setting: the yard of a small stone-and-turf mountain house at night,
wet after rain, as loose wash suggestion — a fenced pen, a woodpile,
the mountain behind lost in deep cool wash.

Composition: the man standing full length in the yard with his BACK
TO THE VIEWER, facing away toward a goat in the pen. He is between
the viewer and the animal. No part of his face is visible.

Behind him and to one side, an open doorway stands empty — a plain
plank door open on a lit room, with NOBODY IN IT and nobody in the
doorway. The lamplight from it lies across the wet ground in a long
bright rectangle, and his own shadow is cut into it, long and black,
reaching away from the door toward him.

THE LIGHT ON THE GROUND IS THE BRIGHTEST THING IN THE PICTURE and
carries the sharpest step from light to dark, wet ground taking the
light in broken reflections. The man is a dark shape against it. The
yard, the pen, the woodpile and the sky are held dark and loose.

He is doing nothing. Standing square and still with his arms at his
sides, both hands empty, not leaning on the rail, not reaching for
the animal, not working. ONE GOAT, side-on in the pen, entirely
indifferent — not looking at him, not curious, not alarmed.

Broad across the top of the back and nothing wasted below it. Coarse
undyed mended working cloth, the ink carrying the cloth at the seams
and the mended places.
```

### PLATE: the-one-who-did-not-laugh-cold

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch08-the-one-who-did-not-laugh-cold.png`, first run.** The second treatment of the plate above, kept as its own block because **one image, one prompt**: this text produced that picture and the block above produced the other.

**WHAT DIFFERS IS COLOUR AND EDGE, AND NOTHING ELSE.** The staging, the four children, the slate, the crop and the two-clause mechanism are identical. Three changes: the cold hues named and sited on the slate, the walls, the benches and the clothing with skin as the only warm thing; three specific objects reserved as bare paper; and the ink protected against the general softening — **with that softening scoped to the three laughing FACES only**, which is what stopped the benches and their clothes going soupy with them.

**IT IS THE COLDEST AND MOST DRAWN PICTURE IN THE FOLDER**, and it sits beside a warm, washed version of the same frame. **That pair is the most useful thing in the set for the finish question the `README` leaves open** — not an argument about which is right, but the same picture rendered both ways with the diff written down.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the girl — her face and colouring; she
is younger here, about eleven.

Setting: a stone lecture room in a temple, as loose wash suggestion —
long plank benches, a great dark slate on the wall behind, the far
corners falling into shadow. Flat cool daylight from a high window on
one side.

THE COLOURS ARE NAMED AND SITED, AND THEY ARE ALL COLD: the slate a
blue-black, the stone walls a cool pale grey, the benches a grey
timber with no warmth in it, all the clothing in blue-greys and
ink-black. THE ONLY WARM THING IN THE PICTURE IS SKIN, and there is
no brown anywhere else in it.

Bare unpainted paper is reserved in exactly three places and nowhere
else: the window, blown out to white; the chalked letters; and the
pale edge of light along the front of the nearest bench.

On the slate, chalked in white letters a hand high: MINIMA. Nothing
else is written on it.

Composition: looking along a bench from one end, at seated height,
cropped at the chest. FOUR CHILDREN OF ABOUT ELEVEN, and no more than
four. THREE OF THEM ARE TURNED TOWARD THE VIEWER AND LAUGHING —
heads back or tipped together, mouths open, eyes creased, one with a
hand half up. THE FOURTH, AT THE FAR END OF THE BENCH, IS NOT
LAUGHING.

THE GIRL AT THE END: sitting straight-backed and completely still,
her head turned the same way as the others, her attention on the same
point out of frame — but her face gives nothing back. Mouth closed
and unmoved, brows level, no reaction anywhere in it. She is not
frowning, not disapproving, not bored, not cold and not tired. She is
looking at something and pricing it, and the pricing is still
running.

She is small and finely built, narrow through the shoulders, with
nothing on her that does not work. Ink-dark hair, a flat neutral
black with no blue in it, drawn back severely and bound tight at the
nape — nothing loose anywhere. Pale indoor skin, no weathering and no
colour in the cheeks. Eyes very dark, near black. Plain dark
close-fitting clothing of very good quality, exactly made and
buttoned high at the throat, in ink-black and dark olive. No
ornament, no metal and no white on her anywhere.

HER FACE IS THE MOST FINISHED THING IN THE PICTURE and carries the
sharpest step from light to dark, and she is the smallest and
quietest figure in it. THE THREE LAUGHING FACES are loose and broadly
washed with soft edges and summary features — no hard edges and no
detail in any of them.

That looseness belongs to their FACES ONLY. Everywhere else the ink
linework stays the strongest element and does not dissolve into the
wash: the benches, the slate frame, the window reveal and all the
clothing keep dark scratchy ink structure over their washes, and the
laughing children's clothes are drawn as firmly as hers. The room is
held at quiet middle values but is never blurred.

The three laughing children wear plainer, looser clothes than hers,
and none of them is dressed like her. Nobody else is in the frame:
no master, no lectern and no adult.
```

# FILE: plates/ch09.md

# Ch. 9 — The Ledger · plate sheet

**Both plates adopted (s42).** Aurelian is thirteen here; `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png` is the reference, cut a year above and close enough.

---

### PLATE: aurelians-candle

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png`. Ch. 9 canon: *Not the wick — the wax. The whole cup of the candle's top went up in a soft orange sheet, a fistful of fire standing off the bench, sudden enough that the boy beside him went backward off his heels; and the bench had turned to him before the flame finished standing, and for one breath, before the yard-master's hand came down over it with a wet cloth that was clearly always ready, the fire lit Aurelian's face from below, and it was the face from the roller morning with the world put right again.* And the roller morning itself: *Aurelian stood off it with his breath tearing and his face bright, a boy standing where he stood best.*

**HIS SHEET NAMES THIS AS THE BEST IMAGE OF HIM IN THE BOOK, and as a PLATE rather than a portrait** — because the portrait system's fixed lighting is soft and directional and this one is lit from underneath by a fire. It was blocked on that being possible. **It is not blocked any more:** `ch05/the-fact-lands` proved an out-of-frame source below a face lights it properly (s42), which is a different finding from `ch01-fallen-stars`, where an *in-frame* warm source rendered as a source and threw nothing.

**THE LIGHTING IS THE ARCHETYPE RISK, AND IT IS THE WHOLE DESIGN PROBLEM.** A face lit from below means menace in every visual tradition there is, and `03` is emphatic that **Aurelian is not a strawman** — *pleasant, open, easy, genuinely charming; never sneering, never smug, never cruel.* So the block has to put a delighted thirteen-year-old inside the lighting convention for villainy and hold him there. The expression is written as the subject, in the positive, with the whole neighbourhood banned: `ch05/the-fact-lands` proved that **banning one archetype delivers its neighbour**, so smug and sneering are banned alongside frightened and startled, or the face will simply move next door.

**WHAT THE MOMENT ACTUALLY IS, and it decides the gaze.** *The world put right again* is internal — a boy looking at fire he made, with everything back where it belongs after a morning in which one small nobody walked the roller alone. **So his eyes are down on the flame, not up at the bench.** A face raised to a cheering crowd is a boy being pleased *at* people, which is the reading `03` forbids; a face down on his own fire is a boy being pleased, full stop.

**HIS WARMTH IS EXTERNAL HERE FOR THE ONLY TIME.** The brief keeps *fire's warmth in his colouring, never stated* and **his ground cool — warm parchment is Aeliana's alone.** In this picture the warmth is a literal fire, so the block spends it on his face and holds the wall, the air and the frost cold behind him. The rule survives intact; it just gets its one exception drawn.

**THE TWO-CLAUSE MECHANISM, ASSIGNED CAREFULLY** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name the winner, and the s42 limit): the fire is the brightest value and would take all the finish if left alone, so it is demoted to a soft loose wash with no hard edge and no detail, and **the sharpest step from light to dark is sited on the boundary of the lit part of his face.** Brightest and most-finished are deliberately two different objects.

**NO FRECKLE INSTRUCTION, DELIBERATELY.** `03` rules freckles never for him, and the medium speckles every face regardless (author ruling, s34: texture, not a mark). Writing the negation would be fighting granulation, which the folder has already ruled against. Expect speckling; do not read it as canon.

**NO INSIGNIA IS LOAD-BEARING, not merely absent** — `07/planted-clues` reserves the Corvane insignia for the Part IV fire.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch09-aurelians-candle.png`, first run.** The block below is what produced it and is not reassembled: it was amended after the run and the amendments were reverted when the author adopted the image.

**THE DESIGN PROBLEM IS SOLVED, and that is what this run is for.** The face is lit from underneath and **is not menacing** — open, bright, mouth open, wholly delighted, no smugness and no cruelty anywhere in it. **A boy inside the lighting convention for villainy, reading as a boy.** `03`'s *never sneering, never smug, never cruel* survives its hardest possible staging. The cool ground held with it: stone wall in cold grey-blue behind him, the warmth landing only on his face, so *his ground stays cool* survives its one literal-fire exception. No second face; the other child is a dark featureless shape at the left edge.

**AUTHOR RULINGS (s42), both against the sheet's own reading:** the **age holds** — this session flagged the face as reading several years above thirteen and the author read it as correct — and the **gaze is fine** where the sheet wanted it turned down onto the fire. Both stand as drawn, same scope as the s40 eye rulings: one picture, and nothing in `03` or the brief moves.

**ONE THING THE SHEET STILL OWES THE AUTHOR A SENTENCE ON: the wax did not catch.** What is drawn is an ordinary candle burning at the wick, and the page's beat is precisely the distinction — *Not the wick — the wax. The whole cup of the candle's top went up in a soft orange sheet, a fistful of fire standing off the bench, sudden enough that the boy beside him went backward off his heels.* A reader arriving from that paragraph sees a boy delighted at a normal candle. **Not raised again beyond this line; it is on the record and it is the author's to spend or not.**

**AND THE RUN PROVED A GENERAL RULE ANYWAY.** The word `candle` supplied its own default and beat every adjective attached to it — third instance after `dagger` and `coat`, in three unrelated domains. `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name a thing by construction is generalised from those three and does not depend on how this picture is judged.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: a winter training yard at the foot of a high stone wall, as
loose wash suggestion — rough stone behind him going into deep cool
shadow, frost on the ground, no detail anywhere. The edge of a plank
bench crosses the very bottom of the frame.

Composition: close on his head and shoulders, the face filling most
of the frame, seen from slightly below. His head is tipped down
toward the flame in front of him. Nothing below his chest is in
frame.

THE ONLY LIGHT IS THE FIRE AND IT IS BELOW HIM. On the bench in front
of him a soft sheet of orange flame stands up off the top of a tallow
candle, a double handful of fire, low in the frame and cropped by its
bottom edge. It lights his face entirely from underneath: the
underside of the jaw and the chin, the underside of the brow, the
septum and the underside of the nose, the front of the throat. The
forehead, the top of the head and the hair above it fall away into
shadow. Everything behind him stays cold — the wall, the air, the
frost — and no warm colour touches any of it.

THE FIRE IS THE BRIGHTEST VALUE. HIS FACE IS THE MOST FINISHED THING.
The flame is soft-edged and loose, a wash with no hard edge anywhere
in it and no detail in it at all. The sharpest step from light to
dark in the picture happens along the boundary of the lit part of his
face, at the cheekbone and the jaw.

HIS EXPRESSION IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. Lit up, open and
delighted — a boy standing exactly where he belongs with the world
put back the way it should be. Lips parted, the breath still coming
hard, brows raised, the whole face bright and unguarded and young.
NOT smug, NOT sneering, NOT triumphant over anybody, NOT cruel, NOT
sly, NOT knowing — and equally NOT frightened, NOT startled, NOT
alarmed. He is not looking at any person and there is nobody in the
frame he could be pleased at. He is looking at the fire.

Eyes amber, gold-brown, wide open and turned down to the flame,
catching it. The skin immediately around the eye stays cool, so the
amber holds and does not wash out into the firelight.

Dark-gold hair pushed back off the forehead, thick, dry and clean.
Clear well-fed skin with sun on it, unweathered, no dirt and nothing
worked into it anywhere.

Thirteen years old and built a year ahead of it: broad through the
shoulder and the neck for his age, the frame already going toward a
man's, and the face still soft and unfinished with the jaw not yet
come in.

Good dark wool, well cut and clean, a plain collar. No insignia, no
badge, no chain, no metal and no ornament of any kind anywhere on
him.

NO OTHER FACE IS IN THIS PICTURE. The other children are present only
as dark unlit shapes at the extreme edges of the frame, out of focus,
no features drawn — one at the left edge going backward and off
balance, away from the fire.
```

### PLATE: the-roller-alone

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-9.png` — Kael is ten here. Ch. 9 canon: *It was a drum of grey stone the length of a man, kept for flattening the yard after rain*... *"Good," the yard-master said. "Again. Alone."*... *Kael went out, because, of course, a question was standing. There was a pry-bar in the well-shed and a chock of wood by the racks, and the rest was the spring he was eight: where a boy who came up to a thing's shoulder stood so that the thing's own bigness went to work against it. The roller came back across the yard in stiff little tips and shoves, one boy walking it the way you walk a barrel, and the yard went quiet in a way it had not been for the seven. / He set the bar down. He was not much out of breath, and he heard how that sounded in the quiet.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch09-the-roller-alone.png`, first run.**

**RUN 1 (s42).** The lever reads: bar under the stone's edge, wooden block as fulcrum, both hands on the free end, the drum tipped and held. **And the ease held** — weight settled, shoulders down, nothing braced, no strain in the face — which was the archetype risk and the whole beat. The watching line, the three bent over their knees, no faces among them, no adult, frost as bare paper, and the cold-with-named-warm all came through.

**MISS: HE READS ABOUT THIRTEEN.** And the cause is specific rather than general — **the block gave the STONE three measurements and the BOY none.** *The top edge level with his shoulder* and *longer than the boy is tall* both size the drum against him; nothing anywhere sizes him against himself or against another person. `ch07-kael-of-what` put him beside an adult and he came back ten; `ch01-vask-kael-goat` measured him head-to-body and against a man's hip and came back four.

**So: a solo figure has no age reference in the frame, and the fix is his own proportions, not the object's** — head as a fraction of his height, limbs, the build. Recorded here, per picture, not promoted: this is the third run to drift old and the author has already judged one of the three correct.

**THE SUBJECT IS A DISPROPORTION, so it is stated as measurement** (`../scene-prompt-system.md` §Scale between figures): the top of the drum at the boy's shoulder, the drum longer than the boy is tall. Adjectives cannot carry this picture; the whole point is a ratio.

**THE ARCHETYPE RISK IS EFFORT.** A child moving a great stone is an image that wants strain, heave and triumph — and the page says the opposite twice: *stiff little tips and shoves, the way you walk a barrel*, and *he was not much out of breath.* The ease **is** the beat, and the yard goes quiet because of it. So the block bans the heroic register and its neighbours and states the positive: a boy doing a familiar chore.

**FORTY CHILDREN, WRITTEN AS FOUR AND A MASS.** The tested ceiling is four resolved figures (§Four figures), so three bent over their knees carry *the seven who did it first* and the rest are summary shapes with no faces. Same solve as `ch08/the-one-who-did-not-laugh`.

**AURELIAN IS DELIBERATELY NOT IDENTIFIABLE IN IT.** *The bright went off the face by degrees* is a real beat and it would need his reference, his colouring and a second resolved face, and it would split a picture whose subject is one boy and one stone. It is available as its own plate and is not this one.

**THE WARM IS NAMED, because this is a cold picture** (§Name both temperatures, s42). `ch05/the-two-pails` went greyscale by having no warm to hold against the cold; here the skin, the pry-bar and the chock are the warm, and they are said so.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: a walled stone training yard at a temple on a winter
morning, as loose wash suggestion — flat flagstones with frost on
them, a long wall behind with wooden weapon racks against it, the far
corners going into cool wash. Flat cold overcast daylight, no sun and
no shadows with edges.

Composition: the boy and the stone together in the middle of the
frame, cropped just below his knees, seen from the side and a little
below him.

SCALE, AND IT IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE: a drum of grey stone lying
on its side, longer than the boy is tall, its top edge level with his
shoulder. It is one solid piece, plain and unmarked, with no axle, no
frame, no handles and no rope on it.

The drum is tipped up a few degrees on the near end and is held there
by ONE IRON PRY-BAR, its tip under the stone's edge and a small block
of wood set under the bar as a fulcrum. WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: both
the boy's hands are on the free end of the bar, one above the other,
pushing it down and across. Nothing else is in either hand and there
is no rope, no lever and no second bar anywhere in the picture.

HE IS NOT STRAINING. Weight settled, shoulders down, arms not locked,
back straight, breathing easily — the ease of somebody doing a chore
he has done a hundred times. NOT heaving, NOT braced, NOT triumphant,
NOT proud, NOT grimacing, no teeth showing, no effort anywhere in the
face or the body. His attention is on the point where the bar meets
the stone.

Behind them, at a distance along the wall, a line of children of
about his own age, standing and watching, silent and completely
still. THREE OF THEM, NEARER, ARE BENT OVER WITH THEIR HANDS ON THEIR
KNEES, still getting their breath back. No face in the line or among
the three is drawn — they are dark summary shapes, loosely washed,
with soft edges and no features. There is no adult in the picture.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: the boy's skin, the
pale wood of the fulcrum block and the worn wooden grip of the bar
are the only warm colour anywhere. The stone, the flagstones, the
frost, the wall and all the clothing are cool grey and grey-blue with
no brown in them. The frost on the flagstones is reserved as bare
unpainted paper.

His face and his two hands on the bar are the most finished things in
the picture and carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The drum
is a large quiet grey mass with no detail and no hard edge on it, and
the watching children are quieter still. The ink linework stays the
strongest element throughout and does not blur into the wash.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless — no more colour in
the iris than in the whites around it. Dark hair cut short and
uneven. Plain undyed working cloth, coarse and mended and a little
too large for him, the ink carrying the cloth at the seams and the
mended places. He wears no ornament and no metal.
```
