# FILE: plates/ch10.md

# Ch. 10 — The Asking · plate sheet

**Both plates adopted, each on its first run (s43).** Kael is ten in this chapter (`07/timeline` — the birthday passes unmarked between chapters), so the anchor is `images/portraits/kael-9.png`, one year light and the same portrait that already carried Ch. 7 at ten. Severin is `images/portraits/severin.png`, Vask `images/portraits/vask.png`.

**THE CHAPTER IS TWO CHAPTERS AND THE PAIR SPLITS ON IT.** The Temple study and the mountain garden are two settings, two casts and two weathers inside one number, and taking one plate from each buys the pair more distance than any two moments from the same half. It also puts the only machine in the plate set on a page.

**BOTH TAKE THE DRAWN TREATMENT AND HUE GOES PER PICTURE (session ruling, s43, author-delegated).** `ch08.md`'s controlled pair separates on three clauses, and only two of them are about finish: **bare paper reserved by name**, and **the ink protected from a general softening.** The third — cold hues sited on named objects — is colour, and colour is adjusted per picture by author ruling (s42), not by rule. So both blocks below carry the two finish clauses, which is where the set was actually drifting (`../README.md`: the doorframe glossier than the long game), and each names its own temperature: the study cool with the honey as its one warm thing, the garden warm with the water and the stone named cool.

**ANCHORING COST, PER PLATE.** Severin's reference takes the half-figure COMPOSITION override, so it carries him to the waist and the table crop is anchored exactly where this picture needs it. Kael's and Vask's are head-and-shoulders: everything below their collars is authored in these blocks, anchored to nothing, and will not repeat in the next plate.

---

### PLATE: the-honey-and-the-bread

*Promoted out of the Ch. 10 alternate on `candidates.md`, which had the jar as set dressing beside the empty book; this takes the jar and the two faces instead, and the alternate keeps the book.* Anchors: `images/portraits/severin.png` and `images/portraits/kael-9.png`. Ch. 10 canon: *The study was a room with too many rooms in it. Books to the ceiling on two walls; a table of instruments — a balance, glass, a thing of nested brass rings he had no name for; and on the sill, in a row of clay pots, green things growing that had no business being green in that light. The master set out bread, and took down a jar of honey and put it by the bread without one word being said about it, and Kael looked at the jar, and the master looked at him looking, and neither of them said the thing, and it got said anyway.*

**THE JAR IS A SPINE OBJECT AND THIS IS WHERE IT GETS DRAWN.** Four chapters plant on it and none of them explains it. Ch. 2: *there was honey in his milk at supper. There was not usually honey.* Ch. 4, Severin's second sentence in the book: *"So," the man said. "You're the reason the honey's always gone."* Ch. 7, on the cleaned knife back in its place: *The wall said what the honey said, in the wall's language.* Ch. 18, four years on: *a blank book, an empty column, a jar of honey that was never mentioned.* **Honey is this book's word for a kindness nobody will admit to**, and one picture pays all of it.

**THE GAZE IS ONE-SIDED, WHICH IS THE EASY VERSION AND THE BETTER ONE.** The page hands it over exactly built: *Kael looked at the jar, and the master looked at him looking.* `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Getting expression rates a one-sided gaze both more reliable than mutual and more interesting, and here it is also the beat — two people declining to say the same thing in two different directions.

**THE CROP IS THE POINT: IT IS WHERE THE WRISTS FINALLY RENDER.** *The wrists coming out of the sleeves were too thick for a man whose work was paper* is the tell of his whole body, and `ch04.md` spent a full-length frame on it and lost it, concluding that **the fix is crop, not emphasis** (§Name the winner, the limit). A seated table shot with both his forearms down on the boards is that crop, and it costs nothing to take here because the moment already puts his hands on the table.

**THE COAT IS NOT DRAWN AND MUST NOT BE.** *It was the one room below the split rock where the coat came off* is the sentence this plate sits under, and `../canon-brief.md` is explicit: the coat is the book's metaphor for Kael's concealment, not a garment to style. **No garment is removed, hung, folded or laid over a chair anywhere in this picture.** What the sentence means is drawn by the boy's shoulders being down and his hands still, and by nothing else.

**COSTUME REGISTER, PRICED.** The page does not dress Severin in Ch. 10 at all. The study is inside the Temple, so this is the robes register rather than the mountain coat — and **the chain stays off**: `../canon-brief.md` holds it as *Temple and the games* canon, it is public dress, and nothing in a room with bread in it needs it. The garment is written by construction, not by name, per `ch04.md`'s proven fix — the word *coat* is what produced a Dickensian frock coat there, and the word is avoided here for both reasons at once.

**THE REFERENCE'S FACE IS THE ONE REAL RISK.** `kael-9` is deliberately *ALERT AND DIRECT... a boy in the middle of asking something*, and §What the reference carries says a reference supplies its own mood and wins a close crop. Two things make this survivable: the boy's eyes are **down on an object**, which is a mechanical change the reference holds no version of and therefore cannot fight, and the moment's load sits on Severin's face, which `severin.png` holds exactly. **If he comes back asking at the viewer, the fix is the still variant and not more words** — and that file does not exist: 317a984 wrote `prompts/portraits/kael-9-still.md` and the run was never made. `../README.md` names it as though it were in the library; it is a prompt only.

**WHAT IS IN THE ROOM AND OUT OF THIS FRAME.** The table of instruments — *a balance, glass, a thing of nested brass rings* — is page canon and is out of the crop; naming it would fill the frame with objects that are not the subject. **The fern stays**, on the sill behind them, because Ch. 18 comes back to this room and finds *the fern on the sill had not been touched*, so the establishing costs one clause and no separate plate.

**THE JAR WINS SATURATION, THE FACES WIN FINISH.** Two channels, per §Name the winner AND demote the rival, and they are deliberately not the same object: the honey is the one warm and saturated thing in a cool room, held loose and soft-edged, and the sharpest step from light to dark is sited on the two faces.

**SIZE IS ANSWERED, NOT DECLINED.** Both are seated, so height cannot carry the ten-against-old and the age gap goes onto the pair of hands instead (§Size contrast must be stated per hand, which is written for exactly this).

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch10-the-honey-and-the-bread.png`, first run.**

**THE STAGING CAME THROUGH WHOLE.** Books to the ceiling in loose wash, the sill with its clay pots and the fern, thin daylight from the window and no second source; both seated at the corner of the table; the jar set down with his right hand still against it and his left flat on the boards; the boy's hands on the table edge and reaching for nothing; four hands, two objects. **The one-sided gaze landed exactly** — the boy's eyes down on the jar, the old man's on the boy — and the jar is the most saturated thing in the picture with the loaf's cut face reading as bare paper beside it.

**AND THE BEARD AND THE LOOSE GREY HAIR ARE THE REFERENCE'S OWN**, not drift: `images/portraits/severin.png` carries both. The chain stayed off, which was the one register instruction that had to hold.

**THE FINDING OF THE RUN, AND IT IS A GENERAL ONE: A POSE BEATS A REFERENCE'S MOOD WHERE AN OVERRIDE DOES NOT.** `../scene-prompt-system.md` §What the reference carries records two runs where a stated override lost to the reference's own expression — `ch01/fallen-stars` and `ch05/the-fact-lands`, the second with the override in caps in priority position — and concludes that the fix is a second reference. **This block never argued with `kael-9`'s asking face. It moved his eyes onto an object**, and the asking face did not arrive, because a reference holds no downcast version of itself to supply. **Changing what the body is doing is cheaper than a new reference and it is what should be tried first.** One instance; recorded here, pointed at from that section, not promoted.

**THE MISS IS THE WRISTS, AND THE BLOCK CAUSED IT.** This crop was chosen because `ch04.md` lost the wrists at full length and concluded the fix is crop, not emphasis — and the wrists are still not what arrives. **The instruction was physically self-contradictory:** *plain full sleeves ending square at the wrist with no cuff... so both forearms lie bare on the table.* A sleeve that ends at the wrist covers the forearm. The renderer resolved it to the sleeve, correctly, exactly as §What a warm source will and will not light says it will refuse a physically false instruction.

**THE FIX IS TO NAME WHERE THE CLOTH STOPS ON THE ARM, NOT WHERE THE SLEEVE ENDS** — *the sleeves pushed back above the elbow, the forearms bare from the elbow to the hand.* The garment-construction habit puts every clause on the garment, and the garment is not the subject; the arm is.

**WHAT ARRIVED INSTEAD IS WORTH KEEPING: the hands.** Heavy, knuckled and out of proportion to a scholar, and they carry the same fact the wrists were meant to — this is not a man whose work is paper. **The per-hand size contrast held** too: the boy's hand beside his reads a child's.

**THE ROOM CAME BACK WARMER THAN THE BLOCK ASKED FOR.** The boards and the wall are warm brown where the block said cool grey and grey-blue. **Third time now** — `ch05/the-two-pails` went the other way into greyscale, `ch08/the-one-who-did-not-laugh` came back warm and was judged the best of its set — and it is left alone here for the same reason, per the s42 ruling that colour is looked at rather than ruled: the jar still wins saturation against warm boards, which is all the clause was protecting.

**TWO THINGS FOR THE AUTHOR, both judgements on the image rather than sheet business.** His face sits a shade heavier than *courteous and giving nothing away* — closer to sombre — which is the same direction `ch04.md` recorded as *drifted slightly stern* and is now twice. And **the grey reads white at the ends**, where the brief wants a true even neutral that resolves neither age nor drain.

**THE GREY IS NOT SPENT IN THIS PLATE.** The lids are down, so his eyes carry no visible colour — a consequence of the pose and not of the s42 constraint, which is retired. No plate owes it.

**THE ONE NEGATION THAT EARNS ITS PLACE** is the boy's hands not reaching. Bread and honey in front of a hungry village child is a real pull toward a reach, which is the test `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Negative inventory sets, and the restraint is the whole of what he is doing.

```
Image 1: severin.png — portrait reference of the old man.
Image 2: kael-9.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: a scholar's study at a temple, as loose wash suggestion —
books to the ceiling on the wall behind them, a stone window sill to
one side carrying a row of small clay pots with one tired fern among
them. Thin cool daylight from the window, and it is the only light in
the room: no lamp, no candle and no fire anywhere in it.

Composition: waist-up, the two of them across the corner of a small
table, the tabletop crossing the bottom of the frame. The boy near
side on the left, seated, turned three-quarters away from the viewer
and toward the table. The old man on the right, seated, facing him.

WHAT IS ON THE TABLE AND WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: a stoneware jar of
honey standing between them, and a plain round loaf beside it with one
cut face. The old man's right hand still rests against the jar where
he set it down; his left hand lies flat on the boards. Both the boy's
hands rest on the table edge in front of him and are not reaching —
they touch neither the bread nor the jar. Four hands,
two objects, nothing else on the table and nothing held in any hand.

THE GAZE IS ONE-SIDED AND IT IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. The boy's
eyes are down on the jar, the lids lowered, his face turned to it. The
old man's eyes are on the boy's face, watching him look. The two lines
of sight do not meet, neither of them looks at the viewer, and neither
mouth is open.

THE JAR IS THE MOST SATURATED THING IN THE PICTURE AND THE TWO FACES
ARE THE MOST FINISHED. The honey is a deep warm gold, loose and
soft-edged with no detail in it, and skin is the only other warm
colour anywhere. The room is cool — the books, the stone, the boards
and all the cloth in cool grey and grey-blue, going quiet at the
edges. The sharpest step from light to dark in the picture happens in
the two faces. The light on the window sill and the cut face of the
loaf are reserved as bare unpainted paper. The ink linework stays the
strongest element throughout and does not blur into the wash.

THE OLD MAN GIVES NOTHING AWAY. Courteous, unhurried and entirely
attentive, the brows level and not lowered — not warm, not kindly, not
stern, not amused and not sad. Hair fully grey, the whole head, a true
even neutral grey with no streak, no seam and no patch anywhere in it,
neither warm silver nor white. Tall, lean and still broad at the
shoulder, not frail. His garment, by construction: one long straight
dark undyed overgarment closed edge to edge down the centre front,
standing collar shut at the base of the throat, no lapel, no turned
collar, no buttons, no braid and no metal on it anywhere. Plain full
sleeves ending square at the wrist with no cuff and no sleeve beneath,
so both forearms lie bare on the table — thick and heavy through the
wrist, too heavy for a man whose work is paper.

THE BOY IS TEN AND THE HANDS CARRY IT. His hand on the table is a
child's, small and short-fingered, and the old man's hand beside it is
nearly twice its width. Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and
colourless, the lightest value in his face. Dark hair cut short and
uneven at home. Plain undyed working wool, coarse and much mended, the
collar loose at the throat — the ink carries the cloth in dense
scratchy linework at the seams, the patches and the mended places.

NO OTHER PERSON IS IN THE ROOM: nobody in the doorway and nobody
behind either of them.

Preserve both exactly as shown in the reference images — faces, hair,
colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features and do not mix
their clothing: the long overgarment belongs only to the old man and
the mended wool only to the boy.
```

---

### PLATE: the-waterer

*Candidate 2, tightened.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 10 canon: *A split-log channel from the runnel above the plot; a trough on an iron pin, hung a little off its middle; a counterstone lashed at the short end. The trough filled, and passed its balance, and tipped — one measured pour down the cuts between the rows, the same to each — and the stone stood it back up to fill again.* And the beat itself: *He stood over the plot with his arms folded and watched the trough fill, and tip, and right itself with a knock of wood on wood, and fill again — the whole round of it, three times, saying nothing. Then he crouched, stopped the trough with one finger, tested the pin's seat, and moved the counterstone in by the width of two fingers.*

**KAEL IS OUT OF FRAME AND THAT IS THE DESIGN.** *It was the longest Kael had ever seen him stand in front of a thing Kael had made* — the observing consciousness is the boy's, so the viewer stands where the boy stands. **Put him in the picture and it becomes a scene about approval**, with a child waiting on a verdict; leave him out and it is a man's entire attention on a thing, which is what the page actually gives and is the only way this father ever says it.

**THE INSTANT IS THE FINGER.** Of the three actions in that sentence — crouched, stopped, moved the counterstone — only the middle one is a picture: a machine caught mid-cycle and held by one finger. The pour is stopped halfway, water still running into the trough from the channel above and the last of it running off the low lip. **A man who moves exactly as much as is required, stopping a working machine with the smallest part of himself.**

**THE MACHINE IS FOUR NOUNS AND EVERY ONE OF THEM ARRIVES WITH A DEFAULT** — trough, pin, counterstone, channel. §Name a thing by construction, with its order rule: **the defining feature first, with a measurement, then the shape corrected.** The defining feature is that it is hung off its middle and swings, so that is the first clause; *no wheel, no frame, no crank* comes last, where it trims rather than replaces. `ch07.md`'s waster is the warning on the other side — banning a thing's features can take the thing with them — so the construction is stated positively and the negations only kill the mill-machinery default.

**THE ARCHETYPE RISK IS APPROVAL.** A father crouched over his son's invention is an image that wants a smile, a nod, a proud tilt of the head, and the chapter is emphatic that none of it is on his face — the pride is spent in the seed corner, off the page and in the next season. `../canon-brief.md`: *his eyes are the character, and they are empty. Not hostile, not cold, not sad, not kind, not tired.* So both sides are banned, per the `ch09.md` shape: the warm neighbours and the stern ones together, or the face moves next door.

**SCARS STAY OUT BY CONSTRUCTION, NOT BY NEGATION.** The tally-marks are on his forearms and are on the page in Ch. 3, so bare unmarked forearms in this frame would contradict, and a negation would name them into a picture that has no use for them. **The sleeves are down to the wrist and the question never arises.**

**SIZE IS INVENTED HERE, AND IT IS SAID SO.** The page gives the trough no dimension, and an image cannot decline to answer — so the size is set by framing, against his forearm, and it is the plate answering with framing rather than the sheet claiming canon. Nothing in `07` fixes it and a later picture may set it differently.

**HIS HAND WINS, NOT HIS FACE.** Faces are what a renderer finishes by default, so the finish is claimed for the hand and the pin explicitly and the plot behind is demoted on both channels — quiet middle values and no hard edges — per §Name the winner, which is two clauses because edge and finish are two things.

**THE WARM PICTURE NAMES ITS COOL.** The inverse of `ch09.md`'s roller: this one is summer daylight on dry ground and timber, so the water, the lashed stone and the shadow under the bed are named cool, or the whole frame goes one brown the way `ch08.md`'s supper did.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch10-the-waterer.png`, first run.**

**AUTHOR RULING (s43), and it settles the reference question the sheet raised below: *it looks like Vask to me.*** The picture stands as drawn and **`vask.png` is not re-cut.** Scope, stated the same way as the s42 rulings on the candle: this is a ruling on the pictures, and nothing in `03` moves. What it costs is written into `../canon-brief.md` §VASK so no future session re-opens it — the temple-grey stays true on the page and is simply a fact the image library does not carry.

**The two machine misses below therefore stand as drawn**, and are kept as findings for the next picture with a mechanism in it rather than as a reason to re-run this one.

**RUN 1 (s43).**

**THE BEAT LANDED.** One finger on the trough's high edge with the pour stopped under it, the other hand flat on the ground taking his weight, water still arriving from the channel above and the last of it running off the low lip into the cut between two rows. His face is in profile with his attention on his own hand, and **none of the approval register is anywhere in it** — not pleased, not proud, not fond, and not stern either. The three rows, the drystone wall and the thin grass beyond are all there, and there is nobody else in the picture.

**THE FACE IS THE REFERENCE'S OWN, AND THAT IS WHERE THE PROBLEM IS.** He reads a lean, long-haired man in his middle thirties with no grey anywhere in him. **That is `images/portraits/vask.png` reproduced faithfully** — the anchor carries no grey at the temples and does not read *weathered far past his years*, so no plate anchored on it will. **This is not a prompt fault and no rewording reaches it.** `../canon-brief.md` says that where it and a sheet disagree the sheet wins and the brief is the bug; here `03` and the brief agree with each other and the picture in the library is the odd one out.

**AND THE POSE HID THE ONE CLAUSE THAT COULD HAVE FOUGHT IT.** The block does say *going grey at the temples as a diffuse scatter* — and the profile crop with long hair falling forward puts both temples behind hair. **Second time this session that a named tell was staged out of its own picture**, after the wrists went under a sleeve in `the-honey-and-the-bread`. Same shape, same session, same cause: the block named a tell and then chose a framing that covered it. Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name the winner, as a second failure mode beside the size limit.

**THE COST OF EACH WAY, so the ruling is priced.** Re-cutting `vask.png` with the temple-grey and the weathering leaves every adopted plate of him — `ch01-vask-kael-goat`, `ch03-the-back`, `ch07-the-wasters`, `ch08-the-hand-over-the-face` — showing a face the reference no longer holds. REFERENCES ARE RE-CUT, NEVER EDITED covers the mechanics, but the set stops reading as one man. Leaving it means the temple-grey is a fact the pictures never carry and this plate matches four others.

**THE TROUGH CAME BACK A FLAT PLANK.** *A hollowed half-log a little longer than his forearm* produced a board with one raised edge — water would run off its sides. **The phrase is a name wearing construction's clothes:** one adjective on a compound noun, where §Name a thing by construction wants the surface itself described — *a channel scooped out along its length, the cavity a hand deep, so water lies in it and runs to one end.* Fifth instance of the noun rule and the first where the failed phrase already looked like construction.

**THE COUNTERSTONE'S JOB IS NOT LEGIBLE.** It arrived hanging from a ring below the pivot rather than lashed to the short end, so the balance that stands the trough back up does not read. Same fix shape: say what the cord does — *the cord passes round the stone and round the trough's short end and is knotted there* — rather than naming the fixing.

**THE DEMOTION LOST, AND THERE IS A HYPOTHESIS.** *The rows, the wall and the ground behind stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no detail* did not happen: the wall is drawn stone by stone and competes with the hand. **In every picture where the two-clause mechanism has worked, the rival had somewhere to go** — deep shadow, a dark room, a cool wash. This is flat open daylight, where nothing is dark enough for a background to recede into, and the stated *high side sunlight, hard and clear* arrived flat as well. **Hypothesis, not a finding:** a demotion may need a value structure to demote into, and where there is none the instrument is the crop. One run; do not act on it until a second says the same. **SECOND RUN AGREES (s44):** `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` ran the same clause in the opposite condition — a dark wet yard with one small warm source — and the demotion held completely. Two runs, opposite conditions, one cause. Promoted to `../scene-prompt-system.md` §Name the winner.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the man.

Setting: a mountain kitchen garden on a summer afternoon, as loose
wash suggestion — a dug bed with three low rows of young bean plants
running away behind, a drystone wall and thin grass beyond it, the far
edge falling into quiet wash. High side sunlight, hard and clear, and
it is the only light.

Composition: close on the man and the machine together, the machine
filling the lower third of the frame and the man crouched over it,
cropped at mid-thigh. He is seen from the side, his face above his own
hand.

THE MACHINE, BY CONSTRUCTION: a hollowed half-log a little longer than
his forearm, hung crosswise on one iron pin driven through it nearer
one end than the other so that it swings like a balance, its long end
tipped down toward the rows. At the short end a rounded stone the size
of two fists is lashed on with cord. Behind it a split log laid open
as a channel brings a thin fall of water down into the trough from
higher ground. It is rough farm carpentry, cut and pegged by hand,
with no wheel, no frame, no crank and no metal on it but the one pin.

THE TROUGH IS STOPPED MID-POUR AND ONE FINGER IS STOPPING IT. His
right forefinger rests on the trough's high edge and holds it still,
and nothing else touches it. The water from the channel is still
running into it, and the last of the pour runs off the low lip in a
thin thread into the cut between two rows. His left hand is flat on
the ground taking his weight. Nothing is held in either hand.

HIS HAND AND THE PIN ARE THE MOST FINISHED THINGS IN THE PICTURE and
carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The rows, the wall and the
ground behind stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no
detail in them, and nothing back there competes for finish. The sunlit
top edge of the trough and the falling water are reserved as bare
unpainted paper. The ink linework stays the strongest element
throughout and does not blur into the wash.

THE PICTURE IS WARM AND THE COOL IN IT IS NAMED: the timber of the
trough and the channel, the dry ground and his skin carry the warmth;
the water, the lashed stone and the shadow under the bed are cool grey
and grey-blue with no brown in them.

HIS FACE GIVES NOTHING. His attention is entirely on the pin's seat
under his finger, mouth closed, brows level and not lowered. NOT
pleased, NOT proud, NOT approving, NOT fond, NOT amused — and equally
NOT disapproving, NOT critical, NOT stern, NOT angry and NOT tired.
Level, and giving nothing back.

Weathered far past his years, a face like used tools. Broad across the
top of the back and no further, nothing wasted below it, the crouch
settled and easy with no strain in it. Iron-dark hair going
grey at the temples as a diffuse scatter through the dark, salt
through it, with NO streak, NO seam, NO band and NO patch of white
anywhere in it. Iron-dark eyes. Village working cloth, coarse and
mended, closed at the throat, the sleeves down to the wrist — the ink
carries the cloth in dense scratchy linework at the seams and the
mended places. He wears no ornament and no metal.

THERE IS NO OTHER PERSON IN THE PICTURE: no boy, no woman, nobody at
the edge of the plot and nobody in the background.
```

---

# FILE: plates/ch11.md

# Ch. 11 — The Dish · plate sheet

**Both plates adopted, each on its first run (s44).** Kael is eleven here and Elarine is eleven; **both anchor on the fourteen-year-old references** — `images/portraits/kael-14.png` and `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` — under the s44 ruling in `../README.md`.

**SO THIS PAIR IS THE FIRST TEST OF THE AGE INSTRUCTION, and both blocks carry the rung.** `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A close-up plate and the age line says to write the eye-line rung into a plate only once a face has actually come back the wrong age, rather than pre-emptively. **The anchor ruling makes it structural rather than pre-emptive:** every anchor is now three years above its chapter, so the rung goes in by default and this pair is where that starts. Both use the ladder as a dial, which `kael-9` proved is a continuous control — *the eyes a little below the vertical midpoint, nearer that midpoint than a young child's and still below it* — and both add a landmark for the body, because `ch09/the-roller-alone` found that **a solo figure has no age reference in the frame unless one is written**.

**ANCHORING COST.** Both references are head-and-shoulders, so everything below either collar is authored here and anchored to nothing.

---

### PLATE: the-white-stitch

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 11 canon: *at the head of the court stood a table Kael had never seen: an iron dish the size of a cartwheel hub, and around it, in a half-ring, the plain things of the world — a candle, a bowl of water, a box of dry sand, a green sprig in a pot, a copper rod on a cloth.* And the beat: *The crack was small and white and absolutely clear. / It came off his fingers and went to the copper rod like a thing going home — one thin stitch of light, a hand's width, there and gone, with a sound like a knuckle against glass.* The plate is the breath after it, with his eyes still down on his own hand.

**THE STITCH IS BARE PAPER, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE REASON TO MAKE THIS PICTURE.** The folder reserves paper by name in every plate it builds; it has never had a subject that *is* the reserve. A grey morning, cold iron, and one white thing that is not painted at all — the ink drawing stopping at its edges and the paper doing the work.

**LIGHTNING IS A NOUN WITH A DEFAULT AND THE DEFAULT IS A FORKED BOLT.** §Name a thing by construction, with the order rule: the defining feature first with a measurement — one straight thread, a hand's width, unbroken end to end — and the default killed afterward, where it trims rather than replaces. `ch07.md`'s waster is the warning on the far side, so nothing here bans what the thing is made of.

**IT LIGHTS NOTHING, AND THAT IS STATED.** §What a warm source will and will not light: the run will be asked for a flat grey overcast morning and told the stitch throws nothing. A spark that size lighting a face is the kind of physically false instruction two runs were already spent on, and the block says so rather than leaving room for a glow.

**THE FACE IS SOLVED BY THE POSE, NOT BY AN OVERRIDE.** `kael-14`'s reference is the coat — *still and level, mouth closed and relaxed, gaze flat and direct*, at the viewer — and this moment is a boy looking down at his own hand with a door opening behind his face. **The s43 finding is that a reference holds no downcast version of itself to supply**, which is what took `kael-9`'s asking face off `the-honey-and-the-bread` with one clause. Same move, second use. The positive is written and both neighbourhoods are banned — the delight side and the fright side — per `ch09.md`.

**WHAT IS OUT OF FRAME.** The rest of the half-ring, the yard-master behind the table, Priscian, and the clerk with the book open. *At the book, the clerk's pen paused over the word* is the chapter's other great small beat and it is a second figure and a second face; it stays a candidate, not this picture.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch11-the-white-stitch.png`, first run.**

**THE STITCH CAME BACK EXACTLY AS SPECIFIED, WHICH NOTHING IN THIS FOLDER HAS DONE WITH A HARD NOUN BEFORE.** One straight unbroken thread of white from his fingertips to the copper bar: **no fork, no branch, no zigzag, no second strand, no scattered sparks and no halo** — the forked-bolt default killed outright, and the line reading as reserved paper rather than paint. **And it lights nothing.** The flat grey morning is the only light in the picture and no glow was added, which is the first time the folder has written *this source throws nothing* in advance instead of learning it from a failed run.

**THE REST OF THE STAGING HELD.** The copper bar on its folded cloth as the one warm object beside his skin, the iron dish seen from slightly above with its far rim showing, the table edge across the bottom, his open hand a hand's width above the bar and touching nothing, the left hand out of frame. His face and his open hand are the most finished things in the picture; the court behind is dark summary shapes with no features and no adult anywhere. **The pose solved the face for the second time** — eyes down, lids lowered, still, and nothing of `kael-14`'s flat-and-level-at-the-viewer arrived.

**AND THE AGE WORKED HERE, WHICH IS THE FINDING, BECAUSE THE FAN IS THE CONTROL.** Both blocks in this pair carry the same eye-line rung, in the same words, under the same anchor ruling and the same treatment. **He reads eleven or twelve. She read a young woman.** The one thing that differs is what sat beside the rung:

- **This block gave a ratio against an object in the frame** — *his hand is a child's, small and short-fingered, and the copper bar is longer than his forearm.*
- **`the-fan-in-the-rain` gave adjectives** — *the jaw has not come in, the cheeks still full under the bone, the neck slender.* No landmark, nothing to measure against.

**That is exactly what §Scale between figures predicts** — *adjectives lose; ratios win* — and it is the same law that fixed a four-year-old who kept arriving seven. **So the eye-line rung is not the instrument on its own; the rung plus one in-frame ratio is.** Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A close-up plate and the age line, which has been logged raised-and-unconfirmed since s42 and now has its evidence. **The confound is named honestly:** two different characters, two different references and two different crops, so this is one controlled pair and not a proof.

**SMALL DRIFTS, NONE WORTH A RE-RUN.** The stitch is nearer a hand and a half than a hand's width; the hand itself came back longer-fingered than *small and short-fingered* asked for, so the ratio moved the read without landing the clause exactly; and the dish carries water, which is the half-ring's bowl arriving inside the dish. The freckles are the medium's granulation and are not canon on him (author ruling, s34).

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the open stone court of a temple on a grey autumn morning,
as loose wash suggestion — wet-looking flagstones, a pale stone wall
behind, the far side of the court going into quiet wash. Flat
overcast daylight, no sun and no shadows with edges, and it is the
only light in the picture.

Composition: close on the boy's head, shoulders and one hand, seen
from the side, his head tipped down toward his own hand. The near
edge of a low table crosses the bottom of the frame, and on it a
shallow round iron dish about two hands across, seen from slightly
above so its far rim shows, with a plain round copper bar the length
of a man's forearm lying on a folded cloth beside it.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: his right hand is open, fingers together
and a little spread, held a hand's width above the copper bar and
touching nothing. His left hand is down at his side and out of
frame. Nothing is held in either hand.

THE STITCH OF LIGHT IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE AND IT IS DRAWN AS
BARE UNPAINTED PAPER. One single straight thread of white runs from
his fingertips to the copper bar, a hand's width long, no thicker
than a drawn line, unbroken from end to end — reserved paper with
the ink drawing stopping at its edges, not white paint. NO FORK, no
branch, no zigzag, no second strand, no scattered sparks, and no
halo or bloom of light around it. Nothing in the picture is lit by
it.

HIS ATTENTION IS ENTIRELY ON HIS OWN HAND. Eyes down, the lids
lowered, the head tipped to it. Still, mouth closed, the face not
yet caught up with what has happened — a boy recognising something
he turns out to have known for years. NOT delighted, NOT triumphant,
NOT proud, NOT pleased — and equally NOT frightened, NOT startled,
NOT alarmed, and not blank. There is nobody in the frame he could
be looking at.

HE IS ELEVEN AND THE FACE CARRIES IT: the eyes sit a little below
the vertical midpoint of the head, nearer that midpoint than a young
child's and still below it; the jaw has not come in, the cheeks are
still full under the bone, the neck narrow. His hand is a child's,
small and short-fingered, and the copper bar is longer than his
forearm.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless, the lightest
value in his face. Dark hair cut short and uneven at home. Plain
undyed working wool, coarse and much mended, the collar loose at the
throat — the ink carries the cloth in dense scratchy linework at the
seams, the patches and the mended places. No ornament and no metal.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: his skin and the
copper bar carry the only warm colour, and the iron, the flagstones,
the wall, the cloth and all the clothing are cool grey and grey-blue
with no brown in them. His face and his open hand are the most
finished things in the picture and carry the sharpest step from
light to dark. The court and the rest of the table stay at quiet
middle values with soft edges and no detail, and nothing back there
competes for finish. The ink linework stays the strongest element
throughout and does not blur into the wash.

NO OTHER FACE IS DRAWN. The other children stand behind him as dark
summary shapes at the edges of the frame, loosely washed, with no
features, and there is no adult anywhere in the picture.
```

---

### PLATE: the-fan-in-the-rain

*Candidate 3.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 11 canon: *She came to the middle with her waster in one hand and the off-hand open at her shoulder, and made the taught salute — and before its echo finished, her open palm was burning.* And the instant this draws: *Between sweeps the fire did not go out. It stood at her palm, a handspan of flame burning on nothing at all — no wick, no wood, rain falling past it — the way a lamp burns when somebody is paying for it and intends to keep paying all night. Her breathing had not changed since the salute.* The stance is the chapter's own: *weapon hand and art hand, the waster forward, the off-hand open and empty at the shoulder, for the Art.*

**IT IS SIDE-LIT, DELIBERATELY, AND THAT IS WHAT KEEPS IT OFF `ch09-aurelians-candle`.** Both are a child with fire near the face; the candle is a soft sheet of orange under the chin and the whole design problem there was a boy inside the lighting convention for villainy. **Here the flame stands at her own shoulder height, so it lights the near side of the face across, not up** — a different picture of a different child, and the difference is written as which surfaces take the light.

**THE WARM SOURCE IS NEAR, SO IT WILL LIGHT HER**, which is settled (§What a warm source will and will not light). What is written is how far it reaches: her palm, her forearm, the near side of her face and throat, and nothing else — the wall does not take it.

**FIRE STANDING ON A PALM IS THE NOUN PROBLEM AGAIN.** Every instance the renderer knows has something burning in it. The construction goes first with a measurement, then the fuel is killed by name: no wick, no candle, no lamp, no torch, no wood, no coal, nothing in the hand.

**HER CHEEK-TELL IS NOT SPENT HERE AND MUST NOT BE READ INTO THE RUN.** `../canon-brief.md` reserves *spots of colour high on her cheeks* for Ch. 13 and Ch. 17. **Warm firelight landing on a pale face is not that tell**, and the block deliberately writes no negation about cheeks: nothing in the picture pulls toward a flush, and §A negation puts the word in the prompt is the reason not to introduce one.

**THE BOUND HAIR IS DOING PRODUCTION WORK THREE CHAPTERS EARLY.** `../README.md`'s separation method has Elarine bound and Valeria loose, and Valeria arrives in Ch. 14. Every plate of Elarine that keeps the binding severe is paying that debt in advance.

**AND THIS PICTURE TESTS THE s43 HYPOTHESIS.** `ch10/the-waterer`'s demotion failed in flat open daylight, and the guess written on that sheet was that a rival needs somewhere to go. **A dark wet yard with one small warm source is the opposite condition**, so if the demotion holds here that is the second data point the hypothesis was waiting for — and if it fails here too, the hypothesis is wrong and the note says so.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch11-the-fan-in-the-rain.png`, first run (author, s44: *adopt both*).**

**THE AGE AND THE GAZE STAND AS DRAWN.** Both were put to the author as faults with a re-run costed against them, and the picture was adopted over both. Same scope as every ruling of this kind in the folder: **it is a ruling on the picture, and it moves nothing.** The findings underneath it are unaffected and are the more valuable half — the rung needs a ratio beside it, and a plate that empties its frame has to say where the subject is looking.

**RUN 1 (s44).**

**ALMOST ALL OF IT LANDED.** The flame stands upright off the flat of the palm with a clear gap of air under it and nothing burning — no wick, no candle, nothing in the hand — which is the noun problem solved on the first run. Rain crosses the whole frame and is reserved as bare paper; the wet gleam on the flagstones is reserved with it; the wooden sword is low in the right hand and crosses the bottom edge; the hair is bound tight at the nape with nothing loose; the clothing is close, buttoned high, no ornament and no metal; nobody else is in the picture. **The flame is the brightest thing and her face is the most finished**, and the wall and the flagstones stayed quiet behind them.

**AND THAT SETTLES THE s43 HYPOTHESIS IN ITS FAVOUR — SECOND DATA POINT.** `ch10/the-waterer`'s demotion failed in flat open daylight and the guess was that **a rival needs somewhere to go**. This is the opposite condition — a dark wet yard, one small warm source — and the same clause held completely. Recorded on both sheets; two runs in agreement, opposite conditions, one cause.

**THE EXPERIMENT FAILED, AND IT IS THE USEFUL RESULT.** This pair was built to test §A close-up plate and the age line by writing the eye-line rung into the block body. **The rung was written and she came back a young woman** — long jaw, defined chin, long neck, none of the fullness the block asked for. She does not read eleven and she does not read fourteen either: **the face drifted past its own anchor.**

**SO THE ANCHOR RULING COSTS MORE THAN IT LOOKED LIKE COSTING**, and it is worth saying plainly because it is the author's ruling and this is the first evidence under it: a close plate does not merely inherit the anchor's age, it drifts above it, and the rung does not pull it back. **What the block used alongside the rung were adjectives** — *the jaw has not come in, the cheeks still full, the neck slender* — and §Scale between figures is unambiguous that adjectives lose and ratios win. The rung is a ratio inside the head; everything else in that paragraph was not. **If a re-run is wanted, the age instrument is a ratio against something else in the frame** — her own hand against the flame, the grip of the sword against her fist — not more description of the face.

**AND THE GAZE WENT TO THE VIEWER, WHICH IS THE BLOCK'S FAULT.** She is looking straight out, so the picture reads as a portrait of a person rather than a plate of a moment — the exact line `../README.md` draws between the two. The block named the absence of everybody else (correctly, per §Plates) **and then gave her eyes nowhere to go.** An absence instruction has a consequence for the gaze, and where a plate empties the frame it has to say what the subject is looking at, or the subject looks at you. On the page her eyes are on Kael, three strides off and out of frame: *her eyes doing over the flames what his own were doing: pricing.*

**THE FLAME IS TALLER THAN ITS MEASUREMENT** — nearer two hand-spans than one, and tapering — and the light on her face is fainter than the block asked for, because the head is turned away from the source toward the viewer. **The second follows from the first fault**: fix the gaze and the side-lighting arrives with it.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.

Setting: a walled stone training yard in falling rain, as loose wash
suggestion — wet flagstones, a low stone wall behind her going into
cool shadow, the far corners lost in wash. Flat grey rain-light, and
the only other light in the picture is the flame below.

Composition: chest-up, the girl turned three-quarters toward the
viewer with her open left hand raised to her own shoulder height, so
that her hand and her face stand side by side in the frame at the
same height.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: her left hand is open, palm up, fingers
straight, holding nothing. Her right hand holds one plain wooden
practice sword and nothing else, carried low and crossing the bottom
edge of the frame. Nothing else is in either hand.

THE FLAME STANDS ON THE OPEN PALM AND IT IS BURNING ON NOTHING. One
body of flame stands upright off the flat of her palm, as tall as
the span from her thumb to her little finger, with a clear gap of
air between the flame and her skin. NO WICK, no candle, no lamp, no
torch, no wood and no coal — there is nothing there to burn and
nothing in her hand. It stands straight and holds its size: not
guttering, not streaming sideways, not spreading.

RAIN FALLS PAST THE FLAME AND DOES NOT TOUCH IT. Fine rain crosses
the whole frame in one direction as flicked spatter and dry-brush
drag, falling past the flame and through its light, and the flame is
unchanged by it. No steam and no smoke anywhere in the picture.

THE FLAME IS THE ONLY WARM LIGHT AND IT REACHES ONLY HER: the palm,
the inside of the raised forearm, and the near side of the face and
throat, lit from the side at her own height and not from below.
Everything else stays cold — the rain, the flagstones, the wall, the
wooden sword and all her clothing in cool grey and grey-blue with no
brown in them. The wall behind her takes none of the warm light.

HER FACE IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. Attention fully engaged and
giving nothing back, mouth closed and still, brows unmoved, the
breathing easy — a girl who has not hurried once and does not intend
to. NOT fierce, NOT angry, NOT triumphant, NOT strained, NOT
effortful — and equally NOT cold, NOT haughty, NOT bored, NOT tired.
Nothing has been decided in the face.

SHE IS ELEVEN AND SMALL FOR IT: the eyes sit a little below the
vertical midpoint of the head, nearer that midpoint than a young
child's and still below it; the jaw has not come in, the cheeks are
still full under the bone, the neck slender, the shoulders narrow.
Finely built, with nothing spare on her anywhere.

Ink-dark hair, a flat neutral black with no blue in it, drawn back
severely and bound tight at the nape, wet with the rain, nothing
loose anywhere. Pale indoor skin, and the only warm colour on it is
the flame's. Eyes very dark, near black. Plain dark close-fitting
clothing of very good quality, exactly made and buttoned high at the
throat. No ornament, no metal, no badge and no white anywhere on
her.

THE FLAME IS THE BRIGHTEST THING IN THE PICTURE AND HER FACE IS THE
MOST FINISHED. The flame is soft-edged and loose with no hard edge
and no detail in it, and the sharpest step from light to dark
happens along the lit boundary of her face. The yard, the wall and
the flagstones stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no
detail, and nothing back there competes for finish. The falling rain
and the wet gleam on the flagstones are reserved as bare unpainted
paper. The ink linework stays the strongest element throughout and
does not blur into the wash.

THERE IS NO OTHER PERSON IN THE PICTURE — no opponent, nobody
watching at the edge of the yard, and nobody in the background.
```

# FILE: plates/ch12.md

# Ch. 12 — The Telling · plate sheet

**Both plates adopted (s44); a third candidate was built, run and rejected by the author on its way there.** Kael is eleven; anchor `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Vask: `images/portraits/vask.png`.

**THE PAIR SPLITS THE CHAPTER'S TWO WEATHERS.** A crowded hall at midday with four faces in it, and a cold empty dawn with one forearm and a tree. `candidates.md`'s third — the law on the table, *"You will not enter it"* — is the chapter's largest story beat and is passed over for now on set grounds: it would be the third lamp-lit table in the book after `ch08/the-hand-over-the-face` and `ch10/the-honey-and-the-bread`.

**BOTH BLOCKS CARRY THE TWO FINDINGS THIS FOLDER LEARNED LAST RUN.** The age instrument is the eye-line rung **plus a ratio against something else in the frame** (§A close-up plate and the age line, answered s44) — here a hand against another hand, which §Size contrast must be stated per hand already prescribes. And **every visible face gets its gaze named**, because `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` emptied its frame and gave its subject's eyes nowhere to go, and the eyes went to the viewer.

**CUT BACK AFTER THE AUTHOR FLAGGED THE NEGATION HABIT (s44), before either block ran.** *This is so inefficient and redundant for image gen.* **The rule was already there and was the author's own** — §Negative inventory, s40: *ban what could plausibly arrive, not everything that could theoretically arrive*, written up after the author cut precisely these lines from every run of that session and no strays appeared. Both blocks were carrying them again.

**What went:** the absence lines in both blocks, where the positive inventory already names every figure in the frame and says what the rest are; four gaze negatives down to one; a ten-term expression list down to six; nine burning terms down to four; *no ornament, no metal, no ring, no band* on a boy already dressed in mended undyed wool; and six ways of saying nothing is glowing.

**What survived, by the channel test** (§Over-writing contradicts — *count the channel, not the sentence*, and the re-cut that lost two jobs by trimming on word-count instead): **not at the viewer**, because `kael-14`'s reference points its gaze there and `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` proved that pull is real; **NO CHAR, no scorch, no smoke**, because a hole in wood pulls hard toward burning; **not blank**, the opposite-side risk of asking for a face with nothing in it; and **no glow, no spark**, because the thing that made the hole was lightning.

**ANCHORING COST.** Both references are head-and-shoulders. The hall plate authors everything below Kael's collar; the tree plate is a forearm and a hand, so it is anchored on skin and cloth alone and its anchor is doing almost nothing.

---

### PLATE: furniture

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 11 canon for the hall's shape and Ch. 12 for the moment: *A kitchen boy brought it to the middle tables, hot from the source and proud of the carrying... "Two winters gone," the boy said, the tray still on his hip.* And the line the picture is built on: *Kael was at the bench-end, being furniture, and furniture hears everything.* Then: *Kael went on eating.* And, at the end of the same telling: *Kael's hands stayed quiet on the bread, and none of it showed.*

**THIS IS THE ONE LEGAL WAY TO DRAW THE COAT.** `../canon-brief.md` forbids designing the coat, because it is the book's metaphor for concealment and not a garment — *Literalising it flattens the image.* **A room telling a boy's own night back to him while his face stays at the room's temperature is the metaphor without the garment**, and it is the only picture in the book where the reader knows something that no face in the frame does.

**THE COMPOSITION PUTS THE STORY BEHIND HIM AND HIM IN FRONT.** He is nearest the viewer and the largest figure; the telling is further off and smaller. That is the two-clause mechanism doing the chapter's work rather than decorating it: **the teller is the animated thing and the boy is the finished thing**, and the eye lands on the one nobody in the picture is looking at.

**FOUR RESOLVED FIGURES, WHICH IS THE TESTED CEILING** (§Four figures) — the boy with the tray, two children turned round to him, and Kael. The rest of the hall is summary shapes with no features, the same solve as `ch08/the-one-who-did-not-laugh` and `ch09/the-roller-alone`.

**AND HERE THE REFERENCE'S OWN FACE IS FINALLY THE RIGHT ONE.** `kael-14`'s block is *still and level, mouth closed and relaxed, gaze flat and direct* — the coat, exactly. **The only thing that must move is the gaze**, which the reference points at the viewer and this picture points down at the bread, and a downcast gaze is the change a reference holds no version of and therefore cannot fight (s43, twice proven).

**NO ADULT IS IN THE FRAME, AND THE BLOCK DOES NOT SAY SO.** The masters are not named as present in this scene, and an authority in a picture whose whole subject is that nobody is watching him would be wrong — but the composition already names every figure in the frame and says what the rest are, which is what makes the ban unnecessary rather than merely short.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch12-furniture.png`, first run.** *Adopted on the standing pattern below rather than on a fresh ruling; one word and it comes back out.*

**THE STAGING CAME THROUGH ENTIRELY.** He is nearest, largest and turned away; the telling is behind him and smaller; four figures resolved and the rest of the hall summary. The high window is the only light and reserves as bare paper with the torn face of the bread. **Skin and bread are the only warm colour in the picture** and everything else holds cool. His face and his two hands are the most finished things in it, the three children behind are looser, and **the ink carries the cloth** so hard that the mending is legible at every seam. His eyes are down on the bread and not on the viewer, and nobody in the picture is looking at him.

**AND THE CUTS COST NOTHING, WHICH IS THE FIRST DIRECT EVIDENCE THIS SESSION.** The absence lines came out of this block before it ran — *there is no adult in the picture and no master anywhere in it* — and **no adult arrived**, no stray figure, nothing extra on the table. §Negative inventory's ruling holds exactly as the author wrote it: the positive inventory was already complete, so the ban was buying nothing.

**MISS: HE READS FOURTEEN OR FIFTEEN, AND IT COMPLICATES LAST RUN'S FINDING.** This block carried both instruments — the eye-line rung **and** a ratio — and the face still came back years above eleven. That is a third data point and it does not agree with the second, so §A close-up plate and the age line has been walked back from *answered* to part-answered.

**THE DIAGNOSIS IS SPECIFIC AND IT IS THE BLOCK'S FAULT.** The ratio written here was *his hand is plainly smaller and shorter-fingered than the standing boy's hand on the tray* — **and the picture cannot make that comparison.** The two hands are at opposite ends of the hall, one large in the foreground and one small in the far middle distance, so there is nothing for a renderer to measure against. `ch11/the-white-stitch` put the hand and the copper bar **side by side, at the same depth, inside the same crop**, and that face came back eleven. **A ratio is only an instrument where the framing can actually perform the comparison** — which is the occlusion lesson from s43 in a new coat: a fact named but staged out of reach.

**The second measurement was no better staged.** *Seated at a grown man's table the top of the boards comes to the height of his lower ribs* is a landmark the crop cuts off, since the tabletop enters the frame only in front of him.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the long hall of a temple at midday in winter, as loose wash
suggestion — a stone wall and one high window behind, long plank
tables and benches running away into quiet wash, lamp-smoke in the
upper air. Flat cold daylight from the high window, and it is the
only light in the picture.

Composition: waist-up. The boy sits at the very end of a bench in the
near foreground, closest to the viewer and the largest figure in the
frame, turned three-quarters away from the others. Behind him and
further off, at the middle tables, three other children: one standing
with a serving tray and his mouth open mid-word, and two seated
and turned right round toward him.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: the boy's two hands rest on the table in
front of him, the right one holding a torn piece of bread. The
standing child's left hand holds the tray against his hip and his
right hand is open in the air in front of him. Nothing else is held
by anybody.

WHERE EVERY FACE IS LOOKING. The
two seated children are turned right round and are looking at the boy
with the tray. He is looking at them. The boy in
the foreground is looking down at the bread in his own hands and not
at the viewer.

NOTHING IS SHOWING IN HIS FACE, AND THAT IS THE SUBJECT OF THE
IMAGE. Still, mouth closed, the eyes lowered
and easy, the brows unmoved, both hands quiet on the table. NOT
shocked, NOT grieving, NOT frightened — and equally NOT amused, NOT
curious, and not blank. He is eating.

HE IS ELEVEN AND TWO THINGS CARRY IT: the eyes sit a little below the
vertical midpoint of the head, nearer that midpoint than a young
child's and still clearly below it, the jaw not come in and the
cheeks still full under the bone. AND THE MEASUREMENT: his hand on
the table is a child's and is plainly smaller and shorter-fingered
than the standing boy's hand on the tray, and seated at a grown
man's table the top of the boards comes to the height of his lower
ribs.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless, the lightest value
in his face. Dark hair cut short and uneven at home. Plain undyed
working wool, coarse and much mended, the collar loose at the throat
— the ink carries the cloth in dense scratchy linework at the seams,
the patches and the mended places.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: skin and the bread
carry the only warm colour, and the stone, the tables, the benches,
the air and all the clothing are cool grey and grey-blue with no
brown in them. The light from the high window and the cut face of the
bread are reserved as bare unpainted paper.

HIS FACE AND HIS TWO HANDS ARE THE MOST FINISHED THINGS IN THE
PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The three
children behind him are looser and softer-edged, and the rest of the
hall is dark summary shapes at the edges with no features drawn at
all. The ink linework stays the strongest element throughout and does
not blur into the wash.
```

### PLATE: the-hole-through-the-tree

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 12 canon: *His father began. He threaded the lightning through his own hand, and put the hand through the trunk of a tree, wrist-deep, as if the wood were wool, and drew it out. The tree stood there with a hole through it.* And what the boy took from it: *Kael read the holding, not the hole.*

**THE HOLE IS BARE PAPER, THE SAME INSTRUMENT AS THE STITCH.** The pale dawn sky on the far side of the trunk is seen through it, reserved rather than painted, with the ink drawing stopping at the hole's edge. It is the second plate in two chapters where the subject *is* the reserve, and that is not a habit — it is what these two moments happen to be.

**A HOLE IN WOOD PULLS HARD TOWARD BURNING, so the negation earns its place** (§Negative inventory). Nothing on the page is scorched: *as if the wood were wool.* Char, smoke, embers, thrown splinters and broken bark are all banned by name, and the construction is stated first with a measurement so the ban trims rather than replaces (§Name a thing by construction, the order rule, and `ch07.md`'s waster as the warning on the far side).

**THE FOREARM IS BARE AND THE TALLY-MARKS ARE THEREFORE DRAWN.** `../canon-brief.md` keeps Vask's scars out of frame unless the moment needs them — but this crop is a forearm, an image cannot decline to answer, and **the marks are page canon in Ch. 3**, described exactly: many, all the same length, all the same slant. So they are written in rather than left to invention, and **the cloth is stated by where it stops on the arm, not by where the sleeve ends**, which is the fault that lost the wrists on `ch10/the-honey-and-the-bread`. It also rhymes on purpose with the adopted `ch03-the-back`: the marks a bad employer left, on the arm that has just gone through a tree.

**NO FACE, AND THE BOY IS OUT OF FRAME.** The page has Kael watching, and the viewer stands about where he stands — the same solve as `ch10/the-waterer`, used a second time deliberately, because what the page says he took from it was *the holding, not the hole*, and the holding is an arm.

**NOTHING GLOWS.** No thread, no light, no spark and no residue: the working is finished and the hand is already out. The plate is the second after, and the only evidence left in the world is a hole.

**REJECTED AS A CANDIDATE (author, s44), and the block below is kept as a record rather than a plate.** *I just think it's a bad illustration to show, should pick something else from the chapter.* **The staging fault described below is real and is not why it was rejected** — the moment itself is not worth a plate. Nothing here is owed a re-run.

**RUN 1 (s44).**

**THE HOLE IS BARE PAPER AND IT IS THE BEST RESERVE THE FOLDER HAS MADE.** Round, clean-edged, the pale ground beyond seen through it, the ink drawing stopping at its rim. **No char, no scorch, no smoke, no broken bark** — and that ban ran on four terms where the first draft had nine. The forearm is bare from the elbow, **the tally-marks are there and are right**: many, short, all the same length, all the same slant. Skin is the only warm colour in the picture; the trunk, the pines, the frost and the cloth all hold cool; the frost reserves as paper with the sky. No face, no head, no glow, no spark.

**AND THE CUTS COST NOTHING FOR THE SECOND TIME.** *There is no other person in the picture — no boy, nobody at the edge of the yard* came out before the run and **nobody arrived.** Two runs, two blocks, both trimmed, no strays. §Negative inventory stands exactly as the author wrote it at s40.

**THE MISS IS THE ONE THING THE PICTURE EXISTS TO SAY: the hand does not read as having made the hole.** The arm comes out from behind the trunk at the elbow and hangs down its left side; the hole sits lower and to the right, on its own. **A man standing behind a tree, and a tree with a hole in it** — two facts in one frame with nothing joining them. The causality is the plate.

**AND IT IS THE BLOCK'S FAULT, IN THE FAMILY THIS SESSION KEEPS FINDING.** The relationship was named — *his right hand has just come out of the hole and hangs open beside it, a hand's width clear of the trunk* — and **the framing was never told how to stage it.** Which side of the trunk the man stands on, and where the hole sits relative to the hand, are both unstated, so the renderer put the body behind the trunk and the hole wherever there was bark. Same shape as the wrists under a sleeve, the temple-grey behind hair, and a ratio measured across a hall: **the fact is in the block and out of the picture.** The word *hangs* did the rest of the damage — it invited an arm dropped straight down, where the page's gesture is a hand drawn back out of something.

**THE FIX IS ONE PARAGRAPH AND IT IS STAGING, NOT EMPHASIS.** Put the man on the near side of the trunk so the arm crosses in front of the bark rather than emerging from behind it; site the hole **at the hand's own height, directly inboard of it**, so the two sit on one horizontal line a hand's width apart; and describe the arm as drawn straight back out — forearm level, fingers pointing away from the trunk — rather than hanging.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the man.

Setting: a mountain yard at first light on a winter morning, as loose
wash suggestion — frost on the ground, a stand of pines going into
cool wash behind, and the sky beyond them bare unpainted paper. Cold
flat half-light before sunrise, no sun and no shadows with edges, and
it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: close on one pine trunk and a man's right arm, seen from
the side. The trunk stands upright through the middle of the frame,
about as thick as a man's thigh. The picture is cropped at his
shoulder and at his waist, so no head and no face is in the frame.

THE HOLE IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE AND IT IS DRAWN AS BARE
UNPAINTED PAPER. One clean round hole the width of his wrist goes
straight through the trunk, and the pale dawn sky on the far side is
seen through it as reserved paper, with the ink drawing stopping at
the hole's edge. Its edges are clean and sharp, as if cut. NO CHAR, no
scorch, no smoke and no broken bark.

WHAT THE HAND IS DOING: his right hand has just come out of the hole
and hangs open beside it, a hand's width clear of the trunk, the
fingers relaxed and half-curled as if around something set down, and
holding nothing. His left hand is out of frame.

No glow, no spark and no thread of light anywhere: the working is
over.

THE ARM IS BARE FROM THE ELBOW DOWN — the sleeve is pushed back above
the elbow and stays there, so the whole forearm, the wrist and the
back of the hand are uncovered. Across the outside of that forearm
run many short old white scars, all of them the same length and all
of them at the same slant, healed long ago and flat to the skin. The
forearm and the hand are heavy and worked, the tendons standing, the
knuckles broad.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: his skin is the only
warm colour anywhere. The trunk, the frost, the pines, the ground and
his clothing are cool grey and grey-blue with no brown in them. The
frost on the ground is reserved as bare unpainted paper with the sky.

HIS HAND AND THE EDGE OF THE HOLE ARE THE MOST FINISHED THINGS IN THE
PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The pines and
the ground behind stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no
detail, and nothing back there competes for finish. The ink linework
stays the strongest element throughout and does not blur into the
wash.

His garment, by construction: coarse undyed working cloth, much
mended — the ink carries the cloth in dense scratchy linework at the
seams and the mended places.
```

---

### PLATE: the-law-on-the-table

*Candidate 3, taken after the author rejected candidate 2.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/vask.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`. Ch. 12 canon: *His parents looked at each other, one look, level. / "You will not enter it," his father said.* Then, after no reason comes and the oldest register in the house is set between them: *Kael looked at the law lying on the table, and did not pick it up. / "Then I have other questions," he said. His voice stayed level, because they had built it to stay level, and his hands went flat on the table, because hers went flat when a thing mattered.* And the room it happens in: *The fire ate what it was given. Nobody moved.*

**THE PICTURE IS THE ECHO, NOT THE REFUSAL.** The drawable instant is the one after the No, when the boy sets his hands down — **and puts them down in his mother's gesture, without knowing he is doing it.** That is the chapter's whole argument in one frame: he is being refused by people who built him, using a register they built into him, and he answers in their own language.

**AND THE ECHO IS STAGED SO THE FRAME CAN PERFORM IT**, which is the lesson `furniture` just cost. Her flat hands and his flat hands are **directly opposite each other on the same boards, a hand's width apart, at the same depth and the same size in frame.** A hand half the width of the hand facing it is a comparison a renderer can make; the same fact written across a room is not (§A close-up plate and the age line). **So the echo carries the age as well as the beat** — one staging doing both jobs.

**IT MUST NOT READ AS `ch08-the-hand-over-the-face` RESTAGED.** That plate is the same two parents at the same lamp, and the difference is written into the composition rather than hoped for: this one is **table-led and hand-led**, cropped low with four hands across the boards, three faces open and level and nothing hidden by anybody — where the supper plate is a hand covering a face and a woman looking only at the bread.

**THE FACES ARE NOT DEMOTED, DELIBERATELY.** The instinct was to name the hands as the winner and hold the faces back, and §Name the winner records two runs where a face beat a small tell anyway because faces are what a renderer finishes. **So the hands are staged rather than prioritised** — put where they cannot be missed, and the faces are allowed to be finished too.

**NERIS'S HANDS ARE IN FRAME AND DOING HER ONE THING.** `../canon-brief.md`: her hands are where the book keeps her, and any scene must have them in frame and occupied. The gesture is established on the page two chapters earlier — Ch. 7: *His mother's hands were flat on the table, the way they had been the day of the knot* — which is what makes the boy's copy of it in Ch. 12 a plant paying out rather than a coincidence.

**ONE SOURCE.** The page has a fire as well as the lamp; the block names only the lamp, because two warm sources in one small room is two lighting problems and the one on the table is the one that reaches the hands.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch12-the-law-on-the-table.png`, first run.**

**THE ECHO IS THERE AND IT IS UNMISTAKABLE.** Four hands flat on the boards, hers and his in the same gesture directly opposite each other across the same wood, a hand's width apart. **That is the thing the plate was built for and it is the thing you see first.** Staging it rather than prioritising it was the right call — the faces are fully finished and the hands still win the picture, which is what §Name the winner's two failures said emphasis could not do.

**AND EVERY GAZE LANDED.** The boy on the man, the man back on the boy, the woman on the boy, nobody on the viewer. Second block running where naming each face's direction worked, after `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` proved what happens when it is left out. Three closed mouths, nobody moving, the lamp the only source with its flame reserved bright and the light lying along the boards.

**MISS: THE PICTURE IS UNIFORMLY WARM, AND THE BLOCK ASKED FOR SOMETHING PHYSICALLY FALSE.** It named the lamp as the only light and then asked for *all the clothing* to stay cool grey and grey-blue — **on three people sitting inside the lamp's throw.** A single warm source lighting a small room makes everything it reaches warm, and the renderer resolved the contradiction correctly, exactly as §What a warm source will and will not light says it will. **The cool has to be sited on what the source does not reach** — the far wall, the corners, the end of the room — and never on cloth in the light.

**KEPT AS EVIDENCE, NOT PROMOTED.** `ch08.md` and `ch09.md` both cite a §Name both temperatures that no longer exists: it was one of the two colour rules the author removed at s42, *no need to be a rule — case by case we do adjust.* This observation is about a self-contradictory instruction rather than about colour policy, and it stays on this sheet.

**THE HAND RATIO DID NOT LAND AS A MEASUREMENT.** *His flat hand is half the width and half the length of the woman's flat hand directly across from it* — and his hands came back nearly the size of hers. **The staging carried the beat; the proportion clause did not carry the number.** He reads twelve or thirteen, which is nearer eleven than `furniture` got, so the stageable comparison helped and did not finish the job.

**AND NERIS READS EARLY THIRTIES AND STRIKING** where `../canon-brief.md` wants *handsome and unremarkable in the same breath.* **Checked before calling it drift: that is `images/portraits/neris.png` reproduced faithfully** — the same face, the same long loose dark hair. Same shape as the Vask reference question at s43, and it is the author's to rule on or leave; nothing here treats it as a fault.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: vask.png — portrait reference of the man.
Image 3: neris.png — portrait reference of the woman.

Setting: the single room of a mountain house at night, as loose wash
suggestion — a plank wall behind, the corners going into deep cool
shadow. One small oil lamp stands on the table and is the only light
in the picture.

Composition: waist-up, three people at a plank table, the tabletop
crossing the lower third of the frame. The boy sits at the near left,
seen from the side so his face is in three-quarter view. The man and
the woman sit opposite him, facing him and the viewer. The lamp
stands at the far end of the table.

FOUR HANDS ON THE BOARDS, AND THEY ARE THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. The
woman's two hands lie flat on the table, palms down, fingers
together. Directly opposite them, a hand's width away across the same
boards, the boy's two hands lie flat in exactly the same way, palms
down, fingers together. The man's two hands rest together on the table beyond them and are
still. Nothing is held by anybody and there is nothing on the table
but the lamp.

WHERE EVERY FACE IS LOOKING: the boy is looking at the man, the man
is looking back at the boy, and the woman is looking at the boy. None
of them is looking at the viewer. Three closed mouths, and nobody is
moving.

THE BOY has just been refused something and has not accepted it: the
face level, the eyes steady on the man, the jaw set but easy. NOT
angry, NOT pleading, NOT tearful, and not blank. Eyes near-white
neutral grey, cool and colourless, the lightest value in his face.
Dark hair cut short and uneven.

HE IS ELEVEN, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS THE HANDS: the eyes sit a little
below the vertical midpoint of the head, nearer that midpoint than a
young child's and still below it, the jaw not come in and the cheeks
still full under the bone — and his flat hand on the boards is half
the width and half the length of the woman's flat hand directly
across from it.

THE MAN gives nothing: level, unhurried, the brows unmoved, the eyes
empty — not angry, not cold, not kind, not sad. Iron-dark hair with
grey scattered through it at the temples, salt through the dark, no
streak and no band of white anywhere. A face weathered far past its
years, broad across the top of the back and no further.

THE WOMAN is completely still, and the stillness is the whole of her:
fine bones under worked hands, weather on her but no coarseness, the
face handsome and unremarkable at once. Not warm, not maternal, not
soft, and not stern. Dark hair, dark eyes.

All three wear coarse undyed village cloth, much mended and closed at
the throat — the ink carries the cloth at the seams and the mended
places on all three.

THE LAMP IS THE ONLY WARM THING AND ITS LIGHT FALLS FIRST ON THE
HANDS: the boards and the four hands take the warm light, the faces
take less of it, and the walls, the corners and all the clothing stay
cool grey and grey-blue. The lamp's flame and the lit edge of the
tabletop are reserved as bare unpainted paper.

THE FOUR HANDS AND THE THREE FACES ARE THE FINISHED THINGS IN THIS
PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark; the room
behind them stays at quiet middle values with soft edges and no
detail. The ink linework stays the strongest element throughout and
does not blur into the wash.

Preserve all three exactly as shown in their reference images —
faces, hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features.
```

---

# FILE: plates/ch13.md

# Ch. 13 — The Coat · plate sheet

**Composed at s45, and both adopted on their first run.** Kael is eleven turning twelve across this chapter (`07/timeline`: no pin, the birthday passes unmarked) and the burns are in its opening spring; Elarine is twelve at the autumn hall. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` and `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`.

**THE PAIR SPLITS THE CHAPTER'S TWO PRICES.** What the dawn school costs, written on a body and read off the face that keeps paying it; and what the hall costs, written on the face of somebody who did not choose to pay it. **Both are chest-up closes on one face** (`../README.md`, s45). The chapter is titled for a garment nobody may draw (`../canon-brief.md`), so both plates take the price instead of the thing.

**ANCHORING COST.** Both references are head-and-shoulders and both plates are chest-up closes, so both anchors are doing the whole of their work and neither picture authors much below a collar. **This is the first Ch. 13 pair to be able to say that** — the receipts was composed as a hand and a forearm, anchored on skin alone, and the author sent it back (`../README.md`, s45).

**BOTH BLOCKS CARRY THE CORRECTED AGE INSTRUMENT** (§A close-up plate and the age line, as walked back at s44): the rung **plus a ratio the framing can actually perform** — both terms in the crop, at the same depth, near enough to read against each other. Here that is his own hand held up below his own chin, and her hand on the boards beside a cup. Both are the white stitch's geometry, which is the one that worked, and neither is furniture's hand-across-a-hall, which is the one that did not.

**AND THE RECEIPTS STATES NO AGE NUMBER, WHICH THE OTHER BLOCK DOES.** §Damage records that stated ages plus injury cause refusals, and the receipts has marks on a child in it. The rung and the ratio both work without a number; the number is the one part of the instrument that is optional.

**CANDIDATE 3 IS DEFERRED, AND THE REASON IS A MISSING FILE.** Oss's face after the laugh crests is the chapter's most human beat and there is no `images/portraits/oss.png`. A plate would have to author his face out of `03/oss` — and `REFERENCES SHOW THE PERSON. PLATES SHOW THE MOMENT.` says a plate is not where a person's face gets decided; whatever it drew would become his reference by default. **The work that unblocks it is a portrait, not a plate**, and it is named here so the next session does not re-propose the plate first. He is in the second plate as a loose dark shape at the far end of a hall with no features drawn, which is the most he can legally be until that portrait exists.

**AND THE SAME BLOCK FALLS ON THE JUDGE.** `candidates.md`'s alternate — Halden at the trough, talking to the yard and not to Kael — is worth planting and has no reference either. **Aurelian's apology is the one alternate that is fully anchored** (`aurelian-14`, `elarine-14`, `kael-14`, and `ch09/aurelians-candle` proves the face): it is passed over here because it is a wide three-figure staging whose subject is a measured distance, and the folder's standing preference is the close crop on a face doing something. It stays a candidate.
