### PLATE: the-receipts

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 13 canon, the first burst: *a white bite up the forearm that dropped the waster out of his hand and dropped him after it, and he sat in the frost with the arm ringing like struck iron.* And the season's tally, which is the picture: *He was burned three hundred and twenty times that spring. He counted, because counting was his. Little white-pink speckles came up on the wrist and the back of the hand, the Art writing its own receipts.*

**THE PICTURE IS ONE OF THREE HUNDRED AND TWENTY MORNINGS, NOT THE FIRST ONE.** That is what makes it legal: on the first morning there were no speckles yet, and by the middle of that spring a dropped waster and a marked wrist are the same morning. Both halves are Ch. 13's own page and nothing is composed across chapters.

**RECOMPOSED AT s45, BEFORE IT RAN.** The first version of this block was a hand and a forearm cropped at the elbow, and the author ruled on the set rather than on the picture: *we do lots of close ups of hands... chest up shots, creative expressions, i like better* — and specified the replacement, *same scene of the receipts, but we can actually see more. Hurt but focused expression.* **The moment is unchanged. The crop widened until the face was in it**, and the hand-and-forearm version is gone rather than kept as a variant, because it was not rejected for being wrong.

**THE TELL IS STACKED UNDER THE FACE, WHICH IS THE WHOLE COMPOSITION.** He holds the burned forearm up hard against his own chest with the other hand closed around it below the elbow — so the marked wrist sits **directly below his chin, at the same depth, at the size of a hand in frame.** That answers both halves of §Name the winner at once: its limit says a tell smaller than a hand in frame cannot carry finish however it is prioritised, and its s44 correction says to stage the small thing rather than shout at it, as `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` did with four hands on a table. **Face and marks are named as the two most finished things and they are touching.**

**AND THE POSE IS WHY THE AGE INSTRUMENT CAN BE THE FACE ITSELF.** With the hand held under the chin, the ratio is his hand against his own head — **a child's hand is plainly shorter than his own face, and a grown hand is about the same length as it.** Both terms in the crop, same depth, same size, impossible to stage out of reach. **This is new and untested**, and it is written down as an instrument a chest-up can always perform, not as a rule: the folder has one run's evidence for the copper bar and none at all for this.

**HIS EYES GO UP AND OUT OF FRAME, AND THAT IS THE ONE REAL COST.** `ch10/the-honey-and-the-bread`, `ch11/the-white-stitch` and `ch12/furniture` all solved Kael's face by putting his eyes down on an object, and it worked all three times because **a reference holds no downcast version of itself to supply** (s43). **A fourth would be a habit, and this face has somewhere better to look:** up and to his left, past the corner of the frame, at the man standing over him who does not help him up. The page puts his father there and speaking. **So the gaze is named — the fan proved an emptied frame sends the eyes to the viewer — and the man is banned by name in the same block, because naming him in the gaze line is exactly the pull §Negative inventory says a negation has to have.**

**THE EXPRESSION IS THE CHAPTER'S, ONE STEP BACK FROM ITS STRONGEST WORDING.** *his eyes streaming* belongs to the first burst; this is a morning in the middle of that spring, so the block asks for eyes open and wet with the attention already back on, and not for a face in the act of crying. **The negations earn their place on the pull test:** a child, cold, and wet eyes is a hard pull toward weeping, and the picture is a boy who has stopped noticing that part.

**THE FRESH MARK IS ONE CLAUSE AND NO TISSUE.** §Damage: name the mark and where it is, do not describe what it is made of. One new mark at the wrist, red where the others are pale, the same size as the others — which is the page's own cruelty (*The burn was small. It was the size of the thread*) and the only red in the picture.

**THREE SMALL THINGS THE FIRST VERSION GOT RIGHT AND THIS ONE KEEPS.** The **right** hand, because `../canon-brief.md` puts the Ch. 6 blade scar on the left forearm and an image cannot decline to answer — a left-arm crop would have to draw a second family of marks beside the receipts and split the subject, and the page does not say which hand held the waster, so the framing answers. The **arm is bare by where the cloth stops**, not by where a sleeve ends, which is the fault that lost the wrists on `ch10/the-honey-and-the-bread` and is the one clause here that could put the subject under a cuff. And **the reserve is not the subject**: `ch11/the-white-stitch` and `ch12/the-hole-through-the-tree` both centred bare paper and a third would be a habit, so here the frost and his breath reserve while the marks are drawn as ink rings — the two whites in the picture are made by different means and cannot compete.

**THE TWO-FIGURE VERSION STAYS AVAILABLE AND IS NOT THIS BLOCK.** Drawing the father — boots, or the whole standing man — turns a plate about what the school costs into a plate about the pair of them, and it puts a downed child and a grown man standing over him in one frame, which is near enough to §Some moments are undrawable's one refusal that it is not what a first run should be spent on. **The gaze buys most of him for nothing.**

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch13-the-receipts.png`, first run (author, s45: *love it*).**

**THE STACK IS THE FINDING AND IT LANDED WHOLE.** The face and the marked wrist are in one column, the wrist directly under the chin, both at the size the block asked for, and **they are the two most finished things in the picture** — the marks are the second thing the eye goes to and nothing had to be shouted at. §Name the winner's limit says a tell smaller than a hand in frame cannot carry finish however it is prioritised, and its own answer is *the fix is the crop.* **This is that answer run as a composition rather than quoted as a caution.** The pose chain held exactly: forearm up against the chest, the left hand closed around it below the elbow, the fingers half-curled and slack, nothing held.

**AND THE UP-GAZE WORKED, WHICH GENERALISES THE s43 FINDING.** He is looking up and out past the top corner at somebody who is not in the picture, and **`kael-14`'s flat-and-level-at-the-viewer gaze did not arrive.** The rule was written as *a reference holds no downcast version of itself to supply* off three runs that all moved the eyes DOWN. This one moved them up and away and the reference lost the same way. **So the mechanism is not downcast — it is any head position the reference does not hold**, and the folder now has a fourth solve for Kael's face that is not the same solve as the other three.

**THE DEMOTION HELD IN FLAT LIGHT, AND THAT REOPENS THE s44 HYPOTHESIS IN A USEFUL DIRECTION.** `ch10/the-waterer` failed to demote a drystone wall in flat open daylight and the conclusion was **a rival needs somewhere to go**; `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` confirmed it in a dark yard. **This picture is flat, sourceless, pre-dawn light with no dark anywhere in it and the trees, the fence and the far ground all went soft and quiet.** The difference is the mist: depth haze gave the background somewhere to recede into without a single dark value. **A rival needs somewhere to go and it does not have to be darkness.** One run, directional, and it does not disturb either earlier reading.

**MISS: HE READS FOURTEEN OR FIFTEEN, AND THE NEW INSTRUMENT IS THE ONE THAT FAILED.** The hand-against-face ratio was written into the block, was perfectly stageable, and **came back at the adult proportion the block wrote against** — his hand reads about as long as his own face. So the staging fix from s44 was satisfied and the number still did not land.

**AND THE PATTERN ACROSS FOUR RUNS NOW POINTS SOMEWHERE SPECIFIC.** The one ratio that ever worked — `ch11/the-white-stitch`, eleven or twelve — measured a hand against **a copper bar**. Every failure since has measured a piece of the subject against another piece of the subject: a hand against a hand across a hall, a flat hand against his mother's flat hand, and now a hand against his own face. **An object has a size the renderer cannot argue with; a body can be scaled entire and satisfy the ratio at any age.** Written up as an open question at `../scene-prompt-system.md` §A close-up plate and the age line, and deliberately not promoted to a rule: it is one clean instance and three suggestive ones.

**ADOPTED WITH THE AGE AS DRAWN, WHICH IS THE SIXTH TIME.** `../README.md` carries the standing bias — one consistent face across the book, over accuracy in any single picture — and this run joins the candle, the roller, `vask.png`, the fan and `neris.png`.

**MISS, AND IT IS THIS FOLDER'S ERROR RATHER THAN THE RENDERER'S: THE MARKS RUN TOO FAR UP THE ARM.** `03/kael` sites them **wrist to second knuckle**. The block asked for *from the knuckles to a hand's width up the forearm*, which already exceeded that, and the run took them most of the way to the elbow. **The renderer drew what it was told.** `../canon-brief.md` did not carry the receipts at all, which is exactly the blind spot that file exists to close, and it has been repaired from `03` in the same session. The picture stands as adopted; nothing here asks for a re-run.

**AND THEY CAME BACK RAISED WHERE THE BLOCK SAID FLAT.** *Flat to the skin and healed* lost to *drawn with the ink as a small ring* — a ring reads as a rim and a rim reads as a dome, so they sit up off the skin and catch light. **If this is ever re-run, the relief has to be named and not only the colour**: no shadow under any of them, no rim, drawn as flat dots of colour. Same shape as every noun failure in §Name a thing by construction — the more concrete instruction won.

**SMALLER THINGS, NONE WORTH A RE-RUN.** The breath in the cold air did not arrive. The clothing came back warm brown-grey where the block named cool grey-blue with no brown — **the same drift as `ch08/the-one-who-did-not-laugh`, which the author kept and which is the reason colour is not a rule in this folder.** The wet eyes did not arrive either: the *hurt* half of hurt-but-focused is carried by the arm, the shoulders and the held breath rather than by the eyes, and the face reads level and watchful. The speckling on the face is the medium's granulation, texture and not canon (author ruling, s34).

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the flat ground of a mountain smallholding before sunrise on
a cold spring morning, as loose wash suggestion — frozen ground under
frost, the yard falling away into cool wash behind him, the far side
of it lost. Cold flat half-light before sunrise, no sun and no shadow
with an edge anywhere, and it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: chest-up and close. The boy is down on the frozen
ground, sitting back on one hip, filling the frame, his shoulders
squared and his head up. A plain wooden practice sword lies on the
frost across the bottom corner of the frame where it fell, held by
nobody.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: his right forearm is held up hard against
his own chest, the back of the hand and the wrist turned out toward
the viewer, the fingers half-curled and slack. His left hand is
closed around that forearm just below the elbow, holding it in.
Nothing is held in either hand. Both hands are a child's, small and
short in the fingers.

THE MARKS ARE THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE AND THEY SIT DIRECTLY BELOW
HIS FACE. Across the back of the right hand and up the wrist runs a
scatter of small round marks, pale pink and white, flat to the skin
and healed — thirty or more of them, none larger than a grain of
barley, spread from the knuckles to a hand's width up the forearm.
Each one is drawn with the ink as a small ring, not left to the grain
of the paper. One more of them, at the wrist, is new: red where the
others are pale, and no bigger than they are.

HIS FACE IS HURT AND STILL WORKING. The jaw set hard, the mouth
closed, the breath held, the eyes open and wet with the lower lashes
stuck together — and the attention already back on, level, pricing
what it has just cost him. NOT weeping, NOT sobbing, NOT crying out,
NOT frightened, and not blank.

WHERE HE IS LOOKING: up and to his left, past the top corner of the
frame, at a man who is standing over him and is not in the picture.
Not at the viewer.

THE MEASUREMENT IS THE HAND AGAINST THE FACE: his right hand, held up
below his own chin, is plainly shorter from the heel of the palm to
the fingertips than his face is from the chin to the hairline. The
eyes sit a little below the vertical midpoint of the head, nearer
that midpoint than a young child's and still below it, the jaw not
come in.

Eyes near-white neutral grey, cool and colourless, the lightest value
in his face. Dark hair cut short and uneven at home.

The cloth stops above the elbow: no cuff, no sleeve edge and no cloth
of any kind lies on the hand, the wrist or the forearm anywhere in
the frame. Plain undyed working wool everywhere else, coarse and much
mended, the collar loose at the throat — the ink carries the cloth in
dense scratchy linework at the seams, the patches and the mended
places.

His breath stands in the cold air in front of his mouth. The frost is
broken and smeared under him where he went down, and stands unbroken
everywhere else.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: his skin is the only
warm colour anywhere, the marks on it are pinker than the skin around
them, and the new one is the only red in the picture. The frost, the
ground, the wooden sword, the yard and all his clothing are cool grey
and grey-blue with no brown in them.

HIS FACE AND THE BACK OF HIS RIGHT HAND ARE THE TWO MOST FINISHED
THINGS IN THE PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark.
The frost is the brightest thing in it and is reserved as bare
unpainted paper along with his breath. The wooden sword, the ground
and the yard behind stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and
no detail, and nothing back there competes for finish. The ink
linework stays the strongest element throughout and does not blur
into the wash.

There is no other person in the picture: no man standing over him,
and nobody at the edge of the yard.
```

### PLATE: the-spots-of-colour

*Candidate 2.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 13 canon: *It was a crop of laughter, many throats, one animal* — and, at the far side of it: *At the far table Elarine sat straight as a mast with spots of color high on her cheeks, and the laughter was running over her too — Sedrane and the goat boy.* The teller is on the page in the same paragraph: *up on the bench-end with his hands drawing, the middle tables turned to him.*

**THIS IS ONE OF TWO CHAPTERS WHERE THE TELL IS LEGAL AND IT IS ALREADY ASSIGNED HERE.** `../canon-brief.md` reserves *spots of colour high on her cheeks* for Ch. 13 and Ch. 17 and forbids it in the canonical portrait; `candidates.md` spends it at Ch. 13 rather than Ch. 17 on the ground that here she is being spent in public **without having chosen it**, and records the non-choice so it is not re-proposed at Ch. 17. `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` deliberately wrote no cheek negation at all so that nothing would be read into that run. **This is the payout.**

**IT RESTAGES `ch12-furniture` ON PURPOSE, AND THAT IS THE ARGUMENT FOR IT.** Same hall, same midday, same laughter, one figure near and large with the room falling away behind — **and the opposite face.** Two chapters ago the boy nobody was looking at kept his face at the room's temperature; here the girl everybody is laughing at keeps everything still except the one thing she cannot. Set against each other they are a pair; the difference is written into the lighting as well as the face — furniture takes flat light from a high window at its back, this one takes it across from the side, which also gives the demotion somewhere to sit (§Name the winner, s44).

**THE COLOUR IS THE WINNER AND IT WINS ON HUE, NOT ON VALUE.** Same shape as Kael's grey, which cannot win on saturation and takes the lightest value instead. Here the two patches are the only warm colour in the entire picture and her face is the most finished thing in it — **staged rather than merely prioritised**, since §Name the winner has two runs where a small tell lost anyway.

**HER GAZE IS NAMED BECAUSE THE FAN'S WAS NOT.** `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` emptied its frame, gave its subject's eyes nowhere to go, and the eyes went to the viewer and turned a plate into a portrait. The page does not say where she is looking, so the plate answers with framing: straight ahead, level, down the length of the table at nothing, which is a girl declining to turn round.

**AND HER EYE COLOUR IS NOT IN THE BLOCK.** `../canon-brief.md`: the page has never said what colour her eyes are and must not; *a portrait may show them; a plate may not name them.* The reference carries them anyway (§What the reference carries). Noted, not re-raised: `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain`'s block does name them and that plate is adopted and stands.

**THE TELLER IS IN THE FRAME AND HAS NO FACE.** One child standing up on a bench at the far end, small, a loose dark shape with no features — which is the whole of what a plate may draw of Oss until `images/portraits/oss.png` exists.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch13-the-spots-of-colour.png`, first run (author, s45: *looks good*).**

**THE TELL LANDED AND IT WON THE PICTURE ON HUE, EXACTLY AS STAGED.** Two patches of warm pink high on the cheekbone, soft-edged, sized as written — **and the only warm colour anywhere in the frame.** Her skin stayed pale and cool, the boards, the cup, the stone and the benches all held cold, so the one warm thing in the picture is the one thing in her that moved. **This is the second proof that a winner can be named on a channel other than value** (`../canon-brief.md`'s grey wins on value; this wins on hue), and neither needed emphasis once the rest of the palette was denied the colour.

**AND THE GAZE HELD FOR THE THIRD BLOCK RUNNING.** Level, off to her left, at nothing, not at the viewer. `ch11/the-fan-in-the-rain` is the only Elarine plate whose eyes went to the camera and it is the one that did not name them.

**THE COSTUME IS THE BEST THE FOLDER HAS PRODUCED AND IT MAY BE ONE CENTURY TOO SPECIFIC.** Bound tight at the nape with nothing loose, plain dark, close, exactly made, no ornament, and unmistakably not Valeria. **But *buttoned high at the throat* is a fastening convention, and a fastening convention carries a century the way a garment name does** — it came back as a placket of small buttons on a high-collared bodice, which is the `ch04/the-master-arrives` failure arriving through a smaller door. **Recorded here and deliberately not promoted to §Name a thing by construction:** that section's own bar is *the correction has been wanted every time*, and this correction was not wanted — the author adopted the picture as drawn.

**MISS: THE LAUGHTER DID NOT ARRIVE, AND ONE CLAUSE CANCELLED ANOTHER.** The block asked for the two children behind her *turned away up the hall, seen from behind, their heads back and their mouths open* — and **seen from behind removed the only part of that a viewer can read.** They came back seated and quiet, so the hall is calm where the page has *a crop of laughter, many throats, one animal.* **The fix, if it is ever wanted, is side-on rather than behind:** one head thrown back in profile against the window wall, where a mouth is actually in frame. Same family as the wrists under the sleeve — the instruction was in the block and staged out of the picture.

**WHAT SAVED IT IS THE FIGURE THE BLOCK SPENT LEAST ON.** The child standing up on the bench at the far end with both arms out came back exactly as written, a loose dark shape with no features — **so the joke is legibly being told and she is legibly not in it.** That is also the whole of what a plate may draw of Oss until `images/portraits/oss.png` exists, and it turns out to be enough to carry a chapter beat.

**MISS: SHE READS MID-TEENS, AND THIS TIME THERE IS A CAUSE ON DISK RATHER THAN A THEORY.** `../portrait-prompt-system.md`'s Elarine AGE block — the block that cut `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` — reads *Fifteen, and small for it*, and sets the eyes **at** the vertical midpoint rather than below it. **The file is named `-14`, the block that made it says fifteen, and `03` has her at ~13 in Book One.** Both of her plates have now drifted the same way. Noted at that block; nothing has been renamed or re-cut, because a reference is never edited and every citation in the folder points at that filename.

**AND THE RUN BRUISES THE HYPOTHESIS THIS SESSION LOGGED ONE PICTURE EARLIER.** The receipts proposed that an age ratio only works against **an object** the renderer cannot resize. **This block gave exactly that** — her hand against the cup, both in the crop, same depth — and the face still came back years above the chapter. Six runs now, and **the only one that landed at its chapter's age is `ch11/the-white-stitch`, which is also the only one where the face was not the dominant mass in the frame.** Written up at `../scene-prompt-system.md` as the surviving correlation, with the confound named: this subject's anchor was cut at fifteen.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.

Setting: the long hall of a temple at midday in autumn, as loose wash
suggestion — high windows down one wall, long plank tables and
benches running away behind her into quiet wash, the far end of it
going dark. Flat cold daylight coming across the hall from the
windows on her left, and it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: chest-up and close. One girl sits at a plank table in
the near foreground, nearest the viewer and much the largest figure
in the frame, seen in three-quarter view, bolt upright, her shoulders
square and level. The near edge of the tabletop crosses the bottom of
the frame and one plain wooden cup stands on it beside her hand.
Behind her and much further off, at the middle tables, two children
turned away up the hall, seen from behind, their heads back and their
mouths open; and beyond them, small and at the far end of the hall,
one child standing up on a bench with both arms out.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: her right hand rests on the boards beside
the cup, loosely closed and still. Her left hand is in her lap and
out of frame. Nothing is held in either hand.

WHERE EVERY FACE IS LOOKING: hers is the only face in the picture,
and she is looking straight ahead, level, down the length of the
table at nothing, and not at the viewer.

THE COLOUR ON HER CHEEKS IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. High on each
cheekbone, under the eye, one patch of warm pink with a soft edge,
about the size of her own thumbprint. The rest of her face and her
throat stay pale and cool with no other colour in them.

HER FACE GIVES NOTHING BACK. Completely still, mouth closed and
level, the brows unmoved, the eyes steady, the shoulders not moved at
all. NOT embarrassed, NOT ashamed, NOT angry — and equally NOT cold,
NOT haughty, NOT bored.

SHE IS TWELVE AND SMALL FOR IT, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS THE CUP: the
eyes sit a little below the vertical midpoint of the head, nearer
that midpoint than a young child's and still below it. Her hand on
the boards is a child's, and the wooden cup standing beside it is as
tall as her hand is long.

Ink-dark hair, a flat neutral black with no blue in it, drawn back
severely and bound tight at the nape, nothing loose anywhere. Pale
indoor skin. Plain dark close-fitting clothing of very good quality,
exactly made and buttoned high at the throat. No ornament, no metal
and no badge anywhere on her.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: the two patches of
colour on her cheekbones are the only warm colour in the whole
picture. Her skin is pale and cool, and the boards, the cup, the
stone, the benches, the air and all the clothing in the hall are cool
grey and grey-blue with no brown in them.

HER FACE IS THE MOST FINISHED THING IN THE PICTURE and carries the
sharpest step from light to dark, along the lit edge of it where the
window light comes across. The two children behind her are looser and
softer-edged with no detail drawn in them, the standing child beyond
them is a loose dark shape with no features at all, and the
rest of the hall is summary shapes at the edges with nothing drawn in
them. The daylight from the high windows and the light lying along
the scrubbed boards are reserved as bare unpainted paper. The ink
linework stays the strongest element throughout and does not blur
into the wash.
```

# FILE: plates/ch14.md

# Ch. 14 — The Measure · plate sheet

**Both composed at s45 and both adopted — `both-hands-full` on its first run, `one-finger` on its second.** Kael is twelve and Valeria is twelve (`07/timeline`: never pinned, carried by *a girl his own age*). Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, and — on the second plate only — `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`.

**THE PAIR SPLITS THE CHAPTER AT ITS HINGE.** She arrives and takes two wasters and the yard has nothing to spend; and it ends with a boy on his back looking up a stopped blade at the first person who ever read him back. **Both are chest-up or closer and both have faces in them** (`../README.md`, s45).

**THE ANCHOR GAP IS TWO YEARS HERE, NOT THREE, AND NEITHER BLOCK SPENDS A RATIO ON IT.** §A close-up plate and the age line now has six runs: the rung is cheap and stays, and **a ratio clause at portrait scale has never once moved a face** — six runs, object and body, staged and unstaged. So both blocks carry the rung and nothing else, and the words that would have gone into a measurement went into the two things that have actually worked, which are the gaze and the crop.

**AND THE SECOND PLATE IS A CONTROLLED TEST THE FOLDER HAS NEVER RUN: TWO REFERENCES OF ONE PERSON.** Valeria is the only character besides Kael and Aeliana with a full-figure sheet, and **her grey is her station** — `../canon-brief.md` has the plainness carrying the rank, which is the one costume in the book that a prompt cannot improvise. `../scene-prompt-system.md` opens by saying scenes attach the full-body sheets and plates in practice never do. **So one plate this session attaches the portrait alone and the other attaches both**, same character, same session, same treatment. **The risk is named in the block and is the reason this is worth a run:** two images of one person may produce two people, so the block says in one line that they are the same girl and that there is one figure in the picture.

**ANCHORING COST.** The fight plate authors everything below two collars, including how a twelve-year-old lies on stone; the racks plate authors almost nothing, which is the point of attaching the sheet.

**CANDIDATE 3 IS HELD, NOT REJECTED.** *"No." Valeria Vel did not look at him* — the blood standing along the cut, the First Master already risen with the word in his mouth — is the chapter's other great beat and it is the same fight as the plate below, one minute earlier. **Two plates of one bout is one too many**, the same call `candidates.md` made about Elarine's cheek-tell across Ch. 13 and Ch. 17. It stays on the candidate list with its reasoning intact.

### PLATE: both-hands-full

*Candidate 2.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png` and `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`. Ch. 14 canon: *Valeria Vel went to the racks and took two wasters, one for each hand. / The yard made its sound. The off hand was for the Art — every child in the lines had been taught the stance, the open empty hand at the shoulder, the boast the empire fought with. Both her hands were full. It read as ignorance, or as insult, and nothing else about her read as either, so the yard did not know what to do with its own sound, and swallowed it.* And her, from the same chapter: *she was bored. Plainly, evenly bored, the way you are bored by weather you have already had.*

**THIS IS THE CANONICAL PICTURE OF THE TRAIT THE PRODUCTION RULE EXISTS TO PROTECT.** `../README.md`'s silhouette table separates her from Kael on six axes and the load-bearing one is **blades in both hands against a bundle on a back.** Nothing in the library has drawn it yet. **Both hands full is written as a staging instruction rather than as a fact** — both arms hanging clear of the body so each hand and each sword reads separately against the ground behind — because §Name the winner's whole history is facts that were stated and then staged out of the picture.

**THE VALUE STRUCTURE IS BUILT, NOT HOPED FOR.** `ch10/the-waterer` failed to demote a background in flat open daylight and `ch13/the-receipts` held one in flat light because mist gave the rival somewhere to go. **Here the yard supplies it directly:** she stands at the wall in the colonnade's shade with the bright open yard to her left, so the light comes across her face and the dark wall stands behind her head. **The bright yard reserves as bare paper on one side and the shaded wall takes the demotion on the other** — and her two occupied hands read against the light half.

**THE RACK IS THE ONE PLACE A NEGATIVE WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG.** §Hand inventory bans stray blades by name, and this frame has a rack of wooden swords in it on purpose. The block states ownership positively instead — two in her hands, the rest standing in the rack — because *no other blade anywhere in the frame* would contradict the setting.

**HER BOREDOM IS THE EXPRESSION AND HER SHEET FORBIDS TWO OF ITS NEIGHBOURS.** `../canon-brief.md`: *unimpressed and faintly tired*, and **the tiredness is hers and is not banned** — Elarine owns assessment-in-progress and cannot have this one. The negations go to the neighbourhoods a renderer reaches for when told a child is unimpressed: sneering, hostile, amused, proud.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch14-both-hands-full.png`, first run (author, s45: *adopt*).**

**THE TWO-REFERENCE TEST PASSED, AND IT PASSED ON BOTH QUESTIONS.** One girl arrived, not two — **the doubling risk this block was written around did not materialise** — and **the costume repeated.** The run reproduces `valeria-14-full.png` down to its parts: the sleeveless dark over-tunic over grey sleeves, the steel vambraces on both forearms, the wide buckled belt, the split skirt. **No plate in the folder has ever repeated a costume**, because §Plates has every plate anchored on a head-and-shoulders portrait with everything below the collar authored in words and anchored to nothing. **Valeria's grey now has a body that will come back next time**, and the instrument is one extra `Image N:` line.

**AND THE COST IS THE CROP, WHICH IS A REAL TRADE AND NOT A FAULT.** The block said **waist-up** and the run came back at the knee. **The full-figure sheet appears to pull the framing wider along with the costume** — one run, so it is a hypothesis, and the confound is honest: the block also named a wall, a rack and an open yard, which is a wide setting to describe.

**WHAT THE WIDTH COST IS HER EYES.** `../canon-brief.md` says her green is *spent at reading distance only, so it lands in close framing*, and this is no longer close framing: the green went pale and grey at that size. **The reference is not at fault and was checked before this was written** — `images/portraits/valeria-14.png` carries a clear green at portrait scale. **So the sheet and her one colour beat compete for the same picture**, and a plate that needs both will have to fight for the crop in words the setting does not undercut.

**WHAT THE WIDTH BOUGHT IS THE TRAIT.** Both arms hanging clear, each sword separately legible against the ground behind it, the whole silhouette in frame — **and `../README.md`'s separation table is a thumbnail test.** This reads at thumbnail. The wider crop served the subject of this particular plate even while it cost the eyes, which is why it is adopted rather than re-run.

**THE REST OF THE STAGING HELD.** Points down, low and loose, not crossed; the rack standing behind her shoulder with the other wooden swords in it and no stray blade anywhere; the ranks of children pale and small at the left edge; her gaze out across the yard past the frame and **not at the viewer**, which is the fourth block running. The bright yard reserved as bare paper on one side and the shaded wall taking the demotion on the other — **the built value structure worked exactly as designed**, and the s44 rule that a rival needs somewhere to go now has three runs behind it.

**SHE READS MID-TO-LATE TEENS.** Seventh run under the anchor ruling and consistent with all of them. Nothing new; `../scene-prompt-system.md` carries the state of that question.

```
Image 1: valeria-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.
Image 2: valeria-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl.

Setting: the edge of an open flagstone training yard on a bright
spring afternoon, as loose wash suggestion — a stone wall directly
behind her in shade, the upright posts of a weapon rack close behind
her shoulder, and the open yard beyond her to her left running away
pale and bright. Soft open daylight coming across her from the left
out of that bright yard, and it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: waist-up, one girl standing at the racks, turned three
quarters toward the viewer, filling the frame. The rack of plain
wooden practice swords stands upright behind her shoulder. Behind her
and much further off, at the edges of the frame, the lines of children
stand three deep as loose dark shapes.

THE TWO REFERENCE IMAGES ARE THE SAME PERSON AND THERE IS ONE GIRL IN
THIS PICTURE.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: her right hand holds one plain wooden
practice sword and nothing else. Her left hand holds one plain wooden
practice sword and nothing else. Both are carried low and loose at her
sides with the points down, not raised, not crossed and not touching
each other. Two hands, two swords, and the only other wooden swords in
the picture are the ones standing in the rack behind her.

BOTH HER HANDS BEING FULL IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE, AND THE POSE
SHOWS IT: both arms hang clear of her body, so that each hand and each
sword is separately visible against the ground behind it and neither
is lost against her clothing.

WHERE SHE IS LOOKING: out across the yard, level, past the left edge
of the frame, at nothing in particular. Not at the viewer and not at
the swords in her hands.

SHE IS BORED AND IT IS NOT A POSE. Even and unhurried, the mouth
closed, the brows unmoved, the eyes level and unimpressed, faintly
tired — bored by something she has seen many times already. NOT
sneering, NOT hostile, NOT amused, NOT proud, and not blank. She is
completely still, and the stillness is not restful.

SHE IS TWELVE: the eyes sit a little below the vertical midpoint of
the head, nearer that midpoint than a young child's and still below
it, and the jaw has not come in.

Black hair well past the shoulders, worn loose, with no ornament, no
band and nothing tied in it. Eyes a clear hard green, deep and cold,
the one saturated colour in the picture. Plain grey cloth, close,
exactly made and austere, with no badge, no house colour and no
decoration anywhere on it. A lean working build with nothing spare on
it anywhere, the shoulders and the forearms plainly worked, and
knuckles older than the face.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: her skin is the only
warm colour anywhere, and the wall, the rack, the wooden swords, the
flagstones and all the clothing in the picture are cool grey and
grey-blue with no brown in them.

HER FACE AND HER TWO OCCUPIED HANDS ARE THE MOST FINISHED THINGS IN
THE PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark. The rack,
the wall and the standing children stay at quiet middle values with
soft edges and no detail, and nothing back there competes for finish.
The bright open yard to her left is reserved as bare unpainted paper.
The ink linework stays the strongest element throughout and does not
blur into the wash.
```

### PLATE: one-finger

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`. Ch. 14 canon: *he was on his back in the drawn measure with her point one finger from his face. Exactly one finger. Not touching. The point did not waver, and neither did anything behind it. / She had all of it in hand. Every grain.* And what he is doing on the ground: *he lay looking up her blade at the awake eyes.* Her eyes, spent in this chapter and only at this distance: *a clear, hard green, the green of deep water over rock.*

**THE EYE CONTACT IS THE SUBJECT AND THE SYSTEM HAS A PROVEN FORM FOR IT.** §Getting expression: gaze alignment between two figures is unreliable and **putting it in capitals as its own paragraph worked** — *his eyes up, hers down, the lines of sight meeting. This eye contact is the subject of the image.* That paragraph is reused here almost verbatim, because it is the one instruction in the folder written for exactly this geometry. **Only one thing may be declared the subject**, so the stopped point is staged in full and never claims the word.

**THE LOW ANGLE IS WRITTEN AS SURFACES, NOT AS A CAMERA.** `ch04/the-master-arrives` failed twice at *the viewer is low, at the eye height of a boy of nine* and was fixed by naming what the angle exposes. So: the underside of her jaw and chin visible, the flagstones filling the bottom of the frame and running away behind his head, her near arm reaching down the frame.

**THE GAP IS THE PICTURE AND IT IS GIVEN A MEASUREMENT.** A clear width of air between the blunt wooden tip and his cheek, one finger wide, with nothing in the arm behind it moving. *It has been stopped, not checked* is the difference the page spends a paragraph on, and it is stated as stillness in the wrist, the elbow and the shoulder rather than as an adverb.

**NEITHER FACE IS DOING WHAT A YARD WOULD EXPECT, AND BOTH NEGATIONS ARE THERE FOR THAT.** He is beaten and still counting — not frightened, not pleading, not dazed. She has stopped a killing stroke and is not collecting anything for it — not angry, not triumphant, not gloating, **and not bored**, which is the one time her canonical affect has to be banned: the page says the boredom is gone, and this is the moment it went.

**REFUSAL RISK, NAMED IN ADVANCE.** §Some moments are undrawable records one refusal and it fell on a child in a yard. **This frame is a weapon point held at a child's face**, and although the weapon is wooden, blunt and rounded and the whole beat is that it stopped, a classifier reads nouns. **If it refuses, the first two things to cut are the thin red mark on his jaw and the word for the yard's drawn measure**, and if it still refuses the moment is not reachable and `candidates.md`'s third — the blood, and the refusal — is not a safer ask. **Do not iterate on a refusal**; the alternate that survives the crop test is *the name entered*, two faces and no weapon at all.

**CUT AFTER THE AUTHOR READ IT (s45), BEFORE IT RAN: *one-finger prompt looks very bloated.*** The block went **734 words to 515** — the paste-ready file, which carries the house header too, 816 to 598, **the leanest plate prompt in the folder's last four chapters**, where the adopted ones run 637 to 820 — and **no channel was traded away**: the low angle as surfaces, the hand inventory, the measured gap, the eye-contact paragraph, both expressions, both colourings with ownership stated, the warm/cool, the finish hierarchy, the reserve, the ink and the preserve line are all still in it. **What came out was words, not instructions** — a composition that said where the camera was three ways, a stillness clause listing the wrist, the elbow and the shoulder before saying the arm was still anyway, five nouns where three carry a palette, and two expression lists a term longer than they needed to be.

**AND THE AGE RUNG CAME OUT ON THE EVIDENCE, WHICH IS THE ONE REAL CHANGE RATHER THAN A TRIM.** §A close-up plate and the age line has seven runs now and **the rung has never once moved a face on its own**; the anchor gap here is two years; and the author has ruled six times that a face reading above its chapter is adopted anyway. **Thirty words that seven runs say buy nothing are the first thing that should go when a block is too long.** Flagged rather than assumed: if a face comes back badly wrong, the rung is one line to put back.

**AND THE PRE-FLIGHT PASSED IT CLEAN, WHICH IS THE POINT OF §The pre-flight is a script now.** *Not sure a script is the way to do it — I'd like you to just be better at knowing what a good prompt is.* The linter reported no restatement, no abstraction and a caps count inside range, **on a block twice the length it needed to be**, because bloat by paragraph is not bloat the script can see. **Second session running that the author has had to cut this folder's own habit out of a block by hand** — after s44 took 160 words out of the Ch. 12 pair. The rule was never the gap; applying it while writing is.

**AND THE WORD THAT BROKE IT WAS *LEVEL* (author, s45): *they should be looking at each other. why is kael looking into nothing.*** The block said **his eyes up** in the gaze paragraph and **eyes level** in his expression paragraph two lines below it. **This folder uses *level* as a temperament word in almost every block it writes** — level and unhurried, the face level, the warmth running level — and **to a renderer it is a direction: horizontal.** Two instructions for one channel, resolved independently, and the horizontal one won. §Over-writing contradicts, arriving through a vocabulary door rather than a length one.

**The fix is not more emphasis on the eye contact.** The word is out, the direction is now stated inside the expression paragraph as well as the gaze paragraph, and both ends of both lines of sight are named — *up at her face*, *down at his*, *each one ending on the other's face.* Same lesson as §Describe a limb as a chain: when two paragraphs describe one thing, say the thing in both or one of them will be resolved without the other.

**Checked across the session's other blocks before writing it down.** `ch13/the-receipts` carries the same collision — *the attention already back on, level*, with a gaze written up and out of frame — **and that run came back looking up anyway**, so the word is a risk and not a guarantee. Everywhere else in these two chapters *level* and the stated direction agree, so nothing else moves.

**RUN 1 (s45), AND THE AUTHOR'S FIX IS BETTER THAN THIS SHEET'S.** The staging came back whole — the viewer down at flagstone height, the boy's head and shoulders filling the lower frame, the flagstones running away behind him and reserved as bare paper, the girl seen from below against the colonnade, the watching children as featureless dark shapes, his open empty hand large in the foreground, his own wooden sword on the stones out of reach, and the gap at his face a finger wide with nothing touching. **His gaze was fixed and held** — the *level* repair worked on him.

**HER HALF OF THE EYE CONTACT DID NOT, AND THE BLOCK APPLIED ITS OWN LESSON TO ONE FIGURE OUT OF TWO.** He was given a physical instruction — *eyes turned up under the brows*, *his gaze goes upward, not out along the ground* — and she was given **the whole attention aimed down at him.** *Attention* is not drawable. The renderer drew the physical one and left her looking off past him, so the two lines of sight never met and the declared subject of the picture was not in it. **Both paragraphs now name the same physical thing.**

**AND THE EXPRESSION CHANGES ON AN AUTHOR RULING: *I think Kael should be kinda mad/surprised.*** This sheet had written him *mouth closed, jaw set, still counting* — **which is the coat**, and this is the one scene in the book where the page takes the coat off him in public: *every eye in the yard arrived on him at once, and all of it showed.* The anger is page canon in the same chapter — *the anger sitting in him with its new name* — and *still counting* was never drawable in the first place. **A face that shows something is both the better picture and the more faithful one**, and the sheet had the wrong instinct by habit.

**KAEL'S OWN WASTER IS ON THE STONES OUT OF REACH**, because §Describe a limb as a chain records a run that lost its causality when a held object and a grip were entered as two facts. The page has the twelfth exchange take it out of his hand entirely; the picture says where it went.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch14-one-finger.png`, run 2 (author, s45: *adopt*).**

**THE AUTHOR'S EXPRESSION FIX IS THE WHOLE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE TWO RUNS.** Same staging, same crop, same light, one paragraph changed — and the picture went from a boy lying still to a boy who has just been beaten and cannot keep it off his face. **The eyes wide and turned up under the brows, the brows down, the mouth open on a caught breath.** Run 1's version of him was *mouth closed, jaw set, still counting*, which is two mistakes in one clause: **it dressed him in the coat in the one scene where the page takes the coat off him in public**, and *still counting* was an abstraction a renderer cannot draw, so what arrived was a blank.

**AND BOTH GAZES LANDED ONCE BOTH WERE PHYSICAL.** His eyes go up to her face and hers come down to his. The only thing that changed on her side was replacing *the whole attention aimed down at him* with a head tipped down and eyes turned down under the brows. **Two runs, one variable each side, and the rule holds in both directions: name the direction as anatomy, in every paragraph that touches it.**

**THE STAGING CAME BACK TWICE AND IS THE FOLDER'S BEST LOW ANGLE.** The viewer at flagstone height, his head and shoulders filling the near frame, the flagstones running away and reserved as bare paper, the girl seen from below against the colonnade, the watching children as featureless dark shapes, his open empty hand large in the foreground, and his own waster on the stones out of reach. **Written as surfaces rather than as a camera position, per §Altitude, and it has now worked on two consecutive runs of the same block.**

**AND THE REFUSAL RISK NAMED IN ADVANCE DID NOT MATERIALISE.** A blunt wooden tip stopped a finger from a child's face ran twice without difficulty. **Recorded because the risk was written down before the run rather than discovered after it** — the fallbacks were never needed and are not owed a test.

**SMALL DRIFTS, NONE WORTH A THIRD RUN.** The tip sits nearer his chin than his cheek and reads a shade closer than the stated finger's width. The thin red mark on his jaw did not arrive on either run. **And her costume is authored grey rather than the over-tunic, vambraces and split skirt of `both-hands-full`** — this block anchors on her portrait alone, so **the same chapter now carries two different costumes for the same girl.** That is §Plates' two-reference finding showing its cost from the other side, in the same session that found it: **the sheet is what makes a costume repeat, and a plate that leaves it off is authoring clothing that will not come back.**

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: valeria-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.

Setting: an open flagstone training yard on a bright spring afternoon,
as loose wash suggestion, a stone colonnade behind. Flat open
daylight, and it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: the viewer is down at flagstone height beside the boy's
head. He lies on his back in the near foreground, head and
shoulders filling the lower half of the frame. The girl stands over
him beyond his head, seen from below with the underside of her jaw
visible, her near arm reaching down the frame. The flagstones
fill the bottom of the frame and run away behind his head.

WHAT EACH HAND IS DOING: her right hand holds one plain wooden
practice sword with a blunt rounded tip, held down so the tip stops
beside his cheek, touching nothing. Her left hand is out of frame. His
two hands lie open and empty on the stones, and his own wooden sword
lies out of reach at the edge of the frame.

THE TIP IS STOPPED SHORT OF HIS FACE: a clear gap of air one finger
wide between the wood and his cheek, the arm behind it still. Nothing in the picture is moving.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER. His eyes up at her face,
hers down at his, the two lines of sight meeting past the wooden tip
and each one ending on the other's face. This eye contact is the
subject of the image. Neither is looking at the viewer and neither is
looking past the other.

THE BOY is angry and caught out at the same time, and it is all over
his face: the eyes wide open and turned up under the brows to
hold hers, the brows drawn down and together, the mouth open a little
on a caught breath, the jaw tight. His gaze goes upward, not out along
the ground. NOT frightened, NOT pleading, NOT dazed, and he is not
shouting. One thin red mark along his jaw.

THE GIRL has decided nothing: standing easy, mouth closed, brows
unmoved, her head tipped down toward him and her eyes turned down
under the brows to hold his. Her gaze goes downward, not out across
the yard. NOT angry, NOT triumphant, NOT bored.

HIS eyes are near-white neutral grey, the lightest value in his face;
dark hair cut short and uneven; plain undyed working wool, coarse and
much mended, the ink carrying it at the seams.

HERS are a clear hard green, deep and cold; black hair well past the
shoulders, loose and hanging forward as she looks down; plain grey
cloth, close, exactly made, no badge and no decoration. The grey is
hers alone and the mended wool his.

Their skin is the only warm colour in the picture; the stone, the
wood, the watching children and all the clothing are cool grey and
grey-blue with no brown in them.

THE TWO FACES AND THE WOODEN TIP BETWEEN THEM ARE THE MOST FINISHED
THINGS IN THE PICTURE and carry the sharpest step from light to dark.
The children watching from the edges are dark summary shapes with no
features, and the colonnade stays at quiet middle values with no
detail. The lit faces
of the flagstones are reserved as bare unpainted paper. The ink
linework stays the strongest element and does not blur into the wash.

Preserve both children exactly as shown in their references — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features or mix
their clothing.
```

# FILE: plates/ch15.md

# Ch. 15 — The Hours · plate sheet

**Both plates composed at s45 and neither has run.** Kael is twelve, Aurelian twelve and built a year ahead of it. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png`.

**THIS IS THE FIRST CHAPTER WHOSE SHORTLIST COLLIDES WITH THE s45 RULING.** All three of `candidates.md`'s entries are a back, a pair of hands on a block, or a wide staging of people walking away — **no face doing anything in any of them**, and the ruling is that a chest-up with a face is the default and everything else has to earn itself. **One earns it and the other two do not**, so the pair is candidate 1 plus a beat the list did not carry.

**THE PAIR IS WHAT THE SUMMER COST AND WHAT IT BOUGHT.** A body with the marks stopping exactly where the shirt does; and a boy who has never in his life laughed at nothing laughing at nothing, in a court he was not born to be in.

**THE ALTERNATE IS THE BEST FACE IN THE CHAPTER AND IT IS NOT DRAWABLE.** *His father was standing over him with the blade at his side, absolutely still, looking at him. Not at the mistake. At him. / Kael had been read by that face his whole life and had never once seen the thing that was in it now, and did not have anywhere to put it.* **The page names the face as the subject and then refuses to say what is in it** — and an image cannot decline to answer, so any close-up of it spends what the chapter deliberately withholds. THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE settles it: *if a moment cannot be composed without spending something unspent, the moment is wrong.*

**One framing answer does exist and is passed over on set grounds.** Put the lamp behind the man so his face goes dark and draw the boy's face reading a face the reader cannot see. **It works, and it restages `ch14/one-finger` one chapter later** — a child on the ground, an adult standing over him, a blade in the adult's hand — with a night yard and a fallen boy adding refusal risk on top. Recorded so nobody rediscovers it; the moment stays available if the author wants it.

**AND TWO MORE OF THIS CHAPTER'S IMAGES ARE DELIBERATE NON-CHOICES.** *Twice that summer he came in from the dark and his mother was in the doorway... one hand on the frame and the light behind her... her knuckles were pale on the wood* is **`ch02-the-doorframe` again, almost line for line** — the book makes that rhyme in prose on purpose, and drawing it twice spends it. And the calibration — grown men in the capital's grey striking a twelve-year-old with weighted wasters, forty times before breakfast — is the chapter's most terrible image and sits squarely in the neighbourhood of §Some moments are undrawable's one refusal. **Not attempted, and not owed a test.**

**`candidates.md`'s third, *nobody stands beside her*, is held.** It is the chapter's title-idea and its last movement, and it is a wide staging of people walking away — **and it would be Valeria's third plate in two chapters.** It stays on the list.

**NEITHER BLOCK CARRIES AN AGE INSTRUMENT.** Eight runs now: no rung and no ratio has moved a face at portrait scale, `ch14/one-finger` shipped without one and read no worse, and the anchor is the lever, which is an author ruling.

---

### PLATE: the-sons-back

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 15 canon: *He was hit a great deal that summer. The blunted steel did not cut and it did not need to; it went into the meat of the arm and the ribs and the outside of the thigh and left marks that came up black and went yellow and were replaced before they finished going. He learned to dress in the loft with his back to the ladder.* And the thing the picture exists for: *in a whole summer of it his father never once put a mark anywhere above the collar or below the wrist. / Not once. Not by accident, either... Everything the Temple would see was left alone. Everything the shirt covered was fair.*

**IT IS THE DELIBERATE RHYME WITH `ch03-the-back`, TWELVE CHAPTERS LATER.** That plate is the father stripped at the trough, read the way you read goat paths — a seam, a through-wound, the tally-marks. **This one is the son, and the whole difference is that his stop.** Same crop, same read-the-body composition, one generation on. `candidates.md` calls it and it is right: the father's marks are a record somebody else wrote, and the son's are an argument his father is making about what the Temple may see.

**AND THAT IS WHY THE FACELESSNESS IS EARNED RATHER THAN PRICED** (`../README.md`, s45). The subject is a line on a body — where the marks stop — and **the line only reads if the untouched skin above the collar and below the wrist is in the same frame.** A chest-up with a face would crop away half the evidence. The picture is the argument; the argument is anatomical.

**THE COMPARISON IS STAGED, NOT STATED.** Marked skin and unmarked skin, both in frame, adjacent, at the same depth — the neck and the hands clean, everything between them marked. That is the one thing this folder knows how to make a renderer perform (§A close-up plate and the age line, and the copper bar).

**LIGHT FROM BELOW, FROM THE ONE PLACE THE LOFT HAS ANY.** The ladder hole, with the room's lamp under it, is the loft's only source and it throws upward — proven since `ch05/the-fact-lands`. **It also puts the light behind and below him**, raking across the back, which is what makes bruising read at all. §What a warm source will and will not light: the source is close and low, so it lights him and nothing else, and the cool goes on what it does not reach.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch15-the-sons-back.png`, run 1 (author, s45: *adopt both*).** *The re-run was written and never spent; the block below is the one that made this picture, and it is not retrofitted.*

**THE ARGUMENT CAME BACK WHOLE.** Bruises of many ages across the back, the shoulder blades and the upper arms, purple and black and gone yellow-green, laid over one another with no pattern and no broken skin. **The neck clean above them and both hands clean below them**, so the line the plate exists for is legible in one look. The loft dark, the light coming up warm from below and reserved as bare paper where it enters, the shirt in his hand mended at every seam, and nobody else in the picture.

**AND THE BLOCK'S COLLAR LINE WAS ANATOMICALLY WRONG, WHICH THE RUN QUIETLY CORRECTED.** It asked for *the back of the neck, the tops of the shoulders* to be clean **above the line of a shirt collar** — but a shirt covers the shoulders, and the page says *never once above the collar or below the wrist.* The run marked the shoulder caps and left the neck clean, **which is the page and not the block.** Corrected here; the picture was right.

**MISS: IT READS AS A GROWN MAN'S BACK, AND IT IS THE COST OF A DECISION THIS SESSION MADE WITH ITS EYES OPEN.** The age instrument was dropped at `ch14/one-finger` on eight runs of evidence — **all of them faces.** A faceless body plate is a different case entirely: the anchor is head-and-shoulders, so **nothing below the collar is anchored to anything**, and the whole body was authored from *a lean, hard, worked body, strong without bulk.* §Scale between figures is unambiguous that adjectives lose, and `ch09/the-roller-alone` already found that a solo figure has no age in the frame unless one is written. **So: the rung is dead for faces and alive for bodies**, and the two questions were wrongly treated as one.

**AND THE AUTHOR ADOPTED IT OVER THAT DIAGNOSIS: *I think the back is good, Kael is pretty strong.*** The instinct is canon — `../canon-brief.md` has him *lean, hard, visibly fit; strong without bulk* — and this is the seventh ruling in the direction `../README.md` already records: **one consistent body across the book, over age accuracy in any single picture.**

**THE FIX WAS WRITTEN AND NEVER SPENT, AND IT IS KEPT HERE BECAUSE THE NEXT FACELESS BODY PLATE WILL WANT IT.** Proportion rather than slightness, which is the only version that does not contradict the brief: *his shoulders are about two of his own head-widths across, not three — a hard, worked, lean body with real strength in it and none of a grown man's depth or mass, the frame not finished, the blades and the lower ribs standing under the skin, the muscle flat and stringy rather than filled out* — with the back of the skull kept in frame so the ratio has both its terms. **Untested. The half that is tested is the diagnosis above:** a body with no face in the frame has no age anchor at all, and adjectives will hand you a grown man.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the sleeping loft of a small mountain house at night, as
loose wash suggestion — bare roof timbers close overhead, straw and a
folded blanket, the corners going into deep shadow. The open ladder
hole is in the floor behind him with the room's lamp somewhere below
it, out of frame: the light comes up through that hole from below and
behind him, and it is the only light in the picture.

Composition: the boy stands with his back square to the viewer,
stripped to the waist, cropped at the hips and at the top of the neck
so that no face and no head is in the frame. A shirt hangs from one
hand at his side. He fills the frame.

THE MARKS AND WHERE THEY STOP ARE THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. Across the
back of the ribs, the shoulder blades and the backs of both upper arms
lie many bruises of very different ages, some almost black and some
gone yellow-green, some fading and some new, laid over one another in
no pattern. They are flat under the skin and the skin is not broken
anywhere: no cuts, no blood.

WHERE THEY STOP IS THE WHOLE PICTURE, AND BOTH SIDES OF THE LINE ARE
IN FRAME. Above the line of a shirt collar — the back of the neck, the
tops of the shoulders — the skin is completely clean and unmarked.
Below the wrists, both hands are completely clean and unmarked. Every
mark on him lies between those two lines, and the change is abrupt.

A lean, hard, worked body, strong without bulk, the strength in the
back and the forearms. Dark hair cut short and uneven at home. The
shirt in his hand is plain undyed working wool, coarse and much
mended, and the ink carries it at the seams and the mended places.

THE LAMPLIGHT COMES UP FROM THE HOLE BEHIND HIM AND REACHES HIM ONLY:
his back and the backs of his arms take the warm light, raking across
from below so the bruises stand out against it, and the timbers, the
straw, the far wall and the corners of the loft stay cool grey and
grey-blue with no brown in them.

HIS BACK IS THE MOST FINISHED THING IN THE PICTURE and carries the
sharpest step from light to dark. The loft around him stays at quiet
middle values with soft edges and no detail, and nothing back there
competes for finish. The lit edge of the ladder hole is reserved as
bare unpainted paper. The ink linework stays the strongest element and
does not blur into the wash.

There is nobody else in the picture and nobody on the ladder behind
him.
```

### PLATE: the-unaimed-laugh

*Not on `candidates.md`; proposed at s45 under the ruling that the chapter's shortlist has no face in it.* Anchors: `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 15 canon: *Aurelian stood in the middle of the cistern court with soot on his forearms, and something went across his face that his tier would not have recognized, and he laughed. It was a real laugh, short, surprised out of him, the first one Kael had ever heard from him that was not aimed at anybody.* And, in the same breath: *Kael's breath went to the long shape before he could stop it. He caught it, put it back, and hoped the count had not showed.*

**IT IS THE FIRST PICTURE IN THE BOOK OF THESE TWO NOT FIGHTING.** Three years of the yard, a hall, a kettle joke and an apology measured to the inch — and here they are filthy in a disused court with the light going, one of them laughing at nothing and the other one hiding a breath. **The chapter's whole transaction is in it:** *Kael got a magnifying glass. Aurelian got the why.*

**THE TWO FACES ARE WRITTEN AS A CONTRADICTION, WHICH §Getting expression SAYS TO STATE OUTRIGHT.** One face wide open and surprised out of itself; the other giving nothing at all, in the same light, at arm's length. *State contradictions as contradictions* is the documented instruction and this is a two-face version of it.

**AND THE GAZES GO DIFFERENT WAYS ON PURPOSE, BOTH AS ANATOMY** (§Getting expression, as corrected at s45): **the laugh is not aimed at anybody**, so Aurelian's head goes back and his eyes go up and away — and Kael's eyes are on him. **One-sided gaze is easier than mutual and often more interesting**, which the system already says, and here it is the actual content: the boy who cannot be laughed at any more, watched by the boy who is not going to show anything.

**`../canon-brief.md` DOES THE REST AND IT IS UNUSUALLY SPECIFIC ON HIM.** Never sneering, never smug, never cruel — *pleasant, open, easy, genuinely charming* — so the one thing this plate must not do is make a real laugh look like a jeer. His warmth lives in his colouring and is never stated; **his ground stays cool**, which is written into the palette here rather than left to chance.

**NO INSIGNIA ON HIM ANYWHERE.** `07/planted-clues` reserves the Corvane insignia for the Part IV fire, and good cloth well cut is the whole of his station.

**RUN 1 (s45), NOT ADOPTED — re-cropped on an author note: *looks nice. their bodies look kinda like they're floating a bit.***

**THE FACES ARE EXACTLY RIGHT AND THAT IS THE HARD HALF.** The laugh came back real — head gone back, eyes creased almost shut, mouth open, the whole face open — **and not a jeer**, which is the one failure mode `../canon-brief.md` warns about on him: *never sneering, never smug, never cruel.* Beside it the other face gives nothing at all, still, mouth closed, eyes on him. **Two faces in one light doing opposite things**, which is §Getting expression's *state contradictions as contradictions* working across a pair rather than inside one body.

**THE FLOAT IS A CROP THAT WIDENED, AND IT IS THE SECOND TIME.** The block said **chest-up** and the run came back near three-quarter length, so both boys run down out of the frame with no feet, no ground contact and nothing sitting them in the flagstones. `ch14/both-hands-full` did the same thing — waist-up asked, the knee arrived. **Twice now a stated crop has lost, and both times the word was an abstraction.** §Altitude settled the same argument for camera height: *any instruction phrased as where the camera is should be rewritten as what is therefore in frame.* **A crop word is the same kind of instruction**, so the block now names the cut as a landmark on the body — *the bottom edge crosses both boys just below the collarbone* — and says what that puts out of frame. Promoted to §The crop rule.

**AND THE GROUNDING CLAUSE WAS MISSING, WHICH IS AN OLD LESSON THIS BLOCK SIMPLY DID NOT CARRY.** §Settings: *both figures grounded on the same floor plane matters — without it, one stands on a ground line and the other floats on bare paper.* Written for two figures in a hall and just as true for two boys in a court. Both are now placed at the same distance from the viewer with the wall a stated two strides behind them.

**WHAT THE TIGHTER CROP COSTS: THE SOOT.** *Soot on his forearms* is the page's own detail for what that afternoon was, and at a collarbone crop there are no forearms. **It comes out rather than migrating somewhere the page never put it** — a smear invented onto a jaw is exactly the small invention THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE exists to stop. The day's work now reads off the faces and nothing else.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch15-the-unaimed-laugh.png`, run 2 (author, s45: *perfect*).**

**THE LANDMARK CROP FIXED THE FLOAT ON ONE CHANGE.** Naming where the frame cuts the body instead of saying *chest-up*, plus the wall sited two strides behind them, and both boys sit in the court instead of hanging on the paper. **The wall is doing the work** — it gives the flagstones somewhere to recede to, which is what a cropped standing figure needs and what §Settings' grounding clause has always been for. **Two runs, one variable, and the second is the folder's own documented instruction finally being carried.**

**AND THE FACES SURVIVED THE RE-CROP INTACT**, which was the risk worth naming: run 1 got them right and a second run could have lost them. The laugh is still real and still not a jeer, the other face still gives nothing, and the eyes still go different ways — his up and away at nothing, the dark-haired boy's on his face, neither at the viewer.

**THE COSTUME OWNERSHIP HELD ACROSS TWO BOYS IN SIMILAR TONES.** Good plain cloth well cut with no badge, no chain and no ornament on one; coarse mended undyed wool with the ink at every seam on the other. **Nothing bled**, which §Costume contamination says is what happens only when ownership is stated per character rather than assumed.

**THEY READ MID-TEENS.** Ninth run under the anchor ruling, no age instrument in the block, consistent with every other. Nothing new.

```
Image 1: aurelian-14.png — portrait reference of the fair-haired boy.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Setting: a disused stone cistern court in the last of a summer
afternoon, as loose wash suggestion — a low wall, dry flagstones and scrub
going into cool shade behind them. Late low daylight from one side, and it is the only
light in the picture.

Composition: the bottom edge of the picture crosses both boys just
below the collarbone, so that no arms, no hands and no legs are in the
frame at all and the two heads and shoulders fill it. They stand
shoulder to shoulder in the near foreground, turned three quarters
toward the viewer, both at the same distance from the viewer, with the
low wall about two strides behind them. The fair-haired boy is nearer
the middle and stands a head taller; the dark-haired boy is at his
shoulder and half a pace behind.

THE FAIR-HAIRED BOY IS LAUGHING AND IT IS A REAL ONE, SURPRISED OUT OF
HIM: the head gone back a little, the mouth open, the eyes creased and
almost shut, the whole face open. It is not aimed at anybody. NOT
sneering, NOT smug, NOT mocking, and not posed.

THE DARK-HAIRED BOY IS GIVING NOTHING BACK, AND THE TWO FACES SIDE BY
SIDE ARE THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. Still, the mouth closed and
relaxed, the brows unmoved, the face at rest — a boy holding a breath
where nobody can see him do it. NOT laughing, NOT smiling, NOT sullen,
and not blank.

WHERE EACH IS LOOKING: the fair-haired boy's eyes go up and away past
the top of the frame at nothing. The dark-haired boy's eyes are on the
fair-haired boy's face. Neither is looking at the viewer.

Both boys' arms and hands are out of frame below the bottom edge.
Nothing is held by anybody and there is no weapon anywhere in the
picture.

The fair-haired boy has dark-gold hair, well cut and groomed, and
amber gold-brown eyes; clear well-fed skin with sun on it. He is
tall and already broad through the shoulder, a head taller than the
other boy and heavier through the frame. He wears good plain cloth,
well cut and well kept, with no badge, no chain and no ornament of any
kind on him.

The dark-haired boy has near-white neutral grey eyes, cool and
colourless, the lightest value in his face; dark hair cut short and
uneven. He wears plain undyed working wool, coarse and much mended,
the ink carrying it at the seams. The good cloth belongs only to the
fair-haired boy and the mended wool only to the dark-haired one.

THE PICTURE STAYS COOL AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: their skin and the
fair-haired boy's hair carry the only warm colour, and the walls, the
flagstones, the scrub and all the clothing are cool grey and grey-blue
with no brown in them.

THE TWO FACES ARE THE MOST FINISHED THINGS IN THE PICTURE and carry
the sharpest step from light to dark. The wall, the court and the
scrub stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no detail. The
lit tops of the wall and the flagstones are reserved as bare unpainted
paper. The ink linework stays the strongest element and does not blur
into the wash.

Preserve both boys exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features and do not
mix their clothing.
```
