# FILE: plates/ch16.md

# Ch. 16 — The Terms · plate sheet

**Both composed at s45. `nine-strides` is adopted on its first run; `the-ear-on-the-brick` on its second (s47).** Kael twelve, Aurelian twelve and built a year ahead, Elarine twelve, Valeria twelve. Anchors: `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, and `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png` on the second plate.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S TWO COURTS.** The cistern court, where three children solve a problem on their hands and knees; and the old sea court, where a girl who has not turned around says a number out loud. **One is the only comic plate the book has and the other is the coldest arrival in it.**

**AND THE FIRST ONE BREAKS THE DEFAULT CROP ON PURPOSE, WHICH IS WHY IT IS ARGUED HERE.** `../README.md` s45 makes a chest-up with a face the default. **A boy lying on brick, a boy walking a circle and a girl up on a wall cannot be got into a chest-up**, and the height difference between them *is* the joke. So the frame is wide and **the ruling is honoured a different way: one face is resolved and large and near, and the other two are demoted** — which is `ch12/furniture`'s solve, and the point of §Name the winner.

**BOTH BLOCKS CARRY THE s45 CROP LESSON.** The cut is named as a landmark and the ground is named as a plane, after *chest-up* returned three-quarter length and two boys floated (§The crop rule, as amended).

**NO AGE INSTRUMENT ON ANY FACE.** Nine runs. On the second plate the small distant figure gets **scale by measurement instead**, which is a different instrument for a different job and the one that has never failed: a figure whose size in frame is load-bearing gets a stated fraction of something else in the frame.

**THE DOORWAY IS HELD, AND IT IS HELD FOR CONSISTENCY.** *Valeria Vel was a foot away, looking straight at him, with an expression he had never seen on her.* It is the chapter's climax and a close two-shot, and **the page calls the expression unprecedented and then declines to say what it is** — which is exactly the test that ruled Ch. 15's father undrawable one chapter ago. **The difference is real and does not settle it:** her state is spoken aloud four lines later — *there is nobody on my floor, I have been holding it since the bell, and you are standing in it* — so a face caught mid-question is arguably legible rather than invented. **Available on request; not taken unasked**, because a folder that applies THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE to one man's face and not to one girl's is not applying it.

**`candidates.md`'s third is held under the s45 ruling.** The hand flat on the door with nothing coming through is eleven weeks in one object — and it is a hand and an object, which is the crop the author ruled oversubscribed.

---

### PLATE: the-ear-on-the-brick

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchors: `images/portraits/aurelian-14.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 16 canon: *the heir of House Corvane, who would one day hold a fleet, lay face down on the wet brick of a disused cistern court with his cheek in a puddle while a herder's son walked circles around him, and after the fourth circuit said, in an entirely different voice, "Oh."* Then: *He got up on his elbows with his hair full of moss. "I can feel where you are with my face. That is revolting. Do it again."* And, at the wall: *Elarine... did not look up from the flame standing on her palm, which had not moved in four minutes and was the only tidy thing in the court.*

**IT IS THE ONLY COMIC PLATE IN THE BOOK AND THE COMEDY IS ENTIRELY IN ONE FACE.** Not in the situation, which a reader has to be told, but in the expression of a great-house child who has just discovered something with his cheek in a puddle and wants it done again. **So his face is resolved, near and large, and the other two are held back** — the boy walking the circuit is what the picture is about and is not what the picture looks at.

**THE THREE HEIGHTS ARE THE STAGING AND THEY ARE NAMED AS THREE DEPTHS.** One on the brick, one on his feet mid-stride, one up on the wall — near, middle, far. §Four figures found that **depth is what makes a group work and an arc makes a lineup**, and this is that finding with three.

**EVERY GAZE IS NAMED AND NOT ONE OF THEM MEETS.** He is looking at nothing, delighted, at ground level; Kael is looking down at his own feet, which is the pose that has solved that reference four times; Elarine is looking at the flame and **not up**, which is what the page says twice. Three faces, three directions, nobody at the viewer.

**THE FLAME THROWS NOTHING AND THE BLOCK SAYS SO.** A coin of fire on a palm in flat daylight lights nothing (§What a warm source will and will not light, and `ch01/fallen-stars` where a whole town threw nothing). It is the only warm colour in the picture and it is not a light source in it.

**RUN 1 (s45), NOT ADOPTED — re-run: *this one didn't land. Aurelian's body looks like it's coming off the ground.***

**THE FLOOR BECAME A MIRROR AND NOTHING COULD LIE ON IT.** The block asked for *wet dark brick with shallow standing water in the hollows* and then, at the bottom, for **the standing water and the wet sheen on the brick to be reserved as bare unpainted paper.** Together those made one continuous bright reflective sheet with no bricks and no joints in it — **so the near boy reads as rising out of water and the walking boy's boot hangs above it.** The reserve is a good instrument aimed at the wrong surface: **pointing it at the floor itself dissolved the one thing three figures needed to stand on.**

**AND THE BODY WAS NEVER GIVEN A CONTACT INVENTORY.** *Lying face down and propped up on both elbows* with *two hands flat on the brick under his shoulders* is a pose, not a set of contacts, and the hands arrived splayed out ahead of him with the elbows nowhere. §Describe a limb as a chain says two instructions about one limb get resolved independently unless the order along it is given; **a body on a floor is that problem one size up.** The block now names every part that touches — chest, both forearms elbow to hand, both palms — and does the same for the walking boy's leading boot.

**THE COMEDY DID NOT ARRIVE EITHER, AND THAT IS A SEPARATE FAULT.** *Delighted and disgusted at once: the eyes wide open, the brows up, the mouth open on a word* returned alarm — a wet face with a wide-open mouth low over water reads as difficulty, and there was no smile written anywhere in the block. **Delight has to be in the mouth shape, not in the openness**: grinning, corners up, teeth showing, eyes creased at the outer corners. The bans now point at the neighbourhood the run actually landed in rather than the one this folder expected.

**WHAT LANDED AND IS UNTOUCHED:** the girl on the wall with the coin of flame on her open palm, looking at it and not up; the walking boy looking down at his own feet; the three depths; the moss in his hair; nobody looking at the viewer. The flame threw a faint glow it was told not to throw, which is small and not worth a clause.

**AND THE COLOUR CLAUSE CAME OUT BEFORE RUN 2 (author ruling, s45).** This block ended, as nearly every block in the folder has ended, with *cool grey and grey-blue with no brown in them.* **That clause is retired** (`../README.md`) and colour is named positively on named objects instead. Nothing else in the block moved, so if run 2 lands, this plate is also the first evidence on the new colour instruction.

**REWRITTEN UNDER THE s47 TEMPLATE BEFORE RUN 2** (`../prompt-system.md` §Plate template): the built prompt from 884 words to 524, the run-1 fixes kept — the matte brick in courses, the contact inventory, the grin in the mouth shape. Cut: the value-structure paragraph (flat daylight, no dark to demote into; the crop already makes the near face largest), the per-figure appearance lines (the references carry them), the *only light in the picture* clause (nothing pulls toward a second source), and *it lights nothing and throws no shadow* (run 1 threw a faint glow anyway; the sheet called it not worth a clause). The hues moved onto the objects that have them. **First evidence on the new register, if it runs.**

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch16-the-ear-on-the-brick.png`, run 2 (author, s47: *pretty nice* · adopt).** The first picture on the new register.

**WHAT LANDED.** The grin — mouth wide, teeth, eyes creased, brows up — the comedy run 1 could not find, in the one face it was written into. The floor: brick in courses, matte, the joints drawn and mossed, the puddles small and grey, nothing a mirror, all three on the one plane. The contact inventory: chest on the brick, both forearms along it, palms down. Moss in the hair. The walking boy looking at his own feet, hands empty; the girl on the wall looking at the flame on her palm and not up, the flame a coin of orange and the one saturated point. Three depths; nobody at the viewer; nobody else in the court. **And the palette is the scene tests' — warm umber and grey-brown throughout, the wall a pale wash behind them — which the plates under the colour ban did not have.** The tool chose landscape unasked; every plate before it is portrait.

**WHAT DRIFTED, none of it worth a run.** *Nothing running* did not hold: water runs down his cheek from the puddle, and with the grin it reads as puddle, not distress. The girl's *plain close-fitting dark clothing* arrived as a long dark dress to the floor — a noun default (`../prompt-system.md` §Nouns) on a line that gave no construction; the quartet's identical line gave a fitted coat, so it is variance as much as instruction. She reads older than her anchor, as both earlier plates of her did; the standing ruling takes the consistent face. The leading boot is mid-step rather than flat, and he stands on the other one.

**ON THE REGISTER.** Five clauses were cut from the run-1 block and none was missed: no value-structure paragraph, and the near face is still the finished one because the crop makes it so; no appearance lines, and all three faces are their references; no *only light* clause, and there is one light. 524 words against 884.

```
Image 1: aurelian-14.png — portrait reference of the fair-haired boy.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.
Image 3: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.

Setting: a disused cistern court on a grey afternoon, as loose wash
suggestion — a low brick wall across the back, the far side of the
court in cool shade. Soft flat daylight. The floor is old brick laid
in courses, warm dark grey-brown, matte and damp, the joints drawn and
mossed dull green, two or three small shallow puddles in its hollows
taking the grey of the sky; nothing in the picture is mirror-like.

Composition: the viewer is down at the height of the near boy's face.
The fair-haired boy is nearest and largest, lying face down on the
brick propped on both elbows, his face turned across the frame to the
viewer's left; the bottom edge crosses just behind his shoulders, so
his legs are out of frame. Behind him the dark-haired boy walks across
the frame mid-stride at full length, and further back the girl sits on
the low wall. One brick floor runs unbroken from the near boy to the
wall, and all three are on it.

The fair-haired boy is delighted: grinning, the mouth open and pulled
wide with the corners up, teeth showing, the eyes bright and creased
at the outer corners, the brows up. Not alarmed, not sneering. His hair
is full of green moss. His chest rests on the brick, both forearms lie
along it from elbow to hand with the elbows under his shoulders, both
palms down; a shallow puddle stands a hand's width in front of his
face and his cheek and jaw are wet from it, nothing running. Good plain
cloth, well cut, in deep umber and warm charcoal, soaked down the
front.

The dark-haired boy mid-stride, his leading boot flat on the brick,
hands empty at his sides. Undyed working wool, coarse and much mended,
in dull oatmeal and dead olive.

The girl sits with her left hand in her lap and her right open, palm
up, one small flame the width of a coin standing on it with a clear
gap of air beneath — no wick, no candle, nothing in her hand. It is the
one bright saturated point in the picture. Plain close-fitting dark
clothing of very good quality, ink-black and dark olive, no ornament.

Where each is looking: the fair-haired boy past the left edge of the
frame at nothing, at ground level; the dark-haired boy down at his own
feet; the girl at her own open hand, not up. None of them at the
viewer. Nobody else is in the court.

Preserve all three exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. Do not mix
their clothing: the good cloth belongs only to the fair-haired boy, the
mended wool only to the dark-haired boy, the close dark clothing only
to the girl.
```

### PLATE: nine-strides

*Candidate 2.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 16 canon: *She set her blades against the base of a column and walked out to the middle of the stones and stood there.* Then, with her back to him: *"Nine strides," Valeria Vel said. / She had not turned around. / "You are too light to be one of mine," she said, to the empty court in front of her. "They come in at forty and they land on their heels. Stand up."* And what he does: *Kael stood up out of the gorse with his hands where she could see them.*

**HER BACK IS THE PICTURE AND IT IS PAGE-EXACT RATHER THAN A DODGE.** *She had not turned around* is stated twice in four lines. **So the plate answers with framing and invents nothing** — no part of her face is in frame, and the reason is the page's, not the folder's. It is the one composition in the book where THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE and the best staging are the same instruction.

**AND IT IS AN OVER-THE-SHOULDER, WHICH THIS FOLDER HAS NEVER BUILT.** Her back and shoulder near and large on one side, the court running away, and a boy standing up out of the scrub forty feet off. **The distance is the subject**, so it gets §Scale between figures rather than an adjective: the whole standing boy is stated as a fraction of something in the near frame, which is the instrument that fixed a boat that kept arriving too close.

**THE GROUND IS ONE CONTINUOUS PLANE AND THE BLOCK SAYS SO** (§Settings, as amended at s45) — the flagstones run unbroken from her feet to the scrub at the far edge, which is what stops a distant figure floating on bare paper. **It is also the chapter's own physics:** *it is one piece of ground with joints in it, and you are hitting one end of it.*

**HIS HANDS ARE UP BECAUSE HIS FATHER TAUGHT HIM THAT**, and the page says so in the same breath — open, empty, where she can see them. It is the one gesture in the picture and it reads at that distance.

**THE FULL-FIGURE SHEET IS ATTACHED AGAIN.** It cost a wide crop at `ch14/both-hands-full`, and **this plate wants a wide crop**, so the trade that hurt there is free here. Her grey should repeat from that plate rather than being authored a third time.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch16-nine-strides.png`, first run (author, s45: *adopt*).**

**THE OVER-THE-SHOULDER WORKED ON A FIRST RUN AND THE FOLDER HAD NEVER BUILT ONE.** Her back and the back of her head near and large, no part of her face in frame, and the whole court and its far edge legible past her. **The composition that THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE forced turned out to be the best staging available** — which is worth writing down, because the rule is usually described as a constraint and here it did the design.

**AND THE DISTANCE LANDED WITHOUT AN ADJECTIVE ANYWHERE NEAR IT.** *The whole standing boy is about as tall in the frame as the back of the girl's head is wide* — he came back smaller than that, so the court reads even deeper than specified. **§Scale between figures again: state the fraction, get the depth.** The same instrument that fixed a boat which kept arriving too close.

**THE GROUNDING CLAUSE IS NOW THREE FOR THREE.** *The flagstones run away unbroken from her feet to the far edge of the court* — and the plane came back continuous, with a small figure standing in it rather than floating on it. Written into §The crop rule at s45 off the two boys who floated; it has held every time it has been asked since.

**AND THE SMALL THINGS ALL ARRIVED.** Two wooden practice swords leaning against the base of a column, held by nobody. Both his hands out and open and forward, holding nothing — his father's lesson, which the page names in the same breath. The colonnade on three sides, the scrub crowding the fourth, the sea beyond in pale wash, the low sun the only warm thing and the lit tops of the flagstones reserved as bare paper. Nobody else anywhere in the court.

**ONE DRIFT, NOT WORTH A RUN.** Her hair is half drawn back and tied rather than fully loose, where `../canon-brief.md` has it *worn loose, well past the shoulders, no ornament.* It is hair holding hair rather than a band, so it sits on the line; recorded rather than corrected, and the plate stands.

```
Image 1: valeria-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.
Image 2: valeria-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl.
Image 3: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: an old open flagstone court at the seaward edge of a temple,
early on an autumn morning, as loose wash suggestion — broken
colonnade on three sides, gorse and low scrub crowding the fourth,
the sea beyond going into pale wash. Low early sunlight coming across
the court from behind the scrub, and it is the only light in the
picture.

Composition: seen from behind the girl and slightly above her
shoulder. She stands in the near foreground with her back squarely to
the viewer, cropped at the waist by the bottom edge, filling the right
of the frame; the back of her head and her shoulders are in frame and
no part of her face is. The flagstones run away unbroken from her feet
to the far edge of the court, and at that far edge, small in the
frame, a boy stands upright at the top of the scrub. Two plain wooden
practice swords lean against the base of a column at the left, held
by nobody.

THE DISTANCE BETWEEN THEM IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE, AND IT IS
STATED AS A MEASUREMENT: the whole standing boy, head to foot, is
about as tall in the frame as the back of the girl's head is wide. If
he is bigger than that, the distance is wrong.

THE BOY HAS JUST STOOD UP AND HIS HANDS ARE WHERE SHE CAN SEE THEM:
both arms out from his body and lifted a little, palms open and
forward, holding nothing. He stands square to her, not moving.

THE GIRL HAS NOT TURNED AROUND. Her head is level and faces away
across the court, her shoulders square and still, her weight even.
Nothing about her is turned toward him.

Her black hair is well past the shoulders, worn loose, no ornament.
She wears plain grey cloth, close, exactly made and austere, with no
badge and no decoration. The boy wears plain undyed working wool,
coarse and much mended. The grey belongs only to the girl and the
mended wool only to the boy.

THE PICTURE IS COLD AND THE WARM IN IT IS NAMED: the low sun on the
tops of the flagstones and on the boy's outline is the only warm
colour, and the colonnade, the scrub, the sea, the wooden swords and
all the clothing are cool grey and grey-blue with no brown in them.

THE BACK OF THE GIRL AND THE SMALL STANDING BOY ARE THE TWO FINISHED
THINGS IN THE PICTURE, and the sharpest step from light to dark
happens along her shoulder. The colonnade, the scrub and the sea stay
at quiet middle values with soft edges and no detail. The lit tops of
the flagstones are reserved as bare unpainted paper. The ink linework
stays the strongest element and does not blur into the wash.

There is nobody else in the picture: no third person anywhere in the
court, in the colonnade or at the edge of the scrub.
```

```
Setting: a disused cistern court at the end of a winter afternoon, as
loose wash suggestion — old brick walls close behind them, warm dark
grey-brown with moss in the joints, the corners going into shade. The
last grey daylight comes in low from the open side of the court, soft,
with about half an hour of it left.

floor. The dark-haired boy sits on a plank bench against the wall at
the right, nearest, in frame from the chest up, his face turned up and
across to the fair-haired boy. The fair-haired boy stands left of
centre, near, turned to him, one hand half lifted and open toward the
bench, the other at his side. Half a step further back, the small girl
leans against the wall between them with her arms folded, and the
long-haired girl stands at the left with a blade in one hand, point
down, and a cloth in the other held against the flat of it. The bottom
edge crosses the three standing figures at the belt. Nobody is looking
at the viewer.

The dark-haired boy has just asked a question and the three have
turned to look at him. Nothing else is happening; no blade is raised.

THE FAIR-HAIRED BOY'S FACE IS THE SUBJECT: he does not understand the
question and there is no contempt in him anywhere — lips parted on the
first word of an answer, brows drawn a little together, the eyes open
and puzzled on the boy on the bench. Not amused, not sneering, not
smug. Good dark wool, well cut, in deep umber and warm charcoal, an
open collar over a clean white undershirt, no ornament.

The dark-haired boy waits with his mouth closed, his face plain and
giving nothing away, a healing split in his lower lip and a green
bruise under the jaw. Undyed working wool, coarse and much mended, in
dull oatmeal and dead olive.

The small girl's head has come up; her attention is entirely on the
boy on the bench, her mouth closed, her brows still, giving nothing
back. Plain close-fitting dark clothing of very good quality, a high
buttoned collar, in ink-black and dark olive, no ornament.

The long-haired girl has stopped cleaning the blade. Her head is
turned to the viewer's right, toward the bench, and her eyes are on
the seated boy across the court and nowhere else — not at the viewer.
Her face gives nothing away. Her clothing as in her full-figure
reference: the charcoal sleeveless coat over the grey-blue underlayer,
the steel vambraces.

Preserve all four exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. Do not mix
their clothing: the steel belongs only to the long-haired girl and no
one else wears metal; the white collar and the umber wool belong only
to the fair-haired boy; the small girl and the dark-haired boy wear no
metal, no white and no ornament.
```

### PLATE: her-own-hands

*New at s47.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`. Ch. 17 canon: *Valeria Vel stood in her doorway with a blade in each hand and looked at him for what felt like a considerable portion of his life.* … *She looked at the blades in her hands. / "Yes," Valeria said. "All right." / And her face, for about a quarter of a second, did something he could not read at all, and if he had been able to read it he would have seen a girl who had just surprised herself so badly that she had to look at her own hands to make sure they were still hers.*

**THE PAGE HANDS OVER THE EXPRESSION HE COULD NOT READ**, so the plate may draw it: the narrator's description, not invention. A chest-up in a doorway is the register's shape exactly, with both hands full at the bottom edge — her trait, and not a hand crop: the face is the subject and the hands are what it is looking at. Her scars stay out: below a hand's size, and under the vambraces.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch17-her-own-hands.png`, first run (author, s47: *amazing* · adopt).**

The doorway as jamb and lintel with the room in shadow behind her. **Her whole kit off the full-figure sheet with not a word of it authored** — the coat and its buttons, the gorget, the collar insignia, the vambraces, the belt — the second plate to attach both of her and the second to get the costume back. Head tipped down, eyes on her own hands, hair loose, cool stone, warm skin, nobody else.

**WHAT DRIFTED.** The face is pensive rather than surprised: the brows came back level and the lids low, where the block asked for brows lifted a fraction and eyes a little wide. It reads as a girl looking at her hands and thinking — a face Kael could not read, which is what the page says. She reads sixteen or so; the anchor. The hair carries the half-tie at the back again, as at `ch16/nine-strides`.

```
and the lintel implied close around her, the room behind her falling

She is looking down at her own hands, and her face is the subject of
the picture: she has just surprised herself and is looking at her
hands to make sure they are still hers — the eyes down and a little
wide, the brows lifted a fraction, the lips barely parted, the whole
face still. Not frightened, not sad, not smiling; the surprise is at

Her black hair is loose, well past the shoulders, no ornament. Her
clothing and her two swords as in her full-figure reference: the
charcoal sleeveless coat over the grey-blue underlayer, the high
collar, the steel vambraces on both forearms; the two swords a matched

The stone of the doorway is cool grey; the coat charcoal, the
underlayer grey-blue, the vambraces dull steel; her skin is the warm

behind.

Preserve her exactly as shown in her reference images — face, hair,
colouring, apparent age; the two images are the same girl and there
```

# FILE: plates/ch18.md

# Ch. 18 — The Entry · plate sheet

**Composed at s48, on the author's delegation (*you choose*), from the scene: the chapter had no `candidates.md` entry before this session.** Kael is twelve on this page and the page says so — *"You are twelve years old," Severin said.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, and `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the second for the body below the collar.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S TWO HALVES.** The name arriving on a wall in a public hand, and what the name puts on the road behind him the same evening. One is a face in a gap in the rain; the other is the only picture in the set with real depth stacked vertically, and the two share nothing — not the weather, not the crop, not the number of figures, not the light.

**THE JUDGE IS BLOCKED, AND IT IS THE SAME BLOCK AS OSS.** The rail above the yard is the chapter's best two-hander — *"Congratulations," the judge said, and put his own hand out. / Kael looked at it.* — and there is no `images/portraits/halden.png`. A plate would author his face and it would become his reference by default (`ch13.md`, s45). **The work that unblocks it is a portrait, not a plate.** The one legal version — Kael near, his face down on the offered hand, the judge a wool shoulder and forearm at the frame edge with his head above the top of it — is a picture built around a missing file, and the chapter has two better moments that are not. Recorded on `candidates.md` so the next session does not re-propose the plate first.

**THE STUDY IS PASSED OVER AND THE REASON IS THE SET, NOT THE MOMENT.** *"Nothing," said Severin* is the chapter's argument, both faces are anchored, and the room's furniture is already on a page — `ch10/the-honey-and-the-bread` put the fern and the jar there specifically to establish the room Ch. 18 comes back to. **A second study interior would be the set's fourth close two-figure room in nine chapters** (honey, the law on the table, who lives in it), and the pair above buys more ground. It stays candidate 3 and it is fully composable the day it is wanted.

**A DOCUMENT IS THE FIRST PLATE'S OBJECT, AND §LETTERING FORBIDS DRAWING IT.** *One short word, capitals, named exactly … Not a page.* Two ruled sheets of four hundred names is exactly a page, so the list can only be texture: pinned paper at a raking angle, ruled columns, no legible word anywhere. **The chapter loses nothing by it** — the reader knows what is on the sheet, and the picture is the face reading it, which is what the chapter is about. The one real cost is that the second column cannot be shown, so the plate takes the breath before it instead.

**BOTH BLOCKS WRITE THE AGE IN AND NEITHER SAYS *apparent age*.** The standing anchor ruling (s44) is that age is written into the block or the picture will be fourteen, so the closing preserve clause carries face, hair and colouring only; carrying *apparent age* as well, as the Ch. 17 blocks did, tells the tool two things at once. **Neither block stages a ratio**: nothing in the first crop can perform one — his hands are down and there is no second body in frame — and the second one's ratio is spent on distance, which is that picture's subject. The rung is doing this alone on plate 1, and that is the test.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: caught-it-halfway

*Candidate 1, the chapter's pick.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 18 canon: *Kael came in off the road with his boots wet to the ankle and the rain still in his hair, and the first court was solid at the colonnade, three deep* … *the pillar stood off the wall by the width of a hand and a small boy could get his shoulder into the gap and read sideways, and he read the list sideways, in the grey light, over the top of somebody's collar.* And the subject: *And for about the space of one breath, before anything else got there, he was happy.* … *He felt his own face start to do something. He caught it about halfway and put it back.*

**THE SUBJECT IS AN EXPRESSION IN THE ACT OF BEING STOPPED**, which is the one thing this book has that no other picture in the folder has drawn: every other Kael plate is a face already closed. The page hands over both halves — the thing that started and the catching of it — so the plate draws a beginning, not a state. **§Gaze's rule does the work: point at the mouth, not the abstraction.** *Halfway* is not renderable and *the lips a little apart, one corner begun, the brows up, and the rest of the face not moved* is. The risk is a plain smile coming back, which would be the wrong picture entirely — a happy boy at a list is not this chapter — so the arrival is banned by name in the same paragraph, on §Absence's pull test.

**THE CROWD IS ONE SHOULDER AND THE PAGE PUT IT THERE.** *over the top of somebody's collar* — a stranger's dark collar at the near edge, cut by the frame above it, no head and no face. That is the whole of the three-deep court, and it does the job a crowd of drawn faces would fight the subject for.

**THE PAPER IS THE RIVAL AND IT IS DEMOTED ON BOTH CHANNELS.** §The winner and the rival: pale paper beside a face is a real competitor for the eye, so the sheets are held at quiet middle values with soft edges and the sharpest step from light to dark is sited on the face. This is the first block in the folder where the rival is the thing the scene is nominally about.

**RUN 1 (s48) — author: *i like it a lot*, and a re-run taken anyway.** The run stands as a fallback; it is not rejected.

**WHAT LANDED.** The pinned sheets at the frame's edge in ruled columns with **not one legible mark** — the §Lettering constraint came back as the picture's best texture. The rain off the colonnade roof reserved as bare paper, the arcade going into shade behind it. Wet hair pushed off the forehead, the rain still on the face, the shoulders dark with it. The stranger's shoulder at the near edge, blurred, cut above the collar, no second face anywhere. **The rival demoted on both channels exactly as written** — the paper at quiet middle values with soft edges and the sharpest step from light to dark on the face. Grey eyes the lightest value in it.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND THE BLOCK CAUSED IT.** The sheets are at one edge and his eyes are cut hard to the other, at something out of frame; the *not at the viewer* ban held and the object of the gaze did not. **The block steered the body with absolute directions** — *turned away to the viewer's left … head turned back to the right* — and the tool mirrored the arrangement whole instead of reading it as a chain. **So the fix is to take every left and right out of the block** and site the paper, the head and the eye-line relative to one another: the sheets at the edge on the side his face is turned toward, the stranger's shoulder at the opposite edge, and the eyes named onto the paper.

**AND THE EXPRESSION CAME BACK WARY RATHER THAN CAUGHT.** Lips parted, brows level, eyes wide — a face in the middle of something, but the something reads as a boy who has just been seen. **`one corner of the mouth has begun to lift` is an abstraction wearing anatomy's clothes**, which is the *level* and *flat* failure one layer down: a smile that has started lifts the **cheek under the eye**, and without that companion the mouth alone does not read as a beginning. Run 2 writes both, ties the asymmetry to the paper rather than to a side of the frame, and bans the wary face by name on §Absence's pull test, which run 1 has now earned.

**THE AGE TEST HAS ITS ANSWER: THE RUNG ALONE DOES NOT PULL IT DOWN.** He reads fourteen or fifteen off *The boy is twelve years old* with no ratio staged — as the sheet predicted it might, and one more entry under the anchor ruling. Left as it is; nothing in this crop can perform a ratio. **The hand's-width gap did not render either** — he reads as pressed to a pillar, which contradicts nothing on the page and loses *reading sideways*. Not chased: it costs the picture nothing a viewer can see.

**RUN 2 CHANGES TWO PARAGRAPHS AND NOTHING ELSE**, so it is a clean test of both fixes at once.

**RUN 2 (s48) — NOT ADOPTED.** Two paragraphs changed and nothing else. **The sheets came forward and read unmistakably as a posted document**, which is the one thing it improved. **Neither fix landed.** The gaze went the same way again — paper at one edge, eyes cut hard to the other — so relational siting (*the edge on the side his face is turned toward*) binds the two no better than absolute left and right did. **The tool places the object and the head independently, and twice now no wording has coupled them**; the only remaining fix is geometry — the wall he is reading put in front of his face, viewer behind his shoulder — and that costs the face, which is the subject. So it is a wall, not a clause. The smile did not arrive either: the cheek stayed down and the brows level, and the anatomy rewrite, which was the right instrument, did not move the face. **AND IT BROKE THE ONE THING RUN 1 HAD:** enlarging the paper pushed it past *no legible word* into rendered characters, in a script that is not this world's. **§Lettering's *Not a page* has a scale term nobody had written down — a pale ruled sheet stays illegible while it is small and renders glyphs as it grows.** Both findings stay on this sheet under §Findings are not automatically rules; if either recurs on another plate they go to `../prompt-system.md`.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch18-caught-it-halfway.png`, RUN 1 (author, s48: *i like it a lot* · *sure adopt run 1*).** Run 2 buys a better document and pays for it with a wrong alphabet; run 1 has the cleaner paper and the stronger face, and neither has the caught smile. **THE BLOCK BELOW IS VERBATIM AS RUN 1**, style header and all, and it is `PLATE-VERBATIM` because the run-2 edits (`fa9f302`) and a leaning-out pass made after run 2 both changed a block that had already run. Both are reverted here and neither produced a picture; `git log -p` has them. **The second plate keeps its leaner text**, which has never run.

**AND THE LEANING-OUT PASS PROMOTED A RULE THAT THE AUTHOR THEN STRUCK, WHICH IS THE MORE USEFUL RECORD.** The s48 note was *i'm already seeing you kinda drift from the simplicity of the ch17 prompts … a lot of times less is more*, and the promotion written from it told a session to measure a new block against the nearest adopted plate of the same figure count. **The author struck it the same session:** *cutting for cutting's sake is not the goal. I just want to follow good prompts, principles, non redundancy, optimized. If it turns out a bit bigger sometimes, well that's that.* A word count is a proxy, and a proxy becomes the target. **§Before the run already owned this** — *each fact once, in the place it belongs* — and every clause the pass actually cut was a violation of that sentence and not of a length: legibility named twice, the wet shoulders in two paragraphs, an ink-linework clause the style header already carries, three bans where one has a pull. **So the failure was not applying the rule, not the absence of one**, and what `../prompt-system.md` carries now is only the author's ruling that a long block earns it clause by clause.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the colonnade of a temple school on a wet grey morning, as
loose wash suggestion — a stone pillar standing a hand's width off a
stone wall, close behind him and beside him, the depth of the
colonnade falling away into shadow. Behind him, rain comes off the
roof in a straight falling line. Flat grey daylight from the open
side, no sun and no hard shadow.

Composition: chest-up, one figure. The boy is twelve years old. He
stands sideways in the narrow gap between the pillar and the wall
with his near shoulder wedged into it and his cheek close to the
stone, his body turned away to the viewer's left and his head turned
back to the right. The bottom edge crosses him at the chest, below
the collarbone, so no hands and no arms are in the frame at all. At
the right edge of the frame, pinned flat to the wall, two sheets of
pale paper seen at a steep raking angle, ruled into faint vertical
columns; the writing on them is a grey suggestion of marks and no
word anywhere on them is legible. There is no lettering of any kind
anywhere in the picture. At the near left edge, the dark shoulder and
collar of a stranger standing in front of him, blurred and out of
focus and cut off by the edge of the frame above the collar, so that
no head and no second face appears anywhere.

THE FACE IS THE SUBJECT AND IT IS AN EXPRESSION CAUGHT HALFWAY.
Something has just started in it and been stopped: the lips have come
a little apart, one corner of the mouth has begun to lift, the brows
are up, the eyes are wide and bright and fixed. The rest of the face
has not moved and is already going still again. The smile has not
arrived — he is not smiling, not grinning, not laughing. Not
frightened, not sad, not blank.

His hair is wet and pushed off his forehead, the rain still on his
face, his shoulders dark with it.

He is looking at the pinned sheets at the right edge of the frame and
nowhere else — not at the viewer, and his head does not turn toward
the viewer.

Undyed coarse working wool, much mended, the collar loose at the
throat, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, wet through and darker at the
shoulders. No ornament, no metal, no badge and nothing carried.

The stone is cool grey and the falling rain behind him is reserved as
bare unpainted paper. HIS FACE CARRIES THE SHARPEST STEP FROM LIGHT
TO DARK IN THE PICTURE and is the most finished thing in it; the
pinned sheets, the stranger's shoulder and the colonnade behind stay
at quiet middle values with soft edges and no detail, and the paper
does not compete with the face for finish. The ink linework stays the
strongest element throughout.

Preserve him exactly as shown in his reference image — face, hair,
colouring. Pale grey eyes, the lightest value in his face. Dark hair
cut short and uneven. One figure in the picture.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-man-at-the-second-bend

*Candidate 2, and the pair's second half.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`. Ch. 18 canon: *At the bend above the village the road gave you two hundred strides of the way you had come.* … *There was a man on it at about a hundred and fifty, walking.* … *the bank gave out into scree, a long tilted field of loose grey stone* … *He went up eleven feet of bank on the outsides of his feet and lay down in the stones.* … *The man came along at the same unhurried pace he had kept for two miles and went by twenty feet under him: wool cap, wool coat, a man about his father's build and nothing whatever like his father's carriage, no badge, no colour, nothing on him anywhere that meant a single thing. Kael never saw the face and did not need it.*

**THE FACELESS FIGURE IS THE PAGE'S OWN AND NOT A WORKAROUND.** *Kael never saw the face* — this is the one moment in the book where the two bans §Plates prescribes are not the plate hiding something but the chapter's plainest fact. It is also why this is the second plate and the rail is not: the same absence that blocks Halden is, here, the subject.

**IT IS THE SET'S FIRST VERTICAL DEPTH STAGING**, and it is built on §Light's recipe for depth exactly — where the viewer stands, said first; the frame stacked bottom to top; the distance pinned with a ratio. The ratio is the picture's whole content: **a hand's length of man, twenty feet under a boy's face that fills the near corner.** Every other plate in the folder is one plane or two.

**NOTHING IS HAPPENING, WHICH HAS TO BE SAID.** A hillside, loose stone and a man walking below a hidden boy pulls hard toward a chase or an ambush, and the page is the opposite: *The man did not slow, and did not look up, and did not come back.* The stillness is the achievement, so the block states the absence of action outright, as the template prescribes where the subject implies violence.

**THE FACE CARRIES THE SPLIT AND IT IS WRITTEN AS A CONTRADICTION** (§Gaze: state a contradiction as one). *The first half arrived at once and it was very nearly joy* … *it was enormous, and it was fun.* … *The other half came in underneath it about ten strides later and was the size of the mountain.* Bright steady eyes and a mouth closed hard: good at this, glad of it, and carrying something heavy underneath — and no grin, because the chapter says he was ashamed of about half of it for years afterward.

**NO WRAP AND NO BLADES.** The page does not put them on him anywhere in this chapter and `../canon-brief.md` says his full-figure sheet carries neither; where the page is silent the plate answers with framing, so the block says he carries nothing rather than deciding it.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch18-the-man-at-the-second-bend.png`, RUN 1 (author, s48: *adopt*).**

**WHAT LANDED, AND IT IS THE WHOLE RECIPE.** §Light's depth clause had no specimen in this folder and now has one: the frame stacked as written — a strip of grey sky, the bracken bank, the road across the upper third with the man on it, the scree falling away, the boy near and large along the bottom — **on the first run.** The ratio carried the twenty feet: a hand's length of man against a head that fills the near corner. The man's two bans both held, from above and behind, wool cap and coat, no badge, no colour, nothing carried, and the head not turned. Flat overcast with no hard shadow, the wet mud road, lichen in the grey stone, the skin the one warm thing, one hand flat on the stone by his chin, and nobody else on the road or the hill.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND THE BLOCK CAUSED IT GEOMETRICALLY.** His eyes are on the viewer. The block put the camera *above the boy and a little behind him* and then sent his eyes *up the slope to the road* — **the same direction**, so *not at the viewer* had nothing to push against. **This is a different fault from the first plate's**: there the wording failed to couple an object and a sightline, here the wording was fine and the staging put the object behind the lens. **A gaze ban is only as good as the angle between the object and the camera**, which nothing in §Gaze says yet; it stays a finding on this sheet until a second plate hits it.

*The unspent fix, recorded and not run: the eyes raised up the slope to the man, the irises sitting high with the whites showing along the lower lids, the head still down and the cheek near the stone — anatomy rather than a ban, the lever that worked on `ch17/her-own-hands`.*

He reads mid-teens again, which is the anchor, and his wool reads grey rather than the named oatmeal and olive — the full-figure reference carrying its own cloth over the block's hues, as it did on both of Valeria's plates.

**AND THE BLOCK WAS LEFT UNTOUCHED UNTIL THE AUTHOR RULED**, so it is `PLATE-VERBATIM` with no revert behind it. That is the practice the first plate's churn bought: **propose a re-run in the record, in italics, and change the block only on the word.**

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same boy, for his build and his clothing.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a bare hillside above a fishing village at the end of a wet
afternoon, as loose wash suggestion — a long tilted field of loose
grey stone, and below it a narrow wet mud road cutting across the
slope. Flat grey overcast light, no sun and no hard shadow, the sky
low and coming on to rain.

Composition: real depth, seen from up the slope. The viewer stands on
the loose stone above the boy and a little behind him, looking down
across the stone to the road. The frame stacks from bottom to top:
nearest and largest, filling the bottom third, the boy lying flat on
his front along the slope, his cheek almost on the stones, his face
in three-quarter view seen from above, one hand flat on the stone
near his chin; then the tilted stone field falling away; then, across
the upper third, the road, and on it one grown man walking away along
it; then the far bank and a strip of low grey sky. The boy is twelve
years old.

SCALE — the distance is the picture: the walking man is no taller in
the frame than the boy's hand is long, and the boy's head alone is
wider in the frame than the man is tall.

Nothing is happening. The man walks on at an even unhurried pace and
the boy lies still and watches him go by below. Nobody is running,
nothing is drawn or raised, and nothing in either body says a chase.

THE MAN IS SEEN FROM ABOVE AND BEHIND: a brown-grey wool cap and
heavy wool coat, worn, the build of a working man, no badge and no
colour on him anywhere, nothing carried. His face is not visible
anywhere in the picture and he does not turn his head.

THE BOY'S FACE IS THE SUBJECT AND IT IS DOING TWO THINGS AT ONCE. The
eyes are wide open, bright and completely steady, tracking the man
down on the road; the mouth is closed and firm; the face is still and
the body is stiller. He is very good at this and glad of it, and
underneath that he has just understood something heavy. Not
frightened, not grinning, not blank. He is looking at the walking man
and nowhere else, not at the viewer.

His clothing as in his full-figure reference. He carries nothing and
nothing is strapped to his back.

The stone field is cool grey with green-grey lichen in it, the road
pale wet mud, the far bank dull olive and bracken brown, the man's
cap and coat the same brown-grey as the hillside. The boy's skin is
the one warm thing in the picture. His face and the hand beside it
carry the sharpest step from light to dark; the road, the man and the
bank stay at quiet middle values with soft edges.

There is nobody else on the road and nobody else on the hillside: two
figures only, no other walker, no house and no animal in the frame.

Preserve him exactly as shown in his reference images — face, hair,
colouring; the two images are the same boy and there is one boy in
the picture. Pale grey eyes.
```

# FILE: plates/ch19.md

# Ch. 19 — The Winter · plate sheet

**Composed at s48, on the author's delegation (*you choose*), from the scene; the pair changed shape mid-session when the first plate was rejected at design.** Kael is twelve; Neris is three months along and it is the night she says so. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` and `images/portraits/neris.png` on the adopted plate, `images/portraits/vask.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE HOUSE'S ONE WARM MINUTE AND THE FORTY YEARS MISSING OUT OF THE MIDDLE OF IT.** A boy who asks whether he will be allowed to hold the baby, and a man who lays his forearms on a table and spends three sentences on a life. Neither is at the council table.

**AND THE COUNCIL TABLE IS WHY THE CHAPTER'S LARGEST MOMENT IS NOT HERE.** The parents disagreeing in front of him for the first time in his life — *"It may not be them." / "It is them."* — is the biggest fact in the chapter and it is three people at that table by that light, which is `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` exactly: same room, same boards, same three, hands flat. **So the pair is built to go nowhere near it**, and neither block below shares the light, the crop or the cast of that plate: fire rather than a lamp, and two faces close or one face across a table rather than three at a waist-up.

**A REAL COLLISION, AND IT IS RULED IN THE PLATE RULE'S FAVOUR.** `../canon-brief.md` says Neris's hands go in frame and occupied in any scene — *her hands are where the book keeps her* — and the chapter gives her exactly one occupied gesture, *her palm flat on a bar that was already down*, which belongs to a different beat and is in any case the composition `ch10` rejected as a near-duplicate of `ch02-the-doorframe` (same woman, same door, same hand). At the moment the adopted plate draws, the page puts nothing in her hands. **THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE says a silent page is answered with framing and never with invention**, so her hands are out of the frame and the block says they are, rather than inventing a gesture to satisfy a note about how she is drawn. The boy's hands stay in, flat on the boards, because this chapter puts them there in its own words. **The fence is not weakened**: it governs a full figure, which this is not.

**THE PREGNANCY IS DECIDED AND NOT DEFAULTED** (`../canon-brief.md` requires the choice): three months, nothing showing, and the crop has no waist in it — so no clause is spent on it at all.

---

### REJECTED AT DESIGN: the-third-board

**REJECTED BEFORE IT RAN (author, s48: *the third board is not good, i dont like just a boot and kael at the end, does not move me*).** Not an execution note and not a fence — the moment is legal, the instruments were right, and it does not move him. **The block is deleted rather than kept as a variant.** `ch12/the-hole-through-the-tree` was kept because it had run and its picture had to stay explainable; this one has no picture to explain, and `git log -p` has the text. **Do not re-propose it.**

**WHAT THE REJECTION SAYS ABOUT THE SET IS WORTH MORE THAN THE PLATE WAS.** The composition put the object near and the only person far — a boot and a line of iron in the near field, the face at the other side of the room. **Every plate this folder has adopted does the reverse**: the face is near, and the object is the thing that face is doing something about. §The winner and the rival can make a small thing win a frame; winning the frame is not the same as being worth it. *An object plate needs a face at the object's own distance* — a finding, not a rule, and the candidate to test it against next is `ch16/the-hand-on-the-door`, held since s45 for the same reason.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: we-were-owned

*Candidate 4, taken at s48 when candidate 1 was rejected.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 19 canon: *Now he put both forearms on the table, the way he did in the yard when he was going to explain a thing once, and the tally-marks came up out of the sleeve and lay there where they had lain in front of Kael for twelve years.* … *"Three sentences," Vask said. "They are not the history. You will not have the history from me tonight or on any night I can see from here. You are going to hate me for how small they are. Listen anyway."* … *"We were owned. We stopped being owned. People who own things do not forget."*

**THE VIEWER SITS WHERE KAEL SITS AND THE BOY IS NOT IN THE PICTURE** — `ch10/the-waterer`'s device, and the reason it is right here is that the page says these marks *had lain in front of Kael for twelve years*: the plate puts them in front of the reader on the same terms. **It also means the man is looking straight out of the picture, which is the one composition where a gaze at the viewer is the design and not the drift** that cost three runs on Ch. 18.

**THE MARKS ARE THE SECOND SHOWING AND THE FIRST DELIBERATE ONE.** `ch03-the-back` spent them as something a son reads off a father's body without permission; here the father lays them on a table on purpose, in the same gesture he uses in the yard when a thing gets explained once. Same marks, opposite transaction. `ch03.md` called itself the only moment that needed them *so far*, and this is the one it was leaving room for.

**AND IT KEEPS CLEAR OF `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` ON EVERY AXIS THAT PLATE OWNS:** one figure rather than three, firelight rather than the oil lamp, forearms rather than four flat hands, and no boy's hands in the frame at all — which is the exact echo `ch12` was built on.

**PRICED: BOTH PLATES ARE THE SAME ROOM ON THE SAME NIGHT**, which `ch10.md` would have split if the chapter gave two settings. This one does not — it is one room from the first line to the last — so the distance is bought on cast, crop and camera instead: two faces close by the fire, and one face across a table with the forearms in the near field.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch19-we-were-owned.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *adopt*).**

**WHAT LANDED.** The whole composition on the first run: both forearms flat on the boards with the sleeves pushed back, the hands loose and open and holding nothing, the arms running across the lower half nearest the viewer and the face above them. **The marks read as a tally** — a dozen and more, one length, one slant, on both forearms — and they hold the near field without being shouted at. Mouth open mid-word, the eyes steady straight out of the picture at the son who is not in it. Fire low and warm from one side, the wall black, mended coarse wool, no metal anywhere. **And the gaze at the viewer is the design here and behaved like one**: the fault that cost three runs on Ch. 18 is an asset the moment the viewer is standing in for somebody.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND BOTH ARE THE REFERENCE BEATING THE BLOCK.** No grey at the temples — `images/portraits/vask.png` carries none, which was ruled correct at s43 and not re-cut, so *scattered thinly at the temples* was never going to win. And the face reads less weathered than *a face like used tools*: handsome, mid-thirties, lightly worn. **Same pattern as `ch17/her-own-hands` returning its costume off the full-figure sheet — in a close crop the reference wins, and a clause that argues with it is spent breath.** The next block that wants weather on this face has to get it from a new reference, not from words.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the man.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the single room of a mountain house late at night, as loose
wash suggestion — a plank wall behind him, the room falling into deep
dark. A low fire out of frame at one side is the only light in the
picture: it comes in low and warm across the tabletop and the near
side of his face, and leaves the wall and the corners black.

Composition: the viewer sits at the near side of a plank table, close
to it. Across the table the man has laid both forearms flat on the
boards, sleeves pushed back to the elbow, the hands loose and open and
holding nothing. The forearms run across the lower half of the frame,
nearest the viewer; his face is above them, chest-up. Nobody else is
in the picture and no part of anybody else is in the frame.

THE TALLY-MARKS ON HIS FOREARMS ARE THE SUBJECT AND THEY LIE IN THE
OPEN. Short pale scars on the inside of both forearms, a dozen or
more, every one the same length and every one at the same slant, old
and flat and lighter than the weathered skin around them. They carry
the sharpest step from light to dark in the picture and are the most
finished thing in it; the table, the wall and the room stay at quiet
middle values with soft edges and no detail.

He is saying something short that he knows is too small for what it
is standing in for: mouth open mid-word, and nothing else in the face
moving. Level, unhurried, the brows unmoved, the eyes empty and
steady — not angry, not cold, not kind, not sad, not ashamed.

HE IS LOOKING DIRECTLY OUT OF THE PICTURE AT THE VIEWER, at his son,
and his are the only eyes in the frame.

Iron-dark hair of medium length with grey scattered thinly through it
at the temples — scattered, with no streak, seam or band of white
anywhere. A heavily weathered face. Coarse undyed working wool, much
mended, closed at the throat, the sleeves pushed back; no ornament and
no metal anywhere on him.

Preserve him exactly as shown in his reference image — face, hair,
colouring. Dark eyes.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: will-i-be-allowed-to-hold-it

*Candidate 2.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`. Ch. 19 canon: *"I'm going to have a brother. Or a sister." / "One or the other," his mother agreed.* … *"Will I be allowed to hold it." / He heard it come out of his own mouth and could not think where it had come from. It was not a count and it priced nothing and nobody in that room needed it, and he was twelve years old … and he had asked it in the voice he had asked things in when he was five.* … *His mother looked at him for some time. / "Yes."* … *Something came up into her face that had not been anywhere in it since he came through the door, and stood there a moment, and went.*

**THE PAGE HANDS OVER THE EXPRESSION, SO THE PLATE MAY DRAW IT** — the same licence as `ch17/her-own-hands`. What it may not do is let it arrive as warmth in general: `../canon-brief.md` says *not warm, not maternal, not soft*, and the page says something came up **and went**. So it is written as anatomy and as an easing rather than a smile, on a face that is otherwise still.

**THE EYE CONTACT IS THE SUBJECT** (§Gaze), and it is also this plate's insurance: two people looking at each other cannot send a gaze to the viewer, which is the fault that took two runs off `ch18/caught-it-halfway` and one off `ch18/the-man-at-the-second-bend`.

**IT IS THE ONLY PLATE IN THE BOOK WHERE KAEL'S FACE IS OPEN.** Every other one gives nothing away by design — the coat. Here the page takes it off him for one line, in his own house, and the block asks for the face of a much younger boy on a twelve-year-old.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch19-will-i-be-allowed-to-hold-it.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *great!*).**

**WHAT LANDED.** The eye contact, named as the subject and delivered on the first run — his eyes up, hers down, the lines meeting, and **nobody at the viewer**, which is the first plate this session to get a gaze right the first time. Two faces close and warm-lit off a fire out of frame, the plank wall black behind them, the table edge across the bottom and her hands out of it. **The boy's face open** — brows up, eyes wide, the mouth closed again after the question — which no other Kael plate in the book has. Both in mended undyed wool with the stitching drawn, no ornament and no metal on either.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT IS ONE NOTCH.** The easing in her face is fainter than the block asked for; she reads grave and attentive rather than a hard face with one thing moving in it. **Nothing was over-drawn, which was the risk** — no maternal softness arrived, and `../canon-brief.md`'s *not warm, not maternal, not soft* held. She reads early thirties, which is the reference and is ruled correct (s44).

**AND THE GAZE INSURANCE WORKED.** Two people looking at each other cannot look at the viewer — after three runs lost to that fault on Ch. 18, this is the cheapest fix the folder has: where the moment allows it, make the eye contact the subject.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: neris.png — portrait reference of the woman.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the single room of a mountain house late at night, as loose
wash suggestion — a plank wall behind them, the room falling into
deep dark. A low fire out of frame at one side is the only light: it
comes in low and warm across the near side of both faces and leaves
the far side of the room black.

Composition: chest-up, two figures, close. The boy of twelve sits at
the plank table at the lower part of the frame, his head tipped back
a little and turned up. The woman stands beside the table, close to
him, in frame from the chest up, her head tipped down to him. Their
faces are near each other and fill the upper half of the frame. The
bottom edge crosses the boy at the chest, and the woman's hands are
not in the frame.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER. His eyes up, hers down,
the lines of sight meeting. This eye contact is the subject of the
image. Neither of them is looking at the viewer.

THE BOY HAS JUST ASKED FOR SOMETHING, AND HIS FACE IS OPEN, which is
rare in him: the mouth closed again after the question, the brows
lifted a little, the eyes wide and waiting, nothing held back and
nothing guarded anywhere in the face. Not anxious, not pleading, not
smiling.

THE WOMAN HAS BEEN LOOKING AT HIM FOR SOME TIME AND SOMETHING HAS
JUST EASED IN HER FACE. The mouth stays closed and the face stays
still; the easing is at the outer corners of the eyes and along the
jaw only. It is not a smile and it is not tenderness on display — a
hard, watchful face with one thing moving in it.

Nothing else is happening. Nobody else is in the room. Both wear
plain undyed village working wool, coarse and mended, closed at the
throat, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no ornament and no metal
on either of them.

Warm firelight on the near side of both faces, the plank wall behind
them cool and dark. Their faces are the two most finished things in
the picture and carry the sharpest step from light to dark.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference image — faces,
hair, colouring. Do not blend their features. His eyes pale grey,
hers dark.
```

# FILE: plates/ch20.md

# Ch. 20 — The Word · plate sheet

**Composed at s48 from the scene.** Kael is twelve; Elarine twelve; Valeria twelve, and reading older off her anchor as she always does. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` on the first, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png` and `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S TWO COURTS AND ITS TWO REFUSALS OF THE SAME WORD.** A girl hands him the word and he cannot say it back; a girl tells him not to look for a word at all, and he uses a name instead and it is not answered. First light and last light, brick and water, and the same boy failing to price the same thing twice.

**THE CISTERN COURT IS ON ITS THIRD CHAPTER AND THE SECOND PLATE HAS TO PAY FOR IT.** `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick` and `ch17/who-lives-in-it` are both that court; this one is the same brick in the opposite light — twenty minutes before anybody has any reason to be there, the rain stopped in the night, the walls wet and cold. **The pair's other half is the antidote:** open water, dusk, and two people side by side who never once look at each other.

**AND THE SECOND BLOCK DELIBERATELY BREAKS THE REGISTER'S ONE CONSTANT.** *Faces near and engaged with each other* is the scene-test shape and every adopted plate in the folder obeys it. Ch. 20 ends with a word going out and not coming back, and **the picture of that is two profiles and no eye contact** — the page states it twice (*She was looking straight out at the water*). It is the folder's first two-shot where nobody looks at anybody, and it is written that way on purpose, not for variety.

**ONE DETAIL LEFT OUT ON PURPOSE.** *She had blood on one knuckle from something earlier and was not attending to it* — `ch17`'s candidate 5 is held because fresh blood on a girl who reads sixteen is the refusal pattern, and a knuckle is below a hand's size in frame and could not carry finish anyway. Nothing is lost; the risk is a run that produces no picture at all.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: you-are-my-friend

*Candidate 1.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 20 canon: *She was in the cistern court twenty minutes before anybody had any reason to be, sitting on the bench nobody sat on, with her satchel on her knees and her hands on the satchel.* … *Elarine Sedrane turned round on the bench and looked at him properly for the first time that morning.* … *"I am going to say something now, and you are going to do something strange with it, and I have decided in advance not to hold that against you."* … *"You are my friend," Elarine said. "It is not a complicated word. I have been using it about you privately since the summer, and I did not say it, because I could see exactly what you would do with it." Her chin came up a fraction.*

**HER FACE IS THE SUBJECT AND IT IS DOING SOMETHING NO OTHER PLATE OF HER HAS DRAWN.** `ch13/the-spots-of-colour` is her being spent in public without choosing it and `ch17/who-lives-in-it` is her attention with nothing given back. This is Elarine Sedrane choosing to spend herself, in an empty court, with the sentence already out of her mouth and her chin up a fraction — **and watching what he does with it, which is the assessment her sheet describes turned on the one person it has ever cost her anything to run.**

**THE CHEEK-TELL IS BANNED BY NAME, WHICH IT HAS EARNED HERE.** `../canon-brief.md` reserves the spots of colour to Ch. 13 and Ch. 17 and nowhere else, and a girl of twelve saying this to a boy is the hardest pull toward a blush in the book. §Absence's test is exactly that: ban what the picture pulls toward.

**AND HIS FACE IS THE CHAPTER'S ARGUMENT.** *He did not have the word* — so the block asks for nothing arriving: not moved, not pleased, not embarrassed, the attention full on her and the face doing nothing at all with what it has been handed. **The picture is a girl who has said it and a boy who has no word.**


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch20-you-are-my-friend.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *adopt*).**

**WHAT LANDED.** Both on the bench against wet brick with moss in the joints, flat cold morning light, nobody else in the court. She has turned round to face him squarely with the satchel set down beside her and her hands loose in her lap; his hands on his knees. Ink-dark hair bound severe, the high buttoned collar, fine dark cloth against his mended oatmeal wool — **two dark-ish figures and no garment bled.** **The eye contact held and nobody went to the viewer**, which is the third plate running for the named-eye-contact instrument. **And the cheek-tell stayed out**, which was the one ban this block spent and the hardest pull in the chapter.

**WHAT DRIFTED.** The crop came back a notch wider than *the bottom edge crosses them both at the lap* — it lands at the thigh, so both faces sit smaller than the folder's default close. **Third time a named landmark has come back one notch loose**, after `ch17/who-lives-in-it` twice at the belt: §The cut already owns the general fact that *chest-up* and *waist-up* come back wider, and the working correction is to name the landmark tighter than the line you want. **It stays a finding and not a rule** — twice of the three the looser crop was simply accepted, so the correction has not been wanted every time, which is §Findings' bar. Her chin reads level rather than up a fraction: the declaration is carried by the staging, not by the face.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the small girl with bound hair.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a disused cistern court very early on a winter morning, as
loose wash suggestion — old brick walls close behind them, cold
grey-brown, wet from rain that stopped in the night, moss in the
joints, the corners going into shade. Flat cold daylight from the
open side, no sun.

Composition: waist-up, two figures on a plank bench against the wall,
close together and near the viewer. The girl sits at the right and
has turned round on the bench to face the boy squarely, her satchel
set down on the bench beside her, her hands loose in her lap. The boy
sits at the left, facing along the bench, his hands on his knees. The
bottom edge crosses them both at the lap. They are twelve years old.
Nobody else is in the court.

THE GIRL HAS JUST SAID SOMETHING SHE DECIDED IN ADVANCE TO SAY AND IS
WATCHING WHAT HE DOES WITH IT. Her chin is up a fraction, the mouth
closed again after the sentence, the brows level, the eyes steady on
his face and giving nothing back. Not shy, not pleading, not smiling,
and no colour anywhere in her cheeks. Small and neat and finely
built; ink-dark hair bound severe with nothing loose; plain
close-fitting dark clothing of very good quality, a high buttoned
collar, ink-black and dark olive, no ornament of any kind.

THE BOY HAS BEEN HANDED SOMETHING HE CANNOT PRICE AND NOTHING IS
ARRIVING IN HIS FACE. The eyes on hers, the mouth closed, the brows
unmoved — attentive and entirely still. Not moved, not pleased, not
embarrassed, not blank. Undyed coarse working wool, much mended, dull
oatmeal and dead olive, no ornament and no metal.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER and neither is looking at
the viewer. This eye contact is the subject of the image.

Their faces are the most finished things in the picture; the brick,
the bench and the satchel stay at quiet middle values with soft
edges.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference image — faces,
hair, colouring. Do not blend their features. Do not mix their
clothing: the fine dark cloth and the high collar belong only to the
girl, the mended undyed wool only to the boy. Her eyes very dark
brown, his pale grey.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: val

*Candidate 2, and the chapter's last beat.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`. Ch. 20 canon: *She sat down on the stones beside him with her forearms across her knees.* … *"Then do not use a word," said Valeria Vel.* … *"Val," Kael said. / She did not answer. / It went on not being answered for long enough that he heard it going, the way you hear a stone going down a shaft and keep waiting after the sound should have come back.* … *the lamps came up along the water one at a time.*

**NO EYE CONTACT, AND THAT IS THE PICTURE.** The one word he had is out in the air between two people who are both looking at the sea. Every other adopted plate puts the faces at each other; this one may not, because the chapter's last page is a thing said and not answered.

**THE FAR LAMPS ARE PROVEN AND DO NOT LIGHT ANYBODY.** `ch01/fallen-stars` established it in this folder's first plate: an in-frame warm source at distance renders as a source and throws nothing, so the two of them are lit by the last of the sky and the lamps are the one warm thing in the frame. That finding is the reason this composition is safe to attempt at dusk.

**HIS HAND IS ON THE PAGE AND THE SHAKE IS NOT DRAWABLE.** *He sat with his hand shaking on his knee and did not take it back.* A tremor cannot be rendered, so the block asks for the hand open and slack on the knee rather than closed — the emptying written as a hand that is not gripping anything.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch20-val.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *adopt*).**

**THE EXPERIMENT WORKED ON THE FIRST RUN.** Two figures side by side in profile, both looking straight out at the water, **no eye contact anywhere in the picture and nobody at the viewer.** The register's one constant — faces near and engaged with each other — broken deliberately because the chapter's last page is a word going out and not coming back, and the tool held it without a fight. **So the gaze has two reliable shapes now, not one:** name the eye contact as the subject, or give both sightlines the same object outside the frame and say neither is looking at the other.

**AND THE FAR-LAMP FINDING IS CONFIRMED A SECOND TIME.** `ch01/fallen-stars` established that an in-frame warm source at distance renders as a source and throws nothing; the lamps came up along the waterline, lit nobody, and left both faces to the last of the sky. Second run, second confirmation, seven chapters apart.

**WHAT ELSE LANDED.** Worn stone and the low wall, the sky nearly out, her forearms across her knees, his near hand open and slack on his knee rather than closed — the emptying carried by a hand that is not gripping. Her kit off the full-figure sheet, vambrace and coat and underlayer, against his mended undyed wool with no metal anywhere near him.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT TOUCHES A FENCE.** Her face edges toward wistful — calm and a little sad — where `../canon-brief.md` says *level, flat, appraising, unimpressed and faintly tired; not vulnerable, not wistful, not searching.* Adopted as drawn on the author's word. **The unspent fix, recorded and not run:** her paragraph written as anatomy rather than temperament — the lids a fraction low, the mouth level, nothing lifted or drawn anywhere — because *tired* and *flat* are direction words this tool has already misread twice (`ch14/one-finger`, `ch17/who-lives-in-it`). The crop came back loose again off *the bottom edge crosses them at the shin*, which is the fourth instance and no longer news.

```

Image 2: valeria-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl, for her clothing.
Image 3: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: an old open flagstone court above the sea at the very end of
a winter afternoon, as loose wash suggestion — worn flagstones, a low
broken wall at the edge, and beyond it open water going dark. The
last flat grey light is off the water and the sky is nearly out.
Along the far waterline, small warm lamps have come up one at a time;

the two figures are lit by the last of the sky.

Composition: the viewer is at one side of them, level and close, so
both faces are seen in profile. Two figures sit side by side on the
cold flagstones with their backs to the low wall, the boy nearer the
viewer, the girl beyond him, both facing out to the water. She sits
with her forearms across her knees. His near hand lies open and slack
on his knee, not closed on anything. The bottom edge crosses them at
the shin. They are twelve years old. Nobody else is anywhere in the
picture.

NEITHER OF THEM IS LOOKING AT THE OTHER AND NEITHER IS LOOKING AT THE
VIEWER. Both are looking straight out at the water, level, and the
sightlines run out of the frame on the same side. This is the subject
of the image: two people side by side and no eye contact anywhere in
it.

He has just said one word and it has not been answered: his mouth
closed, his face still, nothing asking and nothing withdrawn.

Her face gives nothing away — level, the mouth closed, the brows
unmoved, tired around the eyes, entirely unhurried. Not sad, not
softened. Her black hair is loose, well past the shoulders, no
ornament. Her clothing as in her full-figure reference: the charcoal
sleeveless coat over the grey-blue underlayer, the high collar, the

He wears undyed coarse working wool, much mended, dull oatmeal and
dead olive, no ornament and no metal on him anywhere.

Their two profiles carry the most finished drawing in the picture;
the flagstones, the wall and the water stay at quiet middle values
with soft edges.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring; the two images of the girl are the same girl and
there are two figures in the picture. Do not blend their features. Do
not mix their clothing: the steel and the charcoal coat belong only
to her, the mended undyed wool only to him. Her eyes green, his pale
grey.
```
