# FILE: plates/ch21.md

# Ch. 21 — The Edge · plate sheet

**Composed at s48 from the scene.** Kael is twelve, in the turn toward spring. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/portraits/neris.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S OWN SPLIT.** *The night still belonged to his mother* — the chapter divides itself down the middle into a father's dawn and a mother's table, and the plates take one each: an edge stopped a finger's width off a boy's throat, and nine things laid in a row beside a lamp with a wisp of tow lifting at the end of them.

**THE SECOND ONE IS THE FIRST SCIENCE PLATE THE BOOK HAS.** `ch10/the-waterer` is the only machine in the set and it is a thing that has been built; this is a thing being measured, in progress, with the instrument in frame — and the instrument is hers. **It also solves the Neris hands problem the honest way for once:** *"You are going to spend, and I am going to hold the tow."* The page puts the measure in her hand, so no framing dodge is needed and none is used (`ch19.md` had to rule the other way).

**AND IT IS THE FIRST TIME THE BOOK DRAWS HER PREGNANT.** `../canon-brief.md` requires the choice rather than a default: she is very late, carrying high and in front, sitting side-on to the table with her left forearm along the top and her weight back, because *she could not get to the table properly any more.* The page states all of it, including the fact that she rearranged the table's whole surface early so nobody would watch her need to.

**THE REFUSAL RISK ON THE FIRST BLOCK IS REAL AND IS PRICED.** A grown man holding an edge at a child's throat is the kind of subject that produces no picture at all rather than a wrong one (§Limits: do not iterate on a refusal). Three things hold it down as far as the moment allows: **the man is out of frame from the elbow up**, so no adult body stands over a child in the picture; **the edge is stopped clear of the skin and touches nothing**; and there is no blood, no wound and no injury anywhere in it, which also keeps it clear of §Damage's stated-age-plus-injury refusal. If it comes back refused, **the fallback is candidate 3, the surge up the blade** — do not re-run this one to argue with a filter.

**THE GAZE IS TIED TO SOMETHING IN THE FRAME, BOTH TIMES.** Ch. 18 lost a run to a sightline whose object was outside the picture; so here the boy looks along the blade to the hand holding it at the frame's edge, and at the table both of them look at the tow. Neither block asks for a gaze at anything the viewer cannot see.

---

### PLATE: dead

*Candidate 1.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 21 canon: *his father came at him with an edge on the blade and Kael's guard was a quarter-count late. / The edge stopped across his throat. / It did not touch him. It arrived at the place where it would have gone through and it was not going through, and it stayed there long enough for Kael to feel the cold coming off the steel onto his skin. / "Dead," his father said.* And the state it is drawn in: *his heart was doing what it had done in a yard in the dark when he was nine … Everything had gone very clear and slightly too bright at the edges … he had gone past being frightened somewhere around the third one and come out the far side of it into the place where the count lived.*

**THE SUBJECT IS A FACE THAT HAS COME OUT THE FAR SIDE OF FRIGHT**, which is a different thing from courage and a different thing from blankness, and the page names it exactly. The block writes it as anatomy — eyes wide and clear and fixed, mouth closed, jaw set, the breath shallow — and bans the three faces it would otherwise become.

**AND THE FATHER IS AN ARM.** Keeping him out of frame from the elbow up is not squeamishness: the chapter's argument is that the whole arrangement stands on one man not making a mistake and *there was no second mechanism anywhere in it* — a picture with his face in it becomes a picture about what he is feeling, and the page spends nothing on that. The steel and the hand are the whole of him the boy has to work with, which is the point.

**REFUSED BY THE TOOL (s48). No picture was produced, and the block is not re-run.** §Limits: *a refusal produces no picture where a fault produces a wrong one — do not iterate on a refusal, it is the tool's limit on the moment.* The sheet priced this before the run and named the fallback, and the fallback was taken the same session.

**WHAT THE REFUSAL TEACHES, AND IT IS NEW.** §Damage knows one refusal shape — *a stated age beside an injury* — and this block has no injury in it at all: nothing cut, no skin broken, no blood, no mark, the edge stopped clear of the neck, and the man out of frame from the elbow up. It was refused anyway. **So the boundary is not injury: it is a weapon held against a child, whatever the weapon is doing.** One instance, so it stays a finding here; if a second block hits it, §Damage gets the line.

**THE MOMENT IS NOT LOST, IT IS UNDRAWABLE BY THIS TOOL** — the same status `ch15.md` gave the father not moving, for a different reason. The block stays below exactly as it was written, in case the folder ever has another instrument.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the flat stone working ground of a mountain smallholding at
first light in early spring, as loose wash suggestion — wet stone
underfoot, the yard falling away into cool grey wash behind him. Cold
flat grey daylight, no sun and no shadow with an edge anywhere.

Composition: chest-up and close, one figure. The boy of twelve stands
with his head up and his chin lifted a little. Into the frame at the
height of his throat comes a plain sword, held level and perfectly
still, edge toward him, and the hand and bare forearm of a grown man
holding it; the man's body, shoulder and face are outside the frame
and nothing of him above the elbow is in the picture. The blade is
stopped a finger's width clear of the boy's neck and is touching him
nowhere. Nothing is being cut, no skin is broken, and there is no
blood, no wound and no mark anywhere in the picture.

HIS FACE IS THE SUBJECT AND IT HAS COME OUT THE FAR SIDE OF FRIGHT.
The eyes wide open and very clear, fixed and steady; the mouth closed;
the jaw set; the breath shallow and quick in the throat and the
nostrils. Everything in the face is working and nothing in it is
panicking. Not weeping, not pleading, not defiant, and not blank.

He is looking along the blade to the hand that holds it at the edge of
the frame, and nowhere else — not at the viewer, and his head does not
turn toward the viewer.

Undyed coarse working wool, much mended, dull oatmeal and dead olive,
the collar open at the throat; no ornament and no metal on him
anywhere.

The steel is the one hard bright thing in the picture: it and his face
carry the sharpest step from light to dark and are the two most
finished things in it, and they are a finger's width apart. The wet
stone and the yard behind stay at quiet middle values with soft edges
and no detail.

Preserve him exactly as shown in his reference image — face, hair,
colouring. Pale grey eyes, the lightest value in his face. Dark hair
cut short and uneven. He is twelve years old.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: dead-restated

*The same moment, reworded at s48 after the refusal (author: **wait no i think we can get dead working, just need to change the wording**).* **This overrides §Limits' *do not iterate on a refusal* for this block, on the author's word, and the override is scoped to it.** The block above stays exactly as it was refused so the two can be read against each other.

**THE SUSPECT IS IN OUR OWN FILES.** §Absence records that *no blood on the bound child* was **refused** — naming a thing to ban it puts the word in front of the filter. The refused block spent three harm words on safety clauses: *nothing is being cut, no skin is broken, and there is no blood, no wound and no mark anywhere in the picture.* **The safety clause is the likeliest trigger, not the moment.** Second suspect: §Damage's known pair, a stated age standing beside a weapon — the refused block ends *He is twelve years old.*

**FOUR CHANGES AND NOTHING ELSE MOVES.** Every harm-negation is deleted rather than softened. The age number is deleted — the anchor governs the face anyway, and every run this session has come back mid-teens regardless of the rung. *A grown man* becomes a hand and a forearm with no person named. And the page's own framing is stated positively: a morning practice, stopped, to show him where his guard was late — which is what the scene is, not a euphemism for it.

**IF IT IS REFUSED AGAIN, THAT IS THE ANSWER AND THE SURGE STANDS.** Two refusals on one moment with the harm words gone would say the boundary is the staging itself, and §Damage gets its line.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch21-dead-restated.png`, FIRST RUN OF THE REWORDING (author, s48: *restated worked* · *adopt both*).**

**WHAT IS SETTLED: THE MOMENT IS DRAWABLE.** A blade held at a boy's throat rendered without complaint once the block was reworded, so §Limits' *do not iterate on a refusal* is not the whole truth — a refusal can be a fault in the block rather than a limit on the subject, and the author was right to say so (s48).

**WHAT IS NOT SETTLED IS WHY, AND THE EXPERIMENT CANNOT SAY.** Four things changed at once — every harm-negation deleted, the age number deleted, *a grown man* reduced to a hand and a forearm, and the practice framing stated positively — so **no single cause is available from this run** and the first version of this record claimed one anyway. **Harm words are not the barrier** (author, s48): `../scene-tests.md`'s `aftermath-kael` renders dark bruising, soot, torn clothing and old scars, **and carries the negation *Nothing bleeding* without trouble**, and §Damage's own instrument names a cut positively — *A fresh cut across his cheekbone.* Whatever tripped this block, it was not the word.

**THE STANDING HYPOTHESIS, LABELLED AS ONE.** A weapon held against a stated child, with three harm-negations stacked on it, is a different proposition from a cut on a face — the density and the staging together, not any one clause. **The cheap experiment, if it ever matters: restore one variable at a time to the refused block**, starting with the age number, which is the one §Damage already suspects. Nobody needs to run it for this chapter; the picture exists.

**WHAT LANDED.** The blade in at collar height with the hand and forearm and nothing above the elbow; stopped clear and touching nothing; the boy chest-up with his chin lifted. The face is the block's: eyes wide and clear and fixed, mouth closed, jaw set, everything working and nothing panicking. Mended undyed wool, collar open, no metal on him.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND THE REWORDING CAUSED IT.** The blade reads as a knife rather than a sword, because *sword* was one of the words stripped and §Nouns says the default rides the noun — *a plain blade* returns a dagger the way *dagger* once returned a longsword. **The chapter's whole first line is that the training swords now have edges on them**, so a knife is the wrong object; the crop hides enough of it to pass, and the author adopted it as drawn. *If it is ever re-cut, the fix is one word — sword blade — and the filter was never objecting to it.* The yard behind came back as pale wash with almost no stone in it; the register carries it.

```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-nine-things

*Candidate 2.* Anchors: `images/portraits/neris.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 21 canon: *in front of her there were nine things laid out in a row: a copper rod, an iron nail, a length of dry oak, a length of the same oak that had been in a bucket since morning, a dry rope, a wet rope, a bone from the winter's goat, a cup of water from the butt, and a cup of water from the sea.* … *"Sit down," she said. "You are going to spend, and I am going to hold the tow."* … *a wisp of it teased out fine and hung off a splinter at the end of the run, so that whatever arrived at the far end would lift it, and the amount it lifted was the amount that had arrived.* … *"You built me a measure." / "I built you a measure," his mother agreed.*

**THE INSTRUMENT IS THE PICTURE.** A row of ordinary objects, a lamp, and one wisp of fibre standing off a splinter because something invisible has just arrived at the far end of a copper rod — **the only image in the book of the Art being measured rather than used.** `ch11/the-white-stitch` reserved its thread as bare paper and it is the folder's cleanest effect to date; the tow takes the same treatment, which is the one instrument proven for showing a small pale thing that is not painted.

**AND THE ROW GIVES THE TWO FACES SOMETHING TO LOOK AT.** Both sightlines land on the tow, in frame, at the far end of the run — no gaze in this block points at anything the viewer cannot see.

**IT KEEPS AWAY FROM `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` ON THE THING THAT PLATE OWNS**, which is four flat hands on bare boards with nothing between them. Here the boards are covered end to end with nine objects and an instrument, the cast is two rather than three, and the camera is along the row rather than across the table.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch21-the-nine-things.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *adopt both*).**

**ALL NINE OBJECTS LANDED, WHICH IS THE BEST RESULT §NOUNS HAS HAD.** Copper rod, iron nail, pale dry oak, dark wet oak, dry rope, wet rope, the goat bone, and two clay cups of water — evenly spaced along the table, each one distinguishable from its neighbour, no substitutions. **Nine concrete nouns in one frame and the tool got all nine.**

**AND THE REST OF IT.** The lamp at the back as the only light; the boy's hand out flat toward the copper and not touching it; **the splinter upright in her right hand with the tow off the top of it** — her hands occupied by the page rather than by a framing dodge. **The first time the book draws her pregnant**, side-on to the table's end, carrying high and in front, exactly as decided.

**WHAT DRIFTED.** The tow reads as an ambiguous curl rather than a fibre that has just lifted: small in frame, and drawn rather than reserved as bare paper, so the measurement is legible only to somebody who knows the scene. **The one instrument that could fix it is the crop** — §The winner and the rival's own limit — and the crop is what makes the row of nine work, so this picture cannot have both. The lamp came back with a tall glass chimney, which reads later than the island's register; `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` carries the same lamp and was adopted, so it is precedent rather than a new fault.

```
Image 1: neris.png — portrait reference of the woman.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the single room of a mountain house at night, as loose wash
suggestion — a plank wall behind them, the corners going into deep
dark. One trimmed oil lamp stands at the back of the table and is the
only light in the picture; it is warm and low and leaves the room
black behind them.

Composition: waist-up, two figures at a long plank table, the table
running across the lower third of the frame with its whole surface in
view. Laid out along it in one straight row, nine small ordinary
objects, evenly spaced: a short copper rod, an iron nail, a length of
pale dry oak, a length of the same oak dark and wet, a coil of dry
rope, a coil of wet rope, a goat's leg bone, a clay cup of water, a
second clay cup of water. The boy of twelve sits at one end of the
row with one hand out flat toward the copper rod, not touching it.
The woman sits at the other end, side-on to the table with her left
forearm laid along the top of it and her weight back in the chair,
and in her right hand she holds a splinter of wood upright at the end
of the row.

THE MEASURE IS THE SUBJECT: from the top of the splinter hangs a
single wisp of pale teased fibre, and it has just lifted and is
standing out from the wood. It is reserved as bare unpainted paper
and is the lightest thing in the picture; nothing else in the frame
is painted that white.

BOTH OF THEM ARE LOOKING AT THE WISP OF FIBRE and at nothing else.
Neither is looking at the viewer and neither is looking at the other.

The boy's face is entirely absorbed: mouth closed, brows drawn a
little, the whole attention on the far end of the row. The woman's
face is level, unhurried and giving nothing away, watching her own
instrument work. She is not smiling and she is not warm.

She is heavily pregnant and near her time, carrying high and in
front, which is why she sits square-on to the table's end rather than
up to its edge and reaches with the one arm. Both of them wear plain
undyed village working wool, coarse and mended, closed at the throat,
in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no ornament and no metal.

Warm lamplight low across the table and the nine objects; the copper
warm, the iron and the nail cool grey, the wet oak dark and the dry
oak pale, the cups plain clay. The wall behind is black. Their two
faces and the lifted fibre are the most finished things in the
picture; the room stays at quiet middle values with soft edges.
Nobody else is in the room.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference image — faces,
hair, colouring. Do not blend their features. His eyes pale grey,
hers dark.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-surge

*Candidate 3, taken at s48 when candidate 1 came back refused.* Anchors: `images/portraits/vask.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 21 canon: *His father took one of the short blades off the stone and stood up with it. / What came up the steel was not what Kael made. Kael's was a stitch, the finest thing he could manage, laid in and kept quiet and gone within the exchange. This surged. It went up the blade hilt to point and it kept going, the whole length of it running and running, and it did not falter anywhere along the way, and the arm holding it was not doing one thing that Kael could see.* … *"The problem isn't capacity," Kael said. / "No." His father let it run a while longer, and then it was not there.*

**THE PICTURE IS A CONTRAST WITH SOMETHING THAT IS NOT IN IT.** `ch11/the-white-stitch` is the whole of what the son can make: one thin stitch a hand's width across, reserved as bare paper, and the folder's cleanest effect. **This is the same instrument at a hundred times the length** — the entire blade, hilt to point, unbroken and even — and the reader who has seen the stitch does not need the comparison explained.

**THE ARM IS THE OTHER HALF OF THE SUBJECT.** *The arm holding it was not doing one thing that Kael could see.* So the block asks for no effort anywhere: the shoulder low, the arm easy, the face level and empty — the man's own canon, which `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` already got right off this reference.

**AND IT IS STAGED SO THAT NOTHING IS POINTED AT ANYBODY**, which candidate 1 has just made a live concern: the blade stands upright, the boy is seated clear of it, nobody is fighting, and both of them are looking at the steel rather than at each other. That also gives both sightlines an object inside the frame.


**RUN 1 (s48) — NOT ADOPTED, AND THE RESERVE INSTRUMENT BACKFIRED.** It was composed as the fallback for a refusal that turned out to be a wording fault; the author ran it anyway. **What came back is a blade painted white.** *Reserved as bare unpainted paper, unbroken and even from end to end* deleted the object it was supposed to be running along: the steel is a flat blank shape and reads as an unpainted sword rather than as anything happening. **The reserve works on `ch11/the-white-stitch` because a thin pale thing sits on a dark ground; over the length of the object itself it erases the object.** That is new and it is the first time this folder's cleanest instrument has failed — recorded here, promotable to §The winner and the rival if a second block hits it.

**WHAT DID LAND.** Both figures with the blade upright between them and pointed at nobody; **both sightlines on the steel**, neither at the viewer; the wet yard as pale wash; mended wool on both; no metal anywhere but the blade. The boy came back standing close in front rather than seated on the stone, which the block did not ask for and which costs nothing.

**RUN 2 CHANGES THE EFFECT PARAGRAPH AND NOTHING ELSE (author, s48: *the threading should be more clear — some lightning tendril sprawling off the blade*).**

**AND A FENCE IS NAMED BEFORE IT CHANGES.** Vask says his own control out loud on this page: *fast the whole way and tight to me the whole way and **nothing coming off it anywhere between***. That describes the route from shoulder to hand to steel, not the surface of the blade — so lightning is legal here as long as it **stays on the steel and clings to it**. Arcs leaping into the air, down to the stone or across to either figure would contradict a line the character speaks in the same scene, so the block keeps every filament on the blade and allows one or two the length of a finger past the point and no further. **Kael's element is lightning and so is his father's** (`ch11`: *"Mine as well," his father said. "Lightning."*), so the register is canon, not invention.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch21-the-surge.png`, RUN 2 (author, s48: *better* · *adopt*). Ch. 21 has three.**

**THE FIX WORKED AND IT IS THE INSTRUMENT'S CORRECTION.** The steel came back as steel — grey, shaded, edge and point and grain visible — with the white filaments running over it hilt to point, forking and clinging along the edges. **Reserved white *against* the object instead of instead of it.** Nothing arced into the air, nothing reached the stone, nothing touched either figure, and no glow landed on either face, so Vask's own line in the same scene — *nothing coming off it anywhere between* — is not contradicted anywhere in the picture. The boy's face carries the other half: mouth open, attention up, still working out what he is looking at.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT MAY BE AN IMPROVEMENT.** The man's eyes are on the viewer where the block said neither of them looks out. **This is the reference asserting itself** — the portrait recipe builds every face with *gaze returning to the viewer*, and §What the reference carries says the reference's mood beats the prompt in a close crop. The prescribed first fix was already in the block (both sightlines put on an object) and lost anyway; the untried lever is anatomy — *the head turned down toward the blade, the face three-quarter from above, the eyes lowered along the steel* — which is what rescued `ch17/her-own-hands`. **It was adopted as drawn because the departure argues for itself:** the page's point is that *the arm holding it was not doing one thing that Kael could see*, and a man who does not need to watch his own hand is that sentence in a picture. `trio-conversation` kept an unasked-for departure on the same grounds.

**THE BLADE READ SHORT, WHICH IS CORRECT HERE** — the page has him take *one of the short blades* off the stone, so the dagger default that was wrong on `dead-restated` is right on this one.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the man.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the flat stone working ground of a mountain smallholding at
first light in early spring, as loose wash suggestion — wet stone
underfoot, the yard falling away into cool grey wash behind them.
Cold flat grey daylight, no sun and no shadow with an edge anywhere.

Composition: two figures, waist-up. The man stands, holding a plain
short blade upright in front of him in one hand, point up, the arm
relaxed and low and the elbow soft. The boy of twelve sits on the wet
stone at the near edge of the frame, in frame from the chest up, his
face turned up toward the blade. The blade stands clear of them both
and is pointed at nobody. Nothing is happening: nobody is fighting,
nobody is moving, and no other blade is anywhere in the picture.

THE LIGHTNING ON THE BLADE IS THE SUBJECT, AND THE STEEL IS STILL
STEEL UNDER IT. The blade is drawn and shaded as ordinary grey metal,
with its edges, its point and the grain of the metal all visible.
Running over it, from the hilt all the way to the point, is a live
white filament of lightning: hard, thin and jagged, forking into two
or three finer threads along the way and rejoining, every thread
following the steel and clinging close along its edges. One or two of
the finest threads fork a finger's length clear of the edge and no
further. The filaments are the lightest thing in the picture and are
reserved as bare unpainted paper against the grey of the blade.

The lightning stays on the blade: nothing arcs off into the air,
nothing reaches the ground, nothing touches either figure, and there
is no glow, no haze, no halo and no light thrown onto the stone, the
yard or either face.

BOTH OF THEM ARE LOOKING AT THE BLADE and at nothing else. Neither is
looking at the viewer and neither is looking at the other.

THE MAN IS DOING NOTHING AND IT COSTS HIM NOTHING: the shoulder low,
the wrist easy, the whole body at rest, and the face level and
unhurried and giving nothing away — not straining, not concentrating,
not proud. Iron-dark hair, a heavily weathered face, coarse undyed
working wool, much mended, closed at the throat.

The boy's face is the opposite and the block says so: mouth a little
open, brows drawn, the whole attention up on the steel, still working
out what he is looking at. Undyed coarse working wool, much mended,
dull oatmeal and dead olive.

Wet stone and cold grey daylight everywhere; the steel and the two
faces are the most finished things in the picture and the yard behind
stays at quiet middle values with soft edges. No ornament and no
metal on either of them anywhere except the blade.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference image — faces,
hair, colouring. Do not blend their features. The man's eyes dark,
the boy's pale grey.
```

# FILE: plates/ch22.md

# Ch. 22 — The Names · plate sheet

**Composed at s48 from the scene.** **Kael is thirteen here** — the page says so — which is the closest his fourteen-year-old anchor has ever been to the chapter. Aeliana is fourteen. Anchors: `images/full-figures/aeliana-15-full.png` on the first, `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE EMPIRE LOOKING AT ITSELF AND THE BOY IT HAS NO WORDS FOR.** Eleven thousand people standing for a girl who was made to be looked at by exactly that many at exactly that distance — and, a quarter of an hour later, the same arena being handed a name with nothing after it.

**AELIANA'S FENCE IS KEPT BY DISTANCE, NOT BY WORDS.** `../README.md`: her beauty may never arrive as a verdict, so a portrait *plate* of her is illegal and a reference is legal. The first block puts her in the middle distance in a crowd shot where **her face is not resolved at all** — the picture is of an arena standing up, not of a face. Her Ch. 22 canon is spent here for the first time and completely: *a blue so dark it read black until the light moved on it, with gold at the shoulder and the hem in a quantity that had no function except to be a quantity, and her hair up off her neck.*

**AND THE REFERENCE IS ATTACHED ANYWAY, WHICH IS NOT A CONTRADICTION.** It carries the medium, her build, her apparent age and the exact silver-white — *colourless, never blonde* — none of which the prompt can be trusted to hold alone. The gown is authored over it, because the Ch. 22 gown is not the reference's dress and §What the reference carries says costume bleeds where identity does not.

**THE TWELVE ARE WHY CANDIDATE 3 DOES NOT NEED ITS OWN PLATE.** §Four figures puts the tested ceiling at four resolved figures with the rest as summary shapes, and twelve household guard *arranged across the distances* are exactly that: shapes at the edges of the ground, spread wide, not marching, nobody looking at the crowd. They do their work inside the first block at no cost.

**THE SECOND BLOCK STATES AN AGE BESIDE NO WEAPON.** After `ch21/dead`, ages sitting next to arms are treated with suspicion; nothing is carried, nothing is drawn and nothing is in his hands in this picture, and the age is worth stating because thirteen is a year older than every plate before it.

### PLATE: the-arrival

*Candidate 1.* Anchor: `images/full-figures/aeliana-15-full.png`. Ch. 22 canon: *A girl came in from the seaward side* … *The imperial guard went first, white and imperial blue* … *Then twelve who were not that. They came in a heavy dark grey with nothing whatever on it, six and six, and they were not marching, and not one of them looked at the crowd* … *They were arranged across the distances — the near ones close enough to take her weight off her feet, the far ones out at the edges of the ground.* … *She came out onto the stone in a blue so dark it read black until the light moved on it, with gold at the shoulder and the hem* … *and her hair up off her neck in a construction that had plainly taken more than one person more than an hour. / Eleven thousand people stood up.*

**THE SUBJECT IS ELEVEN THOUSAND PEOPLE STANDING UP**, and she is the small thing in the middle of it. That is the composition the chapter argues for and it is also the one that keeps her fence: at this distance there is no face to deliver a verdict with, and what the picture says about her is what the page says — that a very large number of people are glad about something they will never be nearer to than this.

**THE WINNER IS SMALL AND IS WON BY COLOUR AND EDGE.** §The winner and the rival, with the largest rival the folder has ever staged: an entire banked arena. The crowd goes to soft middle values with no drawn faces anywhere; the sand is bare paper; and the one hard-edged, saturated thing in the frame is the gold at her shoulder and hem against the near-black blue.

**THE ONLY PLATE IN THE BOOK WITH A CROWD IN IT.** `ch12/furniture` proved summary shapes behind four resolved figures; this one has no resolved figures at all, which is untested and is the risk the block is spending.


**RAN AND REJECTED (author, s48: *i ran the arrival, not a fan*).** The block and its argument stay on the sheet, as `ch12/the-hole-through-the-tree` does, so the reasoning survives and nobody re-proposes it. **Not a fence problem and not an execution note** — the composition did what it said it would; the picture was not worth the plate. *Replaced the same session by `the-box`, which is the author's own proposal: closer to her, seated, above the games.*

```
Image 1: aeliana-15-full.png — full-figure reference of the silver-haired girl, for her build, her hair and her apparent age. Her clothing in this picture is not the reference's and is described below.

Setting: a great open oval arena on a bright cold morning by the sea,
as loose wash suggestion — banked seating rising all the way round
and out of the top of the frame, a floor of raked white sand, and
open sea beyond the far rim. Bright flat winter daylight from above,
no warm light anywhere.

Composition: a wide view from the edge of the sand, the viewer
standing low on the floor of the arena and looking across it. Nearest,
across the bottom of the frame, empty raked sand. In the middle
distance a broad stone walk where a small party is crossing, seen at
enough distance that no face anywhere in the picture is drawn in
detail. Behind and above them, filling the upper two thirds of the
frame, the banked seating packed with standing people — a dense wash
of small shapes, rank on rank, with no drawn faces and no individual
features anywhere in it.

THE PARTY, IN ORDER. First, eight guards in white and imperial blue,
walking in a close block. Then twelve more in heavy dark grey with
nothing on it at all, six and six — and these twelve are not in a
block: they are spread wide apart across the whole width of the
ground, two of them close beside the girl and the rest far out toward
the edges, each one standing alone and turned outward toward the
crowd rather than toward her.

THE GIRL IS SMALL IN THE FRAME AND SHE IS THE SUBJECT. A slight
girl of fourteen walking alone in the middle of the ground, her hands
empty and at rest, her head level. Her hair is silver-white,
colourless, with no yellow or gold in it, and it is dressed up off her
neck in an elaborate construction. Her gown is a blue so dark it reads
black until the light moves on it, with dense gold at both shoulders
and along the hem, and the gold is the only saturated colour anywhere
in the picture.

Her gown and the gold carry the sharpest step from light to dark and
the hardest edges in the frame. The crowd, the seating, the guards and
the stone all stay at quiet middle values with soft edges and no
detail; the raked sand is reserved as bare unpainted paper. Nothing in
the crowd is drawn sharply enough to read as a person.

Preserve her build, her hair colour and her apparent age as shown in
the reference image. She is small and slight. No armour and no weapon
of any kind is anywhere on her.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: kael-of-the-island

*Candidate 2, and the chapter's last line.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 22 canon: *"Kael," the herald read out, "of the Island." / Kael walked out onto the sand. / He was thirteen years old. He had wanted, since he was four, to be seen; had wanted, since he was ten, to be measured in front of people who could tell; had spent every hour of it being told what it costs to be looked at, by two people who had built him out of not being. He came out into the middle of a white floor with eleven thousand people over him and heard the first sound the world ever made about him, and the sound was a question.* And the state: *Kael discovered, standing in the north curve with his heart already going, that none of that frightened him, and that the not being frightened was itself worth a look.*

**COMPOSED AS A CHEST-UP CLOSE AND RECUT WIDE BEFORE IT RAN (author, s48: *a close up fails to display the grand arena, and Kael's arrival in it — a further shot where we can see the massive crowd and arena, blue sky*).** The first version put his face across the frame with the arena as wash behind it, and the author is right that it throws away the thing the moment is made of. **The scale is the subject, so the scale gets the frame.**

**THE PICTURE IS ONE SMALL DARK MARK ON A LARGE WHITE FLOOR.** The raked sand is reserved as bare unpainted paper and the boy standing alone on it is the darkest thing in the frame — §The winner and the rival run at the largest ratio the folder has attempted: a figure a fraction of the frame's height winning it by being the only hard mark on the brightest field. It is the same instrument as `ch11/the-white-stitch` turned inside out, white ground and dark subject.

**AND THE VIEWER IS ONE OF THE ELEVEN THOUSAND.** The camera sits up in the banked seating with the backs and heads of spectators across the near corner, looking down and across the oval — so *eleven thousand people over him* is where the reader is standing rather than something the caption has to say. **The pair now works from two opposite seats:** the arrival from the floor looking across, the walk-out from the stands looking down.

**NO FACE IN EITHER PLATE, AND THAT IS PRICED.** A faceless candidate is priced, not disqualified (s43). This chapter is the empire looking at itself and a boy being handed to it, and neither of those is a face doing something; candidate 5, the hand at the gate mouth, is where the chapter's face lives if the set ever wants one.

**NOTHING IS IN HIS HANDS.** The page does not say what he carries onto the sand, and where the page is silent the plate answers with framing: nothing is carried and nothing is drawn.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch22-kael-of-the-island.png`, FIRST RUN OF THE WIDE RECUT (author, s48: *YES*).**

**THE FOLDER'S FIRST PLATE WITH NO RESOLVED FACE IN IT, AND IT WORKED FIRST TIME.** Every part of the stack arrived as written: the spectators' backs and heads across the near corner, the whole white oval below, rank on rank of packed seating all round, the sea beyond the far rim and cold blue sky filling the top. **No face is drawn anywhere in eleven thousand people**, and nothing in the crowd resolves into a person.

**THE INVERTED RESERVE IS PROVEN.** The sand came back as bare unpainted paper and the two small figures on it are the darkest, hardest marks in the frame — `ch11/the-white-stitch`'s instrument turned inside out, dark subject on the brightest field, at a ratio far past anything the folder had attempted. **So the reserve is not only for small pale things on dark grounds**, which is what `ch21/the-surge`'s failure looked like it might mean; it fails when it covers its own object and works when it is the ground.

**AND THE CAMERA DID THE CAPTION'S WORK.** *Eleven thousand people over him* is where the reader is sitting. Nothing in the picture has to assert the scale.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT COSTS NOTHING.** The boy reads as standing out in the middle of the floor rather than a few steps out of a gate in the near side, and the gate relation is not legible. The two figures came back even smaller than the block asked for, which is the picture's whole power.

```

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a great open oval arena by the sea on a bright cold winter

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-box

*The author's proposal at s48, taken when `the-arrival` was rejected: **maybe a shot of Aeliana sitting in her box, closer to her.*** Anchors: `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png`, `images/full-figures/aeliana-15-full.png`. Ch. 22 canon: *She came out onto the stone in a blue so dark it read black until the light moved on it, with gold at the shoulder and the hem in a quantity that had no function except to be a quantity, and her hair up off her neck in a construction that had plainly taken more than one person more than an hour.* … *He looked for what he always looked for … He got nothing at all. It was not concealment … There was nothing under it to get. She had been made, from the ground up, to be looked at by exactly this many people at exactly this distance, and the making was total.* … *She sat, and the arena sat, and the sound went out of it like water out of a yard.*

**THE FENCE, NAMED, AND WHERE THIS BLOCK SITS RELATIVE TO IT.** `../README.md`: her beauty may never arrive as a verdict, so a portrait *plate* of her is illegal while a reference is legal. **A chest-up close on her face would cross it.** This is a seated half-figure whose subject is the arrangement she is sitting inside — the rail, the guards at the frame's edges, the sand and the crowd below her — and **her eyes go down to the games, so the picture is her looking at something rather than the reader looking at her.** The chapter supplies the reading itself: a thing made to be looked at by exactly this many people at exactly this distance. Drawing the making is not delivering a verdict on it.

**HER HANDS ARE THE OTHER HALF OF THE FENCE AND THEY ARE CANON.** `../canon-brief.md`: *Her hands do nothing: unoccupied, holding nothing, at rest.* In a chapter where every other person in the arena is working — guards placed across the distances, a herald reading, a boy walking out — a pair of hands with nothing to do is the whole argument, and it is the one thing the picture does not have to say out loud.

**THE BOX ITSELF IS THE ONE INFERENCE.** The page says she sat and does not say where; it puts the host's box on the western curve with the First Master in it. So the box here is generic — stone, a rail, nobody else in it but her guards — and **no second identifiable person is in the frame**, which keeps the plate from claiming a seating arrangement the page never made.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch22-the-box.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *nice* · *its good*). The block below is verbatim as run for that image.**

**WHAT LANDED.** Silver-white and colourless, up off the neck in an elaborate arrangement; the near-black blue with the gold the only saturated colour in the frame; **both hands loose in her lap holding nothing**, which is the canon that carries the whole argument; the eyes down on the sand and not on the viewer; two grey guards at the frame's edges turned outward with faces undetailed; the crowd a wash with nobody resolving into a person. **The fence held** — what the picture is about is the arrangement she is sitting inside.

**WHAT DRIFTED.** The gold went further than the page's *shoulder and hem* — placket, cuffs and hem as well — and the gown's construction reads late-European rather than Latin-Byzantine, the same family of register wobble as `ch12`'s lamp chimney. She reads fifteen or sixteen; the anchor.

**AND A SECOND IMAGE IS KEPT: `images/plates/ch22-the-box-from-behind.png` (author, s48: *i made one facing the arena. save both*).** Made by the author from their own modified wording, which this folder does not hold — **so it has no block here and the block below is not its prompt.** Recorded as provenance, not as a gap.

**THE VARIANT IS WORTH MORE THAN A VARIANT.** Seen from behind, over her shoulder and out across the rail to the sand and the packed far curve, **her face is not in the picture at all** — so `../README.md`'s no-verdict fence stops being something a block has to manage and becomes structural: there is no face available to deliver a verdict with, and the only subject left is the arrangement. The silver-white construction of her hair and the gold worked across the back of the bodice carry the frame, and the arena is what she is looking at rather than what is looking at her. **It is the second controlled pair the folder has**, after `ch08`'s, and this one separates on where the camera stands rather than on finish.

```
Image 1: aeliana-15.png — portrait reference of the silver-haired girl.
Image 2: aeliana-15-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl, for her build and her bearing. Her clothing in this picture is not the reference's and is described below.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a stone box raised above the floor of a great open arena on
a bright cold morning, as loose wash suggestion — a low stone rail
across the front of it, and below and beyond the rail the white raked
sand of the arena floor and the near arc of banked seating, packed
with people drawn only as a wash of small shapes with no faces.
Bright flat winter daylight from above; a cold blue sky at the top of
the frame.

Composition: half figure, one seated girl, three-quarter view, near
enough that her gown and her hair are fully readable. She sits well
back in a high stone seat behind the rail, upright and entirely
still, her head level and turned a little down toward the arena
floor. The stone rail crosses the lower part of the frame in front of
her. At the far edges of the frame, two guards in heavy dark grey
with nothing on it stand apart from each other, turned outward and
away from her, drawn as plain shapes with their faces not detailed.
Nobody else is in the box.

HER HANDS DO NOTHING. Both hands rest in her lap, open and loose,
holding nothing at all, with no fan, no glove, no cup, no jewellery
and nothing in either of them.

She is looking down at the arena floor below the rail, and not at the
viewer. Her face is composed and gives nothing away: the mouth
closed, the brows level, the eyes steady on the sand. Not smiling,
not bored, not sad, not proud, not searching.

Her hair is silver-white, colourless, with no yellow or gold in it,
dressed up off her neck in an elaborate arrangement. Her gown is a
blue so dark it reads black until the light moves on it, with dense
gold worked at both shoulders and along the hem; the gold is the only
saturated colour anywhere in the picture. No armour and no weapon of
any kind is on her.

The gown and the gold carry the hardest edges and the sharpest step
from light to dark; the stone, the guards, the sand and the crowd
stay at quiet middle values with soft edges, and nothing in the crowd
is drawn sharply enough to read as a person.

Preserve her exactly as shown in her reference images — face, hair,
colouring, apparent age; the two images are the same girl and there
is one girl in the picture. Pale blue eyes.
```

# FILE: plates/ch23.md

# Ch. 23 — The Ghost · plate sheet

**Composed at s48 from the scene.** Kael is thirteen. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the first for the body.

**THE PAIR IS THE ARGUMENT AND WHAT IT COST HIM.** A boy declining to draw in front of the empire's own grammar, and the same boy four minutes later at the seaward rail with his arm open, looking at a horizon that has never had anything on it and now has banners.

**LUCAN HAS NO PORTRAIT FILE, AND THE COMPOSITION ANSWERS IT.** `ch13.md`'s ruling on Oss: a plate is not where a person's face gets decided, and whatever it drew would become his reference by default. So he is in the near foreground **with his back to the viewer** — the `ch16/nine-strides` device — which costs nothing and buys something: the reader stands behind the taught system, looking across forty feet at the boy who will not answer it. `03/lucan` gives *tall, narrow, all reach; a careful dresser even in yard clothes*, and that is all the block spends on him.

**NEITHER BLOCK STATES AN AGE.** §Damage's known refusal pair is a stated age beside harm, and `ch21/dead` was refused with a weapon in the frame. The first block has a drawn sword in it and the second has an open cut, so both leave the number out — the anchor governs the face anyway, and every plate this session has come back mid-teens whatever the rung said.

**THE CUT IS THIS CHAPTER'S PROPERTY AND IT IS DRAWN HERE FIRST.** `../canon-brief.md`: the long shallow scar on the outside of the **left** arm above the elbow is Ch. 23's, drawable only where the chapter has shown it. This is the chapter, and in the second block it is still open — *he felt the cold of it before he felt anything else, and then it was warm all the way to his wrist.* It is written the way §Damage prescribes: name the cut and where it is, never the tissue, and `aftermath-kael` proves the register renders blood, bruising and torn cloth without difficulty.

---

### PLATE: empty

*Candidate 1.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`. Ch. 23 canon: *Lucan Drusane came to the measure the way he had been taught to. The front foot down, the back foot turned out, the sword up into the guard a paid master had spent four years putting into his shoulder. The off-hand open, empty, out to the side, because that was doctrine.* … *Then he came to his own mark, and he stopped. / He did not draw.* … *The two short blades were across his back in their wrap … they were not going to be near one today.* … *He put his hands at his sides and left them empty.* … *"You have to draw," Lucan said, from the measure.*

**THE SUBJECT IS TWO POSTURES AND THE FORTY FEET BETWEEN THEM.** Everything the chapter argues is in the bodies: one built by four years of a paid master into a shape that only works at a distance, the other standing with nothing in either hand and a bundle on his back he has decided not to open. **This is the silhouette-separation table doing the work it was written for** (`../README.md`) — one figure all reach and grammar, the other invisible by stillness.

**AND THE WRAP IS THE POINT OF THE PICTURE, SO IT IS WRITTEN IN BY HAND.** `../canon-brief.md` says his full-figure sheet carries no blades and that the wrap goes in when the moment carries it. This moment is entirely about the fact that it stays shut.


**RUN 1 (s48) — NOT ADOPTED, AND THE COMPOSITION IS INVERTED FOR RUN 2 (author: *not really a fan of this one, composition wise — feel like it should be the characters swapped, since we don't really care about detail on the other*).** The block put the near figure's detail on the boy nobody needs to see and sent the anchored one forty feet away. **The rule it breaks is the one `ch19/the-third-board` was rejected for**, one step sideways: the near field belongs to whoever the picture is about. The over-the-shoulder framing was chosen to solve Lucan's missing portrait file, and distance solves that just as well at no cost.

**TWO FAULTS THE RUN ALSO EXPOSED, BOTH FIXED IN THE SAME PASS.** *A long bundle wrapped in plain cloth and tied* came back reading as a **quiver** — a long thing slung on a back has an archer's default, which is §Nouns exactly, and the run 2 block bans it by name on §Absence's pull test. And *much mended* came back as **rags**: the boy is poor, not destitute, and `../canon-brief.md` says coarse frayed working cloth, never a costume of poverty. Run 2 says the cloth is whole and patched.

**WHAT LANDED AND IS KEPT.** The arena: banked seating packed with a wash of small shapes and no faces, the sand as bare unpainted paper, cold blue sky, the two figures the darkest marks on the floor. Lucan's guard was exactly right — front foot planted, back foot turned out, sword up, off-hand open and out to the side — and **his face never appeared**, which was the point of putting his back to us and is now done by distance instead.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch23-empty.png`, RUN 2 (author, s48: *i changed it for his gaze to be on him, all good, adopt*).**

**PROVENANCE, STATED PLAINLY: THE BLOCK BELOW IS NOT EXACTLY WHAT RAN.** The author adjusted the gaze clause at the tool before running, and this folder does not hold that wording, so the block stays marked `PLATE:` rather than `PLATE-VERBATIM:` — the same call as `ch22-the-box-from-behind.png`. *If the changed sentence is ever pasted back, it replaces the gaze line and the heading becomes VERBATIM.*

**BOTH RUN-1 FAULTS ARE FIXED.** The bundle is unmistakably a cloth package tied at both ends on a cord and **the quiver default is gone**; the wool is whole and patched, with a square patch on the sleeve, and reads poor-and-kept rather than destitute. The swap did what it was for: Kael large in the near field, the other boy small across the sand with the guard, the empty off-hand and no feature of his face drawn.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT MAY BE THE BETTER PICTURE.** The block asked for three-quarters with his face readable and it came back a **full back view** — an ear and a sliver of cheek. It puts the reader where the crowd is, makes the unopened bundle the subject, and leaves the chapter with one plate that has a face and one that does not.

**THE ARENA HELD EVERYTHING IT HELD IN Ch. 22:** packed banking as a faceless wash, the sand reserved as bare unpainted paper and brightest in the frame, cold blue sky, and the two figures the darkest marks on the floor.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same boy, for his build and his clothing.

Setting: the floor of a great open arena on a bright cold morning, as
loose wash suggestion — raked white sand underfoot, banked seating
rising behind and above, packed with people drawn only as a dense
wash of small shapes with no faces, and a cold blue sky at the top of
the frame. Bright flat winter daylight from above.

Composition: a wide view along the sand, at head height. In the near
foreground at one side, large in the frame and in frame from the hips
up, the dark-haired boy stands at his mark, turned three-quarters
away from the viewer toward the far side of the sand, so that his
face is seen in three-quarter view and one shoulder and part of his
back are toward the viewer. Across the open sand from him, at a
distance of many times his own height, a tall narrow boy stands
squared at the measure, small in the picture and drawn without detail
— no feature of his face is drawn at all — with the front foot
planted, the back foot turned out, a long straight sword raised into
a formal guard and his other hand open, empty, held out to the side.

THE DARK-HAIRED BOY HAS NOT DRAWN AND HIS HANDS ARE THE SUBJECT: both
arms hang at his sides, the hands open and completely empty, nothing
held in either of them and nothing raised. Across his back, slung over one
shoulder on a plain cord, rides a long bundle of coarse cloth wrapped
around something straight and tied at both ends — a plain cloth
package, not a quiver: no arrows, no fletching, no open top, no hilt,
no blade and no metal anywhere on it. It has not been touched.

He is standing still and giving nothing: the head level, the face
turned to the boy across the sand, the mouth closed, the whole body
easy and unarranged. Not braced, not defiant, not afraid. He is
looking at the other boy and not at the viewer.

Undyed coarse working wool in dull oatmeal and dead olive, whole and
sound and closed at the throat, worn soft and carrying square patches
at the elbow and the shoulder — poor working clothing kept in repair,
not rags, not torn, not ragged at the hem, with no ornament, badge,
colour or metal on him anywhere. He wears plain worn boots. The other
boy's clothing is well made and carefully worn, in darker cloth.

Nothing is happening yet: nobody has moved, nothing has been thrown,
and no blood, wound or mark is anywhere in the picture.

The raked sand is reserved as bare unpainted paper and is the
brightest thing in the frame; the two figures are the darkest marks
on it and carry the hardest edges. The crowd, the seating and the sky
stay at quiet middle values with soft edges, and nothing in the crowd
is drawn sharply enough to read as a person.

Preserve the dark-haired boy exactly as shown in his reference images
— face, hair, colouring; the two images are the same boy. Pale grey
eyes.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-horizon

*Candidate 2, and the chapter's peak.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 23 canon: *He looked out over the seaward rail while the noise came down on him. / From the middle of the floor you could see over the top of the far seating, and past it there was the sea, and on the sea there were more ships than he had known existed, and behind them a horizon that went on being a horizon. He had been looking at that line since he was four years old from a rock on a mountain, and it had never once had anything on it. Now it had banners on it* … *He was thirteen, and he was bleeding, and he had never in his life been so happy. / He did not notice that thought arrive.*

**THE FACE IS HAPPY AND DOES NOT KNOW IT**, which is the whole instruction. Not a smile: the page is explicit that the thought went into him *the way water goes into cloth* and that he did not notice it arrive. So the block asks for an open, unguarded face with nothing arranged on it — the same rarity as `ch19/will-i-be-allowed-to-hold-it`, and the second time in the book the coat is off him.

**THE HORIZON IS THE BOOK'S OLDEST OBJECT.** He has watched that line from a rock since he was four and it has never had anything on it. Putting ships and banners on it is the payoff of every sea-watching beat in Part I, and it costs one clause.

**THE ARM IS OPEN AND UNBOUND, WHICH DATES THE PICTURE EXACTLY.** Ruthen wraps it in four turns at the mouth of the gate a few minutes later; this is before that, on the floor, with the blood already down to the fingers.


**RUN 1 (s48) — REFUSED AS WRITTEN, THEN RUN BY THE AUTHOR ON A TWO-WORD CHANGE.** *had to change prompt to pass guidelines.* Everything else in the block identical: **`A long open cut` → `A long slash`**, and **`there is blood down the outside of the forearm` → `there is red down the outside of the forearm`.** The heading *HIS LEFT ARM IS CUT* and the later *torn where the cut is* both stayed and neither was a problem.

**THIS IS THE CLEANEST ISOLATION THE FOLDER HAS.** `ch21/dead` moved four variables at once and proved nothing about which one mattered; this moved two words in one sentence. **`../prompt-system.md` §Damage is amended on both counts**: a refusal is often a fault in the block rather than a limit on the moment, and the two nouns that bought this picture are named there. **Harm is not the barrier** — the same block draws a slash, torn cloth and red down a hand, and `aftermath-kael` has been rendering bruising and soot since the scene system was built.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch23-the-horizon.png` (author, s48: *adopt*). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S AS-RUN TEXT**, not the composed one: the folder's rule is that a plate's block is the text that made the picture, and the two changed words are `slash` and `red`.

**WHAT LANDED.** The turn-away with the head out to the sea; the far seating as a wash with no faces; the ships with banners at the mastheads as the only saturated colour in the frame; the horizon under cold blue sky; the sand as bare paper. The slash along the outside of the left arm above the elbow with red down the forearm, the arm bare and unbound — **which dates the picture before Ruthen's four turns of cloth.** Nothing in either hand and no weapon anywhere.

**WHAT DRIFTED.** The face reads a shade closer to stunned than to *giving everything away*; *not dazed* was banned by name and did not quite win. The tear in the cloth is larger than *torn where the cut is* — the whole shoulder is out. He reads fifteen or sixteen; the anchor.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the middle of the floor of a great open arena on a bright
cold morning, as loose wash suggestion — raked white sand, and beyond
the far side of the arena the top of the banked seating, and past
that open sea with many ships standing on it, small with the
distance, banners at their mastheads. A flat sea horizon runs across
the upper part of the frame under a cold blue sky. Bright flat
winter daylight from above.

Composition: chest-up, one figure, near the viewer and turned
three-quarters away, his head turned out toward the sea so that his
face is seen in three-quarter view. The bottom edge crosses him below
the chest. Behind and past him, small and low in the frame, the far
seating with a wash of standing people on it, no faces drawn, and
then the sea, the ships and the horizon line.

HIS LEFT ARM IS CUT. A long slash runs along the outside of the
left arm above the elbow, its edges clean, and there is red down
the outside of the forearm and across the back of the hand, dark and
wet. The arm is bare and unbound and no cloth or wrapping is on it.
His clothing is torn where the cut is and grubby with sand.

HIS FACE IS OPEN AND HE DOES NOT KNOW IT. The eyes wide and out on
the far horizon, the mouth closed, the brows lifted a little, the
whole face unguarded and giving everything away — and no smile
anywhere on it. Not laughing, not grinning, not triumphant, not
dazed, not in pain.

He is looking out at the ships on the horizon and nowhere else, not
at the viewer.

Undyed coarse working wool, much mended, dull oatmeal and dead olive,
with no ornament, badge, colour or metal on him anywhere. Nothing is
in either hand and no weapon is anywhere in the picture.

His face carries the sharpest step from light to dark and is the most
finished thing in the frame; the sand, the seating, the ships and the
sky stay at quiet middle values with soft edges. The banners on the
far masts are the only saturated colour anywhere in the picture.

Preserve him exactly as shown in his reference image — face, hair,
colouring. Pale grey eyes, the lightest value in his face. Dark hair
cut short and uneven.
```

# FILE: plates/ch24.md

# Ch. 24 — The Table · plate sheet

**Composed at s48 from the scene.** Kael is thirteen, on the evening of the first bout; Aeliana fourteen; Valeria thirteen. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png` and `images/full-figures/aeliana-15-full.png` on the first, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png` and `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE EMPIRE LOOKING AT HIM AND FINDING NOTHING, AND THE ONE PERSON WHO LOOKS AT HIM AND SMILES.** A room built to be looked at, with a boy turned to the lamp in it like a piece of work somebody has brought in — and, an hour later, a dark corner of a path where a girl turns her head and does something with her face that he has never seen her do.

**AELIANA'S FENCE, AGAIN, AND KEPT THE SAME WAY AS `ch22/the-box`.** Her beauty may never arrive as a verdict. Here the subject is **the appraisal itself** — *she did it the way you look at a horse, or a field, or a piece of work somebody has brought you* — so what the picture is about is a person examining another person, and her Ch. 24 canon is spent exactly as the page gives it: *a lighter thing now, there was less of it, and it had still taken somebody an hour. Her hair was down.*

**THE ARM IS DRAWN AS THE PAGE HAS IT AND IN THE WORDS THAT PASS.** §Damage, as amended at s48: `slash` and `red` carried `ch23/the-horizon` where `open cut` and `blood` were refused. This block needs neither — the arm is bound in four turns of a yard-master's cloth by now, stained where it has dried — and **the older mark going up out of the cuff is Ch. 24's own property** (`../canon-brief.md`: the burn on the left forearm, wrist to elbow), drawn here for the first time. Neither block states an age.

**AND THE ROOM IS THE ONLY INTERIOR IN THE BOOK WITH ANYTHING IN IT.** Every other interior the folder has drawn is a mountain house, a study, a hall or a brick court — undyed wool, plank walls, one lamp. This one is braziers burning coals in the spring, gold nailed along a screen standing in front of nothing, and a table with three joints and out-of-season fruit going over on it that nobody is eating. **The contrast is the chapter**, and it costs the block its longest paragraph, which is the right place to spend it.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: turn-him-toward-the-lamp

*Candidate 1.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png`, `images/full-figures/aeliana-15-full.png`. Ch. 24 canon: *She looked at him. / She did it the way you look at a horse, or a field, or a piece of work somebody has brought you — from the top, then across, then back to the top — and she went on doing it well past the point at which a person stops.* … *"Turn him toward the lamp," the princess said.* … *Kael had already worked out what was wanted and turned, so that the light came onto the side of his face. That was somehow worse than being told.* … *"There is nothing to him," the princess said.* And the room: *whole fish laid out in a row with their eyes in. Three joints … Fruit that did not grow on this island and did not grow anywhere at this time of year, piled, and some of it was already going. Flowers cut and lying along the middle of it, out of season, wilting in the heat off the braziers.* … *in a wide silver bowl, packed round a jug and sweating into the cloth, there was ice.* … *Nobody was eating.*

**THE SUBJECT IS A BOY BEING LOOKED AT, AND HE IS GIVING NOTHING BACK** — which is the one thing this book's whole coat was built for, tested here against the highest-status gaze in the empire. *He stood the way he stood and put his hands where his hands went and did not fill the silence.*

**THE TABLE IS THE ARGUMENT AND IT IS NOT THE SUBJECT.** It runs between them, loaded and blazing, with the ice in the middle of it — and no plate, no cup and nobody eating anywhere in the frame. §The winner and the rival: the food and the gold are the rival and they are enormous, so the two faces take the hardest edges and the food stays warm, soft-edged and unfinished.


**RUN 1 (s48) — NOT ADOPTED, ON ONE DEPTH FAULT (author: *its good, but Kael looks like he's on top of the table*).** Everything else arrived: the braziers, the hanging lamps, the gold panel behind the chair, the two attendants as shapes with undrawn faces, the table loaded end to end with fish, birds, loaves, piled fruit and cut flowers, **the ice in its silver bowl packed round a jug**, glass everywhere, and not one plate or cup in anybody's hands. Her chair, her hair down, the pale gown with gold at the collar — all as written.

**THE FAULT IS THAT THE TABLE HAS NO NEAR END.** The cloth and the food run out of the bottom of the frame and the boy stands against that edge with nothing between him and it, so the eye puts him *in* the food rather than a pace short of it. **A waist-up crop cannot show a floor**, and a figure standing beside a piece of furniture needs the floor to say where he is — the same fault §The cut names for a cropped figure hanging on bare paper, one object further along. Run 2 drops the crop to the knees, puts the table's end edge and corner fully in frame, and names a strip of visible floorboards between his body and it.

**AND HIS FACE TAKES THE s48 CORRECTION IN THE SAME PASS.** *The whole face plain and unarranged* is the same instruction that produced the vacancy the author flagged on `ch24/the-smile`; the paragraph now names the working part, per `../prompt-system.md` §Gaze as amended.


**THE OLDER MARK IN THIS BLOCK IS TWO CHAPTERS EARLY, AND THE PICTURE STANDS (author, s51).** *The burn on the left forearm, wrist to elbow* was taken from `../canon-brief.md`, which had it at Ch. 24; `03/kael` has it at **Ch. 26** — *the first mark on him he did not spend himself* — and Ch. 24 has no burn on the page at all. The brief was corrected at s51 and this plate is not re-cut. It joins §Pictures that stand as drawn. **Do not re-raise.**

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch24-turn-him-toward-the-lamp.png`, RUN 2 (author, s48: *pretty good, adopt*). Ch. 24 has its two.**

**BOTH FIXES LANDED.** The table has a near end: its edge, its corner and the hang of the cloth are all in frame, there are floorboards between the boy and it, and nothing of him overlaps the food — the depth reads on the first try once the crop dropped to the knees. **And the face is working rather than vacant:** the eyes level and steady on her down the length of the table, the mouth closed, the attention complete. The s48 amendment to §Gaze paid for itself one block after it was written.

**AND THE ROOM IS EVERYTHING THE CHAPTER PUT IN IT** — braziers burning in the spring, hanging lamps, the gold screen behind the chair, two attendants standing as shapes with faces undrawn, fish laid in a row with their eyes in, birds, loaves, piled fruit, cut flowers along the middle, glass everywhere, **the ice in its silver bowl packed round a jug** — and not one plate or cup in any hand in the picture.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.
Image 2: aeliana-15.png — portrait reference of the silver-haired girl.
Image 3: aeliana-15-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl, for her build. Her clothing in this picture is not the reference's and is described below.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a hot crowded room raised in timber, at night, as loose wash
suggestion — walls hung with cloth, lamps along both walls, and four
open braziers of burning coals standing in the room. A tall screen at
the back with gold beaten flat and nailed along its edge. The light
is warm and comes from many small sources at once; the corners are
dark.

Composition: two figures at the two ends of the frame with a long
table between them running away from the viewer, both in frame from
the knees up. The near end of the table is fully in the picture — its
end edge, its corner and the hang of the cloth over it all visible —
and the dark-haired boy stands beyond it, a full pace clear of that
end, with a strip of bare floorboards visible between his body and
the table. He is not touching the table, no part of him overlaps the
food, and the tabletop sits at the height of his hip. He is turned so
that the lamplight falls along one side of his face, his body squared
and his hands loose at his sides. The silver-haired girl sits beyond the far end of
the table in a high carved chair, facing him, her body still. Behind
her chair two women stand as plain shapes with their faces undrawn.
Nobody in the picture is eating and no plate, cup or dish is in
anybody's hands.

THE TABLE IS COVERED END TO END AND NOBODY HAS TOUCHED IT: whole fish
laid in a row with their eyes in, three cuts of roast meat, small
birds arranged in a pattern, piled fruit with some of it going soft,
loaves in a fancy shape, cut flowers lying wilting along the middle,
and glass everywhere. In the middle of it a wide silver bowl packed
with ice around a jug, sweating into the cloth beneath it.

SHE IS LOOKING HIM OVER THE WAY A PERSON LOOKS AT A HORSE OR A PIECE
OF WORK — from the top, then across, then back again, and going on
past the point at which a person stops. Her face is level and
unhurried and gives nothing away; the mouth closed, the brows
untroubled. Not smiling, not cruel, not curious, not kind. Her hands
rest in her lap and hold nothing.

HIS FACE IS TURNED INTO THE LAMPLIGHT AND GIVES HER NOTHING BACK,
AND IT IS WORKING WHILE IT DOES IT: the eyes level and steady on her,
taking her in and reading the room behind her, the mouth closed, the
jaw easy, the attention complete and unhurried. Not proud, not
ashamed, not defiant, not frightened, and not blank or empty.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER, down the length of the
table. This eye contact is the subject of the image, and neither of
them is looking at the viewer.

Her hair is silver-white, colourless, with no yellow or gold in it,
and it is down and loose. Her gown is a light one, close and plain
and finely made, in pale grey-blue with a little gold at the collar
and nothing else on it — much less on it than a court gown, and no
jewellery.

He wears undyed coarse working wool in dull oatmeal and dead olive,
whole and patched and closed at the throat, with no ornament, badge,
colour or metal anywhere on him. His left arm is bound above the
elbow in four turns of plain cloth, stained dark brown where it has
dried. Below the binding, on the same forearm, an older healed mark
runs up out of the cuff toward the elbow, pale against the skin.

Their two faces carry the sharpest step from light to dark and are
the most finished things in the picture. The table, the food, the
gold, the braziers and the standing women all stay at warm middle
values with soft edges and no sharp detail, and nothing on the table
competes with the two faces for finish.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring; the two images of the girl are the same girl. Do not
blend their features. Do not mix their clothing: the fine pale gown
and the gold belong only to her, the mended working wool only to him.
Her eyes pale blue, his pale grey.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-smile

*Candidate 2, and the chapter's last beat.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 24 canon: *Valeria walked out with him as far as the corner where the sea court path went up. / "I am going to miss this," she said.* … *And Valeria Vel turned her head and smiled at him. / It was not much of a smile. It was there and gone, and it did not do anything a smile is supposed to do to a face. Kael had watched her for many mornings from a treeline and stood across from her every day for a year. He had seen her satisfied. He had seen her bored. He had seen her decide not to hit somebody. He had never once seen this.*

**THE PAGE SPENDS THREE SENTENCES SAYING WHAT THIS FACE IS NOT, WHICH IS THE BLOCK'S INSTRUCTION.** *It did not do anything a smile is supposed to do to a face* — so the mouth moves and nothing else does: the eyes do not crease, the cheeks do not lift, the brows do not move. **That is the exact opposite of `ch18/caught-it-halfway`**, where the cheek had to come up with the mouth for a beginning of a smile to read; here the cheek staying down is the whole point, and the two blocks are a matched pair for anybody reading the folder for how a mouth is written.

**AND IT IS THE ONLY WARM-LIT NIGHT EXTERIOR THE SET HAS.** One lamp left behind them in the court, low and to one side; everything else black. `ch01/fallen-stars` is the night picture and it is lit by a town at distance; this is lit by a lamp six feet away and out of frame.


**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch24-the-smile.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s48: *its good, adopt*).**

**THE SMILE CAME BACK AS WRITTEN**, which was the hard part: the corners barely lifted, the mouth closed, the whole thing already going. Everything else held — her head turned back over the shoulder, her hand on the strap, the vambrace and the grey-blue underlayer, his patched wool and the cloth binding above the left elbow, eye contact as the subject with neither of them at the viewer. **And the light is the set's first close warm night source**: one lamp out of frame, a wash of it on the brick and a patch on the path, everything else black.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT IS MINE.** Her eyes warm a fraction with the mouth where the block said nothing else moves — a notch fonder than *it did not do anything a smile is supposed to do to a face.* **And the author's note on the other face is the finding worth keeping: *Kael is kind of expressionless.*** The block asked for exactly that — *his face is doing nothing at all* — and the tool executed it literally. **A face given nothing renders as vacancy, and vacancy is not the coat.** `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` got this right by giving the still face one working part — *the face level, the eyes steady on the man, the jaw set but easy* — and every future Kael block should name the one thing that is working rather than saying the face does nothing.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the corner of a path outside a brick court at night, as
loose wash suggestion — a brick wall implied close behind them, a
dark path going up out of the frame, and black night sky above. One
lamp stands out of frame behind them in the court: its light is low
and warm on one side of each face and everything away from it is
dark.

standing a pace apart and turned three-quarters toward each other,
having stopped on their way apart. She has turned her head back over
her shoulder to him. A case on a strap rides her shoulder and her
hand is on the strap.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER. This eye contact is the
subject of the image and neither of them is looking at the viewer.

HER FACE IS THE SUBJECT AND THE SMILE IS ALMOST NOTHING: the corners
of the mouth have lifted very slightly and nothing else in her face
has moved with them — the eyes are unchanged and do not crease, the
cheeks do not lift, the brows do not move, the head is still. It is a
small closed-mouth smile on an otherwise level face, and it is
already going. Not a grin, no teeth, not laughing, not fond, not sad.
