# FILE: plates/ch25.md

# Ch. 25 — The Door · plate sheet

**Composed at s51 from the scene, and written under the register** (`../prompt-system.md` §Plate template). Kael is thirteen, on the night after the first bout and the dawn before the second. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/portraits/neris.png` on both, `images/portraits/vask.png` on the first.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S OWN SPLIT.** A night table with a lamp and a pot of mint on it, and a stone step at first light. The argument runs through both halves and is never said out loud: **the two people who have run this boy as an operation for eleven years have stopped running him.** Every signal of it is postural — a man who does not sit is sitting, a woman who asks thirty questions asks three, a pot comes off the fire, a hand goes somewhere it has never gone in front of him, and at dawn they are both at the door.

**SO NEITHER BLOCK ARGUES THE ARGUMENT.** A picture cannot say *never*. The first one gets the father into a chair with a cup in front of him, which is the only picture in the set of that man at rest, and the second puts the mother's hands somewhere and lets how long they stay be the content.

**THE ONE-LAMP INTERIOR IS THE SET'S FOURTH** — `ch12/the-law-on-the-table`, both `ch19` blocks and `ch21/the-nine-things` are all a plank table at night with one light on it. **What makes this one different is what is not happening at it:** those four are a refusal, a confession, an asking and a measurement. Nobody at this table is being taught, tested, refused or debriefed. It is the only happy picture in the folder, and it is worth the repeat of the furniture.

**THE DEMOTION IS SPENT ONCE, ON THE SECOND BLOCK ONLY.** Post-register evidence for it is `ch18/caught-it-halfway` run 1 (s48), where pale pinned sheets beside a face were held at quiet middle values and the sharpest step sited on the face — *the rival demoted on both channels exactly as written*. That is what a rival is: something pale, large or bright that will eat the subject. **Two hands against two faces at close range is that. Three faces alone in a dark room is not**, so the first block does not carry the clause and the crop does the work instead, which is what `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick` found when the value-structure paragraph came out of it under the s47 rewrite and the near face stayed the finished one anyway.

**AND THE DOOR REPEATS A PICTURE THE BOOK ALREADY HAS.** `ch02/the-doorframe` is this woman inside this doorway with a hand up on the frame, and the page hands Ch. 25 the same posture — *one hand on the frame, and the whole weight of her carried forward.* The block declines it: the hands come off the timber and onto the boy's face, the door goes behind her as a dark rectangle instead of around her, and the light is cold pre-dawn instead of flat overcast. Two adopted plates of one woman in one doorway is the thing being avoided, and it is avoided by composition.

**NEITHER BLOCK STATES AN AGE**, as `ch24.md`'s pair did not: the anchor carries it, and §Injury has one known refusal shape — a stated age beside an injury — and the boy's arm is bound in both pictures.

**THE ARM IS CONTINUOUS WITH `ch24/turn-him-toward-the-lamp`:** four turns of plain cloth above the left elbow, stained where it has dried, and the older healed burn running up out of the cuff on the same forearm. The page re-tightens it on the step, so the second block draws it fresher.

**AND THE PREGNANCY IS FRAMED OUT OF BOTH BLOCKS** (author, s51, on run 1 of the first: *probably should have avoided illustrations with Neris' body showing — it is very weird with the guidelines and pregnancy*). The ruling is on `../README.md` and the fence is on `../canon-brief.md`. It is not a deletion of canon: the page's *"How close?" / "Days. A week."* stays true and the pictures simply do not answer it, which is what the hard rule prescribes — **a plate answers with framing where it cannot show.** So the term, the carry and the shape are named nowhere, nothing below her collar is authored, and what carries her state instead is posture and cloth: side-on in the chair with her weight back at the table, the shawl and the forward-carried weight at the door. `ch21/the-nine-things` predates the ruling and stands.

**WATCH THE CROP.** `ch20` came back a notch loose off a named landmark for the fourth recorded time. Both blocks name the landmark tighter than the line they want.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: four-seconds

*Candidate 3.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`, `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 25 canon: *there was one lamp lit because the second one was for work.* … *His father got up, went to the fire, came back with the pot, and poured. The smell of it came up between them. It was the mint she grew by the step, and he had drunk it after every hurt of his entire life.* … *"Your sibling is very close now," Neris said. / "How close?" / "Days. A week." She put her hand on the top of it, which she had never done in front of him, and left it there.* … *"You keep saying he." / "I do," his mother agreed. "I have no evidence whatever. Do not write it down." / Kael laughed — properly, once, out of the middle of him — and his father made the small sound in his nose that was as far as his father went, and for about four seconds the three of them sat in a one-room house on a mountain with a lamp and a pot of mint between them, and that was all that was happening anywhere.*

**THE SUBJECT IS THREE FACES DOING THREE DIFFERENT AMOUNTS OF THE SAME THING**, and the amounts are the three people. The boy laughs with the whole of his face. The mother has just made the joke and has not moved her mouth. The father breathes out through his nose, and that is his entire contribution to the happiest four seconds in the book so far.

**IT IS THE MATCHED OPPOSITE OF `ch24/the-smile`, ONE CHAPTER LATER.** There a mouth moved and nothing else in the face went with it, and the cheek staying down was the whole point. Here everything moves at once, because the page says *properly, once, out of the middle of him*, and this face has not done that anywhere in twenty-five chapters.

**VASK GETS A WORKING PART**, per §Gaze as amended at s48. *The small sound in his nose* is a gift — it is an instruction to a specific piece of anatomy, so the block names the breath and leaves the rest of the face where it was. This is the one picture in the set where vacancy would be a lie about him.

**HER HAND IS IN FRAME AND OCCUPIED**, which `03/neris` requires of any scene — and after the s51 ruling it cannot be occupied by the pregnancy, which run 1 drew as a body. The same chapter hands over another job for it four lines earlier: *She turned the cup round on the table with two fingers.* Page canon, the same table, the same minute, and it keeps her hands where the book keeps her without describing anything below her collar.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch25-four-seconds.png`, RUN 2 (author, s51: *this one is good*).**

**BOTH FIXES LANDED IN ONE PASS.** The laugh kept its mouth, its teeth and its lifted cheeks and **gave back the eyes** — open, creased only at the outer corners, the pale grey clear in them — and with the eye line legible again he reads adolescent instead of nine. **The authored hair is the other half**: dark, straight, hacked short and uneven, the long neck and the jaw with an edge on it, which is `kael-14.png` and was not there on run 1. **An expression override large enough to win takes the identity with it unless the identity is authored alongside** — that is the session's finding and this is the picture that proves the correction.

**AND THE s51 PREGNANCY RULING WORKS AS COMPOSITION.** Nothing in the frame reads as a body: she sits side-on with her weight back, the table crosses in front of her, and **her hand is on the cup she turns with two fingers** — page canon from four lines earlier, her hands in frame and occupied as `03/neris` requires, and not one clause below her collar. The chapter's fact is intact and the picture simply does not answer it.

**THE TABLE IS EXACTLY THE FOUR THINGS**: the clay pot with its handle and lip, three cups with green leaves in them, the lamp behind. Plank wall, corners black, warm low light, nobody looking at the viewer, all three in mended undyed wool, the binding above the left elbow.

**WHAT DRIFTED, none of it worth a run.** Vask's one working part did not render — the nostril and the lifted upper lip are not legible, and he reads closed and faintly sombre rather than a man who has just breathed out through his nose. **The smallest facial instruction in the folder so far, and it is below the threshold at this scale**; the same §The winner and the rival limit that lost the tow on `ch21/the-nine-things`, one channel over. Her hair reads half back rather than fully loose. The lamp has its glass chimney for the fourth time. The tool chose landscape unasked, as it did on `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick`.

**RUN 1 (s51) — NOT ADOPTED (author: *kael looks too young here*). THE LAUGH LANDED AND IT TOOK THE ANCHOR WITH IT.**

**WHAT ARRIVED, AND MOST OF IT IS THE BLOCK.** The room: plank wall, corners black, one lamp warm and low, and the table holding exactly the four things it was given — the clay pot with its handle and lip, three cups with green leaves in them, nothing else. Vask on the far side facing the boy, settled back, shoulders down, his face closed and his eyes on him. Neris side-on at the right with her weight back and her right hand flat on top of the pregnancy, drawn exactly as the block asked. **That is the clause the author ruled out of the folder** (`../README.md`, s51) — it renders as a body, and the run is the evidence for the rule. All three in mended undyed wool. Nobody looking at the viewer. **And the laugh is exactly what was asked for**: mouth open, cheeks lifted, eyes creased, one short laugh caught in the middle of itself. That was the hard part and it is not the problem.

**THE PROBLEM IS THAT THE BOY IS NOT HIS REFERENCE.** `images/portraits/kael-14.png` is straight dark hair hacked short and uneven, a long neck, a jaw with an edge on it, a sharp cheekbone and pale grey eyes that are the lightest value in the face. The picture returned tousled brown curls, a round face with no jaw, a short neck and **the eyes creased shut**. The two adults are unmistakably theirs. He is the only one who lost, and he is the only one carrying an extreme expression.

**SO THE FINDING IS NEW AND IT IS THE INVERSE OF ONE THE FOLDER ALREADY OWNS.** `../prompt-system.md` §Scenes and plates says the reference's mood beats the prompt in a close crop. **The other direction is now on record: an expression override large enough to win can take the identity out with it.** `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick` got a full grin and kept its face — but that face is broad, well-fed and soft-jawed, so the grin cost it nothing it was identified by. Kael is identified by the hard jaw, the long neck and the grey, and a laugh with the eyes shut deletes all three at once.

**WHICH IS ALSO WHY HE READS YOUNG, AND THE TWO FAULTS ARE ONE FAULT.** §AGE's eye line is the only age instrument that works on its own — and **a creased-shut eye has no eye line to read.** The block deliberately stated no age (§Injury's refusal shape), so with the rung gone there was nothing left holding him at thirteen.

**RUN 2 CHANGES HIS PARAGRAPH AND NOTHING ELSE.** The laugh keeps its mouth and cheeks and gives back the eyes — creased and still open, the grey visible — the head comes level instead of tipped back, and the adolescent rung goes in **as anatomy with no number in it**, which is how §AGE's own adolescent block is written and costs no refusal risk. The hair is authored in his paragraph rather than left to the reference, since the reference did not carry it. **One paragraph, one channel, a clean test.**

**AND ONE DRIFT THAT IS NOT WORTH A RUN OF ITS OWN.** The binding came back low on the forearm rather than in four turns above the left elbow, where `ch24/turn-him-toward-the-lamp` put it. It is small in frame and half behind the table. The lamp has a tall glass chimney again — third time, after `ch12/the-law-on-the-table` and `ch21/the-nine-things`, both adopted with it, so it is this folder's lamp and not a fault. Neris reads a shade wary where the block asked for level with the eyes full on her son, and her left forearm went to the chair back instead of the tabletop.

**THE TABLE HOLDS FOUR THINGS.** One lamp, a clay pot, three cups with green leaves in them. The sheet of paper her hand was flat on when he came in is deliberately off it: by this beat *that was all that was happening anywhere*, and a written thing on the boards is the other half of the chapter leaking into this one.

laugh. One short laugh caught in the middle of itself. His eyes sit

and the neck is long — the laugh lifts the cheeks and does not round
the face. His hair is dark and straight, hacked short and uneven and
falling over the forehead.

THE WOMAN HAS JUST SAID THE THING HE IS LAUGHING AT AND HER FACE HAS
NOT MOVED WITH IT: the mouth closed and level, the brows unmoved. The
one thing working in her face is that her eyes are full on her son
and she has not looked away. Not smiling, not warm, not amused on the
surface.

THE MAN HAS BREATHED OUT ONCE THROUGH HIS NOSE AND THAT IS ALL: the
nostrils widened a fraction, the upper lip lifted very slightly with
it, the mouth closed, the eyes steady on the boy. Not smiling, not
stern, not sad, and not blank or empty.

Her right hand is on the table turning one of the cups round with two
fingers, unhurried. Nothing is lifted or held in any other hand.

The boy is looking at the woman, and both adults are looking at the
boy. Nobody is looking at the viewer.

All three wear plain undyed village working wool, coarse and much
mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no ornament and no metal
on any of them. The boy's left arm is bound above the elbow in four
turns of plain cloth, stained dark brown where it has dried.

Nobody else is in the room.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. The boy's
eyes are pale near-white grey; both adults' eyes are dark.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: both-hands

*Candidate 5, and the chapter's last beat.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/neris.png`. Ch. 25 canon: *They were both at the door.* … *His mother was at the door too, with a shawl round her and one hand on the frame and the whole weight of her carried forward, at an hour she had no business being out of a chair, on a stone step in a spring wind.* … *Kael stood on the step in the cold with the mountain going grey behind the house* … *And Neris took Kael's face in both hands, which she had done perhaps eleven times in his life. She looked at it. She said nothing. Then she let go. / "Evening," said Vask. / "Evening," said Kael, and went down the hill.*

**THE PAGE GIVES HER NO EXPRESSION, SO THE BLOCK DOES NOT INVENT ONE.** *She looked at it. She said nothing.* Three sentences, all of them verbs of duration — and duration is the one thing a still picture can carry. The content is a pair of hands that have been somewhere longer than they need to be, on a face holding still for it, and neither face is doing anything a viewer could name.

**THE FATHER IS OUT OF FRAME AND THE SHEET SAYS WHY.** *They were both at the door* is the title beat and this block takes half of it, because the hands have to win and a third face is a rival. His half is candidate 6 and stays on the shortlist unspent.

**THIS IS THE PAIR'S ONE DEMOTION AND IT IS THE `ch18` RECIPE.** Two hands against two faces at close range: faces win that by default, so both are held at quiet middle values in the same breath as the hands are promoted, and the sharpest step is sited on her knuckles.

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch25-both-hands.png`, RUN 2 (author, s51: *great*). Ch. 25 has its two.**

**BOTH FACES CAME ALIVE ON HOLDABLE STATES.** Hers: the lids raised, the eyes fixed hard on him, the brows drawn a fraction, the jaw set, the mouth closed and just perceptibly pressed — a woman holding something down, which is the page without a word of it being invented. His: the eyes fully open and up on hers with the grey clear, the brows lifted a fraction, the mouth soft and unpressed — caught, not composed. **The correction is proved: a working part must be a state the picture can hold, and the movement clause that replaced it in run 1 rendered as nothing at all.**

**AND BOTH HANDS ARRIVED ONCE THEY WERE SITED AGAINST THE VIEWER** rather than against each other — the far hand round the cheek turned away, the near hand on the cheek turned toward — so *she had done it perhaps eleven times in his life* has its count on the page and in the picture. **The demotion held for the second run running:** the knuckles and the tendons are the most finished drawing in the frame and both faces sit softer than them.

**THE REST IS AS WRITTEN.** The doorway a dark rectangle behind her with the thatch edge above it, the mountain going light behind him, cold flat pre-dawn with no hard-edged shadow; the shawl slipped, her hair loose in a cold wind; his hair straight and hacked short, the binding above the left elbow; eye contact the subject with neither of them at the viewer; no embrace, nobody weeping, nobody else in the frame.

**WHAT DRIFTED: the crop, for the sixth recorded time**, at the hip against *the lower ribs*. Adopted as drawn.

**RUN 1 (s51) — author: *i like it. their expressions are kind of a bit dead tho.* NOT ADOPTED; RUN 2 CHANGES THE TWO EXPRESSION PARAGRAPHS AND ONE CLAUSE.**

**WHAT LANDED, AND TWO OF THEM ARE EVIDENCE.** The doorway a dark rectangle behind her, the slope behind him going light, cold flat pre-dawn with no hard-edged shadow; both in profile at the same height, the eye contact the subject and neither of them at the viewer; the shawl slipped, her hair loose in a cold wind; no embrace, nobody weeping, nobody else in the frame. **The hand won its demotion** — knuckles and tendons the most finished drawing in the picture, both faces held softer. **And Kael is his reference for the first time this session**: straight dark hair hacked short and uneven, the long neck, the jaw with an edge on it, the grey reading clear. The authored hair clause is why, and `four-seconds` run 1 is why it was there — **a reference that loses a feature once should be authored in every block that follows.** The binding is Ch. 24's, four turns above the left elbow, stained where it dried.

**THE FIRST FAULT IS MINE AND IT IS A NEW SHAPE OF THE s48 ONE.** §Gaze says never tell a face to do nothing, and both paragraphs obeyed it in letter: each named a working part. **But hers was a movement** — *the eyes go over his face from one part of it to another and do not leave it* — **and a still picture cannot draw a movement.** Strip it and what she was actually given is a closed mouth, unmoved brows and four negations. His is worse: *the head steady, the mouth closed, the jaw easy, the eyes up on hers* are four absences of motion and not one visible state. **So the amended §Gaze needs a second half: a working part must be a STATE THE PICTURE CAN HOLD, not an action the face is performing.** Vacancy came back by a different door.

**RUN 2 GIVES EACH FACE ONE THING THAT CAN BE DRAWN STILL.** Hers: the lids fully raised and the eyes wide and fixed, the brows drawn a fraction together, the jaw set, the lips just perceptibly pressed — **a face working, not a face at rest** — with one negation instead of four. His: the eyes fully open and up on hers with the grey clear, the brows lifted a fraction, the mouth closed but unpressed — caught rather than composed, which is `../prompt-system.md` §Gaze's *not smiling — just not braced.* Neither invents a feeling the page does not give; both replace an unrenderable instruction with a renderable one.

**THE SECOND FAULT IS THE PICTURE'S TITLE.** Only one hand arrived. *She took Kael's face in both hands, which she had done perhaps eleven times in his life* — **the count is the beat**, and two hands in a profile is geometrically awkward enough that the tool resolved it by dropping one. Run 2 sites them relative to the viewer rather than to each other: the far hand on the cheek away from the viewer, the near hand on the cheek toward it, both visible.

**AND THE CROP CAME BACK LOOSE FOR THE FIFTH RECORDED TIME**, at the hip against *the lower ribs*, after `ch17` twice and `ch20` twice. Not corrected — the author likes the picture — and it is now well past `../prompt-system.md` §Findings' bar on recurrence, held back only by the correction not having been wanted every time. It has still never been wanted.

**THE ONE REAL RISK IS SENTIMENT** — a mother holding a boy's face at dawn pulls hard toward an embrace and toward tears, and `../canon-brief.md` has this woman *not warm, not maternal, not soft.* §Absence takes that pull and nothing else.

```

ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the doorway of a small stone-and-turf mountain house before
sunrise, as loose wash suggestion. She stands in the open doorway and
he stands outside it on a stone step; the room behind her is a dark
rectangle going into deep shadow, and behind him a bare mountain
slope rises in pale cool grey and is going light. Cold flat pre-dawn
light, no sun and no hard-edged shadow.

Composition: two figures close and near the viewer, seen from a
little to one side, their faces at nearly the same height and turned
toward each other. The bottom edge of the picture cuts both of them
at the lower ribs, so her forearms are in frame to the elbow and no
legs are in the picture.

HER TWO HANDS ARE ON HIS FACE AND THEY ARE THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE.
One hand laid flat along each side of his face, the palms on his
cheekbones and the fingers running back toward his ears. They are
still and settled rather than just arrived, relaxed against the skin,
not gripping and not turning his head. Both hands are visible: the far
one on the cheek turned away from the viewer, the near one on the
cheek turned toward the viewer. Her hands are the only place the two
of them are touching. His own hands hang at his sides, empty.

HER FACE IS WORKING AND IT IS NOT AT REST: the lids fully raised and
the eyes wide open and fixed hard on his face, the brows drawn a
fraction together, the jaw set, the mouth closed with the lips just
perceptibly pressed. She is holding something down. Not tearful.

HE HAS BEEN CAUGHT BY THIS AND HAS NOT ARRANGED HIS FACE YET: the
eyes fully open and up on hers with the pale grey clear in them, the
brows lifted a fraction, the mouth closed but soft and unpressed, the
head still in her hands. Not braced, and not blank or empty.

The two are looking directly at each other and neither is looking at
the viewer.

BOTH FACES ARE HELD AT QUIET MIDDLE VALUES, softly drawn and
understated, with no hard edges and no strong dark in them, so that
the sharpest step from light to dark in the whole picture happens at
her hands — the knuckles and the outer edge of the fingers where they
cross his cheek, drawn pale and hard and the most precisely finished
thing in the image.

She is leaning out of the doorway with her weight carried forward
onto the step. A heavy coarse wool shawl in dull grey-brown is round
her shoulders and has slipped a little; under it plain village
working cloth in undyed oatmeal. Her long dark brown hair is loose
and moving in a cold wind. His hair is dark and straight, hacked
short and uneven. He wears undyed coarse working wool, much mended,
in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no metal on him; his left arm is
bound above the elbow in four clean turns of plain cloth, freshly
tightened and stained dark brown lower down.

They are not embracing and nobody is weeping. Nobody else is in the
picture.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. Her eyes
are dark; his are pale near-white grey.
```

---

# FILE: plates/ch26.md

# Ch. 26 — The Third Bell · plate sheet

**Composed at s51 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Elarine thirteen. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE BOY FINDING OUT WHAT HE IS, AND THE GIRL TELLING HIM WHAT HE DID.** *It was the face of a boy doing the only thing in the world he had never had to be taught to want* — and, twenty minutes later, against the timber of the eastern gate, *"You were not kind to me." / "No." / "Good."* Nothing between them is drawn.

**AND THAT IS THE DESIGN CONSTRAINT, NOT SQUEAMISHNESS.** Most of this chapter is two thirteen-year-olds cutting and burning each other, and `ch21/dead` was refused with far less in the frame — nothing cut, no blood, the edge stopped clear, the adult out of frame from the elbow up. What that refusal exposed is that **the boundary is a weapon held against a child, whatever the weapon is doing.** So neither block puts a blade in frame at all: the first crops above the hands by name, and the second is after, with both swords on the ground.

**WHAT DAMAGE IS DRAWN IS DRAWN IN THE WORDS THAT PASS.** §Injury, as amended at s48 off `ch23/the-horizon`: `slash` carried where `open cut` was refused, `red` where `blood` was. So the smear on his teeth is **red**, and the mark on his face is named as singeing and reddened skin, never as tissue. **Elarine needs none of it** — Severin mends her on the sand inside the chapter, so by the gate her clothing is torn and dark-stained and the skin under it is whole, which is both the page and the safest thing to draw.

**THE BURN IS DRAWN FOR THE FIRST TIME AND IT IS THIS CHAPTER'S PROPERTY.** `03/kael`: *the Ch. 26 burn — the first mark on him he did not spend himself — wrist to elbow on the LEFT forearm*, lying over the blade scar. Severin mends it inside the chapter, so at the gate it is *a long pink new thing that would be a scar by the turn of the year* — pink, new and unbound, with the sleeve gone. **`../canon-brief.md` had this at Ch. 24 and was corrected against `03` at s51**; `ch24/turn-him-toward-the-lamp` drew it two chapters early and stands unless the author rules otherwise.

**BOTH BLOCKS AUTHOR THE HAIR**, per the s51 finding: `ch25/four-seconds` run 1 lost Kael's hair to the reference and got it back only when the block wrote it. A reference that drops a feature once is authored in every block after.

**AND BOTH FACES ARE WRITTEN AS STATES THE PICTURE CAN HOLD**, per the other s51 finding — `ch25/both-hands` run 1 came back dead because the working part it named was a movement. Neither block here asks a face to perform an action.

**WATCH THE CROP.** Five recorded instances of a named landmark coming back a notch loose. Both blocks name it tighter than the line they want.

### PLATE: the-good-part

*Candidate 3.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 26 canon: *And Elarine, backing off her lead foot and getting her point into line for a boy who was already inside where a longsword works, saw his face. / She would think about it for a long time and never once say it to him. / He was smiling. / Not a fighter's grin, and nothing to do with her, and nothing in it that was cruel about anybody. It was the face of a boy doing the only thing in the world he had never had to be taught to want. There was blood on his teeth from somewhere in the last two minutes and he did not know it was there, and he did not know his mouth was doing anything at all.* And the state he is in: *he turned his head and lost his eyebrow and some of his hair and kept coming.*

**THE VIEWER STANDS WHERE ELARINE STANDS, AND THAT SOLVES THE GAZE.** `ch18` lost two runs to a sightline whose object sat outside the frame, and its finding was that **the tool places the object and the head independently** — no wording couples them. Here the object of his look is the person the picture is composed from, so his eyes come straight out of the picture and the instruction is *at the viewer* rather than a ban. **It also makes the reader be the girl he is coming at**, which is the sentence the page actually wrote: *saw his face.*

**THE CROP IS THE INSTRUMENT AND IT IS SPENT ON THE WEAPONS.** §The cut, named as a landmark with what it removes: the bottom edge crosses him below the collarbone, so no arms, no hands and no blades are anywhere in the frame. It costs the beat its *short blade in each hand* — and it buys the only thing that matters here, which is that this is a picture of a face and not a picture of an armed child.

**THE HARD PART IS THAT HE DOES NOT KNOW HE IS DOING IT.** Unawareness is not a feature and cannot be drawn; what can be drawn is a face with nothing arranged on it — the mouth doing the whole of the work and the brows, the chin and the carriage taking no part. The negations go on the archetype the frame pulls toward, per §Absence: a bloodied boy mid-fight pulls hard at a snarl and at triumph, and at nothing else.

**DROPPED (author, s51: *drop both of those*), with run 2 built and never run.** Run 1 was liked (*its good*) and the exertion pass was composed and then let go; the chapter has three plates without it. **What the run taught is kept below** — §The cut is what made this chapter drawable at all, and *a face is not a state; a body under load is* is the note worth carrying. Not re-run, not re-proposed.

**RUN 1 (s51) — author: *its good. but maybe this feeling of exertion could be there more.* RUN 2 ADDED THE BODY AND FIXED ONE DRIFT.**

**THE CROP DID ITS JOB AND THE FILTER NEVER HAD A QUESTION TO ANSWER.** No arms, no hands, nothing held, anywhere in the frame — so a chapter that could not be drawn at all came back on the first run, as a face. **§The cut is the instrument that made this chapter drawable**, and it is worth saying plainly: the way past the `ch21/dead` boundary is not a wording change, it is a landmark on the body.

**AND THE HARD PART LANDED.** The brows level and unarranged, the chin neither raised nor dropped, nothing posed into triumph — *delight, plain and unguarded* rather than a fighter's grin. The red on the teeth arrived as a detail and not a subject. The seating behind as a wash with no face resolved, flat daylight, the grey clear and his, the hair straight and hacked short.

**WHAT IS MISSING IS THE BODY, WHICH THE BLOCK NEVER ASKED FOR.** He is three minutes into the hardest fight of his life with his shoulder opened and his ear singing, and the block described a face and a costume and nothing in between — no sweat, no breath, no flush. **A face is not a state; a body under load is.** The block got the expression it wrote and no more, which is the system working exactly as designed and the design being short one paragraph.

**AND THE EYEBROW DID NOT GO, WHICH IS §BAN THE SHAPE.** *One eyebrow is gone* is a negation of a feature, and the folder already knows that several negations aimed at one feature remove nothing — the fix is to describe what is there instead. Run 2 gives the hairs stopping short against bare skin.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the boy.

Setting: the sand floor of a great arena on a bright morning, as loose
wash suggestion — pale raked sand under him, and behind him the banked
seating rising as a wash of small shapes with no face resolved
anywhere in it. Flat daylight, no sun and no hard-edged shadow.

Composition: one figure, close and filling the frame, coming forward
toward the viewer at a walk. The bottom edge of the picture crosses
him below the collarbone, so that no arms, no hands and nothing held
in them are anywhere in the frame. Nobody else is in the picture.

HE IS SMILING AND HE DOES NOT KNOW HE IS DOING IT: the mouth open a
little with the corners lifted and the teeth showing, the cheeks
lifted with it — and the rest of the face taking no part at all, the
brows level and unarranged, the chin neither raised nor dropped. It is
delight, plain and unguarded and turned toward nobody. Not a snarl,
not a grimace, not a grin of triumph, and nothing cruel or savage
anywhere in it.

His eyes are wide open and bright and completely engaged, the pale
grey clear in them.

HE HAS BEEN FIGHTING FOR MINUTES AND HIS BODY IS UNDER LOAD: the hair
at his temples and across his forehead darkened and stuck down with
sweat, sweat standing on the face and running in clean tracks down
through the soot, the skin flushed with effort under the weather, the
chest lifted high with the breath, the tendons standing in the neck.

A smear of red across his front teeth.

HE IS LOOKING DIRECTLY OUT OF THE PICTURE AT THE VIEWER and his are
the only eyes in the frame.

The outer half of one eyebrow is singed away to bare skin, the hairs
stopping short, and the hair above it on that side is singed short and
uneven. The skin high on that cheek is reddened. Grey soot and pale
sand dust across the face and in the creases of the neck.

His hair is dark and straight, hacked short and uneven, falling over
the forehead and disordered. He wears undyed coarse working wool,
much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, torn open at the
shoulder, with no ornament and no metal anywhere on him.

Preserve him exactly as shown in the reference image — face, hair,
colouring, apparent age. His eyes are pale near-white grey.
```

### PLATE: not-kind-to-me

*Candidate 5, and the chapter's last exchange.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 26 canon: *They got Elarine up at the eastern gate, sitting, her back against the timber. Somebody had brought water. She had refused to be carried. / Kael came and stood in front of her because there was nowhere else he was going. / Somebody had put her sword down beside her, and she had not looked at it once. Her hands lay in her lap with the palms turned up, the way you rest hands that have been doing one thing for a long time. She looked up at him for a while without saying anything, and there was something happening in her face that he could see with total clarity and could not read at all. / "You were not kind to me," she said. / "No." / "Good."* And her state: *her hair had come out of its binding down one side* · *His arm was a long pink new thing.*

**HER HANDS ARE THE PICTURE'S QUIET CENTRE AND THE PAGE HANDS THEM OVER.** *Palms turned up, the way you rest hands that have been doing one thing for a long time* — a Resonant's hands, both of them, empty and open and facing up, at the end of a morning in which she spent everything that was ever in them. She is the girl whose doctrine is that one hand stays free for the Art, and both of them are lying in her lap doing nothing.

**AND THE SWORD IS IN FRAME BECAUSE SHE IS NOT LOOKING AT IT.** *Somebody had put her sword down beside her, and she had not looked at it once.* A gaze can be drawn by where it is not, so long as the thing it avoids is in the picture — which is the one condition `ch18` proved the folder cannot meet with wording alone unless the object is placed where the frame can compare them. Here it lies on the sand at her hip, well inside the frame, and her eyes are on him.

**HER FACE IS THE HARDEST THING IN THIS PAIR.** *There was something happening in her face that he could see with total clarity and could not read at all.* An unreadable face is not a blank one, and the folder has a name for the state already — `../canon-brief.md` has her at *assessment in progress: attention fully engaged, giving nothing back, no verdict reached.* Written as anatomy: the lids fully raised, the gaze unbroken on him, the brows unmoved, the mouth closed and soft. Everything engaged and nothing resolved.

**HIS IS THE CONTRADICTION, STATED AS ONE** per §Gaze: he is standing over somebody he has just taken apart, and there is no victory anywhere in him.

**FLAT DAYLIGHT MEANS NO DEMOTION** — §The winner and the rival's own limit is that in flat light with no dark to recede into, the instrument is the crop. So the hands earn their size from the framing and the block carries no value-structure clause, which is what `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick` found when that paragraph came out of it.

**DROPPED (author, s51: *loose the not kind to me*). Run 2 fixed both faults and the moment was let go anyway; the chapter has its plates elsewhere.** The block and both run records stay below — **the identity finding they produced is the most useful thing this chapter taught**, and it is already promoted to `../prompt-system.md` §Injury. Not re-run, not re-proposed.

**RUN 1 (s51) — NOT ADOPTED (author: *she looks a bit like a beggar or something. and also for some reason her face looks more like valeria here than herself*). ONE ROOT, TWO FAULTS.**

**WHAT LANDED.** The gate timber close behind her with the dark of the gateway falling away beyond, pale sand, flat daylight with no hard-edged shadow. **Her two hands in her lap with the palms turned up**, open and empty and the quietest thing in the frame. The sword flat on the sand at her hip with her eyes off it, the cup beside it. Eye contact as the subject, her eyes up and his down, neither at the viewer. **His face is right** — the head lowered a little, the eyes steady down on hers, nothing of victory in it. His hair straight and hacked short, which is the authored clause working for the third run in a row.

**THE ROOT IS THAT THE BLOCK SPENT HER IDENTITY ON HER DAMAGE.** `../canon-brief.md` gives Elarine two separators and this block surrendered both of them to depict a hard morning. **The first is the binding.** README's §Silhouette separation is explicit that Elarine and Valeria are held apart on *bound vs loose*, and the block wrote *come out of its binding down one side* — so the one feature keeping those two girls distinct was put in play by the block itself, and the tool took it all the way down. Long loose black hair on a girl in dark clothing **is Valeria's signature**, and that is what came back. **The second is the cloth.** *Plain dark clothing of very good quality* is her station, and the block wrote *torn through at the thigh, at one forearm and down one side* — which deleted the quality and left tatters on a girl sitting on the ground.

**AND THE SECOND FAULT IS A KNOWN ONE, ONE CHAPTER OVER.** `ch23/empty` run 1 returned *much mended* as **rags**, and its correction was that the boy is poor, not destitute, and the cloth is whole and patched. **The same failure shape, on a girl whose whole costume argument is the opposite of poverty**, and this block walked into it with a clause that was even easier to over-read.

**SO THE FINDING IS GENERAL AND IT IS WORTH THE SENTENCE: damage must not be paid for out of the features that separate a character.** Write what survives first, then the damage as an exception to it.

**RUN 2 CHANGES HER TWO APPEARANCE PARAGRAPHS AND NOTHING ELSE.** The knot stays up and holds, with two or three strands loose at one temple instead of a fall of hair down one side — which is still the page (*her hair had come out of its binding down one side*) at the smallest quantity that reads. The garment is named as finely made before it is named as damaged, the damage becomes a few clean cuts in good cloth rather than tears, and §Absence takes the one thing the frame now pulls toward: rags. Her build goes in small and neat, per `../canon-brief.md`, since the picture returned a longer, older face than her sheet has.

**AND ONE DRIFT LEFT ALONE.** The new pink of the mended forearm did not read as different skin — below the threshold at that size, like Vask's nose on `ch25/four-seconds`. The crop came back wide again.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the seated girl.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the standing boy.

Setting: just inside the gate of a great arena, as loose wash
suggestion — heavy new timber close behind her, pale raked sand
underfoot, and the dark of the gateway falling away beyond. Flat
daylight, no sun and no hard-edged shadow.

Composition: two figures, close and near the viewer. The girl sits on
the sand with her back against the timber, in frame from the knees up.
The boy stands in front of her and is cut by the top and bottom edges,
in frame from the hip to the top of the head, his body angled toward
her. The viewer is low, at the height of her eyes: the underside of
his jaw is visible and she is not looked down on. Their two faces are
near each other in the upper part of the frame.

HER TWO HANDS LIE IN HER LAP WITH THE PALMS TURNED UP, open and empty
and holding nothing, the fingers loose and half curled. They are the
quietest thing in the picture and they are fully in frame.

A plain sword lies flat on the sand beside her at her hip, well inside
the frame, and she is not looking at it. A plain clay cup stands on
the sand near it.

HER FACE IS FULLY ENGAGED AND GIVES NOTHING BACK: the lids fully
raised and the gaze unbroken on him, the brows unmoved, the mouth
closed and soft. Everything in it is working and nothing in it has
been decided. Not cold, not bitter, not sad, not admiring.

HE IS STANDING OVER SOMEBODY HE HAS JUST TAKEN APART AND THERE IS NO
VICTORY ANYWHERE IN HIM: the head lowered a little toward her, the
eyes down and steady on hers, the mouth closed and unpressed, the
shoulders down. Not proud, not ashamed, not braced.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER, her eyes up and his down,
and neither of them is looking at the viewer.

She is small and finely built, small for her age, with a narrow frame
and a small face. Her hair is ink-dark, a flat neutral black, and it
is BOUND: gathered close against the head and held in a tight severe
knot at the back, with two or three loose strands come down at one
temple and nothing else out of it. The mass of her hair is up and
stays up; none of it falls loose past her shoulders.

Her clothing is finely made and still reads as finely made — plain
dark cloth of very good quality, close-cut and buttoned high to the
throat, with no ornament of any kind. It has taken a few clean
straight cuts in the weave, one at the thigh and one at a forearm,
each with a dark stain around it and the cloth otherwise whole and
holding its shape. Not ragged, not tattered, not frayed, not a
beggar's clothing and nothing about her destitute. The skin under it
is whole and there is no wound anywhere in the picture. She sits
upright with her legs drawn in and folded neatly to one side. Grey
soot and pale sand dust on both of them.

His hair is dark and straight, hacked short and uneven. He wears
undyed coarse working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead
olive, the left sleeve gone entirely at the shoulder. His bare left
forearm from wrist to elbow is new pink skin, smooth and unmarked and
a shade lighter than the weathered skin above it, unbandaged and
uncovered.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. Her eyes
are very dark brown; his are pale near-white grey.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: aftermath

*Author-requested and author-ruled at s51: `scene-tests.md`'s `aftermath-kael` is the folder's best picture of this boy wrecked, and Ch. 26 is the chapter that wrecks him.* Ch. 26 canon: *He came out of the far edge of it moving, with his sleeve gone and his arm the colour of a cooked thing* … *"The bout is decided."* … *Kael got about four strides toward the gate before his own legs found out what he had spent.*

**ADOPTED AS DRAWN — `images/plates/ch26-aftermath.png` (author, s51: *its good enough*). Ch. 26 has three.** The block below is the scene test's own text, verbatim, because **a plate's block is the text that made the picture** — the same rule that put the author's as-run wording on `ch23/the-horizon`. The file also stays in `images/scene-tests/`, where it is still a reference.

**THE COLLISION WAS NAMED AND THE AUTHOR SPENT IT.** A scene test is a reference: it shows what someone is, depicts no scene, and page spend-state does not bind it. A plate is a moment and THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE binds it completely — so this picture arrives on the page with **three departures, recorded here and not argued with**:
- **A stone courtyard**, where the chapter's aftermath is raked sand and the timber of the eastern gate.
- **A charcoal tunic**, where `../canon-brief.md` has undyed oatmeal and dead olive. This is the one that touches a standing costume fence rather than a single scene.
- **No burn on the left forearm** — the mark this chapter exists to give him, and the only one on his body he did not spend himself.

They join `vask.png`'s missing temple-grey and `neris.png`'s apparent age under §Pictures that stand as drawn: ruled correct, not re-cut, `03` unchanged. **Do not re-raise.**

```
Image 1: full-body reference of Kael.

Same medium and style as the reference image: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a stone courtyard as loose wash suggestion, background
falling into shadow.

Composition: waist-up, three-quarter view, body angled away, head
turned back toward the viewer.

His charcoal tunic is destroyed — torn open down the front and
hanging off one shoulder, the left sleeve ripped away entirely, the
fabric shredded and filthy but still on him. Chest and one shoulder
exposed through the tear. Lean, hard, wiry — visible ribs, flat
chest, the build of someone trained rather than grown.

Skin marked from the fight: grey dust and soot across the chest and
forearms, dark bruising over one shoulder and along the ribs, older
pale scars across the upper arm and collarbone. Nothing bleeding.

Standing, weight settled, breathing hard but not slumped.
Expression flat and tired, mouth slightly open, gaze direct.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as
shown in the reference image. Pale grey eyes.
```

**IF IT IS EVER RE-CUT ONTO THE PAGE**, the three departures close without touching anything that makes the picture good — the pose, the destroyed-not-absent garment, the belt over the remains, the bruising and soot over old scars, the flat tired face with the mouth open on the breath. Composed at s51, never run:

HIS CLOTHING IS DESTROYED AND STILL ON HIM: coarse undyed working wool
in dull oatmeal and dead olive, torn open down the front and hanging
off one shoulder, the left sleeve ripped away entirely, the cloth
shredded and filthy. Chest and one shoulder bare through the tear. A
plain leather belt sits over the remains of it, holding the shirt
together at the waist.

HIS BARE LEFT FOREARM FROM WRIST TO ELBOW IS NEW PINK SKIN — smooth,

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-fan

*Author-requested at s51: a hip-up shot of Elarine's fire fanning out of her hand.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 26 canon: *Elarine did not wait for him to be ready. She opened her hand and a fan of flame came across the sand at his legs, wide as a cart* — and, at her mark before the bell, *Her left hand was open and empty and held a little out from her body, because that was doctrine, and doctrine held that a Resonant kept one hand for the Art.*

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch26-the-fan.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s51: *this one is nice*). Ch. 26 has four.**

**THE FIRE CAME BACK AS A BUILT SHAPE AND NOT AS AN EFFECT**, which was the whole risk: a thin sheet leaving the palm, low and level just off the sand, its leading edge broken into separate tongues, narrow at the hand and widening out of the frame. **`ch21/the-surge`'s lesson paid off one chapter later** — that run failed because a reserve erased the object it ran along, and what rescued it was naming a form. This block named thickness, edge, direction and rate of spread, and got all four on the first try.

**AND HER IDENTITY HELD COMPLETELY**, on the same clauses that fixed `not-kind-to-me` run 2: the knot bound tight with nothing out of it, the dark cloth close-cut and buttoned high and unmistakably well made, the small neat frame. **Two runs in a row now, so the s51 correction is not a one-off.** The sword hangs point-down and back, the seating behind is a wash with no face in it, and the fire is the only saturated colour in the picture.

**ONE DRIFT, NOT WORTH A RUN.** The fire throws almost no warm light back onto her — the block asked for warmth on the underside of the forearm and jaw and the flat daylight simply won. `ch16/the-ear-on-the-brick` had the inverse (a flame that threw a glow it was told not to throw), so the tool decides this for itself either way. The palm came back turned forward with the fingers together rather than down and spread.

*Author-requested at s51: a hip-up shot of Elarine's fire fanning out of her hand.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 26 canon: *Elarine did not wait for him to be ready. She opened her hand and a fan of flame came across the sand at his legs, wide as a cart* — and, at her mark before the bell, *Her left hand was open and empty and held a little out from her body, because that was doctrine, and doctrine held that a Resonant kept one hand for the Art.*

**THIS IS THE ONE MOMENT IN THE CHAPTER WHERE HER DOCTRINE IS THE WHOLE PICTURE**: sword in the right, the Art in the left, both hands doing their assigned work at once. It is also the last chapter in which that will ever be true of her, which the picture does not know and does not have to.

**HE IS NOT IN IT, AND THAT IS WHAT MAKES IT DRAWABLE.** The §Injury boundary this chapter is fenced by is a weapon or a working aimed at a child in frame; with the target out of frame there is nothing for the filter to weigh, and the sword hangs point-down and back where it argues with nobody.

**THE GAZE WAS SOLVED BY PROFILE**, to keep `the-good-part`'s viewer-as-target device off two plates in one chapter: she is side-on and **the object of her look is the fire itself**, in frame and travelling — the one arrangement `ch18` never got to try, since its object was always outside the picture.

**AND IT PUTS THE FIRE BETWEEN HER AND THE VIEWER, WHICH IS THE LIGHTING.** The warm source is now in front of her and below, throwing up onto the underside of the jaw and the front of the body, with everything behind her cold and flat — §Light's *a warm source lights a face only when it is close*, and nothing in the folder has been this close.

**SO THE PICTURE HAS A REAL RIVAL AND TAKES THE TWO-CHANNEL INSTRUMENT.** A sheet of fire filling the lower frame will eat a small face without help. The fire keeps brightness and saturation; **her face takes edge and finish.** Both clauses, per §The winner and the rival, because naming the winner is half an instruction.

**THE ONE THING THAT MAY BREAK IS THE ARM.** §Limits: overlapping limbs and objects at odd angles are where this tool fails, and an arm extended at the camera is both. The block keeps it clear of her body with nothing crossing it, which is what carried the raised arm on `ch02/the-doorframe`.

**THE FIRE IS BUILT AS A SHAPE, NOT AS AN EFFECT**, per §Nouns. `ch21/the-surge` run 1 is the warning: the reserve instrument erased the object it ran along, and what fixed it was the author naming a form — *some lightning tendril sprawling off the blade.* So this block gives the fan a thickness, an edge, a direction and a rate of spread, and takes its scale off her own shoulders rather than off *wide as a cart*, which the frame cannot hold.

**AND HER IDENTITY IS WRITTEN BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE**, per this chapter's own s51 finding: bound hair, quality cloth, small build. At this point in the bout she is uncut and her clothing is whole — Kael's first cut lands after the fan — so there is no damage to pay for it out of.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the floor of a great arena on a bright morning, as loose wash
suggestion — pale raked sand underfoot, and behind her the banked
seating rising as a wash of small shapes with no face resolved
anywhere in it. Flat cold daylight.

Composition: one figure, side-on to the viewer, standing in the left
of the frame, the bottom edge of the picture crossing her at the hip.
Her left arm is extended out ahead of her at waist height, the hand
open, palm down and the fingers spread. Her right hand holds a plain
longsword with the point low and angled down and back, away from her
body.

THE FIRE COMING OUT OF HER OPEN LEFT HAND IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE:
a flat fan of flame leaving the palm and spreading away across the
frame, low and level, travelling just above the sand. It leaves her
hand narrow, is already wider than her own shoulders by the time it
reaches the far edge of the picture, and goes on widening out of
frame. It is a thin sheet seen nearly edge-on, its leading edge ragged
and broken into separate tongues — not a ball, not a column and not a
cloud.

The fire is the only saturated colour anywhere in the picture, deep
orange going to pale yellow where the sheet is thinnest, with the
sand, the seating and her clothing in muted greys and dull browns
around it. It is also the only warm light: it throws warmth upward
onto the underside of her forearm, her jaw and her cheek, and
everything turned away from it stays cold and flat.

SHE IS LOOKING ALONG THE FIRE, out past the edge of the frame in the
direction it is travelling, and not at the viewer.

HER FACE IS AT WORK AND GIVES NOTHING AWAY: the lids fully raised and
the eyes steady along the line of the fan, the brows unmoved, the
mouth closed and soft. Not straining and not fierce.

She is small and finely built, small for her age, with a narrow frame
and a small face. Her hair is ink-dark, a flat neutral black, and it
is BOUND: gathered close against the head in a tight severe knot at
the back with nothing out of it, and none of it falling loose past her
shoulders. Her clothing is finely made — plain dark cloth of very good
quality, close-cut and buttoned high to the throat, with no ornament
of any kind — and it is whole and unmarked, with no tear and no stain
anywhere on it.

Nobody else is in the picture.

Preserve her exactly as shown in the reference image — face, hair,
colouring, apparent age. Her eyes are very dark brown.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-fan-downrange

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch26-the-fan-downrange.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s51: *the directed at the camera is better, but keep both*). Ch. 26 has five, the most of any chapter in the book.**

**THE ARM DID NOT BREAK, WHICH WAS THE ONE REAL RISK.** §Limits has overlapping limbs and objects at odd angles as this tool's failure mode, and an arm extended straight at the lens is both. It came back foreshortened, clear of the body, nothing crossing it, the fingers spread — **so `ch02/the-doorframe`'s clause (*the arm raised clear of her body with nothing overlapping it*) generalises off a raised arm onto a foreshortened one.**

**AND THE LIGHT LANDED HERE WHERE IT LOST ON THE SIDE-ON VERSION.** With the fire travelling away, flat daylight beat the warm source outright; with the fire between her and the lens, the hand and the whole forearm come back lit orange from underneath. **§Light's *a warm source lights a face only when it is close* is really about what sits between the source and the camera.** The face still stays cool, which is honest for a source held at waist height.

**THE TWO-CHANNEL INSTRUMENT HELD** against a rival filling a third of the frame: the fire keeps brightness and saturation, her face keeps edge and finish, and neither ate the other. **And her identity held for the third run running** — bound knot, close-cut quality cloth buttoned high, small neat frame. The s51 correction is three-for-three.

**WHAT DRIFTED, AND IT MAY BE RIGHT.** The block asked for *a thin sheet seen nearly edge-on, low and level just above the sand*, and what came is a broad cone opening at the camera. Head-on, a flat sheet is a wide band, so the geometry is arguable — but it reads as a torrent rather than the page's fan at his legs, and it sits higher than his legs would be. Adopted as drawn.

**A BUILD FAULT OF MINE, RECORDED BECAUSE BOTH FAN PICTURES WERE MADE UNDER IT.** Both blocks were written with the scene style paragraph inside the fence while still headed `### PLATE:` — which the build also prepends — so the prompts that made these two images carried that paragraph **twice**. Nothing in either picture depended on it and neither is re-run. **Both fences are corrected here**, and `../prompt-system.md` §Plate template now states the split: a `PLATE:` block starts at `Setting:` and the build supplies the style line; only a `PLATE-VERBATIM:` block carries its own. A folder-wide check found no other block affected.

*Author-requested at s51, before the profile version ran: the viewer as the target would be cool.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. **It is a second picture rather than a correction to the first** — the adopted plate is a fan of fire seen side-on, which is a shape, and this is a fan of fire coming at you, which is an event.

**THE CAMERA GOES DOWNRANGE**, which does to this block what it did to `the-good-part`: the object of her look becomes the lens, so her eyes come out of the picture and the gaze needs no ban at all.

**AND IT PUTS THE FIRE BETWEEN HER AND THE VIEWER, WHICH IS THE LIGHTING.** The warm source is in front of her and below, throwing up onto the underside of the jaw and the front of the body, with everything behind her cold and flat — §Light's *a warm source lights a face only when it is close*, and nothing in the folder has been this close. **The adopted plate is the evidence that it is needed**: side-on, with the fire travelling away, the flat daylight beat the warm source entirely.

**SO THE PICTURE HAS A REAL RIVAL AND TAKES THE TWO-CHANNEL INSTRUMENT.** A sheet of fire filling the lower frame will eat a small face without help. The fire keeps brightness and saturation; **her face takes edge and finish.** Both clauses, per §The winner and the rival, because naming the winner is half an instruction.

**THE ONE THING THAT MAY BREAK IS THE ARM.** §Limits: overlapping limbs and objects at odd angles are where this tool fails, and an arm extended at the camera is both. The block keeps it clear of her body with nothing crossing it, which is what carried the raised arm on `ch02/the-doorframe`.

```

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,

# FILE: plates/ch27.md

# Ch. 27 — The Fog · plate sheet

**Composed at s51 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Severin unpinned. Anchors: `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the first, `images/portraits/severin.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S ARITHMETIC.** A sky that cannot be stopped, and one man who stopped one of them. *He could hold one. He could not hold two. There were going to be a great many more than two.*

**AND THE CHAPTER'S WORST MATERIAL IS LEFT ON THE SHORTLIST, NOT CALLED UNDRAWABLE.** The eastern gate and the crush were first proposed at s51 as things the tool would refuse, **which the folder's own record contradicts**: `ch21/dead` was refused and then **ran on a rewording and was adopted**, and `../prompt-system.md` §Injury says plainly that a refusal is usually a fault in the block. What is actually true is narrower and it is the instrument this chapter runs on — **you do not draw the stroke, you draw the men**, and the crop decides what the filter is ever asked about. Both entries stay on `candidates.md` with the framing that would carry them.

**NEITHER BLOCK RESOLVES A FACE, AND BOTH ARE PRICED FOR IT** (s43: a faceless candidate is priced, not disqualified). `ch22/kael-of-the-island` is the precedent and its record is the argument: *the two figures came back even smaller than the block asked for, which is the picture's whole power.*

**THE FOG IS TWO DISTINCT LAYERS AND BOTH ARE NAMED.** *It lay across the sand in a thin sheet at about the height of a man's knee. It sat over the top rail like a lid.* Nothing else in the folder has been asked for stratified weather, so each layer gets its own clause and its own height.

**AND EVERY BURNING THING IS COUNTED WHERE IT SITS.** §Hands' lesson generalises off hands: *three blades total* failed twice and one line per hand held. So each stone gets its own line and its own place in the frame, and the count is stated after them.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-sky

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch27-the-sky.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s51: *amazing*).**

**THE COUNTING INSTRUMENT CARRIED FOUR BURNING OBJECTS AT FOUR DEPTHS ON ONE RUN.** Three stones in the air — near and huge with a long tail, mid, and far and small — and a fourth already down through the timber with fire in the hole it made and smoke coming up through the boards. **§Hands' one-line-per-item rule generalises off hands and onto anything counted**, and this is the proof: *three blades total* failed twice, and four objects each given its own line and its own place landed first time.

**AND THE FIGURE CAME BACK SMALLER THAN THE BLOCK ASKED FOR, WHICH IS AGAIN THE PICTURE'S POWER** — the same thing `ch22/kael-of-the-island` recorded. A boy alone on an empty floor with his back to the viewer and no face at all carries *none of them was producing an instruction* without a single clause about what he is feeling. **The faceless price was worth paying twice.**

**THE CROWD IS THE SET'S LARGEST WASH** — thousands of small shapes, tier on tier, not one face resolved — and the fires are the only saturated colour in a completely grey picture.

**WHAT DRIFTED: half the weather.** The block named two fog layers at two heights and got one. The lid over the top rail and the mist through the bowl are there; **the thin sheet lying on the sand at knee height is not**, and the floor came back bare. Stratified weather is new to the folder and this is its one data point — **the layer with something to sit against rendered, the one lying on open ground did not.** Not worth a run; recorded for the next block that wants ground fog.

*Author-requested at s51: the fireballs raining down on the arena.* Anchor: `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`. Ch. 27 canon: *Something went over the top of the building. It came in high out of the grey from the seaward side, going fast and going away from him, and it was the size of a cart, and it was burning.* … *It came out of the fog over the rim with fire going backward off it in a tail* … *The third and the fourth came over together.* … *the fire from the fourth stone found the new timber and began to go along it under the seats, and the smoke came up through the boards in fifty places at the same time in neat little rows.* … *Eleven thousand people who had come to watch two children fight got up out of their seats and tried to leave a stone bowl through four gates, and Kael stood on the sand in the middle of it.*

**IT ABSORBS THE PARALYSIS BEAT RATHER THAN COMPETING WITH IT.** A small dark figure standing alone and still on the flattest, most open ground on the headland, with both hands empty, **is** *every school he had was running perfectly, none of them was producing an instruction* — and it does not need a face to say so. **It is also the deliberate inverse of `ch23/empty`**: the identical empty-handed stance on this identical sand, which one chapter ago was the whole of his mastery.

**THE FIRE IS THE ONLY WARM THING IN A GREY PICTURE**, which is what makes the palette do the work: flat fogged daylight everywhere, and four burning objects that are the only saturated colour in the frame.

```
Image 1: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the boy, for his build and silhouette. His face is not resolved in this picture.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the inside of a great stone arena in fog, seen from down on
its floor. Pale raked sand fills the lower part of the frame. Banked
seating rises beyond and above it, packed with people, and goes up
into grey and stops being seats. Flat cold fogged daylight, no sun and
no hard-edged shadow anywhere.

THE FOG IS IN TWO SEPARATE LAYERS AND BOTH ARE VISIBLE: a thin flat
sheet of it lying on the sand at about the height of a man's knee,
level and unbroken across the whole floor — and, far above, a solid
grey lid sitting across the top rail of the building, so that the far
side of the bowl is only a suggestion behind it.

Composition: one small dark figure standing alone on the sand in the
lower third of the frame, well off centre, seen from behind and a
little to one side, both arms hanging at his sides. He is small in
the frame and his face is not visible and not turned toward the
viewer. He is completely still and he is the only person on the sand.

WHAT IS IN THE AIR, EACH IN ITS OWN PLACE: one burning stone high in
the frame near the top rail, just come in over the rim, with fire
streaming backward off it in a long tail. A second burning stone lower
and further off, dropping toward the far seating. A third further away
still and smaller with the distance. Three stones in the air, and no
other flying object anywhere in the picture. Each is a rough block
about as long as three seated people are wide, wrapped and burning
along its whole surface.

A fourth has already gone into the seating on one side: timber broken
downward in three rows and fire burning in the hole it made, and grey
smoke coming up through the boards around it in fifty small separate
threads in neat rows.

The crowd in the seating is standing and moving, drawn as a wash of
small shapes with no face resolved anywhere and nobody drawn in
detail.

The fires are the only saturated colour in the picture — deep orange
and pale yellow — and the only warm light in it. Everything else is
cold grey, pale sand and wet stone.

The boy on the sand is the smallest thing in the frame and the
stillest, and everything else in the picture is in motion.

Preserve his build and clothing as shown in the reference image —
undyed coarse working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead
olive, with no metal on him. Nothing is in either hand.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: i-need-you

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch27-i-need-you.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s51: *its good. her other could be not drawn, its fine*). Ch. 27 has its two.**

**HER FACE IS THE WIN.** Level, lids raised, eyes steady on his, mouth closed and even, urgent and **not angry** — *called and delivered and finished*, with no temper and no pleading anywhere in it. `ch20/val` is the plate this was written against, where *tired* and *flat* as temperament words drifted her wistful; writing every clause as anatomy held her exactly where `../canon-brief.md` puts her.

**AND HER HAIR CAME BACK BOUND ON THE FIRST RUN** — tied at the nape with nothing loose past the shoulders, against a reference that has it loose. **Authoring a feature the reference carries differently is now four-for-four this session** (Kael's hair twice, Elarine's binding twice, this).

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: his back and shoulder on the timber, her fist closed in the cloth at his shoulder, the burn on the side of her neck, red drying on her forearm, the vambraces and the charcoal coat over grey-blue, the packed bowl behind with one curve broken and burning, eye contact the subject and neither of them at the viewer.

**THE SECOND BLADE IS NOT DRAWN, AND THE AUTHOR RULED IT FINE.** The block put it in the same fist that grips his coat and the tool solved the conflict by emptying that hand — §Hands' known weak spot, a hand carrying two instructions. **The ruling is narrow and it is not a repeal:** `../canon-brief.md`'s *twin blades, both hands full — never one sword* is a fence against giving her a one-sword identity, not a requirement that both be visible in every picture. Where a hand is occupied, the second blade may sit out of frame or go undrawn. The fence line now says so.

*Author-requested at s51: this chapter needs Kael and Valeria somehow.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`, `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 27 canon: *Then something took a fistful of his coat at the shoulder and turned him bodily out of where he was standing, and his back met the timber of the judges' enclosure hard enough to empty him, and Valeria Vel put her face in front of his face.* … *She still had both blades. There was blood on her forearm that was not hers and a burn on the side of her neck he had not seen her take.* … *She hit him. Flat hand, open, hard, across the side of the head, and it was not temper, it was the correction of a fault; she did it the way the grey coats on the sea court did it, called and delivered and finished, and the whole arena came back into him at once through the ringing. / "There," she said. "I need you."*

**IT IS THE MOMENT THE TWO OF THEM INVERT.** For a year she has been the one he cannot solve. Here she is holding him against a wall and telling him she needs him, and **the picture is composed the instant after the hand lands** — no strike in progress, his head still turned by it and the arena arriving back through the ringing. The framing is the whole safety margin and it costs the beat nothing, because the beat was never the blow.

**HER FACE IS NOT ANGRY AND THAT IS THE HARD PART.** *It was not temper, it was the correction of a fault … called and delivered and finished.* So she is written entirely professional: level, unhurried, already past it, with the need stated flat rather than pleaded. `../canon-brief.md`'s fence holds — not vulnerable, not wistful, not searching — and `ch20/val` is the warning, where *tired* and *flat* as temperament words drifted her wistful. Every clause here is anatomy.

**HIS IS A FACE ARRIVING BACK INTO ITSELF**, which is a state and not an action: the eyes just refocused on her, the head still carrying the turn.

**HER HAIR IS BOUND, AND THIS IS THE FIRST TIME.** *She came out of the western gate with her hair bound* — against `../canon-brief.md`'s loose-past-the-shoulders default, so the block authors it. It does not disturb README's §Silhouette separation, which holds her apart from Kael on build, blades and cloth and never on hair; the bound-vs-loose axis is the Elarine one, and Elarine is not in this picture.

**THE FIRES DO NOT LIGHT THEM.** §Light, confirmed twice this session on the two fan plates: a warm source lights a face only when what is between it and the camera is close. The burning curve is across the bowl, so the faces stay in cold flat fogged daylight and the fire is the one warm thing in the distance behind.

```
Image 1: valeria-14.png — portrait reference of the girl.
Image 2: valeria-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl, for her clothing and kit.
Image 3: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the floor of a great stone arena in fog. Heavy new timber
close behind the boy — the boarded side of an enclosure — and beyond
them the bowl of the building, packed and moving, with one curve of
seating broken and burning a long way off and grey smoke going up
into the fog. Flat cold fogged daylight on the two of them, no sun and
no hard-edged shadow.

Composition: two figures, very close together and near the viewer,
the bottom edge of the picture crossing both at the lower chest. His
back and one shoulder are against the timber. She stands squared in
front of him with her face a hand's width from his, both of them in
three-quarter view and turned toward each other. Nobody else is drawn
near them.

HER LEFT FIST IS CLOSED ON THE CLOTH AT HIS SHOULDER, holding him
against the boards. There is a plain short blade still in that same
hand and another in her right hand, both held clear and pointing away
from him, and neither blade is near either of their bodies. Nothing
is raised and nothing is being swung.

HIS HEAD IS STILL TURNED A LITTLE TO ONE SIDE and he is coming back
into himself: the eyes just arrived on her and focused, wide open with
the pale grey clear, the mouth slightly open, the brows level. He is
not resisting her and not braced.

HER FACE IS ENTIRELY PROFESSIONAL AND ALREADY PAST IT: the chin level,
the lids fully raised, the eyes steady on his, the brows unmoved, the
mouth closed and even. Nothing in it is angry and nothing in it is
pleading. Not fierce, not tender, not desperate, not wistful.

THE TWO ARE LOOKING DIRECTLY AT EACH OTHER, and neither is looking at
the viewer.

Her black hair is BOUND — gathered close against the head and tied
back at the nape, with nothing falling loose past her shoulders. Her
clothing as in her full-figure reference: the charcoal sleeveless coat
over the grey-blue underlayer, the high collar, the steel vambraces.
There is red down one of her bare forearms, dark and drying, and a
reddened burn on the side of her neck. He wears undyed coarse working
wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, torn at one
shoulder, with no metal anywhere on him.

Their two faces are the most finished things in the picture and carry
the sharpest step from light to dark; the timber, the burning curve,
the smoke and the crowd stay at quiet middle values with soft edges.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair, colouring, apparent age; the two images of the girl are the same
girl and there are two figures in the picture. Do not blend their
features. Do not mix their clothing: the steel and the charcoal coat
belong only to her, the mended undyed wool only to him. Her eyes
green, his pale grey.

# FILE: plates/ch28.md

# Ch. 28 — Turning Point · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Valeria is thirteen. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` + `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the first, `images/portraits/valeria-14.png` + `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png` on the second. **The author was offered eight candidates and handed the pick back** (s53: *you choose*), so the two ★ entries on `candidates.md` stand as proposed.

**THE PAIR IS ONE MORNING FROM BOTH SIDES** — the inside of a killing and the outside of one. He is a boy standing in a lane with his hand out in front of him and nothing in it; she is a girl standing perfectly still while the road walks away from her in pieces. **Neither picture has a stroke in it**, and neither has a second figure in it.

**THE CHAPTER IS THE WORST MATERIAL IN THE BOOK AND THE INSTRUMENT IS Ch. 27's:** you do not draw the stroke, you draw the men. Both blocks are composed **on the second after** — `ch27/i-need-you`'s solve, and it costs these two beats nothing either, because in both cases the beat was never the act. *Then the weight went off his arm and down* is a picture of a hand; *She was rearranging the road* is a picture of a road.

**AND BOTH OMIT A BODY THE PAGE PUTS IN FRONT OF THE READER.** The man Kael kills is a second out of frame below the bottom edge, and the man the first column takes *through the middle of him* and leaves *up to the height of the roofs* is simply not drawn. **THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE binds what a plate may add, not what it must include** — the hard rule's own remedy is distance, angle, occlusion and the detail out of frame, and here it is the crop and the smoke. Both are named in their blocks as one ban each, not a stack.

**THE RED IS THE SET'S FIRST FULL USE OF THE `ch23/the-horizon` SPECIMEN ON A HAND.** Route and appearance, never substance — the block gives it a site, a run and two words about how it looks, and dries it darker in the creases where the fingers fold, which is the chapter's own arithmetic: *it had gone into the creases where the fingers folded and dried there, and it was not coming out without water and time*.

**AND THE BURN IS UNDER IT.** Ch. 26's mend is on the page in this chapter — *It was new skin. Severin had put it back on him yesterday at the eastern gate … It had been pink and clean at first light this morning* — so the forearm carries two instructions, and §Hands' rule for that is one chain in order: the skin first, then what is on it. **The whole of the plate's argument is in that stack** — the newest thing on his body and the oldest thing anyone can do to another one, on the same six inches of arm.

**HIS FACE IS THE HARD PART AND THE PAGE FORBIDS THE OBVIOUS ANSWER.** *Kael's own face was doing something. He could not tell what … he reached for his own face and there was nothing there to reach for.* So the block may not name a state and may not leave the face empty either — §Gaze's *never tell a face to do nothing*. It gets three working parts and no verdict: the lids fully raised, the mouth open and the jaw slack, the brows level and unmoved. **What the reader decides it is, is what the boy could not.**

**THE SECOND BLOCK'S SUBJECT IS AN OBJECT, AND `ch21/the-surge` IS THE WARNING.** An effect that erases the thing it runs along came back as a white-painted blade, and what fixed it was giving the effect a form. So the columns are built as construction per §Nouns — **`column` has a default and it is architecture** — torn out of the road and made of what the road is made of, broken off at the top, with the ground opened in a ring at the foot of each. And they are counted the way `ch27/the-sky` counted four burning objects and landed them all on one run: **one line per item, each with its own place in the frame, the count stated after them.**

**HER FACE COSTS NOTHING AND THAT IS THE PICTURE.** *Valeria did not stop, did not speak, did not change her pace by any amount he could measure* … *her breathing was not different.* The road is being taken apart three strides from her and the block gives her chin, lids, mouth, brows and jaw as anatomy, with the eyes on the far end of the lane past all three. `ch20/val` is the standing warning that a temperament word drifts her wistful, and `ch27/i-need-you` is the proof that anatomy holds her.

**HER HAIR IS BOUND, WHICH IS Ch. 27's STATE AND NOT YET THE CROSSROADS'.** *her hair out of its binding on one side* happens at the top of the village, minutes after this; before that the page is silent and the last thing it said was bound. **Silence is answered with the last stated fact, not with a new one.** Authoring a feature the reference carries differently is now five-for-five.

**NOTHING COMES OFF HER HANDS.** Her art is internal and the ground is what moves; a picture of a person raising the road pulls hard toward hands-out casting and a glow, so that is the one absence the block spends — §Absence, and `ch12`'s *no glow, no spark* is the precedent.

**AND HER PICTURE IS ALMOST WITHOUT COLOUR.** The only saturated things in it are the green of her eyes and the red on her arms; the burning roofs of the lane stay out of the frame, because a warm source that far off cannot light her and would only compete. `ch27/the-sky` spent its colour on fire; this one spends it on her.
