### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-hand-back

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch28-the-hand-back.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OWN, NOT THIS SHEET'S** (*prompt that i used. optimized AND able to pass guidelines*) — it replaced mine wholesale and it is stored exactly as run. Anchors, which its `Image` lines no longer name: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` and `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`.

**FOUR CHANGES IN IT ARE CRAFT AND ALL FOUR TRAVEL.**
1. **THE HAND GOT A RATIO AND THAT IS WHY IT WINS.** Mine said the hand was *nearer the viewer than his face and larger in the frame for it* — an adjective. The author's says **the open hand is wider in the frame than his head**, which is §Scale's own rule (*a ratio to something in the frame, never an adjective*) applied to a body part rather than to an age. It came back exactly that size.
2. **THE RIVAL IS DEMOTED BY NAME, AND THE RIVAL IS HIS FACE.** *THE OPEN HAND WINS THE PICTURE. His face and the whole of the rest of him are held at quiet middle values with soft wash-bled edges and no hard-edged dark anywhere; the sharpest step from light to dark in the picture happens at the edge of the red on the hand.* Mine demoted only the background and let the face *carry the next* step, which is not a demotion at all. **§The winner and the rival says it needs a rival to demote; this is the folder's first block that demotes a face, and the face is still fully drawn.**
3. **THE LIMB CHAIN IS IN §HANDS' OWN SHORT FORM.** *Left shoulder, torn sleeve, bare forearm, open hand — one continuous limb, in that order.* I had written the same instruction as a meta-preamble (*THIS READS IN THIS ORDER*) and then cut it entirely at the trim; the rule's own phrasing is better than either.
4. **THE CLOTHING LINE IS GONE AND THE CLOTH CAME BACK RIGHT.** No wool, no colours, no *no metal anywhere on him* — the full-figure reference carried all of it. **First evidence that with a full figure attached and one figure in frame, the clothing paragraph is droppable.**

**AND THE INJURY VOCABULARY IS WRITTEN TO PASS.** *Grazed and raw-looking* for *reddened*; *dark red … gone darker where it has dried* with *wet* removed; the word *bleeding* gone from a picture in which nothing is bleeding; and **a bracketed alternative on the one named mark — `[burn / long-healed mark]` — which is new to this folder.** §Injury's s48 lesson was `open cut` → `slash` and `blood` → `red`; this is the same instrument with a benign reading offered alongside the true one.

**WHAT LANDED.** The hand wins the picture completely — red over the whole of it front and back, gone darker in the creases of the fingers, the palm up and angled to the viewer, the fingers spread and loosely curled. The sleeve torn away at the left shoulder and the marked forearm bare below it. One figure, nothing on the ground, no blade anywhere. The lane, the fog, the flat cold light, the well-head at his side. And the finish did exactly what it was told: soft wash-bled edges everywhere and one hard step, at the edge of the red.

**WHAT DRIFTED, four things, none corrected:**
1. **THE GAZE WENT TO THE VIEWER AGAINST AN EXPLICIT BAN.** The block says *HIS EYES ARE ON HIS OWN HAND* and then *He is not looking at the viewer*, and the picture looks straight out. **`ch12` recorded that `kael-14`'s reference points its gaze at the viewer and that the pull is real; this is the strongest evidence yet that in a crop with the head toward the camera the ban alone does not hold it.** §Scenes and plates already prescribes the fix and it was not used here: move the eyes onto an object **and** turn the head with them, or cut a second reference.
2. **HE READS OLDER THAN THIRTEEN, FOR THE SECOND RUN RUNNING** — nearer seventeen here, after `rearranging-the-road` came back nearer sixteen. **Two for two, and neither block carried an age clause.** The pattern now has a shape: `../README.md`'s standing ruling says age is written into the block or the picture will be fourteen, and both of these went past fourteen rather than to it.
3. **THE CROP CAME BACK LOOSE FOR THE EIGHTH RECORDED TIME.** Waist asked, hip-to-thigh delivered.
4. **THE GRAZE UNDER THE EAR DID NOT RENDER**, and neither did the knee-height smoke in the lane. The graze is the second facial mark in two runs to fall below the scale at which a mark carries, after this chapter's other plate lost a slash on a jaw.

**A MECHANICAL NOTE FOR THE NEXT SESSION.** The `Image` lines in this block name no files, so `prompts/plates/ch28-the-hand-back.md` no longer tells you what to attach. The anchors are named in the paragraph above, which is now the only place they live for this plate.

```
Image 1: portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.
Image 2: full-figure reference of the same boy, for his build and clothing.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a narrow village lane as loose wash suggestion — rough
stone house fronts close on both sides, low roofs above them, the
far end of the lane going up into smoke and fog and stopping. A low
stone well-head behind him at one side. Flat cold fogged daylight,
no sun and no hard-edged shadow anywhere, and smoke lying along the
lane at about the height of his knee.

Composition: one boy alone, standing, close to the viewer and turned
three-quarters toward it, the bottom edge of the picture crossing
him at the waist. His left arm is extended forward and a little
across his body, the elbow nearly straight, the open hand held out
at the height of his own chest and nearer the viewer than his face.
The open hand is wider in the frame than his head. He is completely
still.

HIS LEFT HAND IS OPEN AND TOUCHING NOTHING, the fingers spread and
loosely curled, the palm turned up and angled toward the viewer. His
right arm hangs at his side and that hand is open and empty as well.

Left shoulder, torn sleeve, bare forearm, open hand — one continuous
limb, in that order. The sleeve is torn away below the left
shoulder. A [burn / long-healed mark] runs along that forearm from
wrist to elbow. There is dark red across the whole of that same left
hand, front and back, gone darker where it has dried in the creases
of the fingers and the palm.

Grey dust and soot across the front of him and along both arms, one
side of his face grazed and raw-looking below the ear. There is no
red anywhere on him except that hand.

HIS EYES ARE ON HIS OWN HAND: the lids fully raised, the pale grey
clear, the mouth open and the jaw slack, the brows level and
unmoved. He is not looking at the viewer.

He is the only figure in the picture. No other person, no body, no
weapon anywhere in the frame.

THE OPEN HAND WINS THE PICTURE. His face and the whole of the rest
of him are held at quiet middle values with soft wash-bled edges and
no hard-edged dark anywhere; the sharpest step from light to dark in
the picture happens at the edge of the red on the hand. The house
fronts, the well-head, the smoke and the far end of the lane stay
quieter still.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown
in the reference images, which are the same boy. Pale grey eyes.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: rearranging-the-road

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch28-rearranging-the-road.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53).**

**THE COUNTING INSTRUMENT CARRIED THREE STANDING MASSES AT THREE DEPTHS ON ONE RUN**, in a line up the middle of the lane and diminishing correctly — the third proof, after `ch27/the-sky`'s four burning objects, that §Hands' one-line-per-item rule generalises onto anything counted.

**AND `column` DID NOT RETURN ARCHITECTURE.** The §Nouns treatment held completely: what came back is torn roadbed — crumbling brown earth and broken stone, irregular, no two the same, broken off at the top, with dirt and small stones still hanging in the air around all three. **The construction-then-kill-the-default recipe is now proven on an invented object**, where before it had only ever been used on a real one (a coat, a dagger, a candle, a standing stone).

**THE BOUND HAIR LANDED AGAINST A LOOSE REFERENCE FOR THE SECOND TIME ON THIS CHARACTER**, after `ch27/i-need-you` — authoring a feature the reference carries differently is now six-for-six across s51 and s53.

**AND THE PICTURE IS NEARLY MONOCHROME, WHICH THE BLOCK ASKED FOR.** The only colour of any strength in it is the red on both hands and up the near vambrace; the stone, the fog and the road are cold grey and brown throughout, and no fire was drawn anywhere despite a burning village on the page.

**WHAT DRIFTED, four things, none corrected:**
1. **THE CROP CAME BACK LOOSE FOR THE SEVENTH RECORDED TIME.** The block cut her at mid-thigh; the picture is very nearly a full figure, boots included. `ch17` twice, `ch20` twice, `ch25` twice and now this — **and the correction has never once been wanted**, which is what still holds it below §Findings' bar.
2. **THE GAZE WENT THE WRONG WAY UP THE LANE.** The block put her eyes on the far end, past all three; she is looking back down it, out of the frame on the near side, with her head turned a quarter toward the viewer. The face itself is exactly as written — chin level, lids raised, mouth closed and even, nothing straining and nothing angry — so what drifted is the direction and not the temperament, and it reads as a girl checking what is behind her, which is not wrong for the character.
3. **THE SLASH ON THE JAW DID NOT RENDER.** There is a smudge high on the cheekbone and nothing on the jawline. The smallest injury the folder has asked for on a face, and it fell below the scale at which a mark carries — the same limit that lost Vask's *small sound in his nose* at s51.
4. **SHE READS OLDER THAN THIRTEEN**, nearer sixteen. **The block carried no age clause at all**, on the reasoning that the fourteen-year-old anchor is the lever; `../README.md`'s standing ruling says plainly that age is written into the block or the picture will be fourteen, and at three-quarter length with the face small in the frame the anchor did not hold it. **The lesson is the crop:** §AGE's eye-line rung has only ever been proven in a face-filling crop, and this is the first evidence that it does not travel to a distant figure. Recorded, not corrected.

*Candidate 2, ★.* Anchors: `images/portraits/valeria-14.png`, `images/full-figures/valeria-14-full.png`. Ch. 28 canon: *Then Valeria stopped moving. She did that perhaps four times in the whole of that morning, and each time it was worse than the moving. She put both hands down at her sides and set her feet and the lane came up. / It came up in a column the thickness of a man's chest — packed earth and old stone and four hundred years of people walking on it … / Then the next one, three strides further on, and the next after that, walking up that lane ahead of her in a line.* … *She was rearranging the road.*

**IT IS THE EARTH TWIN OF `ch26/the-fan`** — one girl, side-on, hip to mid-thigh, with her whole art going out of the frame away from her and the man it is aimed at out of the picture entirely. The fan is a sheet of flame off an open palm; this is three hundred tons of road standing up, and **her hands are down at her sides doing nothing at all**, which is the difference between the two characters stated in one picture.

**THE COST IS NAMED ON THE PAGE AND IS NOT DRAWN.** *Kael had spent eight weeks of his life learning to put a thread into two fingers' width of his own forearm. / She was rearranging the road.* The plate does not editorialise the gap; it simply puts the boy's whole discipline nowhere in the frame.

```
Image 1: valeria-14.png — portrait reference of the black-haired girl.
Image 2: valeria-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same girl, for
her clothing and kit.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a village lane wide enough for a cart, as loose wash
suggestion — rough stone house fronts close on both sides, low roofs
above them, the far end of the lane going up into smoke and fog. A low
stone well-head at one side. Flat cold fogged daylight, no sun and no
hard-edged shadow anywhere.

Composition: the girl standing in the near third of the frame at the
left, side-on to the viewer and close to it, the bottom edge of the
picture crossing her at mid-thigh. The lane runs away from her to the
right and a little up, and its whole depth is visible past her. She is
the only figure drawn anywhere in the picture.

SHE IS COMPLETELY STILL: both feet flat and set apart on the road, the
weight even between them, both arms straight down at her sides, the
shoulders level. Nothing about her is raised, drawn back, reaching or
in motion. Nothing is coming off her hands, and there is no light, no
glow and no spark anywhere on her or near her.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: her right hand holds one plain straight short
sword and nothing else, hanging straight down at her side with the
point toward the road. Her left hand holds one plain straight short
sword and nothing else, in the same way. A matched pair, undecorated
and well kept. Two hands, two blades, nothing else in either hand, and
nothing raised.

WHAT THE ROAD HAS DONE, EACH IN ITS OWN PLACE: one broken mass of
roadbed stands upright out of the lane just past her and a little
behind, thicker through than a man's chest and going up to the height
of the house roofs. A second stands a few strides further up the lane.
A third further still and smaller with the distance. Three of them,
standing in a line up the middle of the lane and away from her, and
nothing else is standing upright in the lane.

Each is torn upward out of the road and made of what the road is made
of — packed brown earth, old grey paving stone, gravel and roots —
crooked, irregular and broken off at the top, not flat. The ground at
the foot of each is opened and thrown outward in a ring, and dirt and
small stones are still coming off them and hanging in the air. They are
not smooth, not carved, not built: not architecture, not pillars, not
masonry.

HER FACE IS UNHURRIED AND ENTIRELY PROFESSIONAL: the chin level, the
lids fully raised, the mouth closed and even, the brows unmoved, the
jaw easy. Nothing in it is straining and nothing in it is angry. Her
eyes are on the far end of the lane, past all three, and she is not
looking at the viewer and not looking at the nearest of them.

Red over both hands and up both arms to the elbow, dark and drying. A
short slash across the line of her jaw. Her black hair is BOUND —
gathered close against the head and tied back at the nape, with nothing
falling loose past her shoulders.

Her face is the most finished thing in the picture and carries the
sharpest step from light to dark; the house fronts, the smoke and the
far end of the lane stay at quiet middle values with soft edges, and
the three standing masses are drawn with hard broken edges against
them.

The only saturated colour in the picture is the green of her eyes and
the red on her arms — everything else is cold grey, brown earth, wet
stone and smoke.

Preserve her exactly as shown in the reference images — face, hair,
colouring, apparent age and clothing; the two images are the same girl
and there is one figure in the picture. Her clothing as in her
full-figure reference: the charcoal sleeveless coat over the grey-blue
underlayer, the high collar, the steel vambraces.
```

# FILE: plates/ch29.md

# Ch. 29 — The Gate · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, with `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the first for the kit.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S ARGUMENT AND THE ORDER MATTERS:** an institution that says no to him at the width of one person, and a stranger with a third of a pint who says nothing at all and spends some of it on him. **The wall is the one with the light behind it.**

**HALDEN IS IN THE FIRST PICTURE AND HIS FACE IS NOT.** `ch13.md` ruled it and named him in the ruling: *a plate is not where a person's face gets decided; whatever it drew would become his reference by default*, and there is no `images/portraits/halden.png`. **The block does not work around that — it uses it.** The judge is a broad dark shape filling a lit slot with no features on him, which is what a boy outside a gate can see of the man who is deciding about him, and it costs the beat nothing because the beat is the eighteen inches and not the face. **The work that unblocks a resolved Halden is a portrait, not a plate.**

**THE WOMAN WITH THE SHAWL IS AUTHORED WHOLE, AND THAT IS LEGAL WHERE HALDEN IS NOT.** She has no name, no sheet and no second scene — nothing for a drawn face to become. `ch18/the-man-at-the-second-bend` is the precedent: an unnamed islander built out of the block alone.

**THE TWO LIGHTS ARE THE SET'S FIRST DELIBERATE PAIR.** Everything outside that wall is flat cold fogged daylight with smoke in it; the only warm thing in either picture is *light on stone and the ordinary sound of an ordinary street*, and it is behind the man in the gap and it does not reach the boy's face. §Light is explicit that a warm source lights a face only when it is close, so the warmth stays inside the wall — **which is the chapter, drawn.**

**HE IS COVERED AND THE PAGE SAYS SO TWICE.** *He was a boy of thirteen with blood to the elbow and two short blades across his back* … *"You're bleeding, love."* … *"It's not mine," Kael said, which was true of most of him and not of that.* So the red is old and dry everywhere except the shoulder, and the shoulder is the one thing on him that is his own. **Both blocks site it there and nowhere else**, which is what makes the second picture legible without a word of dialogue.

**AND THE SECOND PICTURE'S SUBJECT IS A LINE OF SIGHT THAT DOES NOT MEET.** She is working and looking at the shoulder; he is looking at her face. *She did not know who he was. That was the thing he could not put anywhere.* §Gaze names eye contact as a subject when it happens; here the block names the miss, on purpose, and gives the working hands the finish instead.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** *she let herself be seen* — Valeria alone in the strip of open ground in front of the spear line — is the chapter's other great picture and it is a solo Valeria one chapter after `ch28/rearranging-the-road`. **The mountain is not empty** — eleven fires read as houses across four miles of ground, men going along the field walls like water in a gutter, boats on the eastern water — is the widest thing in Part IV and it is the same shape as `ch27/the-sky`. Both stay on `candidates.md` with their framing.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-eighteen-inches

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch29-the-eighteen-inches.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). The first of the trimmed blocks to run, and nothing the trim removed was missed.**

**THE FACELESS MAN IN THE LIT SLOT WORKED ON ONE RUN, AND THAT UNBLOCKS A WHOLE CLASS OF PICTURE.** He came back exactly as written — a broad dark shape standing in a vertical band of warm gold on stone, with no features on him anywhere and his head not turned to the viewer — and he reads as a man deciding about the boy rather than as a hole in the picture. **`ch13.md`'s block says a plate is not where a face gets decided; this is the method for putting an unreferenced character in one anyway**, and it is now proven: backlight him, put him in an aperture, and give the ban twice.

**AND THE GAZE BAN HELD, WHICH IS THE COUNTER-CASE TO THIS SESSION'S OTHER PLATE.** `ch28/the-hand-back` said *he is not looking at the viewer* and came back looking straight out; this block said the same thing and came back with the eyes up on the man. **Same reference, same day, opposite results, and the variable is the head:** there the head was turned toward the camera and only the eyes were sent elsewhere; here the whole head is turned away and up. §Scenes and plates already prescribes exactly that — *move the eyes onto an object or change the pose before writing an override* — and this pair is the folder's proof of it.

**THE SLASH AT THE SHOULDER RENDERED WHERE TWO FACIAL MARKS DID NOT.** A red slash through the cloth at the shoulder, plain in the frame — against `ch28`'s lost slash on a jaw and lost graze under an ear. **The variable is size in frame, not the wording:** all three were written the same way, by site and never by tissue, and the one that is a hand's width across is the one that came back.

**AND THE AGE READ CLOSEST YET** — fourteen or fifteen, against sixteen on `rearranging-the-road` and seventeen on `the-hand-back`, with no age clause in any of the three. **The three of them sort by crop:** distant figure, half figure, face-filling crop. `../prompt-system.md` §AGE says the eye line is the only age instrument that works on its own, and an eye line needs an eye big enough to site — which is the same limit the shoulder slash just demonstrated on a different channel.

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: both hands on the edge of the leaf with the fingers hooked over the timber, the iron strap, the dressed blocks going past the top edge, the crowd behind as a wash with no face resolved, the dried red up the forearm and across the back of the hand, the cloth-wrapped bundle with two hilts across his back, the mouth open mid-word, no tears.

**WHAT DRIFTED, three, none corrected:** the gap came back **narrower than his shoulders** rather than a little wider — which serves the beat better than the block did, since the chapter's number is eighteen inches · the lower hand sits at the waist rather than at chest height with the other · and the crop is loose for the ninth recorded time, cutting at mid-thigh rather than just below the waist.

*Candidate 1, ★.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`. Ch. 29 canon: *It opened the width of a person. Not a gate opening — a bar coming off and a leaf coming back about eighteen inches and stopping, with men behind it and their shoulders in it, and the whole weight of that hillside arriving against the front of the spear line in the same second.* … *Behind him, inside, there was light on stone and the ordinary sound of an ordinary street.* … *"Sir." He had never once called anybody sir in his life. It came out anyway. "Sir. Please. My mother is on the mountain."* … *the judge stood in the gap and gave Kael the whole of his attention, which is what he had always done.*

**THE SUBJECT IS A MEASUREMENT.** Eighteen inches is on the page as a number, so the block gives it a ratio the frame can check — the slot is a little wider than the boy's shoulders and no more — per §Scale, which says a ratio to something in the frame and never an adjective. Everything else in the picture is built to make that gap read: the leaf is heavy and dark and takes the left of the frame, the wall goes up out of the top of it, and the warm light is only ever in the slot.

**HIS HANDS ARE ON THE WOOD AND THE PAGE PUT THEM THERE TWICE** — *He got a hand on the edge of the leaf* and then *both hands on the wood now*. It is the only thing in the chapter he does with his body, and it is the thing a boy does to a door that is closing.

**HIS FACE IS ASKING AND IT IS NOT CRYING.** The chapter is explicit that he heard himself climb — *Kael heard his own voice climb and could not get underneath it* — so the block gives an open mouth mid-word, the brows up and drawn in, the chin lifted, and bans the tears; **what is happening to him is entirely in the hands and the chin.**

```
straight. Nothing else is in either hand and no blade is drawn anywhere
in the picture.

INSIDE THE SLOT, AND ONLY INSIDE IT, THE LIGHT IS WARM: pale gold on

It does not reach the boy, and everything on his side of the gate stays
cold grey.

A BROAD MAN STANDS IN THE SLOT, filling it, seen against the light
behind him as a dark shape with no features on him — no eyes and no face
drawn anywhere — and his head is not turned toward the viewer. He is not
hurrying and nothing about him is raised. He is the only person drawn
inside the gate.

HIS FACE IS TURNED UP TO THE MAN IN THE SLOT: the mouth open mid-word,
the brows raised and drawn together, the eyes wide with the pale grey
clear and steady on him. He is not looking at the viewer. There are no
tears on his face.

Dried dark red up both forearms and across the backs of both hands. One
fresh red slash through the cloth at his right shoulder, its edges
clean, and red at the cloth around it. Grey dust and soot over the front
of him. A cloth-wrapped bundle slung across his back with the shape of
two hilts in it, the hilts covered.

The crowd behind him is a wash of small shapes with no face resolved
anywhere in it, at quiet middle values with soft edges. His face, his
two hands and the lit slot carry the sharpest steps from light to dark
in the picture.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown in
the reference images. He is the only figure with a face in the picture.
Pale grey eyes. He wears undyed coarse working wool, much mended, in
dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no metal anywhere on him.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-shawl

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch29-the-shawl.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). Ch. 29 has its two.**

**THE MISS IS IN THE PICTURE, AND IT IS THE SUBJECT.** *THE TWO LINES OF SIGHT DO NOT MEET* was named in caps as the subject and it landed exactly: he is looking at her face and she is looking at the shoulder she is working on. **§Gaze names eye contact as a subject when it happens; this is the folder's first plate that names its absence as one**, and it carries *she did not know who he was* with no other clause doing any work.

**AND AN UNREFERENCED FACE CAME BACK WHOLE.** A woman past fifty, weathered, broad in the face, grey hair pulled back untidily, the knitted shawl on her shoulders and its corner bunched in her working hand — all of it authored from the block with nothing attached. **`ch18/the-man-at-the-second-bend` was the precedent and this is the confirmation:** an unnamed islander with no sheet and no second scene can be built out of the block alone, where a named character cannot.

**THE TWO OBJECTS BOTH LANDED IN THE HANDS THEY WERE GIVEN** — the bunched shawl corner pressed at his shoulder, the small stoppered bottle down at her knee — which is §Hands' one-line-per-hand doing what it does. The bottle is the beat of the whole scene and it is a thumb's width in the frame.

**THE WARMTH IS ENTIRELY IN THE STAGING, AS DESIGNED.** No warm source anywhere in it: flat cold fogged daylight, dry grass, a stone wall, a crowd going grey behind. **The only warm picture in Part IV has no warm light in it.**

**WHAT DRIFTED, three, none corrected:**
1. **The crop is loose for the tenth recorded time**, and here it went furthest — the block said the bottom edge crosses both at the lower chest and the picture holds her lap, his raised knee and the bundle on the ground.
2. **She reads nearer sixty-five than past fifty.** The block gave her *past fifty* as a number and then four clauses of wear on top of it, and §AGE's own warning applies: one age word cannot hold a line against a stack of positives pointing the other way.
3. **His cloth came back ragged rather than much mended**, which is `ch23/empty`'s drift and `ch26/not-kind-to-me`'s — **the damage register runs hot on this boy every time it is asked for.** §Injury's fix is to name what survives first and then the damage as an exception to it; this block named the mending once and the dirt, dust and slash three times.

*Candidate 4, ★.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 29 canon: *"You're bleeding, love."* … *She was somewhere past fifty. She had a shawl, one bundle, nothing else at all, and she was pointing at his shoulder with two fingers, at a cut he had taken at the wells and not looked at since.* … *She got down beside him with the noise people that age make getting down, and she took the corner of the shawl off her own shoulders, and she wet it out of a stoppered bottle that was the only thing in the bundle worth anything, and she cleaned it. She did it briskly and not very well. She talked the whole time about somebody called Ilva who was not there and who she was quite sure had gone to her sister's.* … *She did not know who he was.*

**IT IS THE ONLY WARM PICTURE IN PART IV AND IT IS NOT LIT WARM.** Nothing on that hillside has a warm source in it; the warmth is entirely in the staging — two people close, one of them working on the other — which is the instrument `ch01/talking-the-goat-down` was built on. **The light stays exactly as cold as it is in the picture beside it.**

**SHE IS BRISK AND SHE IS NOT TENDER**, and the block says so in anatomy: the hand doing the work is quick and not careful, and her mouth is open mid-word on a subject that has nothing to do with him. *She did it briskly and not very well.* **Warmth written as competence, never as sentiment** — the same fence `ch01` put on the father.

**THE BOTTLE IS THE POINT AND IT IS SMALL.** *A stoppered bottle that was the only thing in the bundle worth anything.* It gets its own hand and its own line so that the frame can price it, per §Hands.

```
Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: an open hillside outside a wall, as loose wash suggestion — dry
grass, a low field wall of loose stone close behind them, and past that
a packed mass of standing people falling away into smoke and cold grey.
Flat cold fogged daylight, no sun and no hard-edged shadow anywhere.

Composition: two figures, close together and near the viewer, the bottom
edge of the picture crossing both at the lower chest. The boy sits on
the ground at the right with his back against the low stone wall, turned
three-quarters toward the viewer. The woman is beside and above him at
the left, crouched down on her heels and leaning in, her body turned
toward his right shoulder and away from the viewer, her face in three-
quarter view and fully visible. Both faces are in the upper half of the
frame. Nobody else is drawn near them.

A WOMAN PAST FIFTY, weathered, broad in the face, the skin loose at the
jaw and the eyes small and quick. Grey hair pulled back untidily. A
heavy knitted shawl in undyed grey wool over her shoulders, its near
corner taken up off her shoulder and bunched in her working hand. Coarse
village clothing, mended, in dull brown and dead green. There is nothing
ornamental anywhere on her.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: her right hand holds the bunched corner of her own
shawl and is pressing it against the boy's right shoulder, quick and not
careful. Her left hand holds a small stoppered clay bottle down at her
knee and nothing else. His right hand rests palm down on his own raised
knee, open and empty. His left hand is flat on the ground beside him.
Four hands, two objects, nothing else in any hand.

HER MOUTH IS OPEN MID-WORD and her eyes are down on the shoulder she is
working on. She is not looking at the viewer.

HE IS LOOKING AT HER FACE: the head turned up to her, the lids fully
raised, the pale grey clear, the mouth closed, the brows level. He is
not looking at the viewer. THE TWO LINES OF SIGHT DO NOT MEET, AND THAT
IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE.

A fresh red slash through the cloth at his right shoulder, its edges
clean, with the shawl corner against it. Dried dark red up both his
forearms and across the backs of both his hands, older and browner than
the shoulder. Grey dust and soot over the front of him. A cloth-wrapped
bundle is on the ground beside him with the shape of two hilts in it,
the hilts covered.

Both faces are the most finished things in the picture and her two
working hands carry the sharpest step from light to dark after them; the
field wall, the crowd and the smoke stay at quiet middle values with
soft edges.

Preserve the boy's face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as
shown in the reference image. Pale grey eyes. He wears undyed coarse
working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive. Do not blend
their features and do not mix their clothing: the knitted shawl belongs
only to her.
```

# FILE: plates/ch30.md

# Ch. 30 — The Houses · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Vask is unpinned. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on the first, `images/portraits/vask.png` on the second — **there is no full figure of Vask**, so everything below his collar is authored in the block and will not repeat.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S TWO HALVES AND THEY ARE THE SAME SCHOOL AT TWO SCALES:** a boy under a leaf with his forearm over his own face, not closing his eyes — and the man who taught him that, arriving through a gate with lightning held in the whole of his body. **One of them is hiding perfectly and the other one has stopped hiding entirely.**

**THE LIGHT CHANGES IN THIS CHAPTER AND THE SET SHOULD SHOW IT.** Ch. 27, 28 and 29 are flat fogged daylight from end to end; here the sun is out — *the first of them came along the top terrace with the sun behind them* — and the second picture is lit by a man. **After four grey plates, hard backlight and then a cold white source is worth having**, and both are on the page rather than invented.

**THE BIG MAN IS IN NEITHER PICTURE AND THAT IS A RULING, NOT AN OVERSIGHT.** `ch13.md`: *a plate is not where a person's face gets decided; whatever it drew would become his reference by default.* He has no portrait and he is not finished with this book. The first plate does not reach him; the second is composed on the arrival, before the two of them meet, with him out of frame and named as out of frame.

**AND THE SECOND PICTURE IS `ch21/the-surge`'s BIG BROTHER, WITH THE SAME FENCE.** That plate's record settled how this element is drawn: **the reserve instrument erases the object it covers**, so the white is given a form and stays on what it runs along — *not sparking, not spilling, not one thread of it going anywhere it had not been told to go*, which is the page's own clause and the same fence Vask spoke on himself in Ch. 21. Nothing arcs into the air, down to the stone or across the yard. **The difference from `the-surge` is the surface: there it was a blade, here it is a man**, and the sword is written as the least of it.

**THE CAMERA IS WHERE THE BOY IS LYING AND HE IS NOT IN THE FRAME.** *Kael, lying on his side on somebody's threshing floor with his ribs broken, saw it clearly for perhaps a second and a half, understood half of what he was looking at.* `ch26/the-fan-downrange` proved the instrument — head-on is an event where side-on is a shape — and §Light's rule for a low camera is followed: it is written as the surfaces it exposes, never as where the viewer stands.

**AND THE MAN IS A LIGHT SOURCE, WHICH THE PAGE STATES AS A MEASUREMENT:** *the light coming off him put the shadow of the wall halfway up the house.* So the block gives the white a job in the picture — it is what lights the yard, it is cold, and the one burning roof is demoted to a quiet middle value with soft edges so that the two do not compete. §The winner and the rival, with a rival the page supplied.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** *carried* — the father lifting his son off the stones with one arm under the back and one under the knees, the white running down the chest against the boy's cheek and going round him, the mountain black above — is the chapter's other great picture and it is the same figure in the same minute in the same light as the plate that was taken. **The lamp on the tread** and **nobody looks up** are on `candidates.md` with their framing; both are cold and both are good and neither is the chapter.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-dock-leaves

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch30-the-dock-leaves.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53: *really cool*).**

**THE WHOLE INSTRUMENT LANDED AND IT IS AN EYE UNDER AN ARM.** The forearm laid horizontally across the brow with the hand out of frame past the temple, the forehead and one cheekbone covered by it, and both eyes uncovered underneath and wide open — exactly the geometry the block described, and the strip of lit face under that arm is the only lit thing low in the picture.

**AND THE GAZE HELD FOR THE SECOND TIME, WITH THE HEAD TOWARD THE CAMERA.** `ch28/the-hand-back` lost the gaze to the viewer and `ch29/the-eighteen-inches` held it by turning the head away; this one holds it with the head turned toward the viewer, **because the eyes were sent to a target that is inside the frame** — the top of the wall, with six men on it. **The refinement is that a gaze needs somewhere to go that the picture contains**, not merely a ban and a direction. Three runs, three data points, one rule.

**THE BACKLIT FACELESS MEN ARE THE SECOND PROOF IN TWO CHAPTERS**, after the man in the lit slot at `ch29`: cut at the chest by the top edge, dark against bare paper, no feature drawn on any of them, and unmistakably six men walking a wall. **Sun behind and the frame doing the rest.**

**THE MEDIUM'S BARE PAPER IS DOING REAL WORK HERE** — the entire sky is unpainted, which is what the STYLE block's *generous bare unpainted paper* has always asked for and what a fogged grey plate can never show.

**WHAT DRIFTED, and one of them is new:** the crop came back **tighter** than the block asked — it says the bottom edge crosses him at the chest and the picture holds his head, one arm and the leaves and no chest at all. **First tightening after ten loosenings**, and the difference worth noting is that this block sited the subject against two things in the frame (the arm on his own brow, the wall above his head) rather than against a landmark on his body. Recorded, not corrected.

---

*Candidate 2, ★.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 30 canon: *The first of them came along the top terrace with the sun behind them, six men, unhurried, going west toward the district. / Kael went down into the corner where the wall met the bank, into the dock leaves, and put his forearm over the bright part of his face. He did not close his eyes. A closed eye tells a body it may relax, and he could hear his own heart already, and needed it quieter than that. / They passed nine feet above his head.*

**THE SUBJECT IS AN OPEN EYE UNDER AN ARM.** Everything the chapter is about is in that one decision: the face is the bright thing, so the arm goes over the bright part of it, **and the eyes stay open on purpose**. The block gives the arm a landmark — laid across the brow — so that the strip of face below it, and both eyes in it, are what the picture is.

**HIS FATHER'S WHOLE SCHOOL IS IN THE FRAME AND NONE OF IT IS EXPLAINED.** The leaves, the corner where the wall meets the bank, the boots going past above at a stated height. §Scale: nine feet is on the page, so the men are placed against the wall-top rather than described as far away.

**AND THE MEN ARE BACKLIT AND FACELESS.** *With the sun behind them* does the work: no features are drawn on any of the six, they are dark against a bright sky at the top of the frame, and the whole lower half of the picture is in the bank's shadow. **The one thing in that shadow with any light on it is his eyes.**

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the corner of a dry-stone field wall where it meets an earth
bank, on a terraced hillside, as loose wash suggestion. Broad green dock
leaves, big and coarse and ribbed, crowd the lower half of the picture
and lie across the boy. Bright hard daylight above, the whole corner in
the bank's shadow below.

Composition: the boy lies on his side low in the corner, close to the
viewer and filling the lower left of the frame, his head turned up and
toward the viewer in three-quarter view. The bottom edge of the picture
crosses him at the chest, so no legs and no feet are in the frame. Above
and behind him the top of the field wall runs across the upper third of
the picture, and above that the sky is bright and pale.

HIS RIGHT FOREARM IS LAID ACROSS HIS OWN FACE at the brow, the arm
horizontal, the hand out of frame past his temple, so that the forehead
and one cheekbone are covered by it. Below the arm his eyes, his nose
and his mouth are uncovered and fully visible.

HIS EYES ARE WIDE OPEN AND HE IS NOT BLINKING: both lids fully raised,
the eyes turned up and to the side toward the top of the wall. The mouth
is closed. The jaw is set and easy. He is not looking at the viewer.
Nothing else about him is moving and he is pressed close into the
ground.

ALONG THE TOP OF THE WALL, NINE FEET ABOVE HIS HEAD, SIX MEN ARE WALKING
PAST FROM RIGHT TO LEFT, seen from below and against the bright sky.
They are cut off by the top edge of the picture at the chest, so that
boots, legs and the lower body of each are what is in the frame. They
are dark against the light and no face is drawn on any of them. They are
unhurried and none of them is looking down.

Dried dark red up both his forearms and across the backs of his hands.
Grey dust on the side of his face that is against the ground. A cloth-
wrapped bundle is on his back with the shape of two hilts in it, the
hilts covered, half under the leaves.

The picture is lit from behind and above: the sky and the men are the
brightest values, the leaves and the bank are dark and cool, and the
strip of his face under the arm is the one lit thing low in the frame
and carries the sharpest step from light to dark.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown in
the reference image. Pale grey eyes. He wears undyed coarse working
wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with no metal
anywhere on him.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-white-in-all-of-him

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch30-the-white-in-all-of-him.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). IT CARRIES ONE DEPARTURE THAT IS NOT A DRIFT BUT A CANON COLLISION; IT WAS PUT TO THE AUTHOR AND RULED TO STAND** (s53: *its good*). Not re-cut, not re-run, `../README.md` §Pictures that stand as drawn carries it, and it is not to be re-raised.

**THE LIGHTNING ARCS OFF HIM, AND THE PAGE SAYS IT DOES NOT.** Ch. 30: *Held in. Running the length of every part of him at once and not coming off — not sparking, not spilling, not one thread of it going anywhere it had not been told to go.* The block banned it in one compressed clause and the picture came back with long jagged bolts leaping out into the air on both sides and across the background. **The fence is not the block's, it is the character's:** Vask says his own control out loud in Ch. 21 — *fast the whole way and tight to me the whole way and nothing coming off it anywhere between* — and `ch21/the-surge` was built on it.

**AND THE STATE IT DEPICTS EXISTS IN THE BOOK, THREE CHAPTERS LATER.** Ch. 33, at the end: *It came off him in threads that went nowhere, off his shoulders, off his fingers, spilling, wasting. A man with nothing left cannot hold it exact.* **So this picture is a true picture of the wrong minute** — the man with nothing left, not the man arriving. `ch24/turn-him-toward-the-lamp` is the precedent for a plate that draws a later chapter's fact and stands; the author's ruling settles which this is.

**THE WHITE IS ALSO OFF THE BLADE.** The block gave the sword a filament its whole length, lying on the steel — *and the sword was the least of it* — and the blade came back as steel with a light edge and no filament on it. `ch21/the-surge` needed two runs to get lightning to sit on a blade without erasing it; this run lost it in the other direction, with the effect spending itself on the air instead.

**WHAT LANDED, AND MOST OF IT DID.** The low camera exactly as written — the floor as a broad plane, the boots large and near the bottom edge, the head high against a dim sky, the underside of the jaw visible. The rolled sleeves and the bare forearms above a plain coarse working jacket, with nothing tailored anywhere on him and no armour: **§Nouns' construction-then-kill-the-default recipe held on a garment for the second time this session.** The single small dull-orange fire on a far roof, not lighting him. One sword in the right hand, the left hand open and empty, and no second blade anywhere. The face breathing hard with the eyes past the viewer and a little above, giving nothing out.

**WHAT ELSE DRIFTED:** he is not clearly the light in the picture. The block made him the only source and asked for his shadow and the wall's thrown up the house behind him; the light in the picture is flatter than that and the shadows are not his. **When the effect went into the air, it stopped doing the lighting job the block gave it** — the two faults are probably one fault.

---

*Candidate 6, ★.* Anchor: `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 30 canon: *Then there was white in the yard. / Not on a blade. Everywhere. It came in through the gate at the height of a man.* … *It was a man with lightning in him. Not only in his hand. Not only down a blade. In the whole of him. Down both arms, across the shoulders, through the chest, into the ground at every step. Held in. Running the length of every part of him at once and not coming off — not sparking, not spilling, not one thread of it going anywhere it had not been told to go. He carried a longsword in his right hand with the same white standing the whole length of the blade, and the sword was the least of it.* … *Kael's father stood eight feet from his son, in his own working coat, with his sleeves rolled the way they were rolled every morning of Kael's life, and the light coming off him put the shadow of the wall halfway up the house. / He was breathing hard.*

**THE ORDINARY CLOTHES ARE THE PICTURE.** *His sleeves rolled the way they were rolled every morning of Kael's life* — a man in his own working jacket with his forearms bare, walking into a farmyard carrying the largest thing in the book. **Nothing about him is dressed for it**, and the block spends real words on the wool and the roll of the sleeve because that contrast is the whole beat. §Nouns kills the default: *coat* returns a frock coat, so the garment is built by construction and the default named and refused.

**HE IS BREATHING HARD AND THAT IS THE ONLY THING HIS FACE DOES.** §Gaze forbids a face doing nothing; this one gets the open mouth and the working chest, the eyes level on something past the camera, and nothing else — which on this man is correct, because `../canon-brief.md` says his eyes are empty and that is not the same as vacant.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the iron-haired man.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a farmyard threshing floor at the end of the day, as loose wash
suggestion — flat worn flagstones, a low stone yard wall, the dark mouth
of a gateway behind him, a house gable implied at one side with fire
burning in its roof a long way back. The daylight is nearly gone and the
sky above the wall is dim.

Composition: the picture is seen from a hand's breadth above the
flagstones. The floor runs away from the bottom edge as a broad plane
lit end to end, and the man is seen from below: the underside of his jaw
is visible, his boots are large and near the bottom of the frame, and
his head sits high against the dim sky. He is walking in through the
gateway toward the viewer and is not yet close, standing about three of
his own heights away. He fills the middle of the frame and is the only
figure in the picture; the boy whose view this is is not in it.

THE WHITE IS IN THE MAN AND NOT AROUND HIM: hard thin white filaments
run down both arms from the shoulders to the fingers, across the
shoulders, down the middle of the chest, and down both legs into the
stone at each foot, following the length of him. Every filament lies on
him and clings to him: nothing arcs off into the air, nothing leaps to
the stone, and there is no glow or haze around him anywhere.

HIS RIGHT HAND HOLDS ONE PLAIN LONGSWORD and nothing else, carried low
and back, the point behind him and clear of the ground. The same hard
white filament stands the whole length of that blade, hilt to point,
lying on the steel, with the steel drawn as steel underneath it and
still reading as metal. His left hand is empty and open at his side. Two
hands, one blade, nothing else in either hand, and no other blade, hilt
or point is drawn anywhere in the picture.

HE IS THE LIGHT IN THE PICTURE. It is cold and hard-edged and it comes
off him alone: it throws his shadow and the shadow of the yard wall up
the house behind him and puts a rim on the flagstones around his feet.
The fire in the far roof is small, dull orange, soft-edged and low in
value, and does not light him or the floor.

HE IS BREATHING HARD: the mouth open, the chest and shoulders working,
the head level. His eyes are on something past the viewer and a little
above it, and he is not looking at the viewer. The face gives out
nothing else — not angry, not grim, not fierce.

He wears a plain coarse working jacket of undyed dark wool, straight-cut
and closed at the throat, the sleeves rolled up above the elbows so that
both forearms are bare, and heavy village working trousers and boots.
Nothing tailored and nothing decorated: not a frock coat, not a uniform
and not armour, with no metal on him anywhere except the sword.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown in
the reference image — the weathered face, the iron-dark hair, the iron-
dark eyes.
```

# FILE: plates/ch31.md

# Ch. 31 — The Line · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Elarine is thirteen; the two adults are unpinned and unreferenced. Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png` on the first. **The second plate has no reference attached and no face in it.**

**THE CHAPTER IS VALERIA'S AND NEITHER PLATE IS OF HER.** That is deliberate: `ch28/rearranging-the-road` is a solo Valeria and `ch27/i-need-you` is her face at a hand's width, and what this chapter gives that the folder has nothing of is **a room deciding, and a yard saying goodbye.** She is the eye in both and appears in neither, which is what she does for the whole chapter.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S ONE ARGUMENT, PUT TWICE.** *A house stands for its own, and the sums are done in houses, and it is not possible to be cruel to a quantity you have never once had occasion to write down.* Inside: a hand closes a shutter and a sentence is finished. Outside: a woman who has just put her own name to a wall gives her daughter instructions and then puts a hand flat against the side of her head. **The same love makes both pictures**, and that is the thing the chapter will not let anybody off.

**THE FACELESS PLATE IS PRICED, NOT SMUGGLED** (s43). `ch27/the-sky` and `ch22/kael-of-the-island` are the precedents and both records say the same thing — the figure came back smaller than the block asked for and that was the power. This one goes further: **no face is resolved anywhere in it at all**, which §Plates says is written as two bans, and both are in the block. What buys it is that the subject is a hand on a shutter and the beat is what stops being audible when it closes.

**AND THE SHUTTER IS THE CHAPTER'S OWN INSTRUMENT, NOT AN INVENTED ONE.** *A man had begun a sentence about the north bastion when he stopped, went to the window himself rather than sending anybody, closed the shutter, came back, and finished the sentence. / Nobody remarked on it.* Four hours later: *That was what the shutter was for, whether the man who closed it had known it or not.* **The plate is composed on the closing**, with the room lit behind him and the last of the daylight going out of the gap — so the picture is a man doing an ordinary courteous thing, which is exactly how the page plays it.

**THE HAND-AND-OBJECT CROP IS THE EXCEPTION AND IT EARNS ITSELF HERE** (s42, s45): the object is a shutter, the hand is a house's, and the default chest-up would have to resolve a face the folder does not own. `ch13.md`'s rule reaches every unnamed head in that room.

**TWO LIGHTS, AND BOTH ARE ON THE PAGE.** *The light had gone orange along the top of the eastern wall* — so the first plate is lit by a low sun that touches nothing but the top of a wall, with the two of them in the shade under it. The second is the only lamplit interior in Part IV. §Light: a named source says where it is, that it is the only one, what it leaves dark, and where the warmth lands, and both blocks do.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **Drusane's two hands on his son's face** and **Oss's father** — *"You were always enough," the man told him. "You were always plenty."* — are the same beat as the plate that was taken, one yard over, and neither boy has a portrait (`ch13.md` deferred Oss for exactly that and named the portrait as the work that unblocks it). **Severin agreed with by the wrong man** — *there was nothing on his face at all, and she thought that a man who has just been agreed with does not usually look like that* — is the chapter's other great face and it asks the block for a face doing nothing, which §Gaze forbids and which renders as vacancy. It stays on `candidates.md` for a session willing to solve it.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-hand-flat

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch31-the-hand-flat.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). The closest match to its block of anything run this session.**

**THE NAMED LIGHT LANDED EXACTLY AND IT IS THE PICTURE'S WHOLE MOOD.** *The last of the sun is on the top courses of that wall and nowhere else: a narrow band of warm orange along the top of the stone, with the whole of the yard below it in cool blue shade.* That is what came back, to the course — one warm stripe at the top of the frame and everything under it blue — and §Light's four requirements (where it is, that it is the only one, what it leaves dark, where the warmth lands) are what bought it.

**THE HAND IS THE PICTURE.** Flat against the side of the head, over the temple and the ear, the fingers spread and still, the wrist relaxed, resting and not gripping — and it is drawn at the same finish as the girl's face, which is what the closing paragraph asked for.

**AND THE FACELESS NEAR FIGURE HELD FOR THE THIRD TIME THIS SESSION.** The woman is seen from behind and to one side with nothing of her face resolved — after the man in the lit slot at `ch29` and the six backlit men at `ch30`. **Three different methods, three first-run successes:** an aperture with light behind it, a silhouette against a bright sky, and a head simply turned away in ordinary shade.

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: both heads bound close and severe with nothing loose, the girl's near-black closed to the throat and the woman's deep blue of the same quality with no ornament on either, the cistern head behind them, the trestle tables further off, the girl's eyes up on a face the viewer cannot see, her mouth closed and her jaw tight, and nothing in her crying.

**WHAT DRIFTED — ONE THING, AND IT CORRECTS A CLAIM THIS SESSION MADE THREE PLATES AGO.** The reddened burn along her jaw is not in the picture. `ch29.md` recorded that a shoulder slash rendered where a jaw slash and a graze under an ear did not, and concluded that **the variable is size in frame** — this run contradicts it. Her face is nearly a portrait here, the mark had all the room it could want, and it still did not arrive. **Three small facial marks asked for this session and three missed, at three different scales.** What the three have in common is not size: it is that each was named as a *state of skin* — reddened, grazed, a slash — on a face that the same block was also instructing to do something. The shoulder slash had cloth to tear and nothing else competing for that square inch. **Recorded, not corrected, and the next block that wants a mark on a face should give it a torn or dressed thing to sit against.**

---

*Candidate 1, ★.* Anchor: `images/portraits/elarine-14.png`. Ch. 31 canon: *She found her in the corner of the yard by the cistern head, with her mother, and stopped where she was, because you do not walk into that.* … *"Listen to me now, because I am not going to be able to say it twice. If this goes the way the old man says it will, you are going to be what is left of this house."* … *Something moved in Elarine's face and did not get anywhere.* … *Then she put her hand flat against the side of her daughter's head and left it there, and neither of them said anything more.* And the state she is in: *There was a burn along her jaw that nobody had looked at yet.*

**THE SUBJECT IS ONE HAND ON A HEAD, AND THE FACE UNDER IT IS THE OTHER SUBJECT.** The mother is in the frame and her face is not: she is the near figure, turned away, cropped and unresolved, and everything the picture spends is spent on the daughter. **That is not a dodge — it is the chapter**, where the woman gives instructions, does not mention the wall, and puts her hand out at the end instead of saying anything.

**AND ELARINE'S FACE IS HELD, NOT BLANK.** `../canon-brief.md` puts her at assessment in progress, giving nothing back, and the page says something moved and did not get anywhere — so the block writes the holding as anatomy: lids raised, eyes steady on her mother's, mouth closed, jaw tight. **Her one tell, the spots of colour high on the cheeks, belongs to Ch. 13 and Ch. 17 and is not available here**, and the block says so by not naming it.

```
Image 1: elarine-14.png — portrait reference of the small girl with bound dark hair.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the corner of a stone courtyard in the evening, as loose wash
suggestion — the round stone head of a cistern close behind them, tables
carried out into the yard further off, a high wall beyond. The last of
the sun is on the top courses of that wall and nowhere else: a narrow
band of warm orange along the top of the stone, with the whole of the
yard below it in cool blue shade. There is no other light in the
picture.

Composition: two figures, close together and near the viewer, the bottom
edge of the picture crossing both at the lower chest. The woman is the
nearer of the two, at the left, seen from behind and to one side, her
head and shoulder cut by the left edge of the frame and her face turned
away from the viewer so that no part of it is drawn. The girl faces her,
turned three-quarters toward the viewer, her face fully visible and
filling the upper right of the frame.

THE WOMAN'S RIGHT HAND IS FLAT AGAINST THE SIDE OF THE GIRL'S HEAD, laid
over the temple and the ear with the fingers spread and still, the wrist
relaxed, resting there and not gripping. Her other hand and arm are out
of the frame. The girl's own hands are down at her sides and empty.
Nothing else is in any hand and there is no object anywhere between
them.

Nothing is happening and neither of them is speaking.

THE GIRL IS LOOKING UP AT THE WOMAN'S FACE: the lids fully raised, the
eyes very dark and steady on her, the mouth closed and even, the jaw
tight, the brows level. She is not looking at the viewer. Nothing in her
face is crying.

Her ink-dark hair is bound close and severe against the head with
nothing loose anywhere. A reddened burn runs along the line of her jaw
on the near side, dry and unbound, with no dressing and no cloth on it.

Her clothing is plain, close-cut and buttoned high to the throat, very
well made, in a flat near-black with no ornament of any kind on it. The
woman wears the same quality in deep blue — a long-sleeved gown closed
high, undecorated. Do not mix their clothing: the deep blue belongs only
to the woman and the near-black only to the girl.

The girl's face and the hand on her head are the most finished things in
the picture and carry the sharpest step from light to dark; the cistern
head, the tables, the yard and the wall stay at quiet middle values with
soft edges.

Preserve the girl's face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as
shown in the reference image. The woman is older, small and finely built
like her, with the same ink-dark hair bound close, and her face is not
drawn.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-shutter

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch31-the-shutter.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). Ch. 31 has its two, and neither of them is of the girl whose chapter it is.**

**THE TWO LIGHTS DID THE ARGUMENT, WHICH IS THE WHOLE REASON THE PICTURE EXISTS.** A warm orange room off one lamp on a side table, and one narrow band of cold blue daylight coming through what is left of the window gap and lying across the floorboards and stopping. **The band is in the picture exactly as the block sited it**, and it is the coldest thing in a warm room, which is the chapter.

**AND IT IS THE FOLDER'S FIRST PICTURE WITH NO FACE IN IT AT ALL.** Fourteen or so standing figures, one seated at the head of the table, a man at the window with his back turned — and not one eye anywhere. §Plates' two bans, written out, carried a whole interior. **Four faceless figures on four first runs this session, by four methods**, and this is the one that proves it scales past a single figure to a room.

**THE FURNITURE CAME BACK AS READ, NOT AS DESCRIBED.** The long bare table, the chairs pushed back and standing away from it, nobody sitting except the one at the far end, the lamp on a side table rather than on the table itself. Valeria's own reading sequence — exits, weapons, ground, then the people — is what the Setting paragraph was built out of, and the room in the picture is that room.

**WHAT DRIFTED, two:** both his hands are up at the window rather than one on the shutter and one hanging — §Hands' one-line-per-hand was written for exactly that and the second line said *hangs at his side*, which is a harder thing for a picture to assert than a thing being held · and the crop went full length again where the block said the waist.

**A NOTE ON THE BLOCK ITSELF:** it is the only plate in Book One with no reference attached, so the build supplies the STYLE block's Medium paragraph instead of the scene style line. That paragraph is now inside the fence, because the block has run and is stored verbatim.

---

*Candidate 5, ★.* No reference attached. Ch. 31 canon: *The room had been somebody's dining hall and there were nineteen people in it.* … *a long table down the middle with nothing on it, chairs pushed back, everybody standing.* … *Outside, past the shutters, the sound off the hill was still going. / It had been going since before she came in. It was not loud. It came in under the talking the way rain comes in under talking, and every so often a piece of it stood up out of the rest and had one person inside it, and then went back down again. / Nobody in that room had mentioned it once.* … *A man had begun a sentence about the north bastion when he stopped, went to the window himself rather than sending anybody, closed the shutter, came back, and finished the sentence. / Nobody remarked on it.*

**NO FACE IS RESOLVED ANYWHERE IN IT, AND THAT IS TWO BANS, WRITTEN OUT** (§Plates). The man at the window has his back to the room and to the viewer; the nineteen behind him are a wash of standing shapes in lamplight. **What the picture is about cannot be put on a face**: it is a courtesy performed by somebody who is not being cruel.

**THE TWO LIGHTS DO THE ARGUMENT.** Warm lamplight fills the room and stops at the window; the gap in the shutter is cold, and the cold half is going out as the leaf comes across. **The warmth is inside and the noise is outside**, which is the district in one frame.

**AND THE ROOM IS DRAWN AS IT WAS READ.** Valeria's own sequence — exits, weapons, ground, then the people — gives the block its furniture: the long bare table, the chairs pushed back, nobody sitting. **One chair at the far end is occupied and its occupant is not resolved either**, which is all the page needs from the princess here.

```
Medium: loose expressive watercolour and ink on rough cold-press
paper, visible paper tooth and pigment granulation, scratchy fine
ink linework, wash blooms with hard irregular edges, generous bare
unpainted paper.

Setting: a long dining hall in a stone house at dusk, as loose wash
suggestion — a bare table running away down the middle of the room with
the chairs pushed back from it, standing figures along both sides of it,
and a shuttered window in the near wall at the left. Warm lamplight
fills the room from a lamp on a side table, low and orange, and it is
the only light inside.

Composition: the bottom edge of the picture crosses the near man at the
waist. He stands at the left, close to the viewer, seen from behind and
slightly to one side, his back to the room and his face entirely out of
sight. The window is in front of him. Past him the room runs away to the
right and back, and the standing figures are further off and smaller.

HIS RIGHT HAND IS ON THE EDGE OF THE WOODEN SHUTTER at the height of his
own shoulder, drawing it across the window. The leaf is most of the way
closed and a hand's width of the opening is still uncovered. His left
hand hangs at his side. Nothing else is in either hand.

WHAT IS IN THE GAP: the last of a cold grey daylight, a dull sky with
smoke standing in it, and nothing else drawn or resolved out there at
all. The light coming through that gap is thin, cold and blue against
the orange of the room, and it falls in one narrow band across the
floorboards and stops.

The room behind him: eighteen other people, standing, drawn as a wash of
dark shapes at quiet middle values with soft edges. One chair at the far
end of the table has somebody seated in it and that figure is not
resolved either. Nobody is turned toward the man at the window.

NO EYES ARE VISIBLE ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE and no face is drawn on
anybody. Do not turn any head toward the viewer.

The man is broad and heavy through the shoulder, in good plain dark
clothing well cut, with no ornament, no badge and no chain. Nothing
about him is hurrying.

The hand on the shutter and the narrow band of cold light on the floor
carry the sharpest steps from light to dark in the picture; the table,
the chairs, the standing figures and the far end of the room stay soft
and quiet.
```

# FILE: plates/ch32.md

# Ch. 32 — The Mountain · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Neris and Vask are unpinned. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` + `images/portraits/neris.png` on the first, `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE TWO THINGS HE IS COUNTING AT ONCE.** *both of the things he was counting were getting closer, at the same time, on purpose, and that he was the only person on that mountain holding both numbers.* One picture is what it costs inside the house; the other is what is coming up the mountain at it. **They are the same night, forty minutes apart.**

**THE SECOND PLATE IS THE DELIBERATE INVERSE OF THE BOOK'S FIRST ONE.** `ch01/fallen-stars` is this mountain at night with the town in a heap of warm light far below and a boy being turned to look at it. **This is the same boy on the same ground with the lights coming up at him**, and the chapter says so itself: *He had stood up here at four years old and looked down at the lights in their corner and been certain they were something else entirely. / There were lights on the mountain.* The folder's set now opens and closes Book One on the same view.

**THE DRAIN-MARK IS DRAWN ONCE AND IT IS NOT STYLING.** `../canon-brief.md`'s standing fence — white hair is never whitened for drama, and the drain register is **pure quartz white, cold and hard**, never Aeliana's warm silver — is what the first block is built to satisfy. The page gives the mark a direction and a rate, so the block does too: *It came up out of the roots and ran down the length of the piece the way frost runs across the top of a pail.* **One piece of hair, white from the root, and every other hair on her head still dark** — the second piece is a page later and the block does not anticipate it.

**AND THE PREGNANCY IS OUT OF THE FRAME BY THE CROP, WHICH IS THE STANDING RULING** (author, s51). She is kneeling over her son with hours to go; the bottom edge crosses her above the collarbone, so nothing of her below it is authored or drawable, and what the frame holds of her is her face, her hair and **her hands, which is where the book keeps her**. Posture, cloth and crop carry it, exactly as `README.md` prescribes.

**THE FAMILY IS THE CROP.** Her two hands are on his chest; his father's two hands are on his shoulders and his father's face is out of the frame. *His father's hands did not tighten and did not let go.* **Vask is in that picture as two hands and nothing else**, which is what he is in that scene and very nearly what he is in the book.

**THE MENDING SHOWS, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE POINT OF DRAWING IT.** `ch26`'s Severin does it with nothing showing — *nothing had shown. No light. No sound. It had been like watching a man decide something.* — and this is written against that: *This showed.* Not as light and not as effect; as **sweat along a hairline, breath through the teeth, hands shaking on him, and the colour leaving one piece of hair.** No glow is written anywhere in the block, so nothing is put in front of the filter.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **the two unmatched short blades put down beside his hand** is the chapter's quietest beat and it is a second hand-and-object plate in three chapters. **The briefing in the yard** — a boy of thirteen giving ground to a captain of the royal guard, pointing with a short blade — is the chapter's other great picture and it wants five resolved figures, two of whom have no portrait. **The princess in the yard**, in ruined shoes with a soldier's cloak over a gold-hemmed gown, looking at the cloud and not at the house, is legal and good and it is the only one of the three that survives entirely on `candidates.md` with its framing intact.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-white-in-her-hair

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch32-the-white-in-her-hair.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OPTIMISED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run.

**THEIR ONE STRUCTURAL CHANGE FIXES A COMPOSITION THAT COULD NOT HAVE WORKED.** Mine said *THE BOTTOM EDGE OF THE PICTURE CROSSES HER JUST ABOVE THE COLLARBONE* — but she is leaning down over him from above, so a bottom edge cannot cross her at all; her body would have to be cut by the top of the frame or occluded by her own arms. Theirs states it as what is in the picture instead: **Nothing of her torso or clothing is in the frame; her arms enter from the top edge.** The pregnancy ruling is satisfied by composition rather than by a landmark, and it landed exactly — **there is no part of her below the neck in that picture except two bare forearms.**

**THE WHITE PIECE IS THE PICTURE AND IT TAKES NONE OF THE FIRELIGHT.** Root to end, hanging clear in the air between the two faces, hard cold white against an orange room — with every other hair on her head dark brown and nothing else white anywhere in the frame. **The drain-mark's first appearance in the set, and the fence held on the first run.**

**AND THE SIX HANDS ARE FOUR, WHICH CONFIRMS §HANDS RATHER THAN BREAKING IT.** The block names four hands one at a time — her two flat on his chest, a man's two on his shoulders — and then states the total as *six hands in the picture*, which is a slip; the boy's own are excluded two sentences earlier. **The picture drew the four that were described and ignored the total.** §Hands was written off *three blades total* failing twice while one line per hand held; this is the same result from the other direction, and it says the per-item lines are what the tool reads and the count line is only a check on the writer.

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: the fire close at the left as the only light, the two faces near and both finished, her lids lowered with the eyes on her own hands, his mouth open on one word, the fleece, the dust and dried red on his chest, and the man in the picture as two hands and nothing else.

**WHAT DRIFTED, one:** his eyes are up and past the hanging piece rather than fixed on it. **The gaze had a target inside the frame and still went wide** — which is the first exception to the pattern the other three plates set today, and the difference here is that the target is an inch from his own cheek: there is no head-turn available to point at a thing that close, so the block asked the eyes to do it alone.

```
Image 1: neris.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired woman.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the inside of a small one-room stone house at night, as
loose wash suggestion — rough boards under them, a sheepskin fleece,
the edge of a hearth close by at the left with a big fire standing up
in it. The fire is the only strong light and it is low and close and
warm; the room above them goes dark quickly and the far side of it is
lost.

Composition: the boy is on his back on the fleece at the bottom of
the frame, seen from above and slightly to one side, his face turned
up and filling the lower part of the picture. The woman is above him,
leaning down over him, her face in three-quarter view and fully
visible in the upper part, the two heads close together and near the
viewer. Nothing of her torso or clothing is in the frame; her arms
enter from the top edge.

WHAT IS IN THE FRAME, EACH IN ITS OWN PLACE: her two arms come down
into the picture from above, sleeves pushed to the elbow, both
forearms bare and worked, and both her hands flat on the boy's chest
at the bottom edge, the fingers spread, the wrists loose. A man's two
hands come into the frame from the top corner behind the boy's head
and rest on the boy's two shoulders, holding him steady and not
gripping; the man himself is not otherwise in the picture. The boy's
own hands and forearms are not in the frame. Six hands in the
picture, all of them empty, nothing in any hand.

BETWEEN THE TWO FACES, ONE PIECE OF HER HAIR HANGS DOWN out of the
rest, come loose, thick as a finger, falling past the boy's cheek and
hanging clear in the air. THAT ONE PIECE IS WHITE FROM THE ROOT TO
THE END — a hard cold white with no warmth and no yellow in it, the
white of a split stone. EVERY OTHER HAIR ON HER HEAD IS DARK BROWN
AND UNCHANGED: no grey, no silver and no second white piece anywhere
on her, and no white anywhere else in the picture.

HER FACE IS WORKING: the mouth a little open with the breath going
through the teeth, the jaw set, sweat standing along the hairline and
at the temple, the lids lowered and the eyes down on her own hands.
She is not looking at the boy's face and she is not looking at the
viewer.

HIS EYES ARE ON THE HANGING PIECE OF HAIR, not on her face and not on
the viewer: the lids fully raised, the pale grey clear, the head
slightly turned toward it. His mouth is open on one short word.

He is stripped to the waist on the fleece with grey dust and dried
dark red on his chest and shoulders.

The white piece of hair carries the sharpest step from light to dark
in the picture; both faces are finished but held at closer values,
and the fire, the boards and the room stay soft and quiet. The
firelight is orange on the near sides of both faces and on her
forearms, and the white hair takes none of it.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces,
hair colour, colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features.
Her eyes are dark; his are pale grey.
```
