### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-lights-on-the-mountain

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch32-the-lights-on-the-mountain.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53).**

**THE THREE-PLACE COLOUR INSTRUCTION IS THE BEST PALETTE DISCIPLINE OF THE SESSION.** *The picture is nearly black and its colour is entirely in three places: the orange under the cloud, the doorway of the house, and the lights on the slope.* All three are there and there is nothing else warm anywhere in the frame. **Naming where the colour lives, positively and by count, does what a ban never has** — which is the s42/s45 ruling arriving as a technique rather than a prohibition.

**AND THE RHYME IS NOW ON THE PAGE.** This is `ch01/fallen-stars` inverted, as designed: the same boy on the same mountain at night, the lit doorway of the same house behind him, and the lights coming up the slope at him instead of lying in a heap on the water below. **The set can now show the two side by side.**

**THE LIT DOORWAY IS THE PICTURE'S QUIET WIN.** One patch of warm orange thrown out across the stones of the yard, with the house otherwise dark — and it is the only thing in the frame that belongs to him.

**WHAT DRIFTED, and it is a real limit rather than a miss: THE COUNTS ARE NOT COUNTABLE AT THIS SCALE.** Two strings of carried lights arrived at two depths along the slope, exactly as arranged, and neither is seven or nineteen. `ch27/the-sky` carried four burning objects at four depths *and* the number, because each was as big in the frame as a cart; here each light was specified as *no bigger than a pinhead*. **So §Hands' one-line-per-item instrument buys the arrangement at any scale and the number only while the items are large enough to be seen as items.** Recorded; nothing in the picture is worse for it, because a reader counts the strings and not the flames.

---

*Candidate 7, ★.* Anchor: `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png`. Ch. 32 canon: *Kael went up onto the woodpile. / From the top of it you could see the whole of the lower ground. / He had stood up here his whole life. He had stood up here at four years old and looked down at the lights in their corner and been certain they were something else entirely. / There were lights on the mountain. / They were below the second bend and off to the east of it. Four of them.* … *Then there were seven. / Then, further down and further east, on the ground above the runnel where the olive line ran, there was a string of them coming along the terrace one behind another, and he counted that string twice and got nineteen both times.* … *there is something down at the bottom of the path with a lot of light on it that I have not been able to count yet.* And the sky over it: *the cloud had come down onto the top of the mountain and stayed there, and the underside of it was orange from one end of the island to the other, and it moved the way water moves.*

**EVERY LIGHT IS COUNTED WHERE IT SITS.** `ch27/the-sky` proved that §Hands' one-line-per-item generalises off hands and carried four burning objects at four depths on a single run. This picture is that instrument at its limit: **seven points in one group, nineteen in a line, one mass at the bottom**, each with its own place in the frame and the count stated after it.

**AND THE COUNT IS THE CHARACTER.** He counted the string twice and got nineteen both times. A picture of a boy on a woodpile is a picture of nothing; a picture of a boy on a woodpile **with a countable number of lights in it** is the whole of what he is, and the reader can do the count the block did.

**NO FACE, PRICED** (s43). He is small, high in his own yard, with his back to the viewer — the same price `ch22/kael-of-the-island` and `ch27/the-sky` both paid and both recorded as the picture's power. §Plates' two bans are written out.

**THE ONE WARM THING NEAR HIM IS HIS OWN DOOR.** *A fire going too big in it and light coming out of the doorway onto the stones.* So the house is behind and below him with a lit doorway on the yard, and everything else warm in the picture is a long way off and coming.

```
Image 1: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the boy, for his build and silhouette. His face is not resolved in this picture.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a mountainside at night, high up, as loose wash suggestion. In
the near ground a small stone house with a low roof stands at the left,
its open doorway throwing one patch of warm orange light out across the
stones of a bare yard. A stacked woodpile stands at the right of the
yard, chest-high, the split faces of the logs showing. Below and beyond
the yard the ground falls away in a long dark slope of stone walls,
empty pens and terraces, going down and away into black.

Above everything, low cloud sits on the mountain and fills the whole
upper third of the picture, and the underside of that cloud is lit dull
orange from below, end to end, moving and uneven like water. It is the
largest thing in the frame.

Composition: one boy standing on top of the woodpile at the right, seen
from behind and a little below, small in the frame and dark against the
lit cloud. He is upright and completely still, both arms hanging, his
head turned down and out toward the slope below. He is the only person
in the picture.

WHAT IS BURNING BELOW HIM, EACH IN ITS OWN PLACE: seven small points of
warm light close together on the dark slope in the middle distance, low
in the frame and to the left of him, not moving. A line of nineteen
more, further down and further to the right, strung out one behind
another along a level terrace line, evenly spaced. And at the very
bottom of the picture, a long way down where the path leaves the frame,
one larger mass of orange light with no separate flames drawn in it.
Those are all the lights on the slope, and there is nothing else lit
anywhere on it.

Each of the twenty-six small ones is a single carried flame: a point of
warm orange no bigger than a pinhead in the frame, with a little haze
around it. No figures are drawn on the slope anywhere — only the lights.

NO FACE IS VISIBLE ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE and no eyes are drawn
anywhere in it. Do not turn the boy's head toward the viewer.

The picture is nearly black and its colour is entirely in three places:
the orange under the cloud, the doorway of the house, and the lights on
the slope. The stone, the walls, the pens and the boy are cold dark
greys and browns with bare paper left in the lights on the cloud.

Preserve his build and clothing as shown in the reference image — undyed
coarse working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, with
no metal on him. A cloth-wrapped bundle is across his back with the
shape of two hilts in it, the hilts covered.

# FILE: plates/ch33.md

# Ch. 33 — The Breach · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen, Aeliana fourteen; Vask and Severin are unpinned. Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` + `images/portraits/vask.png` on the first, `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE CHAPTER'S TWO ENDS OF ONE NIGHT: A CHILD ARRIVES AND A FATHER IS SPENT.** Both are composed on a pair of hands. The sword goes into a boy's hand with his father's fingers closing it; a lamp is held at the height of an old man's shoulder by a girl who has never carried anything. **Neither picture has a death in it and neither has a birth in it**, and the chapter is entirely in both.

**THE DEATH IS NOT DRAWN AND THIS IS NOT SQUEAMISHNESS.** *The plate follows the page* permits it and the folder's own record advises against it: at s51 the author dropped two built and competent grim plates (`the-good-part`, `not-kind-to-me`) and adopted `aftermath` and the two fans instead. **What the chapter is about is not the sword coming off a dead man's hand — it is the four seconds before, where a man hands his son a weapon he cannot lift and tells him he will grow into it in a year.** The rest is on `candidates.md` with its framing, unspent.

**THE WHITE IS OUT OF HIM IN THE FIRST PLATE, AND THE PAGE PUT IT OUT.** *Vask stood in his own doorway with the longsword hanging and the white going out of him in front of everybody.* It comes back later, outside, and that later is not this moment. **So the man in this picture has nothing on him at all**, which is worth more than any amount of lightning: he is a tired man in a burnt jacket giving his son a sword. `ch21/the-surge` and `ch30/the-white-in-all-of-him` already own the element.

**AND THE HAND ON THE HEAD IS DELIBERATELY NOT THE MOMENT.** *He put his hand flat on the top of his son's head, once, the way he had done perhaps eleven times in Kael's life* — which is `ch31/the-hand-flat` two chapters earlier with the sexes swapped, and the set should not spend the same gesture twice in three chapters. **The transfer is the beat with the object in it**, and the object is the one Book Two is carrying.

**THE SECOND PLATE SPENDS SOMETHING THE FOLDER HAS PROTECTED SINCE Ch. 22.** `../canon-brief.md`: *her hands do nothing: unoccupied, holding nothing, at rest*, and `ch26.md` passed over a candidate on exactly that ground — that a picture of the princess's hands doing something is *an argument worth having later*. **This is later, and the page is what buys it**: she carries a lamp round a fleece for the length of a birth, is sick against a wall, comes back and picks it up again. The fence is against a decorative princess, not against this.

**AND HER BEAUTY DOES NOT ARRIVE AS A VERDICT, BECAUSE THE PICTURE IS OF A JOB.** `README.md` makes a portrait *plate* of her illegal; this is a moment plate, and every clause in it is a task — the arm out, the height held, the eyes off the floor. **Nothing in the block admires her.**

**THE BIRTH IS OUT OF FRAME ENTIRELY AND SO IS THE MOTHER.** Neris is on the floor eighteen inches from the bottom edge and is not in the picture; nothing of her, nothing of the child, nothing of the work is drawn. This is the pregnancy ruling doing what it was written to do (author, s51), and it costs the plate nothing, because **what is worth drawing here is the girl holding the light.**

**THE RED EYES ARE NOT IN EITHER PICTURE.** Neris's and Wren's both belong to this chapter and both are the kind of fact `../canon-brief.md` protects — *two body-marks of magic exist* — and neither is drawn here, because the two moments that carry them are the two that cannot be composed without putting the mother's body or the newborn's face in a frame this sheet has just kept them out of. **Kael holding his brother** is on `candidates.md` for a chapter that can hold it.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **facing the wrong way** — the boy carried down the slot over an old man's shoulder, awake, arms swinging, the sword still in hands that will not open, watching his own roof take at one corner — is the strongest single image left in Book One and it is a third plate on a sheet that has two. **Walking out with nothing in either hand** and **the mountain going** are both on `candidates.md`. **Oss on the lip** is blocked for the same reason it has always been blocked: there is no `images/portraits/oss.png`, and `ch13.md` says the work that unblocks it is a portrait, not a plate.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-sword

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch33-the-sword.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53: *great*). Ch. 33 has its two.**

**BOTH EYE-LINES LANDED AS DESIGNED, AND THE DESIGN WAS AN INVERSION.** The man is looking at his son's face; the son is looking down at the thing arriving in his hand. **That is the whole editorial of the plate and it is carried by two clauses**, and it is the third time today a gaze has held when the block gave it a target the head could turn toward — the exceptions this session are both cases where the target sat an inch from the face.

**THE SIZE IS THE SECOND SUBJECT AND THE RATIO CARRIED IT.** *From pommel to point it is nearly as long as the boy is tall* — and in the picture it is absurd on him, which is what *it is not a knife; you will grow into it in a year* needs a reader to see. §Scale's rule about a ratio and never an adjective has now worked three times this session, on an age, on a hand and on a sword.

**AND THE TWO SMALL PAGE-FACTS BOTH ARRIVED**: the cloth-wrapped bundle with two covered hilts across the boy's back, and the man's sleeve rolled above the elbow over a plain coarse working jacket with nothing tailored on it. The lit doorway is behind them at one side and the burning pens are a low orange line far off at the other, exactly as sited.

**WHAT DRIFTED, two:** the man's left hand is not closed on the flat of the blade halfway down — his other arm is out of the frame, so the picture has three of the four hands the block wrote and the sword is supported at the grip alone · and the crop went to the hip where the block said the waist. **Neither costs the beat**, because the beat is the grip changing owner and the grip is where all three remaining hands are.

---

*Candidate 2, ★.* Anchors: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`, `images/portraits/vask.png`. Ch. 33 canon: *Then he turned round and put the longsword into his son's hands. / He did it the way he did everything, which was with no ceremony in it anywhere: he took the blade in his left hand halfway down and turned the grip over and put it into Kael's right hand and closed Kael's fingers on it with his own, and pressed once, and let go. / It was far too big. It was a grown man's war sword and it weighed what a grown man's war sword weighs and Kael's wrist dropped four inches under it before he got it up. / "Both hands," his father said. / "Father—" / "Both hands. It is not a knife. You will grow into it in a year."*

**FOUR HANDS AND ONE OBJECT, ONE LINE EACH** (§Hands). The blade is held bare in the man's left hand halfway down, the grip is turned over into the boy's right, and the man's right hand is closed over the boy's fingers on it. The boy's left is coming up to meet it and has not arrived. **The whole beat is a grip changing owner**, and if any of those four hands is vague the picture is not of anything.

**THE SIZE IS THE SECOND SUBJECT AND IT GETS A RATIO** (§Scale, a ratio to something in the frame and never an adjective): pommel to point, the sword is nearly as long as the boy is tall. *It is not a knife. You will grow into it in a year.*

**THE EYES GO THE WAY THE SCENE PUTS THEM, WHICH IS THE INVERSE OF WHAT THE PICTURE WANTS.** The father is looking at his son's face; the son is looking at the thing arriving in his hand. `../canon-brief.md` on the father: *iron-dark eyes, and they are empty* — level, giving nothing, and not hostile, cold, sad, kind or tired. Every clause on him is anatomy.

```
Image 1: vask.png — portrait reference of the iron-haired man.
Image 2: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a bare mountain yard at night in front of a small stone house,
as loose wash suggestion — trodden dirt and stone underfoot, the open
doorway of the house behind them at one side with warm light coming out
of it onto the stones, and a line of animal pens burning low and orange
a long way off at the left. The night above is black with dull orange
cloud low over everything. There is no other light.

Composition: two figures, close together and near the viewer, the bottom
edge of the picture crossing both at the waist. The man stands at the
left in three-quarter view, turned in toward the boy. The boy is at the
right, half a head and more shorter, turned up toward him. Their two
faces are in the upper half of the frame and both are fully visible.
Nobody else is drawn near them.

BETWEEN THEM, HELD ACROSS THE FRAME NEARLY LEVEL, IS ONE PLAIN LONGSWORD
— a grown man's war sword, straight, broad, undecorated, with a plain
crossguard and a long grip. From pommel to point it is nearly as long as
the boy is tall. It is the only blade drawn anywhere in the picture.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the man's left hand is closed around the flat of
the blade halfway down it, holding the sword steady. His right hand is
closed over the boy's right hand on the grip, pressing the boy's fingers
shut around it. The boy's right hand is on the grip under the man's. The
boy's left hand is coming up toward the grip and has not reached it yet,
the fingers open. Four hands, one sword, nothing else in any hand.

THE BOY'S WRIST IS DROPPING UNDER THE WEIGHT: the point of the sword is
low, the grip end high, and his forearm is straining.

THE MAN IS LOOKING AT THE BOY'S FACE: the eyes level, iron-dark and
giving nothing out, the mouth closed, the jaw set, the chest working
with hard breathing. He is not looking at the viewer. Nothing in his
face is angry, sad or kind.

THE BOY IS LOOKING DOWN AT THE GRIP IN HIS OWN HAND, not at his father's
face and not at the viewer: the lids fully raised, the pale grey clear,
the brows drawn together, the mouth a little open.

The man wears a plain coarse working jacket of undyed dark wool, closed
at the throat, sleeves rolled above the elbow, with heavy village
trousers and boots — nothing tailored and nothing decorated: not a frock
coat, not a uniform and not armour, with no metal on him anywhere. It is
filthy and torn at one shoulder. The boy wears undyed coarse working
wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, more marked than
clean, with a cloth-wrapped bundle across his back with the shape of two
hilts in it, the hilts covered.

Dark dried red on both the boy's forearms and on the man's, and grey
dust and soot on both of them. Nothing is bleeding and no wound is drawn
on either of them.

The two faces and the four hands on the sword carry the sharpest steps from light to dark; the house, the doorway, the burning pens and the ground stay at quiet middle values with soft edges.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces, hair,
colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. His eyes are pale
grey; the man's are iron-dark, and there is no white anywhere in the
man's hair.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-lamp

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch33-the-lamp.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OPTIMISED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run.

**AND IT PUT BACK A CANON FENCE THIS SHEET HAD DROPPED.** My block said only *the back of an old man's grey head*. `../canon-brief.md` on Severin is explicit and the reason is structural: **hair fully grey, a true neutral grey, even everywhere — no seam, streak or patch, because that shape is the drain-mark.** The author's block states it — *his hair is evenly grey throughout — no streak, no seam, no patch, nothing lighter or colder in any part of it* — and it came back even. **In a book where a white streak is a body-mark of magic, any grey or white head in frame must be told which register it is not**, and this sheet is the reminder that the fence lives in `canon-brief.md` and not in my memory.

**AN UNSTEADY ARM WAS DRAWN AS A TILTED LAMP.** Mine said *the arm is not quite steady* — a state a still picture cannot assert, which is s51's own finding about faces (*a working part must be a state the picture can hold, not an action the face performs*). Theirs puts the instruction on the object: **the lamp tilted slightly off level and the flame leaning.** It is in the picture, and it is the only reason the arm reads as tired. **The s51 rule generalises off faces and onto anything the block asks for.**

**THE RIVAL IS DEMOTED ON BOTH CHANNELS.** The hearth fire is given edge and finish as well as size — *dull orange, soft-edged and low in value, and it does not light her* — where mine gave it only size and position. The lamp wins the picture completely.

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: the lamp held out at arm's length at chest height, warm orange up the underside of her jaw and along the inside of her sleeve with the room black a few feet away; the silver-white hair half down and half pinned, colourless and with no yellow in it; the heavy coarse cloak over the fine gown with the stain down the near sleeve; the old man's shoulder and grey head cut by the lower left corner; and **nothing whatever of the mother, the child or the work in the frame** — the seal this block was built to keep.

**WHAT DRIFTED, three:**
1. **A second small flame stands on a shelf behind her at the right.** The block said the lamp is the only light *on her*, which is true in the picture — but it did not say it is the only flame in the room, and one arrived. Where a picture is lit by one held object, the next block should say so of the frame and not only of the face.
2. **Her eyes are not on her own flame.** They are level and off to the frame's right, past the lamp. The viewer-ban held; the target did not — **the same fault as `ch32/the-white-in-her-hair`, and the same cause: the target is inside the frame but too close to the face to point a head at.**
3. The crop went to the knee where the block said the waist.

```

girl.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the inside of a small stone house at night, as loose wash
suggestion — rough boards, a plain table edge behind her, dark stone
walls close, the room going black a few feet away. A hearth fire
burns somewhere behind her at the right, small in the frame, dull
orange, soft-edged and low in value, and it does not light her.

Composition: one girl standing, near the viewer, turned
three-quarters toward the left of the frame, the bottom edge of the
picture crossing her at the waist. Her face is fully visible in the
upper right of the frame. AT THE LOWER LEFT CORNER, cut by the frame,
is the top of one shoulder and the back of an old man's grey head,
kneeling, turned away from the viewer; nothing of him below that
shoulder is in the picture. His hair is evenly grey throughout — no
streak, no seam, no patch, nothing lighter or colder in any part of
it.

HER LEFT ARM IS HELD OUT AWAY FROM HER BODY toward the kneeling man,
the elbow nearly straight, and her left hand holds a small clay oil
lamp with a single flame in it, at about the height of her own chest,
the lamp tilted slightly off level and the flame leaning. The arm is
not braced against anything. Her right hand is down at her side, half

hand.

THE LAMP IS THE ONLY LIGHT ON HER: one small warm flame at arm's

rest of the room dark.

Her face and the lamp carry the sharpest steps from light to dark in
the picture; the table, the wall, the fire and the kneeling shoulder
stay dark, quiet and soft-edged.

Preserve her face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown
in the reference image. Pale blue eyes. She is the only person with a
face in this picture.

# FILE: plates/ch34.md

# Ch. 34 — The Run · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen, Aeliana fourteen, Wren hours old; Neris is unpinned. Anchors: `images/portraits/neris.png` + `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png` on the first, `images/portraits/neris.png` on the second.

**BOTH PLATES ARE THE MOTHER AND THAT IS THE CHAPTER.** This is where the reader is told what she is, and the telling is done twice — **at a foot, and at three hundred strides.** One picture is the hands that move a girl's elbow an inch to make her hold a newborn correctly; the other is the same hands taking a hillside apart. **Nobody on the page ever says they are the same pair of hands, and the set can say it without saying it.**

**HER FACE IS IN THE FIRST AND NOT IN THE SECOND, AND THE PAGE RULES BOTH.** *He looked at the back of his mother's head. She was standing at the front of the shelf with her hands down at her sides and her shoulders going.* … *She did not turn round. He waited for her to. Everybody on that shelf waited for her to.* **The Art is never once shown across her face**, and `03/neris` holds it as standing state — *her face is never on the page during it and her son never sees it.* So the second plate is composed from where Kael is standing, at her back, and the facelessness is canon rather than a price.

**THE FIRST PLATE PUTS A NEWBORN IN A PRINCESS'S ARMS AND THE FOLDER HAS NOTHING LIKE IT.** `ch33/the-lamp` already spent the fence — that Aeliana's hands do nothing — and this is the second half of that spend and the payment for it: *"Both hands," his mother said. "Under the head. Do not hold him tight, hold him close, they are different."* … *"You can, you will," said Neris. "You will be extremely good at this."*

**THE INSTANT IS THE ELBOW.** *She took the girl's elbow and moved it about an inch, and looked at the result, and let go.* Not the transfer and not the speech — **a correction made with two fingers**, which is how that woman has taught everything in this book. §Hands gets a line per hand because the whole beat is in four of them and one child.

**AND THE LINEN IS ON THE CHILD'S FACE IN BOTH CHAPTERS FROM HERE.** It is bound at Ch. 33 and never explained. The block draws it as Ch. 33 describes it — *quickly, and well, and not tight, clear of the nose and clear of the mouth* — and **draws nothing under it**. The eye belongs to the chapter that spends it.

**THE SECOND PLATE'S SUBJECT IS NINE OLIVE TREES, NOT A HUNDRED MEN.** *They came apart along the grain from the top down, in long white splitting strips, and the strips went out into the bowl flat and level and did not slow down.* The men are in the bottom of that bowl and they are not drawn: **what is in the frame is what the wind did to the wood**, which is the one part of it a picture can hold without becoming a different kind of picture. THE PLATE FOLLOWS THE PAGE binds what may be added; it does not oblige a plate to draw everything the page has, and s51's dropped grim plates are the folder's own guidance on which half to take.

**THE FIRE IS ELARINE'S AND THE BLOCK SAYS SO BY ITS SHAPE.** *It did not spread from anything. There was no first flame anywhere for a second flame to have come off.* Four hundred fires exactly where the fuel was, gathered up by the wind into *one thing eight feet high and two hundred strides wide* — so the block builds the wall as a shape with a height, a width and a bald strip in front of it, per §Nouns, and does not write it as an effect.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **Aurelian talking** — one-handed, his good hand out and not touching a princess, correct in every particular and for nobody — is the warmest thing in Part IV and it is a third plate. **Elarine lighting the bowl** is the same picture as the one that was taken with a different woman in front of it. **I have got you. I love you** is a hand on the side of a head for the third time in four chapters (`ch31/the-hand-flat`, `ch33`'s passed-over hand on the head), and the set should stop there.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-elbow

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch34-the-elbow.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53: *amazing*). Ch. 34 has its two.**

**THE TWO WHITES STOOD IN ONE FRAME AND STAYED DISTINCT, WHICH WAS THE HARDEST CANON ASK OF THE SESSION.** `../canon-brief.md` requires it in the abstract — *Aeliana's silver is a house trait, warm and bright; the drain register is pure quartz white, cold and hard. Keep them distinct* — and no picture had ever had to hold both. The girl's whole head is bright warm silver-white; the woman's are two cold white locks in dark brown. **Two heads, two registers, one run**, and what bought it was naming each by temperature and then forbidding the other by name on each head.

**AND THE LINEN IS DRAWN.** A narrow strip of pale cloth bound level across a newborn's eyes, clear of the nose and clear of the mouth, with no eye drawn anywhere under it. **The book's largest unexplained fact is now a picture**, and the plate says exactly as much as Ch. 33 says and not one thing more.

**THE INSTRUCTION GIVEN BY HAND LANDED.** The girl's right hand under the head, her arm under the body, and the woman's hand closed on her forearm correcting it — which is the whole scene, and no clause in the block explains it.

**WHAT DRIFTED, two:**
1. **The woman's idle hand got recruited.** The block said her left is *open and empty at her side*; in the picture both her hands are on the girl's arm. **Second instance today of the same fault** — `ch31/the-shutter` said *his left hand hangs at his side* and came back with both hands at the window. **A hand told to do nothing joins the action; a hand given a resting place might not.** Recorded on both sheets; not promoted, because no correction has been wanted.
2. A metal ring closes the cloak at her shoulder, against *no gold and no ornament anywhere in the picture* — the same fastening drift as `ch33/the-lamp`, where the block had to spend a clause forbidding a pauldron. **A cloak fastened at one shoulder pulls toward hardware every time.**

---

*Candidate 1, ★.* Anchors: `images/portraits/neris.png`, `images/portraits/aeliana-15.png`. Ch. 34 canon: *Kael watched his mother hand his brother to a princess of the empire. / She did it the way she did things, which was quickly and without any ceremony in it: she got him out through the front of the coat with one hand under the head, turned, and put him against Aeliana's chest before the girl had finished understanding what was being walked toward her. / "Both hands," his mother said. "Under the head. Do not hold him tight, hold him close, they are different."* … *"You can, you will," said Neris. "You will be extremely good at this." / She took the girl's elbow and moved it about an inch, and looked at the result, and let go.*

**TWO FACES, AND NEITHER IS LOOKING AT THE OTHER.** The woman's eyes are on the girl's arms, because she is checking a result. The girl's are down on the child, because she has just been given one. **The whole exchange is conducted by hand**, and the block places the gazes rather than naming them as a subject.

**THE WHITE IS IN TWO PLACES AND IT IS THE DRAIN REGISTER, NOT AGE.** *the white in it going all the way through in two places* (Ch. 33). `../canon-brief.md`'s fence is absolute — never whiten a head for drama, keep the quartz white distinct from Aeliana's warm silver — and this picture has both women in it at once, so the block names the two whites separately and forbids the wrong one on each head.

```

Setting: a bare shelf of rock high on a mountainside at night, as loose

falling away behind them, and low cloud overhead lit dull orange from
below, end to end. That cloud is the only light: a weak warm glow coming
from above and behind, with no sun, no lamp and no hard-edged shadow
anywhere.

Composition: two figures, close together and near the viewer, the bottom

at the left in three-quarter view, turned in toward the girl. The girl
is at the right, a little shorter, turned three-quarters toward the

BETWEEN THEM, HELD AGAINST THE GIRL'S CHEST, IS A NEWBORN CHILD wrapped
in a piece of coarse pale cloth. He is very small — his head is no
bigger than the girl's two hands together. A NARROW STRIP OF PALE LINEN
IS BOUND ACROSS HIS EYES, level, clear of the nose and clear of the
mouth, tucked under itself behind the ear. It is neatly done and it is
not tight. No eye is drawn on him anywhere.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the girl's right hand is under the child's head
and her left arm is under his body, holding him against her. The woman's
right hand is closed lightly around the girl's left elbow, lifting it
about an inch. The woman's left hand is open and empty at her side. Four
hands, one child, nothing else in any hand.

THE WOMAN IS LOOKING AT THE GIRL'S ARMS, not at her face and not at the
viewer: the lids lowered, the mouth open mid-word, the jaw easy,
everything about her unhurried. Nothing in her face is tender and
nothing in it is anxious.

THE GIRL IS LOOKING DOWN AT THE CHILD, not at the woman and not at the
viewer: the lids fully raised, the pale blue clear, the mouth slightly
open, the brows up a little.

THE WOMAN'S HAIR IS DARK BROWN AND LONG, with TWO SEPARATE PIECES OF IT
WHITE FROM THE ROOT — a hard cold white with no warmth and no yellow in
it, the white of a split stone. There is no grey and nothing silver
anywhere on her.

THE GIRL'S HAIR IS SILVER-WHITE ALL OVER, colourless, never blonde, with
no yellow and no gold in it, half of it come down loose on the near side
and half still pinned. It is a warmer, brighter white than the two
pieces in the woman's hair and must not be drawn the same.

The woman wears a heavy coarse village coat of undyed dark wool, worn
and mended, open at the front. The girl wears a heavy dark soldier's
cloak of coarse wool over a fine gown, fastened at one shoulder, plainly
not hers. There is no gold and no ornament anywhere in the picture. Do
not mix their clothing: the soldier's cloak belongs only to the girl and
the village coat only to the woman.

Both faces and the four hands around the child carry the sharpest steps
from light to dark; the stone, the thorn and the cloud stay at quiet
middle values with soft edges.

Preserve each exactly as shown in their reference images — faces, hair,
colouring, apparent age. Do not blend their features. The woman's eyes
are dark; the girl's are pale blue.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-bowl

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch34-the-bowl.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OPTIMISED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run.

**THEIR CHANGE IS THE SAME CHANGE THEY MADE TO `ch33/the-lamp`, AND IT IS THE SESSION'S BEST LESSON.** Mine asked for *three old olive trees splitting apart from the top downward* — an action, over seconds, which a still picture cannot hold. Theirs asks for **the state it leaves**: *already split open from the top down along the grain, the trunks standing as ragged upright shells, and the long pale strips off them out in the air clear of the trunks, low and level and pointing away from the viewer, straight and mid-flight.* **The trunk is an after; the strips are a during; between them the eye supplies the verb.** It landed on one run, and it is the hardest single object anybody has asked this tool for in this folder.

**AND THE WHITE PIECES ARE COUNTED AND CAPPED IN THE SAME BREATH.** *TWO PIECES OF HER LONG DARK HAIR, AND ONLY TWO* — with the third forbidden by name. Two white locks in the picture, taking the light from below, the palest thing on her, on a woman who is otherwise a silhouette. **The drain-mark's second appearance and its second clean run.**

**NOTHING COMES OFF HER HANDS, AND THAT IS WHAT MAKES IT HERS.** The one absence the block spends is on the air between her and the bowl, and the picture keeps it empty: no light, no glow, nothing drawn between the palms and three hundred strides of burning ground. **A woman with her arms out and nothing on them, and the whole hillside doing it.**

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: the unbroken wall of fire right across the bowl at a man and a half high, far and soft-edged; the six whole olive trees further along the same terrace; nothing resolved beyond the fire; no figure anywhere in the bowl; the fire as the only light, from underneath, rimming her shoulders and the outsides of her arms and leaving her front dark.

**WHAT DRIFTED, two, both small:** the bare strip with the scythe-cut edge is not a distinct feature — the ground in front of the fire is dark and read as burnt rather than as taken off · and the crop went past the hip to the thigh.

```

bare paper in the lights.

Setting: a mountainside at night, seen from a bare shelf of rock
looking down into a wide shallow bowl of ground about three hundred
strides across — stone, burnt stubble, dead thorn, an old terrace
wall running along under the shelf. Low cloud sits over everything,
lit dull orange from beneath.

Composition: the woman stands at the front edge of the shelf in the
lower third of the frame, near the viewer, seen from directly behind
and a little above, small in the frame and dark. The bottom edge of
the picture crosses her at the hip. Her head is not turned and her
face is not visible. She is the only person in the picture.

HER TWO ARMS ARE OUT IN FRONT OF HER AT SHOULDER HEIGHT, level and
straight, the palms turned down. Nothing is in either hand. NOTHING
IS COMING OFF HER HANDS: no light, no glow, no spark, and nothing
drawn in the air between her and the bowl.

WHAT IS IN THE BOWL BELOW HER, EACH IN ITS OWN PLACE: a wall of fire
standing right across the bowl from one side of the frame to the
other, unbroken, low and long, about a man and a half high. In front
of it, a bare strip of ground eighty strides long where everything
has been taken off down to the stone, its near edge as clean and
straight as a scythe cut. And on the terrace just below the shelf,
three old olive trees already split open from the top down along the
grain, the trunks standing as ragged upright shells, and the long
pale strips off them out in the air clear of the trunks, low and
level and pointing away from the viewer, straight and mid-flight.

SIX MORE OLIVE TREES STAND FURTHER ALONG THE SAME TERRACE, WHOLE AND
UNSPLIT. Nine trees in the frame and no other tree anywhere in it.

No figures are drawn anywhere in the bowl and no body is lying in it.

THE FIRE BELOW IS THE ONLY LIGHT IN THE PICTURE. It comes up from
underneath: it puts a hard orange rim along the woman's shoulders and

leaves the shelf, the stone and the whole of her front dark.

TWO PIECES OF HER LONG DARK HAIR, AND ONLY TWO, ARE WHITE FROM THE
ROOT — a hard cold white with no warmth and no yellow in it — and
they take the light from below and are the palest thing on her. Every
other hair on her head is dark brown, with no grey, no silver and no
third white piece anywhere on her.

The white pieces in her hair and the rim on her shoulders carry the
sharpest step from light to dark near the viewer; the wall of fire is
the brightest thing in the frame but soft-edged and far.

She wears a heavy coarse village coat of undyed dark wool, worn and
mended. Preserve her build, hair colour and apparent age as shown in
the reference image.

# FILE: plates/ch35.md

# Ch. 35 — The Range · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register.** Kael is thirteen; Wren is a day old. Anchors: `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the first, `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE WHOLE CHAPTER AT ITS TWO DISTANCES: THE ROOM, AND THE BOY IN IT.** One picture is eight people lying on the boards of a boat inside a white room made of steam; the other is the boy kneeling up in it with a day-old child on his shoulder, calling a man's feet. **Neither has a fireball arriving in it**, because what this chapter is actually about is what the sea does afterwards and what a boy does with his mouth.

**THE WHITE ROOM IS THE BEST THING IN BOOK ONE THAT NOBODY HAS DRAWN.** *Every one of them his mother put down went into the sea within thirty strides of the boat, and every one of them took a piece of the sea up with it when it went, and the steam stood along both sides of that hull in walls higher than the mast, and the light came through the walls from underneath and moved, so that eight people lay on the boards of a boat inside a white room with the walls of it going up and going out and going up again.* **The page has already composed it** — walls, ceiling, floor, and where the light comes from — and the block's whole job is to keep it a room and not a fog.

**THE LIGHT IS UNDER THE WATER AND THAT IS THE INSTRUMENT.** Not a flame in the frame; the sea lit from beneath on both sides, throwing everything upward. §Light wants a named source told where it is, that it is the only one, and what it leaves dark, and this one is written that way: **nothing above the top of the steam is lit at all**, so the sky over the boat is black and the room has no roof.

**THE MAST IS STILL STANDING AND THAT DATES THE PICTURE.** The sail goes later in the chapter — *the canvas went all at once, top to bottom* — so a plate with a mast in it is the earlier half of the run and a plate without one is the later half. The block says the sail is whole, because the moment is worth pinning.

**NOBODY'S FACE IS RESOLVED IN THE FIRST PLATE AND THE SECOND ONE SPENDS EVERYTHING ON ONE.** That is the pairing: `ch27/the-sky` and `ch22/kael-of-the-island` are the folder's evidence that a small unresolved figure carries more than a rendered one when the scale is the subject, and `ch27/i-need-you` is the evidence for the opposite when the face is.

**THE SECOND PLATE IS THE FOLDER'S FIRST PICTURE OF THE TWO BROTHERS.** *He put his brother up onto his shoulder with his left hand flat on the small of the back, which was where the child had been the whole time on his mother's chest, and got his right forearm onto the strake, and looked at the island.* And what he does from there is give his mother numbers over the top of a screaming newborn. **It is the first thing Kael has ever done for somebody that was not for himself**, and no clause in the block says so.

**THE LINEN STAYS ON AND THE EYE IS NOT SPENT HERE.** The child's face is turned into the boy's neck, which the page puts there, and the bound strip is drawn and nothing under it is.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **the mother in the bow**, alone against a shore throwing fire, is `ch34/the-bowl` again with water in front of her instead of stubble, and the page has already ruled she is faceless in both. **The mast coming down** and **the burning sail going over the side** are the chapter's only two-handed physical beats and both belong to `candidates.md`. **Watching the man who killed his father get smaller** is the last image of the chapter and it wants a face the folder does not own — the small neat man has no portrait, and `ch13.md` is clear that the work which unblocks that is a portrait, not a plate.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-white-room

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch35-the-white-room.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S RESTRUCTURED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run.

**THEY REORDERED IT SUBJECT-FIRST, AND THE ORDER IS THE POINT.** Mine opened on `Setting:` and put the eight people three paragraphs down; theirs folds the setting into `Composition:`, states the low camera as part of it, **and puts the figures immediately after** — so the block reads people, then the room they are in, then the light on it. The template's paragraph order is a starting shape and not a law, and **a picture whose subject is eight bodies and one standing figure is written that way.**

**AND THEY ADDED A COUNT LINE FOR PEOPLE:** *Seven lying and one standing, eight people in the boat and no ninth anywhere in it.* §Hands' one-line-per-item-and-then-the-total has been used on hands, blades, burning stones and lights; **this is the first time it has been used on figures, and the picture has exactly eight.**

**THE ROOM IS A ROOM, WHICH IS WHY THE STEAM WAS WRITTEN AS ARCHITECTURE.** Two walls standing on the sea, leaning outward as they rise, going up past the mast top, with the sky open and black between them — **weather given a height, a face and a distance rather than asked for as fog.** `ch27/the-sky` found that a layer with something to sit against renders and a free layer does not; this is that lesson used deliberately, and it built an interior out of the sea.

**AND THE LIGHT IS UNDER THE WATER.** No flame anywhere in the frame; the sea lit from beneath on both sides, brightest close to the hull, the steam bright and orange at the waterline and going pale and cold toward the top, the thwart tops rimmed, and nothing above the steam lit at all. **The colour in this picture lives in two places and both of them are water.**

**IT IS THE SET'S SECOND PICTURE WITH NO FACE IN IT** after `ch31/the-shutter`, and the first at landscape scale. **The paralysis instrument from `ch27/the-sky` — small figure, huge event, no face — used a second time and stronger**, because now there are eight people in it and still nobody to look at.

**NOTHING DRIFTED THAT IS WORTH RECORDING.**

```
Image 1: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the boy, for
build and clothing. No face is resolved in this picture.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Composition: the open deck of a small dark wooden boat at night,
taken from low over the stern boards so the deck runs away at eye
level. Wet planking runs away from the bottom edge of the frame; low
thwarts cross it; a single mast stands up out of the middle with its
sail set and whole. Beyond the bow there is black water and black sky
and nothing else.

SEVEN FIGURES ARE LYING FLAT ON THE BOARDS between the thwarts,
spread down the length of the boat, each of them down on their front
or their side with the head low and turned away. ONE SMALL FIGURE
STANDS ALONE IN THE BOW, right at the far end of the boat, seen from
behind, both arms up and out — the only person standing, small in the
frame. Seven lying and one standing, eight people in the boat and no
ninth anywhere in it. NO FACE IS VISIBLE ANYWHERE IN THE PICTURE and
no eyes are drawn anywhere in it. Do not turn any head toward the
viewer.

ON BOTH SIDES OF THE BOAT, STANDING ON THE SEA, ARE TWO WALLS OF
WHITE STEAM. They begin at the water a little way out from each side
of the hull and go straight up past the height of the mast top,
leaning outward as they rise. They are dense, and they have edges,
and they enclose the boat completely on the left and on the right.
Between them, directly above the boat, the sky is open and completely
black. There is no fog on the deck itself and no mist between the
figures.

THE LIGHT IS UNDER THE WATER AND THERE IS NO OTHER LIGHT. It comes up
through the sea on both sides — a moving orange-white glow below the
surface, brightest close to the hull — and it lights the two steam
walls from beneath and from inside, so that they are bright at the
bottom and go dim and cold toward the top. It puts a hard rim along
the top edge of each thwart, along the near side of the mast and
along the wet planking. Nothing above the top of the steam is lit at
all. There is no fire anywhere in the frame.

The base of the two steam walls is the brightest thing in the picture
and the figures on the boards are the darkest; the mast, the sail and
the thwarts sit between them at quiet middle values with soft edges.
The colour is entirely in the water and the low steam — orange going
to white going to cold grey — and everything else is near-black.

The boat is plain, tarred, working, undecorated, with no paint and no
carving anywhere on it.
```

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-spotter

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch35-the-spotter.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OPTIMISED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run.

**AND IT SETTLES THE AGE QUESTION BY DOING IT.** Every plate this session ran without an age clause and came back between fifteen and seventeen; this block carries §AGE's adolescent rung, written as anatomy — *He is fourteen. The eyes sit at the vertical midpoint of the head, the cheek still full beneath the cheekbone, the jaw softly defined and not sharp at the edge, the neck narrow. Adolescent, not grown.* — **and the boy in this picture reads fourteen.** `../README.md`'s standing ruling always said age goes in the block or the picture will not hold it; the session's other plates are the control group and this is the experiment.

**THE KNOT IS THE DETAIL THAT MAKES THE LINEN READ AS BOUND.** *Tied at the back of the head with the knot in the cloth behind* — `ch34/the-elbow` drew the strip and this one draws the tie, and the difference is that here it is unmistakably something somebody did to him rather than a band across a face.

**TWO OPEN MOUTHS AND NEITHER OF THEM IS HEARD.** The boy's jaw dropped on a shouted word with the brows drawn hard and the eyes far off to the left, and the child's mouth square and screaming against his neck — **the loudest picture in the set and it has no sound instrument in it at all.**

**AND THE GAZE HELD AGAIN, THIS TIME ON A TARGET OUTSIDE THE FRAME.** The eyes are fixed off the left edge with the head turned that way. Adding this to the day: the gaze holds when the head can turn toward the thing, whether the thing is in the frame (`ch30`, `ch33`) or out of it (here), and it fails when the target sits within a hand's width of the face (`ch32`, `ch33/the-lamp`).

**WHAT DRIFTED, two, both small:** the child is on his near shoulder rather than his left · and a low burning glow sits on the waterline at the left, against *there is no fire drawn in the frame* — though it is the source the block asked for, and having it just inside the edge costs nothing.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the side of a small dark wooden boat at night, as loose wash
suggestion — a wet plank strake close in front of him, rope and
tarred timber, the sea beyond it black. A wall of white steam stands
off the water further out at the left. No land is drawn.

Composition: one boy kneeling up at the side of the boat, close to
the viewer, turned three-quarters away toward the left of the frame
with his head turned further that way, the face in three-quarter view
and fully visible, both eyes in frame. The bottom edge of the picture
crosses him at the lower chest. He and the child are the only figures
in the picture.

He is fourteen. The eyes sit at the vertical midpoint of the head,
the cheek still full beneath the cheekbone, the jaw softly defined
and not sharp at the edge, the neck narrow. Adolescent, not grown.

A NEWBORN CHILD IS UP ON HIS LEFT SHOULDER, very small, wrapped in
coarse pale cloth, his face turned in against the boy's neck. A
NARROW STRIP OF PALE LINEN IS BOUND ACROSS THE CHILD'S EYES, level,
tied at the back of the head with the knot in the cloth behind, clear
of the nose and clear of the mouth. NO EYE IS DRAWN ON THE CHILD
ANYWHERE. The child's mouth is wide open and square and he is
screaming, and both his fists are shut and drawn up in the cloth.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the boy's left hand is flat and spread on the
small of the child's back, holding him against the shoulder. His
right forearm is laid along the top of the strake, and that hand is
open and empty and gripping nothing. Two hands, one child, nothing
else in either hand, and no blade is drawn anywhere in the picture.

HIS MOUTH IS WIDE OPEN ON A SHOUTED WORD: the jaw dropped, the brows
drawn hard together, the lids narrowed against the heat, the eyes
fixed far off to the left. He is not looking at the child and not at
the viewer. Nothing in his face is crying.

THE LIGHT COMES FROM SOMETHING BURNING OFF THE LEFT SIDE OF THE
PICTURE AND OUT OF THE FRAME. It is hard, warm and orange, and it
lights the near side of his face, his hand on the child's back, the
child's cloth and the top edge of the wet strake, and leaves the rest
of the boat dark. There is no fire drawn in the frame.

His hair, his face and his clothing are marked with soot and dried
dark red, old and brown on the right forearm. He wears undyed coarse
working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, scorched
down the front, with no metal anywhere on him.

His face carries the sharpest step from light to dark in the picture;
the child's wrap, the strake, the rope, the steam and the sea stay at
quiet middle values with soft edges.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown
in the reference image.

# FILE: plates/ch36.md

# Ch. 36 — The Quiet · plate sheet

**Composed at s53 from the scene, under the register. These are the last two plates of Book One.** Kael is thirteen; Wren is a day and a night old. Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png` on both, with `images/full-figures/kael-14-full.png` on the second.

**THE PAIR IS THE SMALLEST THING IN THE BOOK AND THE LARGEST ABSENCE IN IT.** A hand the size of a thumb-joint shut on one finger, and an island with nothing lit on it. **Nothing happens in either picture**, which after Ch. 33, 34 and 35 is the point: the chapter is called The Quiet and the set should go quiet with it.

**AND THEY ARE THE ONLY TWO PLATES IN PART IV NOT LIT BY SOMETHING BURNING.** Ch. 27 to Ch. 35 is fog, then fire, then a man, then a lamp, then a bowl, then the sea lit from underneath. **These two are ordinary afternoon daylight and then no light at all.** The set has earned a plain picture.

**THE LAST PLATE CLOSES A RHYME THE FOLDER OPENED AT `ch01`.** `ch01/fallen-stars` is a boy on this mountain being turned to look down at a town lying in a heap of warm light on the black water. `ch32/the-lights-on-the-mountain` is the same boy on the same ground with the lights coming up at him. **This is the third:** the same water from the other side, and *no lights came on anywhere on that island at all.* Three pictures, one view, and the whole of Book One between them.

**THE ONE BAN IN IT IS THE PICTURE.** §Absence says ban what the frame pulls toward and nothing else. A shore at night pulls toward lights harder than almost anything in this folder, so the block spends its single absence there and spends it plainly: **no point of light, no glow, no fire and nothing lit anywhere on that island.**

**THE LINEN IS DRAWN IN BOTH PLATES AND WHAT IS UNDER IT IS NOT.** *The linen was still across the eyes. Kael did not touch it. Nobody on that boat had touched it, and nobody had said one word about why it was there.* The strip is page canon from Ch. 33 and the eye beneath it is spent in exactly one paragraph of one chapter. **A plate that shows the strip and not the eye is the page**, and the reader who has read Ch. 33 supplies the rest.

**THE FIRST PLATE IS THE FOLDER'S ANSWER TO ITS OWN OLDEST QUESTION ABOUT THIS BOY.** Every picture of Kael in the set has him doing something with his hands — blades, a wall, a hook, a hand out in front of him with nothing in it. **This one has him holding still so that a thing the size of a loaf can keep hold of his finger**, and the only work in the frame is that he is counting its breathing, which no viewer can see and every reader will know.

**PASSED OVER, AND WHY:** **the loaf** — a day-old loaf broken by hand and sent down a boat from person to person — is the chapter's other great quiet beat and it wants eight resolved faces in a hull forty feet long. **The boat words** — the old man giving him twenty of them and making him say each one back — is the book's last piece of teaching and the folder has no Severin plate at all yet; it stays on `candidates.md` as the strongest single thing left unbuilt in Book One. **The princess holding him** is `ch34/the-elbow` with the roles finished, one day later.

---

### PLATE-VERBATIM: the-finger

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch36-the-finger.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). Ch. 36 has its two, and with it BOOK ONE HAS ITS PLATES: thirty-six chapters, every one of them with at least two.**

**AN INCH OF SUBJECT WON A PICTURE WITH A FACE IN IT.** The fist closed on the finger is there, no bigger than the top two joints of it, with the other fist shut up beside the child's own face — and the boy's face is fully drawn and still does not take the picture off it. **§The winner and the rival, on the smallest thing the folder has ever asked for.**

**AND THE BURN IS DRAWN FOR THE FIRST TIME.** `../canon-brief.md` has carried the wrist-to-elbow burn on the left forearm since Ch. 26 and no plate had ever put it in frame; here it is pink and healed-over across the near forearm, under the dust and the old dark red. **The mark he did not spend himself, in the last picture of the book.**

**THE LINEN IS IN ITS THIRD PLATE AND ITS QUIETEST.** No knot in view, no screaming, no fire — a strip of pale cloth across the eyes of a sleeping child, level and clear of the nose and mouth, in ordinary afternoon daylight. **What the set has done with it across `ch34`, `ch35` and `ch36` is show it three times and explain it never.**

**AND THE SET CLOSES ON ITS PLAINEST LIGHT.** Soft daylight from one side, no sun disc, no hard-edged shadow, bare paper for the sky and a flat calm sea — after fog, fire, a man who was a lamp, a lamp, a burning bowl and a sea lit from underneath. **Nothing is happening in it, which is the chapter.**

**NOTHING DRIFTED THAT IS WORTH RECORDING**, and the age came back right without an age clause, in a crop where the face is large.

*Candidate 1, ★.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 36 canon: *"Sit back. Put your arm along the top of your leg. No — flat. Like that." / She put the child into the space he made. / He weighed almost nothing and he was extremely warm* … *His head had no neck under it worth the name; the whole thing worked because Kael's hand was under it. His fists were shut up beside his face, the way they had been shut through the whole of a barrage.* … *His breathing was wrong. That was the first thing.* … *He counted it, because he could not do anything else. / He counted for four minutes and got the shape of it, and found that it repeated, and that it was not wrong at all* … *His brother's hand came open and shut on Kael's finger and stayed shut. / He did not have much grip.* … *and he held on with it anyway, and Kael sat completely still on the boards of a drifting boat and did not take his finger back, because he did not want to be the one who did that.*

**THE SUBJECT IS ONE HAND CLOSED ON ONE FINGER AND IT IS AN INCH ACROSS.** §The winner and the rival is the whole build: the rival is a face, which will always win a picture, so the face is turned down and held at soft middle values and **the sharpest step from light to dark in the frame is sited on the two hands.** The crop comes in far enough that an inch of subject is a real part of the picture.

**AND THE EYES ARE ON THE CHEST, NOT THE FACE.** That is the one clause that makes it him rather than any boy holding any baby: he is counting the breathing. Nothing in the block says so.

```

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the open boards of a small wooden boat by day, as loose wash
suggestion — wet planking, the low top edge of the boat's side behind
him, a knee-high burnt stub where a mast used to stand further off, flat
calm sea beyond it going out to a plain horizon. Soft ordinary daylight
from one side, no sun disc, no hard-edged shadow, nothing burning
anywhere in the picture.

Composition: one boy sitting on the boards with his back against the
side of the boat, near the viewer, turned three-quarters toward it, his
head bent down over his own lap. The bottom edge of the picture crosses
his thigh, so that his forearm, his lap and both his hands are in the
frame with the child, and his face is in the upper part of it and fully
visible. He is the only person in the picture.

A NEWBORN CHILD LIES ALONG THE TOP OF HIS THIGH, wrapped loosely in a
piece of coarse pale cloth, his head at the boy's elbow. He is very
small: from the crown of his head to his heel he is about the length of
the boy's forearm. A NARROW STRIP OF PALE LINEN IS BOUND ACROSS HIS
EYES, level, clear of the nose and clear of the mouth, tucked under
itself behind the ear, neatly done and not tight, and no eye is drawn on
the child anywhere. He is asleep and his mouth is a little open.

WHAT IS IN EACH HAND: the boy's left hand is under the child's head,
open and flat, taking its whole weight. His right hand rests on his own
knee with the index finger out, and THE CHILD'S RIGHT HAND IS CLOSED
AROUND THAT FINGER — the whole fist no bigger than the top two joints of
it, the fingers not reaching all the way round, the grip loose and
holding on anyway. The child's other fist is shut up beside his own
face. There is no object anywhere in the frame.

Nothing is happening and neither of them is moving.

HIS EYES ARE ON THE CHILD'S CHEST, not on the child's face and not on
the viewer: the lids lowered, the head bent, the mouth closed and soft,
the brows level and unmoved. He is not smiling and he is not crying.

The child's fist on the finger carries the sharpest step from light to
dark in the picture; the boy's face is fully drawn but held at closer,
softer values, and the boards, the boat's side, the mast stub and the
sea stay quiet with soft edges.

He is filthy: grey dust, soot and old dark red dried on his forearms and
the backs of his hands, and a fresh healed-over pink burn across the
back of the left forearm from the wrist to the elbow. His clothing is
undyed coarse working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive,
scorched down the front, with no metal anywhere on him. The child's
cloth is the cleanest thing in the frame.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown in
the reference image. Pale grey eyes.

### PLATE-VERBATIM: no-lights

**ADOPTED — `images/plates/ch36-no-lights.png`, FIRST RUN (author, s53). THE BLOCK BELOW IS THE AUTHOR'S OPTIMISED VERSION OF THIS SHEET'S**, stored exactly as run. **The last plate of Book One.**

**THE ONE BAN IS THE PICTURE AND IT HELD.** *Completely dark from one end to the other — no point of light anywhere on it and no glow behind the ridge.* A low black island across a pale horizon with nothing lit on it, and a boy sitting on the boards of a boat looking at it. **The whole of Book One's last page is that absence**, and the block spends its single absence there and nowhere else.

**AND THE RHYME IS COMPLETE.** `ch01/fallen-stars` — the town lying in a heap of warm light on the black water. `ch32/the-lights-on-the-mountain` — the lights coming up the slope at him. This — nothing lit anywhere on it. **Three pictures, one view, and the whole of Book One between them.**

**THEY CUT A FEATURE THE VALUE RANGE COULD NOT HOLD.** Mine asked for *the near eye is open and drawn, and it is looking at the island* inside a face otherwise unrendered; theirs deletes it and says **his head is turned to the island** instead. At that size, in a dark profile against a pale sky, an open eye is below the value range the picture is working in — and the head does the whole job. **A block should not ask for a feature the finish it has chosen cannot carry.**

**AND THE AGE RUNG WORKED A SECOND TIME, COMPRESSED TO ONE CLAUSE** — *He is fourteen — narrow through the shoulders, adolescent, not grown* — after the full §AGE paragraph carried `ch35/the-spotter`.

**THE REST AS WRITTEN**: the sword laid flat with the grip toward him and the point running out of the bottom edge, the only object on the deck; the knee-high burnt stub where the mast was; both hands empty on the boards; the thin smear of smoke well to one side; and the whole picture built out of cold greys and bare unpainted paper, with the sharpest step in it the top edge of the island against the sky.

**WHAT DRIFTED, two, both small:** the island reads a little narrower than his shoulders rather than as wide · and the crop went past the hip to take in his legs, which is the loose crop for the eleventh time and the first time it has arguably improved the composition, since the stretched-out legs are what make him look like somebody who has stopped.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.
Image 2: kael-14-full.png — full-figure reference of the same boy,
for his build and clothing.

Same medium and style as the reference images: loose watercolour and
ink on rough cold-press paper, scratchy ink linework, wash blooms,
bare paper in the lights.

Setting: the open boards of a small wooden boat at night on flat calm
water, as loose wash suggestion. Beyond the boat the sea is black and
still and goes out to a level horizon. The sky above the horizon is a
pale cold grey going darker toward the top of the picture, with a thin
smear of smoke lying across it well to one side, shapeless, drifting.
No moon, no sun and no lamp is drawn anywhere in the picture.

Composition: one boy sitting low on the boards with his back against
the side of the boat at the right of the frame, near the viewer, seen
from behind and a little to one side so that his face is turned away
and shown in profile against the pale sky. The bottom edge of the
picture crosses him at the hip. He fills the right of the frame; the
left two thirds is water, horizon and sky. He is the only person in
the picture. He is fourteen — narrow through the shoulders,
adolescent, not grown.

ACROSS THE HORIZON, LOW AND BLACK, IS AN ISLAND: a long dark shape
with a high shoulder at one end going down to the water at the other,
about as wide in the frame as the boy's shoulders are wide, and no
taller above the horizon than a hand is deep. It is drawn as one flat
silhouette with nothing separated inside it, completely dark from one
end to the other — no point of light anywhere on it and no glow
behind the ridge.

HIS FACE IS A DARK PROFILE AGAINST THE PALE SKY and almost nothing in
it is rendered. His head is turned to the island. He is not looking
at the viewer.

ACROSS THE BOARDS IN FRONT OF HIM LIES ONE PLAIN LONGSWORD, a grown
man's war sword, straight, broad and undecorated, laid flat with the
grip toward him and the point running out of the frame at the bottom
edge. It is nearly as long as he is tall. It is the only object on
the deck and it is the only blade in the picture. Both his hands are
empty and resting on the boards.

A knee-high burnt stub is all that stands where the boat's mast was;
nothing else stands up anywhere on the deck.

The picture is nearly without colour: cold greys, black water, and
generous bare unpainted paper in the sky and along the horizon. The
sharpest step from light to dark is the top edge of the island
against that sky; the boy, the sword and the boards sit dark and
quiet with soft edges.

Preserve his build, hair and clothing as shown in the reference
images — undyed coarse working wool, much mended, in dull oatmeal and
dead olive, with no metal on him.
```

# FILE: plates/ch37.md

# Ch. 37 — The Still · plate sheet

**Ch. 37 stands thirty days after Ch. 36, so nothing has aged:** Kael is thirteen, Wren is a month old, and the anchors do not move — `images/portraits/kael-14.png` and `images/portraits/severin.png`. `kael-17`, `valeria-17` and `aeliana-18` stay in the library for whatever later Book Two does with them.

**Valeria constrains this whole list:** Ch. 36 gives *half her face was burned red and shining down one side* and never says which side, so any entry with her face in it either invents a side or turns it away.

**Both chosen blocks take the chapter's one new fact about the world, which is the light** — *the light came up off it as well as down, so that there was no shade anywhere, not even your own.* The register's default is soft daylight from one side and neither takes it: a high white southern glare out of a bleached sky and back up off the ground, shadows short and weak. **One named light, used twice, because it is the thing the chapter discovered.**

## Candidates

*Composed at s58, at design time, from the scene. **★ = chosen.***

1. ★ **CHOSEN, BUILT, UNRUN — The water at the gate.** A boy stopped dead in a road by water running loud in an open channel of cut stone, brim-full, over a lip and in under the road through an arch the height of a dog — *in a quantity that would have kept eight people alive for a year.* **The chapter's epigraph is this image**, and the folder's first picture of the other side of that sea. → **`../prompts/plates/ch37-the-water-at-the-gate.md`**
2. ★ **CHOSEN, ADOPTED (s58, first run, on the author's optimised block) — The old man on his knees.** *Severin, on his knees, took him without looking up and put his other hand flat on the wet sand and left it there.* Seven people got ready badly in the order their bodies took it, and the eighth sat down on the ground with a newborn and put a palm on a continent. **The folder's first plate of Severin, and the first plate of Book Two.** → **`../prompts/plates/ch37-the-old-man-on-his-knees.md`** · image `images/plates/ch37-the-old-man-on-his-knees.png`
3. **The cloth.** The princess on her knees at Elarine's lid, taking the wet cloth off at the hundred and wringing it over the side away from herself with her palms bleeding through the strips torn off her own hem, and a boy opposite keeping the count. *I should like to know which.* **Passed over only for company** — her third hands-doing-a-job picture in five chapters, after `ch33/the-lamp` and `ch34/the-elbow`. First in line for the next session.
4. **The first drop.** Aurelian cross-legged in the bilge water, bad arm across his lap, good hand palm up, a fire the size of a fist and the colour of a forge standing off the palm with the stone jar above it on nothing — and the first beads finding each other under the lid, into a scoop, *with a noise so small that six people leaned to hear it.* **The chapter's only light source, and the strongest thing left unbuilt in it.**
5. **The street in stripes.** Inside the gate: narrow, going up, pale walls close on both sides, cloth stretched wall to wall so the light comes down in bars, windows small and high, and people standing back in loose pale cloth with their hands doing nothing. **Book Two's establishing shot, and it wants no resolved face at all.**
6. **The eight of them getting ready, badly.** The whole party out of the water in the order their bodies took it — blades, a good arm out, a sword up with the left hand open beside it, a boy three strides in front of his mother — against six spears coming down the brown. **The chapter's largest picture and the folder has nothing like it.** Passed over: eight resolved faces, and Valeria's is one of them.
7. **The mother inside the spears.** Walking the mile with the child on the side of her away from them, head down a little, a woman carrying a baby — *and every step she took knew where every one of the spears was. Nobody else saw it. There was nothing to see.* **The hardest thing on this list to draw and the most Neris**: the picture has to be of nothing.
8. **The cup going down the boat.** The nineteenth day: a princess's cup passed hand to hand the length of a hull to a herder's wife, who looks at her once, the length of the boat, and drinks it. **Passed over:** `ch36/the-loaf`'s problem — eight resolved faces in a boat forty feet long.
9. **The knee on the sand.** Seven soldiers down in the wet sand with their spears upright, at a name, and not one of their eyes off the blades of the girl in front of the princess. **Passed over:** the point of it is what the kneeling is *not* about, which is a joke only a reader can hear — and it puts Valeria's face in frame.
10. **The line, low and brown.** Thirtieth morning, up on his knees: the whole width of the north gone brown with a haze along it, and the sea between changing colour in a long green band. Smelled before it was seen. **Passed over:** a landscape with a back in it, and `ch36/no-lights` is the same horizon four weeks earlier.
11. **The healer's hands.** An old woman who reaches for a jar without turning her head, finding in four seconds how wrong a boy's ribs were mended, looking at his face, and asking nothing. **Blocked:** she has no portrait, and a plate is not where a face gets decided.
12. **The name at the table.** The low table covered end to end, a clerk with a board and a pen, a princess saying *Drusane*, and a boy making his face do nothing under a dead friend's name. **Blocked:** the governor has no portrait, and the moment is his table.
13. **The coats on the sweep.** The rig that moved them a month: a sweep lashed upright to the burnt stub of the mast with four coats hung across it, bellied by the night breeze, over a hull with a hand of water in the low end. **No people in it.** Available if a session wants a plain object picture.

### PLATE: the-water-at-the-gate

*Candidate 1.* Anchor: `images/portraits/kael-14.png`. Ch. 37 canon: *He heard it before he saw it. It ran in a channel of cut stone along the foot of the wall and went in under the road through an arch the height of a dog, and it was running, loud and quick where it went over a lip in the stone. Kael stopped in the road.* **Built, not yet run.**

**The water is given area rather than a demotion** — §The winner and the rival is for a subject an inch across, and this one has the bottom third of the frame, so the instrument is the crop. What the block sites by name is the one true dark available under a shadowless light: **the black mouth of the arch against the moving water.** The running is written as a state and never as a verb — a stone lip a hand high, the water standing over it in one unbroken curved sheet. **Its single absence** is spent where the frame pulls hardest, on a thirsty boy beside water: *he is not touching the water and he is not drinking.* The sword rides on the length of cord and the two shed blades are through his belt, where Ch. 37 moved them.

```
Image 1: kael-14.png — portrait reference of the dark-haired boy.

Setting: the foot of a town wall in a hot brown country, as loose wash
suggestion — mud-brick courses the colour of the ground itself, with the
ground's own grain in them. The top edge of the wall crosses the upper
part of the picture with a strip of bleached sky above it, and standing
above the wall further back are the heads of two tall trees: bare stems
with no branches on them at all and all the green in a burst at the very
top, leaning together in the wind. The light is a high white southern
glare that comes down out of that sky and up again off the pale ground,
so that the shadows are short and weak, there is no dark side to his
face, and there is no deep shadow anywhere in the picture.

Composition: the viewer stands in the road about three strides from him
and low, at about the height of his knee. Across the bottom third of the
frame, running from the near left corner away to the right along the
base of the wall, is an open channel of cut stone. The boy stands beyond
it on the far side, at the right of the frame, turned three-quarters
toward the viewer with his head bent down over the water. The bottom
edge of the picture crosses him just below the knee. He is the only
person in the picture and the road behind him is empty.

THE CHANNEL IS THE SUBJECT OF THE IMAGE. It is cut stone, straight-sided
and square, about as wide as his foot is long, and it is brim-full and
moving fast. A stone lip a hand high crosses it near the left of the
frame, and the water stands over that lip in one unbroken curved sheet
with white broken water below it. At the far left, in the base of the
wall, the channel goes in under the road through a low square-cut arch
no higher than a large dog, and the inside of that arch is completely
black.

He has stopped walking. Both feet are flat, both hands are empty and
hanging at his sides. He is not touching the water and he is not
drinking.

HIS EYES ARE ON THE WATER where it goes over the lip. His head is bent,
his brows are lifted a little, and his cracked lips are slightly parted.
He is not looking at the viewer.

Across his back, slung on a length of plain cord, is a grown man's war
sword — straight, broad, undecorated, nearly as long as he is tall —
with the grip standing up past one shoulder. Two plain short blades that
do not match, one heavier than the other, are through his belt.

A month at sea is on him: he is thin, his lips are cracked open, the
corners of his mouth are split, and a red rash runs the length of both
forearms. Dried salt sits pale in his eyebrows and along his hairline.
Nothing bleeding. His clothing is undyed coarse working wool, sound and
much mended, in dull oatmeal and dead olive, stiff and pale with dried
salt and filthy at the hem — worn hard, not ragged, and not a beggar's
clothing.

He is fourteen — narrow through the shoulders, adolescent, not grown.

Colour: the road, the ground and the wall in dust-brown, dull ochre and
pale bone; the green only at the top of the trees; the water grey-green
and white. The sharpest step from light to dark in the picture is the
black mouth of the arch against the moving water. His face is fully
drawn and held at closer, quieter values, with soft edges.

Preserve his face, hair, colouring and apparent age exactly as shown in
the reference image. Pale grey eyes.
```
